### [1] [Prompt Chaining in Practice: A Case Study in Automated Scholarly Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27210)
*Andrei Lazarev*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates a multi-stage prompt chaining approach for automatic scholarly digests and shows it is more reliable and higher-quality than a strong single-shot prompt baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The volume of scholarly publications is growing rapidly, making manual synthesis impractical. Existing single-shot prompting with LLMs is often unreliable and inconsistent for complex, multi-step synthesis tasks. The authors are motivated to find a more robust prompting architecture that can handle these complex workflows more dependably.

Method: The authors design a multi-stage prompt chaining methodology and implement it in a system called AI SciBrief for generating scholarly digests. They then run a comparative experiment between this chained approach and a carefully optimized single-shot baseline. Both systems are tasked with generating a report in the Education domain, and their outputs are compared against a human-written gold-standard report using reliability measures (success/failure rate) and ROUGE-L F1 for quality, including precision and recall components.

Result: The prompt chaining method achieved a 100% success rate, while the optimized single-shot baseline failed in 50% of runs. In quality metrics, the chained approach obtained a higher ROUGE-L F1 score (0.507 vs. 0.486), with the improvement mainly due to higher precision, indicating more accurate coverage of gold-standard content.

Conclusion: Prompt chaining offers a more dependable and effective engineering pattern for complex, multi-step generative tasks than monolithic, single-shot prompts. It substantially reduces failure and inconsistency and yields better content quality, suggesting that structured, staged prompting should be preferred for sophisticated synthesis applications like scholarly digests.

Abstract: The exponential growth of scholarly publications requires automated tools for effective information synthesis. However, simple, single-shot prompting methods often lack the reliability and quality required for complex synthesis tasks. This paper introduces and empirically evaluates a multi-stage prompt chaining methodology as a more reliable architectural pattern for such tasks. This approach is implemented in our system, AI SciBrief, which automatically generates scholarly digests. We conducted a comparative experiment, measuring the performance of our prompt chaining method against a carefully optimized single-shot baseline. Both systems were evaluated against a human-authored "gold standard" report for the "Education" domain. The results demonstrate a significant difference in reliability: our prompt chaining method achieved a 100% success rate, whereas the optimized baseline failed in 50% of its runs. In terms of quality, the proposed method also demonstrated a clear advantage, achieving a superior ROUGE-L F1-score (0.507 vs. 0.486), driven primarily by higher precision. We conclude that prompt chaining is a more dependable and effective engineering approach for complex, multi-step generative tasks, significantly mitigating the risks of failure and inconsistency inherent in monolithic prompts.

</details>


### [2] [AI-assisted pre-review of open-source software submissions: an experience report from BOSC 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27228)
*Tazro Ohta,Nomi L. Harris,Seth Carbon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper explores using generative AI tools to pre-review conference abstracts for specific criteria to reduce reviewer workload, while keeping humans as final decision makers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conferences are experiencing a surge in submissions, partly due to generative AI making it easier to produce materials. This increases the burden on volunteer reviewers. The authors aim to see whether generative AI can help by automatically checking key criteria (like openness and runnability), thus streamlining the review process without replacing human judgment.

Method: Using the Bioinformatics Open Source Conference (BOSC) as a testbed, the authors implemented an agentic AI-based pre-review system called bosc-pre-review and a companion tool, Runabilly. bosc-pre-review automatically assessed six predefined review criteria derived from BOSC’s existing rubric, focusing on aspects such as openness, open-source licensing, and runnability. Runabilly attempted to build and run each project in a disposable Docker container to safely verify technical runnability. The AI system only collected and summarized evidence; human reviewers then used this evidence during their normal review and later completed a survey on its usefulness.

Result: The AI pre-review successfully generated evidence on the six criteria for submitted abstracts, and human reviewers retained full control over acceptance decisions. Survey responses indicated that most reviewers found the AI pre-review helpful as a support tool. However, reviewers consistently preferred to verify and cross-check the AI’s assessments rather than rely on them blindly.

Conclusion: Generative AI-based pre-review can be a useful assistant for conference abstract evaluation, particularly for checking structured criteria like openness, licensing, and runnability. However, reviewers want to treat AI outputs as suggestive evidence, not as authoritative judgments. Effective integration of such tools should therefore emphasize human oversight and verification rather than automation of final decisions.

Abstract: Most conferences rely on peer-review of submissions, but as generative AI makes it easier than ever to prepare submission materials, some conferences are seeing an overwhelming surge of submissions. We wanted to see if generative AI could help our conference's volunteer reviewers by pre-reviewing abstracts for certain criteria. The Bioinformatics Open Source Conference (BOSC) was well-positioned to experiment with this, as we already had a detailed rubric used by reviewers to evaluate submitted abstracts on multiple criteria, including openness (public availability of the code or other content associated with the project), valid open source license, and "runnability" (how easy it is to download, build, and run the project - an important measure of reusability). For BOSC 2026, we built bosc-pre-review, an agentic skill that assessed six review criteria, and Runabilly, which builds and tests each project in a disposable Docker container for safety. The AI only gathered evidence to present to the reviewers; humans made every decision regarding the acceptance of the abstracts. After the review period, we surveyed the reviewers to determine how useful they found the pre-review. Most of those who responded said they found it useful, but they preferred to check the AI's conclusions against their own, rather than accepting the AI results unquestioningly.

</details>


### [3] [Sympathetic Framing: Evaluating AI Alignment across Sociodemographic Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27232)
*Haran Shani-Narkiss,Michael Fire,Oren Tsur*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper assesses how closely various large language models’ emotional interpretations of politically framed news headlines match those of humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs increasingly mediate information consumption and shape worldviews, it is critical to understand not only their factual accuracy and bias but also whether they capture and potentially influence emotional framing, especially in sensitive domains like political and geopolitical conflicts.

Method: The authors collected a large, demographically representative sample of U.K. adults (n=3011) through a YouGov survey and asked them, along with seven different LLMs, to judge whether specific conflict-related news headlines evoke sympathy for a particular side. They then computed correlations between model and human judgments overall and across demographic subgroups (age, gender, education, geopolitical knowledge, and prior conflict predispositions).

Result: Correlations between LLM and human sympathy judgments span a wide range across models, from very high (0.789 for GPT-5.2) to moderate (0.4 for Mistral Large 2512). Leading models show broad alignment with human perceptions across all examined demographic groups, but there are statistically significant subgroup differences, indicating that alignment is not uniform.

Conclusion: The study provides a comprehensive empirical evaluation showing that while state-of-the-art LLMs can closely mirror aggregate human emotional responses to news framing, their alignment varies across demographic and cultural lines. This highlights the need to consider differential alignment when designing and deploying ethical and socially responsible AI systems, as overlooking it may lead to uneven or biased impacts on different population segments.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly shaping how we consume information and form our worldview. This raises concerns beyond bias in AI: do LLMs grasp the emotional nuances conveyed via textual framing? In this work, we empirically evaluate how well an array of LLMs aligns with human emotional perception. Considering news headlines covering political and geopolitical conflicts, both human participants (n = 3011, a representative sample of the U.K. adult population, via a YouGov survey) and seven LLMs answered whether headlines evoked sympathy for a specified side in a conflict. We find that the correlation between AI and human evaluations varies across models, ranging from very high (0.789, GPT-5.2) to medium (0.4 ,Mistral Large 2512). Crucially, the leading models are broadly aligned with human judgments across all demographic subgroups, including age, gender, level of education, prior geopolitical knowledge, and participants' predispositions regarding the conflict, although there are statistically significant differences between groups. This research, with its robust design and large, demographically diverse dataset, offers the most comprehensive evaluation of LLMs' comprehension of news framing to date. Findings highlight an important, often-ignored aspect of differential alignment: even when aggregate performance is high, AI alignment is not universal -- it may correspond differently with demographic features and cultural norms. Considering or ignoring the need for differential alignment may therefore have significant implications for the development of ethical and useful AI systems.

</details>


### [4] [LayerRAG-Bench: A Cross-Layer Reliability Benchmark for Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27353)
*Musa Shams*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a benchmark, LayerRAG-Bench, to evaluate reliability of agentic retrieval-augmented generation systems across multiple system layers and shows that fixes effective at one layer do not generalize to others, motivating layer-specific evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic RAG systems can produce seemingly well-grounded answers while actually failing at different layers, such as evidence correctness, tool interaction contracts, permissions, and session state. Existing evaluations often focus only on groundedness and overlook cross-layer reliability, which can lead to overestimating system robustness and misattributing the impact of interventions. The authors aim to provide a controlled way to measure where and how these systems fail and how specific interventions affect particular layers.

Method: The authors construct LayerRAG-Bench, a controlled benchmark spanning 8 enterprise domains and 240 tasks. They design 9 fault scenarios affecting different layers (e.g., schema drift, stale evidence, missing tool outputs, permission denial, wrong-session context) and test 2 tool-contract modes. They run nine different large language models from OpenAI, Anthropic, and Gemini on these tasks, collecting 38,880 task-level records. They then analyze how models behave under each fault scenario and how reliability interventions (like schema normalization) impact performance across layers, as well as how groundedness-only evaluation behaves under these conditions.

Result: Schema normalization significantly improves success under schema-drift faults, raising performance from 0.000 to 0.913, showing that targeted interventions can be highly effective at specific layers. However, the same intervention does not resolve other types of faults, such as stale evidence, missing tool output, denied permissions, or wrong-session context. Moreover, evaluations that only check groundedness generate many false positives when evidence is stale or belongs to the wrong session, indicating that such metrics can misrepresent true reliability.

Conclusion: Reliability in agentic RAG systems is inherently layered, and interventions tend to be layer-specific rather than universal. Evaluations that focus solely on groundedness or that do not distinguish between system layers can be misleading and over-credit certain fixes. The authors advocate a layer-specific evaluation principle: each reliability intervention should be assessed based on its impact on the particular layer it targets, without assuming broader or cross-layer benefits. LayerRAG-Bench provides a structured way to perform such nuanced evaluations across realistic enterprise scenarios.

Abstract: Agentic retrieval-augmented generation systems can produce answers that appear grounded while failing at the evidence, tool-contract, authorization, or session-state layer. We introduce LayerRAG-Bench, a controlled cross-layer reliability benchmark with 8 enterprise domains, 240 tasks, 9 fault scenarios, 2 contract modes, and 38,880 live task-level records across nine models from OpenAI, Anthropic, and Gemini. Schema normalization raises schema-drift success from 0.000 to 0.913, but stale evidence, missing tool output, denied permissions, and wrong-session context are not recovered by schema normalization. Groundedness-only evaluation also produces substantial false positives under stale and wrong-session evidence. These results support a layer-specific evaluation principle: a reliability intervention should be credited for repairing its target layer without being mistaken for a universal fix.

</details>


### [5] [BridgeAlign: Bridging Preference Alignment for Humanities and Social Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27366)
*Ru Peng,Haokai Xu,Xijun Gu,Tianyu Zhao,Zhiting Fan,Yawen Zeng,Yihong Zhuang,Jinyang Zhang,Kexin Yang,Jian Wu,Hao Chen,Junyang Lin,Dayiheng Liu,Junbo Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BridgeAlign is a preference-alignment pipeline for large language models targeting broad humanities and social sciences tasks, using curated HSS seeds, synthetic persona-based preference data, and rubric-grounded quality degradation to improve nuanced judgment without sacrificing knowledge ability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most synthetic data and alignment methods for LLMs focus on domains with verifiable answers (e.g., STEM, coding), but open-ended humanities and social sciences need nuanced quality judgments rather than simple correctness. Existing preference-alignment methods are expensive or not tailored to broad HSS disciplines. The authors want a scalable, systematic way to align models for HSS tasks while maintaining knowledge-based performance.

Method: They propose BridgeAlign, a three-phase pipeline. (i) Seed Curation: select and refine humanities and social sciences documents from web corpora using heuristic and LLM-based filters. (ii) Preference Data Synthesis: create synthetic preference triplets by inverting instructions from personas and enforcing Q&A consistency checks. (iii) Preference Optimization: instead of just preferring human over model output, they ground preferences in an HSS quality rubric and then generate transitional responses through controlled quality degradation, yielding near-boundary preference pairs that allow finer-grained discrimination during training. They then align a Qwen3-8B model on 210k such synthetic preference samples.

Result: After training Qwen3-8B with ~210k synthetic preference samples from BridgeAlign, the model achieves the best average performance across 17 benchmarks compared to 11 strong baselines. It improves both human-preference metrics and knowledge-based benchmarks simultaneously, without the usual trade-off between alignment and capabilities, as demonstrated by experiments and theoretical discussion.

Conclusion: BridgeAlign offers an effective and relatively scalable preference-alignment framework specifically designed for broad HSS disciplines. By using curated HSS seeds, persona-based synthetic preferences, and rubric-guided quality degradation to form fine-grained preference pairs, it enables an 8B-parameter model to outperform strong baselines on diverse benchmarks. The key takeaway is that nuanced preference alignment for HSS can be achieved without sacrificing knowledge capabilities, challenging the assumption of an inherent trade-off between human preference alignment and task performance.

Abstract: While data synthesis for large language models (LLMs) is prevalent, it primarily targets domains with verifiable answers, overlooking open-ended humanities and social sciences (HSS), where nuanced quality judgments matter more than objective correctness. This makes preference alignment a natural paradigm for broad HSS tasks. Yet existing methods are either costly or not tailored to broad HSS disciplines. We thus propose BridgeAlign, among the first preference-alignment pipelines for broad HSS disciplines, with three phases: i) Seed Curation: curating HSS seed documents from web corpora via heuristic/LLM-based filtering and text refinement; ii) Preference Data Synthesis: generating preference triplets via persona-based instruction inversion with Q&A consistency checks; iii) Preference Optimization: moving beyond naive human-vs-model heuristics by first grounding preferences in HSS quality rubric, then generating transitional responses via controlled quality degradation to form near-boundary preference pairs for finer-grained quality discrimination. Aligning over 210k synthetic preference samples, BridgeAlign enables Qwen3-8B to achieve the best average across 17 benchmarks against 11 strong baselines; importantly, leading on both human-preference and knowledge-based capabilities at once, with no trade-off between them, as supported by extensive experiments and contextualized by existing theories.

</details>


### [6] [HSS-Synth: Humanities and Social Sciences Data Synthesis for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27379)
*Ru Peng,Tianyu Zhao,Xijun Gu,Zhiting Fan,Haokai Xu,Jinyang Zhang,Yawen Zeng,Yihong Zhuang,Kexin Yang,Junyang Lin,Dayiheng Liu,Junbo Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes HSS-Synth, a subject-centric data synthesis pipeline that generates high-quality instruction-tuning data for humanities and social sciences (HSS), enabling smaller LLMs to reach or surpass state-of-the-art performance on HSS benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-quality and diverse training data are essential for LLMs, but such data are especially scarce and expensive in humanities and social sciences. Existing data synthesis methods focus on closed tasks and capability-centric setups, under-serving open-ended, nuanced HSS tasks. The authors aim to create a systematic way to synthesize reliable, diverse HSS data to improve LLM performance in these domains.

Method: They design HSS-Synth, a three-stage pipeline under a subject-centric domain system covering 14 HSS fields. (1) Seed Document Construction: extract documents from web corpora, apply multi-step filtering and refinement, and use an LLM judge to assess quality. (2) Instruction Backtranslation: define “requirements + persona” prompts to convert seed documents into diverse but faithful instruction–answer pairs, with strict Q&A alignment checks. (3) Teacher-Forced Answering: overcome LLM context/response limits by feeding seed documents during answer generation to anchor semantics, reduce hallucinations, and preserve tone and integrity. They then fine-tune Qwen3-8B-Base with the resulting 237k samples and evaluate across multiple benchmarks and baselines.

Result: HSS-Synth generates 237k high-quality instruction-tuning samples in 14 HSS fields. Models fine-tuned on this data outperform 14 strong baselines on 16 benchmarks. Qwen3-8B-Base fine-tuned with HSS-Synth reaches new state-of-the-art results for its class and approaches the performance of the official Qwen3-8B model, with consistent gains in both human preference and knowledge-oriented tasks and without trade-off effects. Experiments indicate robustness across setups and transferability beyond the original training conditions.

Conclusion: A subject-centric, document-anchored synthesis pipeline can effectively produce high-quality HSS training data, overcoming the open-ended and nuanced nature of these domains. HSS-Synth not only improves HSS performance but also yields general knowledge and preference gains for LLMs, establishing a practical route to scaling HSS capabilities without massive human-labeled datasets. The approach is robust, transferable, and reproducible via the released codebase.

Abstract: High-quality, diverse data are vital for large language models (LLMs) but remain scarce and costly. Data synthesis is a viable alternative and succeeds on closed tasks, yet the humanities and social sciences (HSS) are overlooked, and their open-ended nature makes synthesis challenging. Moving beyond prior capability-centric, fragmented attempts, we adopt a subject-centric paradigm, define the first HSS domain system covering 14 mainstream fields, and introduce HSS-Synth, the first data synthesis pipeline for HSS. HSS-Synth comprises: (1) constructing seed documents from web corpora via multi-step filtering and text refinement evaluated by a judge; (2) specifying "requirements + persona" to backtranslate seed documents into diverse yet faithful instructions with a strict Q&A alignment check; and (3) breaking LLM response limits via teacher-forced Answering that feeds seed documents during response generation to anchor semantics, reduce hallucinations, and preserve tone and integrity. HSS-Synth yields 237k high-quality, diverse instruction-tuning samples that outperform 14 leading baselines on 16 benchmarks. The fine-tuned Qwen3-8B-Base sets a new SOTA and approaches the official Qwen3-8B, improving both human preference and knowledge capabilities without performance seesaws. Extensive experiments demonstrate HSS-Synth's robustness and transferability. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/pengr/HSS-Synth.

</details>


### [7] [Same Facts, Different Diagnosis: Measuring and Mitigating Narrative Anchoring in Clinical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27384)
*Prabhjot Singh,Pritam Deka,Vijay Chennareddy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that large language models’ diagnostic outputs vary systematically when identical clinical facts are written in different sociolinguistic registers, and proposes a multi‑agent pipeline (NarrativeShield) that largely removes this bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether clinical LLMs are biased not only by explicit demographic markers (e.g., race or income) but also by differences in how patients speak or write—their sociolinguistic register—which could unfairly affect diagnostic decisions even when clinical facts are identical.

Method: The authors build a benchmark of 1,000 USMLE-style clinical vignettes, each rewritten into three distinct sociolinguistic personas, under strict, independently audited fact-preservation. A separate verification model, blind to the prompts, checks that the variants differ only in register. They then evaluate seven LLMs from three architecture families using direct prompting, chain-of-thought, and explicit debiasing instructions, quantifying how diagnostic outputs diverge across registers with a Narrative Anchoring Gap and a decision stability score (DSS). Finally, they design NarrativeShield, a three-agent pipeline that first extracts and verifies clinical facts, then performs diagnosis, and test its impact on bias and accuracy, including a stress test on a non-instruction-tuned base model.

Result: All seven tested models exhibit statistically significant Narrative Anchoring under direct prompting, with Narrative Anchoring Gaps between 0.064 and 0.151, meaning diagnostic outputs vary with register despite identical facts. Chain-of-thought and instruction-based debiasing only partially reduce this gap and often introduce an accuracy collapse that confounds apparent gains. NarrativeShield brings the Narrative Anchoring Gap close to zero (−0.004 to 0.037) and yields the lowest proportion of severely unstable decisions (DSS < 0.8) across models, with a modest accuracy cost that aligns with the pipeline’s design. The stress test demonstrates that effective debiasing requires some baseline zero-shot instruction-following ability, not just clever prompt wording.

Conclusion: LLMs used for clinical diagnosis are vulnerable to a previously underexplored bias source—sociolinguistic register—leading to divergent decisions for fact-identical cases. Traditional prompt-level debiasing methods are insufficient and can harm accuracy, while a structured multi-agent approach that separates fact extraction from reasoning (NarrativeShield) substantially stabilizes decisions, albeit with a small accuracy trade-off. The released, fact-preserved dataset offers a new benchmark to study and mitigate register-based bias in clinical and other safety-critical language-model applications.

Abstract: Large language models used for clinical diagnostic reasoning are sensitive to sociolinguistic register, not just clinical content. We term this failure mode Narrative Anchoring: identical clinical facts expressed in different registers cause diagnostic outputs to diverge. Unlike prior demographic-bias work, which manipulates explicit identity tokens such as race or income, our benchmark isolates register as the sole channel of variation, with no demographic marker present in any form. We construct a dataset of 1,000 USMLE clinical vignettes, each rewritten into three sociolinguistically distinct personas under an independently audited fact-preservation guarantee, verified by a separate model that never sees the generation prompt. Across seven language models spanning three architecture families and scales, Narrative Anchoring is statistically significant under direct prompting in every model tested, with a Narrative Anchoring Gap of 0.064 to 0.151. Chain-of-thought reasoning and explicit debiasing instructions reduce the bias only partially, and their apparent gains are frequently confounded by accuracy collapse. We introduce NarrativeShield, a three-agent pipeline that structurally extracts and verifies clinical facts before diagnostic reasoning begins, reducing the Narrative Anchoring Gap to near-zero ($-0.004$ to $0.037$) and achieving the lowest rate of severely unstable decisions (DSS $<$ 0.8) of any method across all models, at a modest and mechanistically expected accuracy cost for most models. A stress test using a non-instruction-tuned base model shows that executing a debiasing intervention at all is gated by zero-shot instruction-following ability, not prompt content alone. We release our dataset, human-validated for fact preservation, as a standalone resource for studying register-based clinical bias.

</details>


### [8] [AHA-Memes: A Fine-Grained Multimodal Benchmark for Understanding Hate in Arabic Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27393)
*Mohamed Bayan Kmainasi,Ali Ezzat Shahroor,Abul Hasnat,Md. Rafiul Biswas,Wajdi Zaghouani,Firoj Alam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces AHA-Memes, the first large-scale, fine-grained Arabic hateful meme benchmark, and establishes baseline multimodal models and evaluation resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hateful memes, which combine images, text, and cultural context, are an increasing form of online harm. Despite progress for high-resource languages, Arabic hateful memes are under-studied, with existing datasets focusing on propaganda or broad harmful categories rather than detailed hate types. There is a need for a culturally grounded, fine-grained resource for Arabic hateful meme detection.

Method: The authors construct AHA-Memes, a dataset of 5,000 manually annotated Arabic memes using a taxonomy capturing hate types and attack strategies, plus about 66,000 silver-labeled memes. They benchmark a range of models: text-only, image-only, and late-fusion multimodal architectures, as well as few-shot in-context learning with large models and both open- and closed-weight vision-language models (VLMs) under zero-shot and fine-tuning setups. They also provide annotation guidelines and evaluation scripts.

Result: The benchmarks provide strong baselines across multiple modeling paradigms, revealing that Arabic hateful meme detection remains challenging, especially due to cultural grounding and multimodal reasoning requirements. The dataset and experimental results highlight where models struggle and where multimodal or advanced VLM approaches help.

Conclusion: AHA-Memes fills a major gap by providing the first fine-grained benchmark for Arabic hateful memes, supporting systematic research on multimodal hate detection in a low-resource language. The released data, guidelines, and code aim to catalyze future work on more robust and culturally aware hateful content detection for Arabic memes.

Abstract: Hateful memes are a growing form of multimodal online harm, where hostile intent is often conveyed through the joint interpretation of images, text, cultural references, and implicit targets. While hateful meme detection has advanced in high-resource languages, Arabic remains underexplored, with existing meme resources focusing mainly on propaganda or coarse harmful-content labels. We introduce AHA-Memes (Arabic HAteful Memes), which is, to our knowledge, the first large-scale Arabic hateful meme benchmark with fine-grained, multi-label annotations. The dataset includes 5K manually annotated memes using a taxonomy that captures hate types, i.e., attack strategies. We further provide ~66K silver-labeled memes to support future studies. We benchmark text-only, image-only, and late-fusion multimodal models, as well as few-shot in-context learning (ICL) and open- and closed-weight Vision-Language Models (VLMs) under zero-shot and fine-tuning settings. Our results establish strong baselines and highlight key challenges in culturally grounded Arabic hateful meme detection. We release the dataset, annotation guidelines, and evaluation scripts to support future research. WARNING: This paper contains examples that may be disturbing to readers.

</details>


### [9] [Benchmarking LLM Competence on Logical Inference over Probability Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27405)
*Nayera Hasan,Jack Greff,Alvin Grissom*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a large benchmark to test how well language models reason with probabilistic expressions like “probably,” “might,” and “must,” and finds that most models fail to reach even basic reliability and show strong answer biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural language frequently uses expressions of uncertainty (e.g., probably, might), and correctly reasoning with them is crucial in everyday conversation and in high‑stakes areas such as medicine and law. Current evaluations of large language models on logical reasoning do not clearly separate genuine symbolic reasoning from surface pattern matching. There is a need for a controlled, large‑scale benchmark that focuses specifically on probabilistic and epistemic language to rigorously test these reasoning abilities and reveal systematic biases.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark of 14,320 procedurally generated English prompts targeting reasoning over probability operators, specifically gradable epistemic modals (e.g., probably, might, must). They design fifteen inference templates and systematically vary dimensions such as question form (e.g., polar questions vs. others), negation strategy, and surface content. They then evaluate 29 different language models on this benchmark. Performance is analyzed via overall accuracy patterns, answer biases (tendencies to respond Yes or No regardless of logical form), and a new metric called the competence floor, defined as the minimum of the model’s accuracy on items where the correct answer is Yes and where it is No. They also probe biases by varying verb phrases/activities and the gender and origin of names within prompts.

Result: Most of the 29 evaluated models display strong answer biases that are largely independent of the underlying logical form of the prompts, often favoring Yes or No systematically. Using the competence floor metric, only 9 of the 29 models achieve performance above random chance, indicating that the vast majority cannot reliably reason with probability operators in this setting. Furthermore, experiments that vary question format, verb phrases, and the gender and origin of names reveal consistent biases across all these dimensions, suggesting that model outputs are heavily influenced by superficial prompt characteristics rather than purely by formal semantics of uncertainty expressions.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current language models generally lack robust, principled reasoning over natural‑language expressions of uncertainty, instead displaying strong yes/no answer biases and sensitivity to superficial prompt variations. The competence floor metric shows that only a minority of models surpass random‑chance reliability on this benchmark. The observed biases across question forms, content, and demographic attributes underscore that models’ treatment of probabilistic language is fragile and systematically skewed. The benchmark thus exposes significant gaps between surface‑level pattern matching and genuine logical competence with epistemic modals, highlighting a need for improved model architectures, training regimes, and evaluation methods focused on probabilistic and epistemic reasoning in natural language.

Abstract: Both expressions of uncertainty and inferences are ubiquitous in natural language, and valid inferences over natural-language expressions of uncertainty are necessary for not only everyday conversations but also for high-stakes domains such as medicine and law. While large language models are increasingly evaluated on logical reasoning tasks, disentangling principled, symbolic reasoning from clever surface-level pattern matching is fraught with difficulty. We introduce a benchmark for reasoning over probability operators--inference over sentences with gradable epistemic modals (e.g., probably, might, must) containing 14,320 procedurally-generated English prompts across fifteen inference templates, systematically varying question form, negation strategy, and surface content. Evaluating 29 models, we find that most show answer biases independent of the logical form, a systematic preference for Yes or No. We summarize this with a competence floor: the worse of a model's accuracy on Yes-correct and No-correct items. Only 9 of 29 models exceed random chance. We also test variations in question form, verb phrases/activity, and both the gender and origin of names used in the prompts, finding biases across every axis.

</details>


### [10] [Selecting Open-Weight Language Models for Zero-Shot Intent Classification: A Systematic Evaluation of 41 Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27421)
*Parishruthi Ganesh,Gerry Dozier,Cheryl Seals*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper systematically compares 41 open-weight language models for intent classification, focusing on real-world deployability under compute, latency, and robustness constraints, and finds small, instruction-tuned 3B models can rival or beat larger 7B base models while many classic benchmarks are now saturated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners lack clear, empirical guidance on which open-weight language models to choose for intent classification in real-world dialogue systems, given trade-offs in accuracy, robustness, calibration, and efficiency. Existing benchmarks and evaluations are fragmented, may be saturated, and often ignore deployment factors like latency and statistical reliability of model rankings.

Method: The authors perform a large-scale, primarily zero-shot evaluation of 41 open-weight language models (15 families, 135M–9B parameters) on eight English single-label intent-classification datasets, plus a five-shot setting on ATIS. They assess not only exact-match accuracy but also confidence calibration, robustness to realistic input perturbations, statistical significance of performance differences via pairwise McNemar tests, deployment efficiency under compute/latency constraints, and evidence of benchmark saturation across datasets including standard benchmarks, a large voice-assistant corpus, and production-like e-commerce data.

Result: Instruction-tuned 3B-parameter models outperform some evaluated 7B base models on intent classification tasks. On the MASSIVE dataset, differences among top-performing models are statistically indistinguishable under pairwise McNemar tests, indicating that apparent ranking differences are not reliable. Popular benchmarks like SNIPS show near-ceiling performance across models, indicating saturation and limited discriminative power. Instruction tuning’s impact on confidence calibration varies by model and is not uniformly detrimental.

Conclusion: Smaller, instruction-tuned open-weight language models can be strong, practical choices for intent classification, sometimes outperforming larger base models while being more efficient to deploy. Many classic intent benchmarks (e.g., SNIPS) no longer differentiate modern open-weight models, so practitioners should rely on harder or more realistic datasets and consider robustness, calibration, and efficiency—not just accuracy—when selecting models. The study offers empirical evidence and recommendations to guide model selection and evaluation in real-world task-oriented dialogue systems.

Abstract: Intent classification is a core component of task-oriented dialogue systems, yet practitioners have limited systematic guidance for selecting deployable open-weight language models under compute, latency, and robustness constraints. We present a systematic zero-shot evaluation of 41 open-weight language models spanning 15 families and the 135M--9B parameter range across eight English single-label intent-classification datasets. A ninth dataset, ATIS, uses five labeled demonstrations and is reported as an auxiliary five-shot result. The evaluation includes standard benchmarks, a large-scale voice-assistant corpus, and production-derived e-commerce datasets. Beyond exact-match accuracy, we analyze confidence calibration, robustness to realistic input perturbations, statistical reliability of model rankings, deployment efficiency, and benchmark saturation. Our results show that instruction-tuned 3B models can outperform several evaluated 7B base models, that differences among leading models on MASSIVE are statistically indistinguishable under pairwise McNemar tests, and that widely used benchmarks such as SNIPS have become saturated and no longer meaningfully discriminate among current open-weight models. Instruction tuning's effect on confidence calibration is inconsistent rather than uniformly harmful. These findings provide practical guidance for selecting and evaluating open-weight language models for intent classification.

</details>


### [11] [SkillSmith: Learning to Compose Parametric Skills and Textual Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27497)
*Lucio M. Dery,Benedict Aaron Tjandra,Siavash Samiei,Adhiguna Kuncoro,Zohar Yahav,Jiajun Shen,Arthur Szlam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SkillSmith, an LLM-based system that can directly generate new parametric skills (prefix weights) by jointly reasoning over text descriptions and existing skill weights, achieving better performance than using text or weights alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent systems either (1) operate on text—composing, reflecting, and retrieving textual knowledge—or (2) operate on model parameters—merging or tuning weights to form reusable skills. These two directions have been treated as separate, which limits the ability to precisely and efficiently acquire new capabilities that are tightly grounded in both prior skills and rich textual descriptions. The authors aim to close this gap by enabling an LLM to treat model weights themselves as a modality it can understand and manipulate, so that new skills can be synthesized in a targeted, instruction-guided way.

Method: The authors instantiate parametric skills as prefix-tuning adapters (trainable prefix weights added to the base LLM). They then augment an LLM—called SkillSmith—to take as input (i) existing prefix weights corresponding to known skills and (ii) related textual information, such as descriptions and relationships to a desired new capability. SkillSmith jointly reasons over these multimodal inputs and outputs a new set of prefix weights that is intended to encode the requested target skill. This is framed as instruction-steered parametric synthesis, where natural language instructions govern how existing parametric and textual knowledge should be recombined into a new prefix.

Result: SkillSmith achieves performance improvements over baselines that use only text-based mechanisms (e.g., composition or reflection over instructions) or only weight-space mechanisms (e.g., direct merging of prefixes) when evaluated on tasks that require acquiring or adapting skills. The gains indicate that combining text and parametric information in a unified generative process yields skills that neither modality alone can match, especially for more complex or targeted capabilities.

Conclusion: Treating model weights as a first-class modality for LLM reasoning enables more powerful and precise skill synthesis. By using an LLM (SkillSmith) to jointly process textual descriptions and parametric prefixes, the system can directly generate new prefix weights encoding desired capabilities, outperforming uni-modal approaches. This demonstrates a promising direction for tightly integrated text–parameter learning in agentic LLM systems and suggests that future models can more seamlessly fuse symbolic (text) and parametric knowledge to autonomously build and refine skill libraries.

Abstract: Agentic systems driven by large language models (LLMs) regularly feature two key mechanisms to autonomously solve complex problems: synthesizing text-based knowledge and procedures from past experiences and building parametric (weight-space) skill libraries for recurring sub-goals. To date, research has largely treated these as orthogonal pursuits: either organizing textual knowledge through composition and reflection, or consolidating parametric skills via weight-space merging. Consequently, the seamless integration of text and model weights for targeted performance improvements remains largely unexplored. This work bridges this modality gap by treating model weights as an additional modality that an LLM can natively reason over. We instantiate parametric learning via prefix-tuning and augment an LLM to ingest both prefix weights and rich textual data which capture relationships to a target capability. Our augmented LLM, which we call SkillSmith, synthesizes these inputs to perform instruction-steered parametric synthesis, directly outputting new prefix weights that manifest the target skill. We demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms both text-only and weight-space-only baselines, unlocking performance gains that are out of reach for uni-modal (text-only or weight-only) adaptations.

</details>


### [12] [Models for minimalist RAG: B1ade 335M Embedding and 1B Parameter Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27506)
*Shreyas Subramanian,Mecit Gungor,Vikram Elango*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes B1ade, a resource-efficient RAG system combining a compact retrieval model and a small language model trained with RL, showing strong QA performance and emergent citation behavior without explicit grounding supervision or large-scale pretraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems typically rely on very large pretrained models and explicit supervision for grounding and citation, which are expensive and resource-intensive. The authors aim to show that high-quality RAG, including grounded answers, can be achieved with much smaller models, modest compute, and simpler reward design, challenging the assumption that large-scale pretraining and explicit grounding labels are necessary.

Method: They design two components: (1) B1ade-embed, a 335M-parameter retrieval model built by parameter-free fusion of five existing pretrained encoders, achieving strong embedding performance without any additional training; (2) B1ade-1B, a ~1B-parameter small language model trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a reinforcement learning method, on 723M tokens of curated context–question pairs. The RL reward is based solely on answer similarity (accuracy) with no explicit signal for citation or attribution. They then evaluate both retrieval quality and end-to-end RAG performance, including how often the model cites its sources and several QA benchmarks (PopQA, PubMedQA, FEVER).

Result: B1ade-embed reaches state-of-the-art MTEB performance among models under 500M parameters without any extra training. B1ade-1B, trained on inexpensive GPUs with 723M tokens, attains competitive QA accuracy: 81.82% on PopQA, 65.8% on PubMedQA, and 51.09% on FEVER. In end-to-end RAG evaluation across dimensions of correctness, completeness, coherence, and faithfulness, B1ade-1B achieves an average score of 0.654, improving 10.8% over a supervised fine-tuned baseline and narrowing the gap to models 1.5× larger. Crucially, the model exhibits emergent attribution behavior: it cites retrieved passages in 42.4% of responses, 5.5 percentage points more frequently than in its training data, despite not receiving any explicit reward for citation.

Conclusion: Strategic composition of smaller, specialized models and a simple RL reward focused solely on answer accuracy are sufficient to build an efficient, competitive RAG system without massive pretraining or explicit grounding supervision. The emergent increase in citation behavior suggests that grounding can naturally arise as an accuracy-maximizing strategy under RL, reducing the need for complex reward engineering and large-scale resources in practical RAG deployments.

Abstract: Language and embedding models used in RAG systems are conventionally assumed to require large-scale pretraining and explicit grounding supervision. We present B1ade, an efficient RAG architecture comprising two purpose-built components: a compact embedding model and a purpose-built SLM. B1ade-embed, a 335M parameter retrieval model constructed via parameter-free fusion of five pretrained encoders achieves top MTEB scores among sub-500M models with zero additional training, and B1ade-1B, an SLM trained on low-cost GPUs using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on 723M tokens (2.2M examples) of curated context-question pairs with rewards that optimize only answer similarity. Our central finding is emergent attribution: despite receiving no explicit supervision for source citation, B1ade-1B cites retrieved passages in 42.4% of responses, exceeding the attribution rate of its training distribution by 5.5 percentage points. This demonstrates that grounding behavior can emerge as an accuracy-maximizing strategy under RL training, without explicit reward engineering. On standard QA benchmarks, B1ade-1B achieves 81.82% on PopQA, 65.8% on PubMedQA, and 51.09% on FEVER. In end-to-end RAG evaluation, B1ade-1B achieves an average score of 0.654 across correctness, completeness, coherence, and faithfulness, a 10.8% improvement over the SFT, while closing the gap with models 1.5x its size. These results show that strategic model composition and reward design suffice for resource-efficient RAG, without large-scale pretraining.

</details>


### [13] [Belief Coevolution in a Social Network of Generalist and Specialist Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27512)
*Germans Savcisens,Samantha Dies,Courtney Maynard,Tina Eliassi-Rad*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Study of how beliefs spread and converge in networks of interacting LLM agents using a simulation framework (CoevolveSim), focusing on specialization, roles, and network structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used in multi-agent and social-simulation settings, we lack a clear understanding of how their beliefs form, change, and diffuse when they interact with each other, which is crucial for safety, reliability, and design of such systems.

Method: Introduce CoevolveSim, where LLM agents placed in social networks iteratively update their beliefs about medical statements after seeing summaries of neighbors’ beliefs. Systematically vary (1) agent domain expertise (generalist vs finetuned specialist), (2) persona-style social roles, and (3) network topology, and run 1,280 controlled simulations across multiple scenarios and statements. Compare emergent belief dynamics to simple opinion-dynamics models.

Result: Persona-style roles and network structure change how individual agents revise beliefs but barely affect overall population consensus. Adding specialist LLMs significantly increases shifts in consensus and creates stable asymmetries in influence. Simple persistence-based opinion-dynamics models can match collective outcomes in all-generalist populations, but for mixed (heterogeneous) populations, reproducing consensus requires modeling belief composition at the population level and knowing agent identity to predict individual transitions.

Conclusion: To realistically simulate belief diffusion in multi-agent LLM systems, merely prompting generalist models with different personas is insufficient; one needs genuinely diverse underlying LLMs (e.g., specialists with distinct finetuning) to capture the true structure of influence and consensus formation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent environments. However, the processes by which beliefs form and propagate among interacting LLMs remain poorly understood. We introduce CoevolveSim, a framework for studying belief diffusion within networked LLM populations. CoevolveSim allows us to isolate and study three factors: domain specialization, social-role assignment, and social network structure. Within this framework, generalist and specialist LLM agents exchange and revise beliefs. In each round, an LLM agent observes a summary of its neighbors' beliefs before updating its own. We run 1,280 controlled simulations spanning four scenarios, two network structures, and 20 medical-indication statements. We find that persona-style role assignment and network structure reshape individual belief revision but have minimal effect on population-level consensus. In contrast, introducing (finetuned) specialist LLMs more than doubles the shift in consensus and gives rise to consistent asymmetries in exerted influence. We further show that simple persistence-based opinion-dynamics models reproduce collective outcomes in all-generalist LLM populations, whereas heterogeneous LLM populations require population-level belief composition to reproduce consensus and agent identity to predict individual belief transitions. Our results indicate that realistic simulation of belief diffusion in multi-agent LLM systems requires a diverse set of underlying LLMs, not persona prompting alone.

</details>


### [14] [Training Skills Like Parameters via Self-Supervised Semantic Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27557)
*Mo Li,Zixin Yin,Ting Cao,Yunxin Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose an unsupervised, self-evolving agent that improves at tasks like screenwriting by reconstructing high-quality human texts, updating an external skill library instead of LLM weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs underperform human experts on highly specialized, open-ended tasks (e.g., creative screenwriting). Standard post-training methods (SFT, RL) need weight access to closed models and heavy compute, and the acquired knowledge is opaque and tied to a single checkpoint. Agentic continual learning helps but depends on expensive human annotation or unreliable LLM-as-a-judge signals. The authors want a scalable way for agents to continually improve at complex domain tasks without external supervision or model weight updates.

Method: They design an unsupervised, self-evolving agent framework inspired by diffusion’s corruption–reconstruction idea. The system takes high-quality human-written artifacts and generates corrupted/variant versions, then tries to reconstruct the original, using the reconstruction gap as a self-supervised loss signal. Instead of updating LLM parameters, the agent updates an external library of textual skills (e.g., patterns, strategies, or templates) via a contrast between its outputs and the human originals. Training uses a loop analogous to standard NN training (forward, loss, backward), but applied to this skill library and agent behavior, not base model weights.

Result: On short drama screenwriting, the agent autonomously extracts broadly useful writing skills and substantially improves its domain-specific generation quality. The learned skills generalize beyond the specific training artifacts, leading to stronger performance in producing screenplays compared with baseline or non-self-evolving setups.

Conclusion: Unsupervised, self-contrastive reflection over high-quality human texts can drive continual improvement in specialized generative tasks without weight access, human labels, or LLM judges. Maintaining and updating an external skill library provides a transparent, extensible route for agents to internalize complex domain skills and scale to producing high-quality human-like artifacts.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate remarkable general instruction-following capabilities, they often fall short of human experts in highly specialized, open-ended domains such as creative screenwriting. Prior approaches typically adopt post-training, yet both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning require weight access that closed-source frontier models do not offer, and demand heavy compute. Moreover, what is learned is tied to a single checkpoint and cannot be inspected by humans. Recent advancements in agentic continual learning instead attempt to bridge this gap by accumulating external textual skills. However, these methods heavily rely on costly human expert annotations or unreliable LLM-as-a-judge feedback for reflection. To overcome this bottleneck, we propose a novel, unsupervised self-evolving agent framework inspired by the corruption-and-reconstruction paradigm of diffusion models. Instead of relying on explicit external scoring, we leverage existing high-quality human artifacts to construct self-supervised signals. Training then follows the familiar loop of neural network training, forward, loss, and backward, with the loss coming from contrasting the agent's reconstruction against the human original. What is updated is not model weights but an external library of textual skills. We evaluate our framework on the challenging task of short drama screenwriting. Experimental results demonstrate that our method enables the agent to autonomously extract and internalize highly generalizable skills, significantly enhancing its domain-specific generation capabilities. Furthermore, this self-contrastive reflection paradigm offers a scalable pathway for agents to teach themselves the production of complex, high-quality human artifacts, without requiring external supervision.

</details>


### [15] [Beyond Similarity: Grounded Agentic Extraction and Expert-Adjudicated Evaluation of Intertextuality in Classical Chinese Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27595)
*Zhaoji Wang,Wanyu Si,Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose and validate an LLM-based, span-grounded, typology-rich method to detect and characterize fine-grained intertextual reuse, and show it scales to large historical corpora while revealing stable yet increasingly non-literal citation behavior over 18 centuries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational methods for intertextuality mostly output similarity scores and lists of parallel passages, which indicate where textual reuse occurs but not how or why it happens. Scholars need richer, interpretable characterizations of reuse—its form, function, and stance—to support literary-historical analysis. There is also a lack of high-quality, expert-annotated benchmarks and systematic evaluations of LLMs on this nuanced task.

Method: They reconceptualize fine-grained intertextual extraction as an agentic task for LLMs: the model reads two text units in full, then, using a constrained tool interface, must (1) propose instances of reuse, (2) ground each in exact character spans in both texts, and (3) label each instance along five dimensions (form, aspect, source-marking, function, stance). They build a gold-standard dataset by exhaustively comparing the Analects with the Book of Han, pooling candidates from multiple models and having three domain experts adjudicate them into 2,533 intertextual pairs. They then evaluate twelve different LLMs on this benchmark, measuring precision, cost, and confidence calibration, and analyzing expert agreement across the five dimensions. Finally, they scale the best-performing system to all pairwise comparisons in the Twenty-Four Histories, extracting 5,766 intertextual pairs from 65,380 comparisons to study long-term citation patterns.

Result: On the Analects–Book of Han benchmark, the twelve LLMs achieve a precision range of 56–93%, with roughly a 51× difference in monetary cost among models providing comparable quality. Confidence calibration varies, and expert agreement reveals which typological dimensions are reliably annotatable: surface-textual properties are consistent, while intent-heavy dimensions are much less so. When applied to the full Twenty-Four Histories, the method uncovers corpus-level structures (e.g., patterns of citation and reuse) that simple similarity scores cannot. Across eighteen centuries of Chinese historiography, the overall interpretive composition of citation remains stable, but individual quotations become progressively less literal over time.

Conclusion: An LLM acting as an agentic, span-grounded annotator can move computational intertextuality beyond raw similarity toward richly characterized reuse, enabling both fine-grained and corpus-level literary analysis. Their expert-adjudicated benchmark clarifies which aspects of reuse annotation are dependable and which are inherently contentious. Scaling the method to a major historical corpus demonstrates that citation practices maintain a stable overall interpretive profile while drifting toward less literal quotation, aligning with cultural-attraction theories. They provide the protocol and benchmark as resources for future research on computational intertextuality and LLM-based text analysis.

Abstract: Computational approaches to intertextuality have advanced from string matching to neural retrieval, yet their outputs, similarity scores and parallel-passage lists, identify where texts reuse one another without characterizing how or why. We recast fine-grained intertextuality extraction as an agentic task in which a large language model (LLM) reads two text units in full and, through a constrained tool interface, must ground each proposed reuse in exact character spans on both sides and label it under a five-dimension typology of reuse (form, aspect, source-marking, function, stance). We validate the approach on an exhaustive comparison of the Analects with the Book of Han, where three domain experts adjudicate a pooled multi-model candidate set into a benchmark of 2,533 intertextual pairs. Against this standard we study twelve LLMs, reporting precision (56%-93%), a 51$\times$ cost spread at comparable quality, and how well their confidence is calibrated. Expert agreement traces a reliability gradient: dimensions legible on the textual surface are annotated consistently, while those requiring inference of intent are contested, delimiting the claims such annotation supports. Scaling the validated extractor to the full Twenty-Four Histories (65,380 comparisons, 5,766 pairs) recovers corpus-level structure a similarity score cannot express. The interpretive composition of citation shows no systematic change across eighteen centuries, yet the same passage is quoted less and less literally. Stability in the aggregate with drift in the individual case is what a cultural-attraction account expects. We release the extraction protocol and the expert-adjudicated benchmark.

</details>


### [16] [AWARE-FX: An Auditable Knowledge-Guided AI System for Measuring Corporate Foreign-Exchange Hedging Disclosure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27611)
*Qi Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces AWARE-FX, an auditable AI/NLP system that converts narrative annual-report text into firm-year measures of foreign-exchange risk management and hedging disclosure, evaluates the system extensively, and shows that its strict FX score aligns with actual FX exposure patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Corporate annual reports contain rich but weakly structured narrative disclosures about how firms manage foreign-exchange risk, including derivatives, natural hedging, and explicit non-use. Traditional data sources miss much of this information, and existing NLP/ML approaches can be opaque, hard to audit, and may not generalize well over time. Researchers and practitioners need a scalable, transparent, and reliable way to transform this unstructured text into quantified, auditable measures of FX hedging practices that can be linked to exposure data for empirical analysis and decision support.

Method: The authors build AWARE-FX, a pipeline AI/NLP decision-support architecture tailored to FX-hedging disclosures in annual reports. Key design elements include: (1) a professional-source lexicon to identify domain-relevant phrases; (2) negation and accounting-status logic to distinguish use vs non-use and current vs historical or hypothetical statements; (3) channel-specific financial encoders based on FinBERT and ModernBERT for classification tasks; (4) exact evidence gates to ensure only text with clear semantic matches is scored; (5) conservative aggregation rules to construct firm-year metrics from snippet-level predictions; and (6) an audit ledger that records evidence, model outputs, and decisions for traceability. They apply the system to 24,909 Hong Kong firm-year reports (2008–2025), retrieving and scoring over 543k snippets. Reliability is assessed via ablation tests, a 300-snippet human audit, comparisons across encoder variants and random seeds, strict temporal generalization tests (2023–2025), probability calibration and selective prediction (abstaining on low-confidence cases), and benchmarks using a fixed-prompt deterministic generative LLM (Qwen3-8B).

Result: FinBERT-based encoders outperform ModernBERT in seven of eight encoder–task–split F1 comparisons, with temporal F1 scores between 0.702 and 0.872 when tested on later years. Selective prediction, where the system abstains from labeling the 20% least-confident temporal observations, improves F1 on the retained sample by 0.050–0.077, indicating well-calibrated uncertainty can enhance effective reliability. Deterministic Qwen3-8B, used with fixed prompts, performs well for commodity-related and negation evidence detection but poorly for foreign-debt and accounting-context labels, revealing uneven performance of a general-purpose LLM relative to the domain-constrained pipeline. The authors construct both a strict FX hedging score and a more generic broad hedging score; the strict FX score is negatively associated with independently measured FX exposure in both baseline and stress periods, while the broad score shows no such relationship, offering external construct validation of the strict metric.

Conclusion: AWARE-FX demonstrates that a carefully engineered, auditable NLP architecture can reliably extract structured measures of foreign-exchange risk management from large-scale annual-report text. Its modular design keeps retrieval, labeling logic, classification, uncertainty handling, aggregation, and external validation separately testable and auditable, contrasting with opaque end-to-end models. Empirical evaluations highlight the relative strengths of domain-specific encoders (FinBERT), the benefits of selective abstention, and the limitations of relying solely on general-purpose LLMs for specialized financial tasks. The negative association of the strict FX score with independently measured FX exposure supports its interpretation as a valid, though not causal, indicator of FX risk management intensity, providing a foundation for future research and practical decision-support applications in risk management and corporate finance.

Abstract: Corporate annual reports contain weakly structured evidence about foreign-exchange risk management, derivative use, natural hedging, and explicit non-use. This study develops AWARE-FX, an auditable AI/NLP decision-support system that converts report text into traceable firm-year hedging-disclosure measures. The system combines a professional-source lexicon, negation and accounting-status logic, channel-specific financial encoders, exact evidence gates, conservative aggregation, and an audit ledger. Across 24,909 Hong Kong firm-years from 2008-2025, it retrieves and scores 543,527 snippets. Reliability is evaluated through ablations, a stratified 300-snippet human audit, three-seed FinBERT-ModernBERT comparisons, strict 2023-2025 temporal tests, probability calibration, selective prediction, and fixed-prompt generative-model benchmarks. FinBERT has the higher mean F1 in seven of eight encoder task-split comparisons; its temporal F1 ranges from 0.702 to 0.872. Abstaining on the 20% least-confident temporal observations raises retained-sample F1 by 0.050-0.077. Deterministic Qwen3-8B performs strongly on commodity and negation evidence but poorly on foreign-debt and accounting-context labels, showing that a general-purpose LLM does not uniformly replace domain constraints. The strict FX score is negatively associated with linked baseline and stress-period FX exposure, whereas the generic broad score is not. These associations provide external construct validation, not causal estimates of hedging effectiveness. AWARE-FX contributes a tested decision-support architecture in which retrieval, status logic, classification, uncertainty handling, aggregation, and external validation remain separately auditable.

</details>


### [17] [DualAnchor: Preserving Language Priors and Improving Lexical Fidelity in Gloss-Free Sign Language Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27614)
*Hongbin Zhang,Junhao Liu,Xuefeng Bai,Youcheng Pan,Yang Xiang,Kehai Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DualAnchor is a gloss-free training framework for sign language translation that couples token-level language prior regularization with optimal-transport-based video–text alignment to improve both fluency and lexical fidelity of LLM-based translations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based sign language translation systems underuse the strong language priors of LLMs, often yielding disfluent text (language-prior degradation), and they rely mainly on sentence-level video–text alignment, which fails to guarantee accurate word-level details, causing a lexical fidelity gap. There is a need for a method that both preserves the LLM’s linguistic fluency and enforces finer-grained visual–textual alignment without requiring gloss annotations.

Method: The authors propose DualAnchor, a gloss-free LLM-based SLT training framework with two key components. (1) Token-level Prior Anchoring (TPA): during training, the multimodal decoder is regularized at every decoding step toward the next-token probability distribution of a frozen LLM that sees the same autoregressive text prefix, thereby anchoring the multimodal model to the language prior. (2) Optimal Transport Alignment (OTA): visual-textual alignment is formulated as entropy-regularized partial optimal transport under a cosine distance cost between video features and textual content tokens; Sinkhorn iterations are used to compute a soft alignment that encourages faithful lexical grounding without explicit gloss labels.

Result: On standard sign language translation benchmarks PHOENIX-2014T and CSL-Daily, DualAnchor achieves strong overall performance, surpassing existing approaches. Ablation and targeted analyses show that TPA and OTA contribute complementary benefits in terms of translation fluency and lexical accuracy.

Conclusion: Coupling token-level language-prior regularization with optimal-transport-based video–text alignment addresses both language-prior degradation and lexical fidelity gaps in LLM-based SLT. The DualAnchor framework improves fluency through TPA and reduces fine-grained lexical errors through OTA, yielding better sign-to-text translations without relying on gloss annotations.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have led sign language translation (SLT), the task of converting sign-language videos into spoken-language text, to increasingly adopt LLMs as textual backbones. However, despite their strong language modeling capabilities, existing LLM-based SLT methods often undermine rather than exploit this language prior, producing disfluent translations, a failure we term language-prior degradation. Meanwhile, existing methods typically align videos and text at the sentence level, which does not ensure accurate lexical details and creates a lexical fidelity gap. To address both issues, we propose DualAnchor, a gloss-free LLM-based SLT training framework that couples two complementary anchors for linguistically fluent and visually faithful generation. Token-level Prior Anchoring (TPA) preserves the LLM's language prior by regularizing the multimodal decoder at each decoding step toward the next-token distribution of a frozen LLM conditioned on the same autoregressive prefix. Optimal Transport Alignment (OTA) improves lexical fidelity by formulating visual-textual matching as entropy-regularized partial optimal transport, with Sinkhorn optimization inducing a soft alignment between visual tokens and textual content tokens under a cosine cost. DualAnchor achieves strong overall performance on both PHOENIX-2014T and CSL-Daily. Targeted analyses attribute these gains to the complementary effects of the two anchors: TPA improves fluency, whereas OTA reduces fine-grained lexical errors.

</details>


### [18] [Harness-G: A Graph-Structured Harness for Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27652)
*Yanning Hou,Haoyuan Chen,Sihang Zhou,Xiaoshu Chen,Xirui Liu,Duanyang Yuan,Lingyuan Meng,Quan Liu,Jian Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies a structural problem in how RL-based search agents formulate retrieval and proposes a graph-structured interface plus a non-myopic credit assignment method, leading to substantially better QA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL search agents treat retrieval as free-form text query generation and optimize mainly via final-answer rewards. Even with denser reward designs, this overlooks whether the retrieval action space itself is well-posed. The authors observe that during RL training, different textual queries for the same question often lead to very similar evidence sets (retrieval-equivalence collapse), making many trajectories nearly indistinguishable in terms of retrieval utility and weakening the learning signal for retrieval decisions. This motivates a rethinking of the policy-environment interface for retrieval so that actions are discrete, comparable, and less prone to linguistic aliasing.

Method: The authors propose Harness-G, a graph-structured retrieval framework. Instead of unconstrained query generation, the policy operates over a finite action space at each step: select an evidence sentence, select an entity, or decide to answer. The environment constructs and updates a retrieval graph, manages the evidence state, and validates/executes each action, thus turning retrieval into structured action selection with explicit state tracking. On top of this, they introduce Structured Non-myopic Credit (SNC), which uses a frozen answer scorer to evaluate not just the chosen action but also plausible alternative actions from the same state. SNC then propagates the resulting downstream improvements back to earlier enabling actions, providing denser, non-myopic credit assignment for retrieval choices.

Result: In experiments on six question answering benchmarks, Harness-G with SNC achieves the best average F1 scores at both 1.5B and 3B parameter scales. It outperforms the strongest baseline, Graph-R1, by 10.74 F1 points at 1.5B and by 3.98 F1 points at 3B, demonstrating substantial gains from the redesigned retrieval interface and credit assignment scheme.

Conclusion: Redesigning the retrieval-policy interface from free-form query generation to graph-structured discrete action selection mitigates retrieval-equivalence collapse and makes retrieval decisions more learnable. Coupled with Structured Non-myopic Credit, this yields significantly better performance on multiple QA benchmarks, suggesting that careful formulation of retrieval as an RL action space and more structured credit assignment are key to advancing RL-based search agents.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) search agents commonly model retrieval as free-form natural-language query generation and optimize multi-turn interactions using final-answer rewards. Current studies mainly improve training with denser or more structured credit signals, but rarely examine whether retrieval is properly formulated at the policy-environment interface. We observe pronounced retrieval aliasing during Search-R1 training: rollouts for the same question continue to generate distinct query strings, yet their accumulated evidence sets increasingly overlap. We call this phenomenon retrieval-equivalence collapse; in this regime, trajectories approach utility equivalence with respect to retrieval decisions, leaving within-group returns with little effective retrieval contrast. To address this problem, we propose Harness-G, a graph-structured retrieval framework that redesigns this interface. It reformulates free-form query generation as finite action selection: the policy selects an evidence sentence or entity, or chooses to answer, while the environment constructs the menu, tracks retrieval state, and validates and executes each choice. This interface reduces linguistic aliasing and makes same-state alternatives directly comparable. Building on this interface, we introduce Structured Non-myopic Credit (SNC), which uses a frozen answer scorer to compare the selected action with its alternatives and assigns downstream gains to the earlier actions that enabled them. Across six QA benchmarks, Harness-G achieves the highest average F1 at both evaluated model scales, outperforming the strongest baseline, Graph-R1, by 10.74 points at 1.5B and 3.98 points at 3B.

</details>


### [19] [From Single- to Cross-Document: Benchmarking Multi-Granularity Event Analysis of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27654)
*Tao Wen,Shuai Shao,Pei Ke,Xu Han,Jie Zou,Guannan Li,Tao Tian,Jinjie Qiu,Lan Wang,Ke Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose MiGUE-Bench, a benchmark to systematically test large language models on multi-granularity event analysis, along with an LLM-based annotation pipeline, and use it to map strengths and weaknesses of current models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of large language models on event analysis are fragmented: they focus on limited document lengths, narrow task types, and restricted data sources. This prevents a clear, comprehensive understanding of how well LLMs really handle event-centric information extraction and reasoning. A unified, large-scale benchmark is needed to probe their abilities across different granularities and task dimensions.

Method: They build MiGUE-Pipeline, an LLM-driven, self-correcting annotation framework that automatically constructs a large collection of event-centric data with labels. Using this data, they construct MiGUE-Bench, defining four core tasks—event detection, relation reasoning, structure induction, and future prediction—designed to test event understanding from fine-grained atomic events up to complex cross-document narratives. They then evaluate a range of state-of-the-art LLMs and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) methods on these tasks.

Result: Experiments on multiple strong LLMs and RAG methods show a clear performance profile: they establish current capability boundaries and expose important weaknesses of LLMs on multi-granularity event analysis, especially in more complex reasoning and narrative-level tasks.

Conclusion: MiGUE-Bench, supported by the MiGUE-Pipeline, offers a systematic way to evaluate LLMs on multi-granularity event analysis. The benchmark reveals that, despite promising results on some subtasks, current LLMs still struggle with more complex, cross-document event reasoning and prediction, highlighting directions for future model and method improvements.

Abstract: Event analysis is an essential and fundamental direction of information extraction, involving various event-centric tasks at different granularity of documents. While large language models (LLMs) have preliminarily achieved promising performance in part of these tasks individually, their capability in event analysis still lacks comprehensive understanding due to restricted document granularity, task designs, and data source of existing benchmarks. To address these limitations, we introduce MiGUE-Bench, a systematic benchmark for assessing the performance of LLMs in multi-granularity event analysis. To support large-scale evaluation, we first develop an LLM-driven self-correcting annotation framework called MiGUE-Pipeline, enabling scalable acquisition of high-quality source data of events with automatic labels.
