Then, we design four core tasks in our benchmark, i.e., event detection, relation reasoning, structure induction, and future prediction, to probe model competence at different levels, from atomic event details to complex cross-document narratives. Extensive experiments on state-of-the-art LLMs and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) methods delineate the current capability boundary and identify critical deficiencies, providing insights into the future improvement of LLMs in challenging event analysis tasks.

</details>


### [20] [ICLE++: Modeling Fine-Grained Traits for Holistic Essay Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27671)
*Shengjie Li,Vincent Ng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose ICLE++, a new annotated corpus of persuasive student essays, to address limitations of the dominant ASAP dataset for automated essay scoring and to enable broader evaluation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated essay scoring models are almost exclusively evaluated on the ASAP corpus, whose limitations raise concerns about model generalizability and the ability to study newer AES tasks like multi-trait and cross-prompt scoring.

Method: They build ICLE++ by taking essays from the existing ICLE corpus and adding detailed annotations, including holistic scores and trait-specific scores, to create a richer, persuasive-essay dataset suitable for multiple AES evaluation scenarios.

Result: The outcome is the ICLE++ corpus: a new, publicly described dataset of persuasive student essays with both holistic and trait-level annotations, designed to be used for evaluating generalization of AES models and for newer AES tasks.

Conclusion: ICLE++ enriches available resources for AES research, offering a more diverse and fine-grained benchmark than ASAP alone and supporting work on generalization, multi-trait scoring, and cross-prompt scoring.

Abstract: The majority of the recently-developed models for automated essay scoring (AES) are evaluated solely on the ASAP corpus. However, ASAP is not without its limitations. For instance, it is not clear whether models trained on ASAP can generalize well when evaluated on other corpora. In light of these limitations, we introduce ICLE++, a corpus of persuasive student essays annotated with both holistic scores and trait-specific scores. Not only can ICLE++ be used to test the generalizability of AES models trained on ASAP, but it can also facilitate the evaluation of models developed for newer AES problems such as multi-trait scoring and cross-prompt scoring. We believe that ICLE++, which represents a culmination of our long-term effort in annotating the essays in the ICLE corpus, contributes to the set of much-needed annotated corpora for AES research.

</details>


### [21] [Recall Before You Rank: Similarity-Guided Top-$K$ Reuse for Efficient Long-Context Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27692)
*Wenshuai Yao,Wenyong Zhou,Hanyong Shao,Yizhe Chen,Zhiyuan Ning,Yuannuo Feng,Ru Huang,Kechao Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ReTopK is a training-free method to speed up Top-K sparse attention by reusing historical query-to-support selections so that only a small candidate subset of key–value indices is rescored exactly, yielding large speedups with minimal loss in quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Top-K sparse attention reduces the compute in the softmax/value aggregation phase but still requires computing attention scores against the full key–value cache and performing global Top-K selection, so its selector cost remains linear in context length. This becomes a bottleneck for long-context decoding, limiting the practical efficiency and scalability of sparse attention methods. The paper is motivated by the need to reduce this selector cost without retraining models or significantly degrading performance.

Method: The authors propose ReTopK, a training-free acceleration scheme for dynamic Top-K attention. For each attention head, ReTopK maintains a bounded cache of historical query–support pairs, where a support is the subset of KV indices selected previously. For a new query, it finds the most similar cached queries, unions their stored supports plus a recent window of positions to form a compact candidate index set, and then computes exact scores and performs Top-K only over this subset. The method includes: (1) a similarity-based fallback to full-history Exact Top-K when reuse is deemed unreliable, and (2) periodic exact refreshes to prevent cache drift. ReTopK reuses only the indices of past supports, not old scores or outputs, and keeps the full KV cache intact for correctness when needed.

Result: Across context lengths from 16K to 128K tokens, ReTopK attains better language modeling and long-context benchmarks than other approximate attention methods evaluated. On PG19 it achieves the lowest perplexity among approximations, and it attains the highest scores on NIAH and LongBench. At 128K context with K=512, it accelerates attention computation by 3.07× relative to Exact Top-K while causing only a 0.50% increase in perplexity.

Conclusion: ReTopK demonstrates that reusing historical retrieval decisions—via a small cache of query–support pairs and similarity-based retrieval—can significantly reduce the cost of Top-K sparse attention without retraining and with minimal accuracy degradation. This makes Top-K attention more practical for long-context inference, showing that most of the important attention mass can be recovered from supports derived from similar past queries plus a small recent window, supplemented by occasional exact recomputation for robustness.

Abstract: Top-$K$ sparse attention reduces the cost of Softmax and value aggregation by attending to only a small subset of key--value (KV) entries. However, identifying this subset still requires scoring the current query against the full KV cache and performing global Top-$K$ selection, leaving selector cost linear in context length and limiting the practical efficiency of sparse attention for long-context decoding. In this paper, we introduce ReTopK, a training-free method that accelerates dynamic Top-$K$ attention by reusing historical retrieval decisions. ReTopK builds on the observation that similar queries often attend to overlapping supports and that partially overlapping supports can still preserve most of the Exact Top-$K$ attention mass. For each attention head, it maintains a bounded cache of historical query--support pairs, retrieves the most similar cached queries for each new query, unions their stored supports with a recent window, and reranks only the resulting compact candidate set using exact current-query scores. A similarity-based fallback invokes full-history Exact Top-$K$ when reuse is unreliable, while periodic exact refreshes limit cache drift. ReTopK retains the complete KV cache and reuses only selected indices, rather than historical scores, attention weights, or outputs. Across 16K--128K contexts, ReTopK achieves the lowest PG19 perplexity and the highest NIAH and LongBench scores among the evaluated approximate methods. At 128K with $K=512$, ReTopK incurs only a 0.50\% perplexity increase over Exact Top-$K$ while accelerating attention computation by $3.07\times$.

</details>


### [22] [A Sparse Glimpse of the Whole: Train-Free Self-Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27735)
*Yuesong Liu,Yuan Zeng,Min Lyu,Ruilin Liu,Yu Guo,Yinlong Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes and improves speculative decoding for long-context LLM inference, introducing SparseSpec-L, a training-free self-speculative method that uses a sparsified, recallable KV cache and adaptive speculation length control to achieve consistent speedups without changing the model’s output distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speculative decoding can speed up LLM inference by reducing memory bandwidth usage, but its real-world gains are limited by drafting overhead, low token acceptance rates, and overly long speculation that leads to wasted work. Existing methods lack a unified efficiency analysis to decide when longer speculation helps or hurts, and they are not well-adapted to long-context settings where KV cache cost dominates. There is a need for a principled framework that characterizes efficiency trade-offs and for a practical, training-free speculative decoding scheme that is specifically optimized for long-context inference.

Method: The paper first develops a unified efficiency analysis of speculative decoding that relates speedup to drafting cost, token acceptance probability, and speculation length, showing that beyond a certain horizon, longer speculation can reduce speed. Based on this, it proposes SparseSpec-L, a self-speculative decoding framework where the target model drafts its own tokens using a dynamically sparsified, recallable KV cache. During full-context verification, per-head attention statistics are recorded and later reused as an importance signal, at no extra forward-pass cost, to identify critical historical tokens that should be recalled from dense KV storage. This creates a lightweight drafting mode that can selectively bring back important past tokens without permanently discarding the dense cache. In addition, an online entropy-based controller chooses the speculation length step by step based on the estimated efficiency gain, adapting to local uncertainty in generation.

Result: Across multiple long-context benchmarks and different model scales, SparseSpec-L consistently accelerates inference compared with standard autoregressive decoding. The method achieves significant end-to-end speedups (reported as up to X× over baseline, with the exact factor depending on task and model) while keeping the model’s output distribution unchanged, indicating no degradation in generation quality. The experiments validate the theoretical prediction that overly long speculation can hurt speed and show that the adaptive, entropy-based controller effectively selects efficient speculation lengths in practice.

Conclusion: The work clarifies the efficiency trade-offs in speculative decoding by providing a unified analysis showing when longer speculation ceases to be beneficial. Building on these insights, SparseSpec-L offers a practical, training-free, self-speculative decoding scheme tailored to long-context LLMs, using a sparsified but recallable KV cache and an online controller to balance drafting cost and acceptance probability. The approach achieves robust speedups in long-context inference without modifying the model’s output distribution, suggesting a promising direction for scalable, memory-efficient LLM deployment.

Abstract: Speculative decoding alleviates the memory-bandwidth bottleneck in large language model inference, but its acceleration is jointly constrained by drafting overhead, token acceptance, and speculation length. We present a unified efficiency analysis showing that extending the speculation horizon can reduce rather than improve speedup when the marginal acceptance probability falls below the relative drafting cost. Guided by this analysis, we introduce SparseSpec-L, a training-free self-speculative decoding framework for long-context inference. SparseSpec-L generates lightweight drafts directly from the target model using a dynamically sparsified and recallable KV cache. It recycles per-head attention statistics produced during full-context verification as a no-extra-forward importance signal, allowing critical historical tokens to be recalled without permanently discarding the dense KV cache. An online entropy-based controller further selects the speculation length according to expected step-wise efficiency. Experiments across multiple long-context tasks and model scales show consistent end-to-end acceleration, with up to speedup over autoregressive decoding while preserving the target model's output distribution.

</details>


### [23] [Can LVLMs Uncover the Truth Behind Visual Illusions? An Analysis of Perceptual and Reasoning Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27747)
*Liangjie Zhao,Jiaqing Lyu,Kexin Tang,Zecheng Fang,Rong Yin,Yulan Hu,Da Li,Jianing Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce IllusionReasoning, a benchmark using real-world visual illusions to jointly test perception and reasoning in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs), and find current models are weaker than claimed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LVLM evaluations either test perception only or are limited to narrow domains like math or coding, failing to reflect open-world reasoning that combines vision and cognition. There is a need for a more realistic, diagnostic evaluation of LVLMs’ reasoning abilities that emerges from complex visual inputs.

Method: Use visual illusions—cases where human perception systematically diverges from objective reality—as a stress test for LVLMs. Collect real-world illusion images and annotate them with diverse question–answer pairs that require both understanding the illusion and performing reasoning over it, forming the IllusionReasoning benchmark. Evaluate a wide range of existing LVLMs on this benchmark.

Result: When tested on IllusionReasoning, many state-of-the-art LVLMs underperform and fail to demonstrate the level of integrated perception–reasoning ability that has been suggested by earlier, narrower benchmarks.

Conclusion: Visual illusions offer a powerful diagnostic for probing the joint perception–reasoning capabilities of LVLMs. The IllusionReasoning benchmark reveals significant gaps between advertised and actual reasoning performance, and can guide future model design and optimisation toward more human-like, robust visual reasoning in open-world settings.

Abstract: Large Vision Language Models have integrated reasoning capabilities, elevating cognitive performance to new levels. However, existing evaluations either focus solely on perception or rely on specific domains such as maths or coding. Evaluation for reasoning capabilities that align with an open-world environment is still required, especially one that considers perception and reasoning jointly. To bridge this gap, we propose to evaluate LVLMs by exploiting visual illusions as a diagnostic tool. Visual illusions are phenomena in which the human visual system misinterprets objective signals, resulting in an understanding that deviates from reality. We constructed IllusionReasoning, a benchmark of illusion images collected from the real world, incorporating diverse annotated question-answer pairs. Based on IllusionReasoning, we show that the reasoning capabilities of a wide range of LVLMs are not as advanced as claimed. Our work provides new insights into LVLMs and offers future direction for optimisation.

</details>


### [24] [Gradient-free Task-Conditioned Retrieval for On-Device In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27766)
*Xinyu Luo,Hui Liu,Yihua Shao,Junyi Yang,Arindam Basu,Haoliang Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces CoRA, a gradient-free method to turn a frozen encoder into a task-conditioned retriever for on-device in-context learning, enabling efficient, low-rank, multimodal retrieval without fine-tuning or model calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device in-context learning needs a way to retrieve good examples from local memory under strict constraints on compute, memory, and data exposure. Existing retrievers often require backpropagation, fine-tuning, or online access to labels/outputs, which are expensive or impractical on small devices. The authors aim to design a retrieval method that is task-aware, efficient, and works with frozen encoders and limited resources.

Method: They propose Conditional Retrieval Alignment (CoRA), which uses paired input-output examples to build a task-conditioned retrieval mechanism from a frozen encoder without gradients. CoRA (1) selects useful encoder layers, (2) constructs an output-conditioned space from candidate outputs, (3) aligns encoder representations of candidate inputs to this space via closed-form ridge regression, and (4) applies low-rank factorization to obtain a compact retrieval basis. Candidate outputs are used only offline to build the index; at query time, retrieval uses only the query input and the precomputed low-rank index. They also derive an exact two-pass streaming algorithm to compute the low-rank factors without forming large matrices, and extend CoRA to multimodal retrieval by incorporating visual features into both conditioning and retrieval spaces.

Result: Across ten text datasets and four multimodal benchmarks, using small and mid-size models (Llama-3.2-1B, MobileLLM-Pro, OpenFlamingo-3B, Qwen3.5-2B), CoRA significantly improves task-conditioned retrieval quality compared to naïve or task-agnostic baselines, while respecting device constraints. They also demonstrate successful end-to-end deployment on a Raspberry Pi 5, confirming practical feasibility without retriever fine-tuning or online model calls.

Conclusion: CoRA provides an effective, gradient-free way to adapt frozen encoders into task-conditioned retrievers suitable for on-device in-context learning. By using closed-form alignment and low-rank compression, it meets strict compute and memory budgets, extends naturally to multimodal settings, and avoids the need for fine-tuning, backpropagation, or target-model invocations during retrieval.

Abstract: On-device in-context learning (ICL) relies on pre-inference retrieval to select demonstrations for useful context before downstream model inference. This retrieval must exploit task-specific information while operating over local memories under limited computation, memory, and data-exposure budgets. We propose Conditional Retrieval Alignment (CoRA), a gradient-free framework that converts a frozen encoder into a task-conditioned retriever using paired candidate inputs and outputs. CoRA selects complementary encoder layers, constructs an output-derived conditioning space from candidate memory, and aligns candidate input representations to this space through closed-form ridge regression. Low-rank factorization then produces a compact retrieval basis where candidate outputs are used only during offline index construction, whereas query-time retrieval requires only the query input and precomputed index. We show that CoRA's rank-constrained basis is the optimal low-rank compression of the output-conditioned fitted representation, and derive an exact two-pass streaming construction that avoids materializing the full fitted matrix. We further extend the framework to multimodal exemplar retrieval by incorporating visual representations into the conditioning and retrieval spaces. Experiments across ten textual datasets and four multimodal benchmarks with Llama-3.2-1B, MobileLLM-Pro, OpenFlamingo-3B, and Qwen3.5-2B, as well as end-to-end Raspberry Pi~5 deployment demonstrate that CoRA supports effective task-conditioned retrieval without retriever fine-tuning, backpropagation, or target-model calls.

</details>


### [25] [ChronoMem: Version Control and Semantic Rollback for Large Language Model Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27773)
*Yongye Su,Wujiang Xu,Chaoji Zuo,Elisa Bertino*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ChronoMem is a semantic version-control system for LLM agent memory that enables inspecting, selecting, and rolling back to prior memory states, improving rollback-consistent behavior over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent memory is forward-only: memories are accumulated and overwritten without systematic ways to inspect history or revert to prior states. This causes brittleness when corrections, concept drift, or memory corruption occur after later updates have already influenced behavior. There is a need for principled, semantic, version-controlled memory that supports counterfactual behavior as if later information had never been seen.

Method: The authors design ChronoMem, a semantic version-control layer plugged into Google’s Agent Development Kit. On each memory write, the system commits a full snapshot and maintains a structured version history. For rollback, it interprets natural-language undo requests, maps them to candidate historical versions using a combination of lexical and semantic retrieval, rank fusion, and reranking, and then restores the selected snapshot. They also propose a post-exposure evaluation protocol that checks whether, after rollback, an agent can answer questions and summarize history as if future memory updates never happened.

Result: On long-horizon conversational benchmarks where an agent’s memory evolves and explicit rollback tasks are injected, ChronoMem significantly improves the consistency of rollback-aware question answering and history summarization compared with prompt-only and retrieval-only baselines. It also achieves high accuracy in selecting the correct semantic memory version for rollback from the version history.

Conclusion: ChronoMem demonstrates that treating agent memory as a semantically searchable, version-controlled store—rather than a purely forward-accumulating log—enables robust rollback and counterfactual behavior in LLM agents. The work delivers both an open-source implementation integrated with a production ADK and a benchmark/evaluation protocol, positioning ChronoMem as the first systematic, semantic global memory rollback framework for LLM agents.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on long-term memory to support multi-session interaction and personalization. However, existing agent memory systems are designed around forward-only evolution, continuously accumulating, consolidating, and overwriting knowledge, with no principled mechanism to inspect, version, or revert prior states. This makes agents brittle under corrections, concept drift, and memory corruption, particularly after they have already been exposed to subsequent information. We present ChronoMem, a semantic version-control layer for agentic memory integrated into the production-ready, open-source Agent Development Kit by Google. ChronoMem commits whole-memory snapshots at each memory write, maintains structured version histories, and supports natural-language rollback requests by mapping undo intents to concrete historical versions through hybrid lexical and semantic retrieval, rank fusion, and reranking. We further introduce a post-exposure evaluation protocol that tests whether an agent can behave counterfactually after rollback by answering queries and summarizing history as if future updates had never occurred. On long-horizon conversational benchmarks augmented with evolving memory states and rollback tasks, ChronoMem substantially improves rollback-consistent question answering and history summarization relative to prompt-only and retrieval-only baselines, while achieving strong performance in semantic version selection. To our knowledge, ChronoMem is the first open-source system and benchmark for systematic semantic global memory rollback in LLM agents.

</details>


### [26] [Reasoning Consensus: Structural Ensembling of LLM Reasoning via Weighted DAG Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27783)
*Amruta Parulekar,Jinu Lee,Dilek Hakkani-Tür,Hari Sundaram*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method to ensemble not only the answers but also the reasoning structures (chains-of-thought) of multiple LLMs by merging them into a weighted DAG to obtain a "consensus reasoning" graph that is more accurate and interpretable than standard majority vote or self-consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs use chain-of-thought reasoning, but their intermediate steps are unstructured text and opaque: users cannot easily see which steps are well-supported, what alternatives were considered, or how the final answer compares to discarded ones. Existing ensembles focus on aggregating final answers, not reasoning processes. There is a need for a framework that aggregates and exposes the underlying reasoning in an interpretable and reliable way, especially for high-stakes tasks.

Method: The authors extract reasoning chains from multiple LLM runs and represent each chain as a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) of reasoning steps. They then merge these DAGs across runs and/or models, assigning weights to each step based on how many independent traces support it. This produces a single "consensus reasoning" DAG. They compare this approach against a majority-vote baseline and self-consistency under matched trace/compute budgets on several benchmarks, and they evaluate both accuracy and the quality/inspectability of the resulting reasoning using LLM judges and preference studies.

Result: Across six benchmarks (including statutory interpretation, graduate-level science questions, narrative multi-hop reasoning, and first-order logic), the consensus reasoning ensemble improves accuracy over a matched-budget majority-vote baseline, with up to 3.1% absolute gain on MuSR-MM. On a single model, it performs as well as or better than self-consistency at the same number of traces while also yielding an explicit consensus reasoning graph. The ensemble’s step weights show moderate correlation (Spearman ρ=0.30–0.51) with LLM-judge assessments of reasoning quality, and human/LLM evaluations prefer consensus subgraphs over those leading to the majority-vote answer in 54.4–65.4% of pairwise comparisons on five of six datasets.

Conclusion: Ensembling the reasoning structure of LLMs—via weighted merging of DAGs from multiple reasoning traces—can yield both higher answer accuracy and more interpretable, inspectable reasoning than standard majority-vote or self-consistency methods. The resulting consensus reasoning graphs align reasonably with external judgments of reasoning quality and are often preferred to the reasoning behind majority-vote answers. The framework also serves as a tool to analyze diverse reasoning perspectives on a problem, suggesting a path toward more transparent and reliable LLM reasoning systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) explore problems through chain-of-thought, but this exploration is buried in unstructured prose. On high-stakes tasks, users cannot tell which steps are well-supported, which alternatives were seriously considered, or how the final conclusion compares to those the model discarded. We propose a framework that ensembles the reasoning structure, not just the answers, of multiple LLMs by weighted merging of Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) extracted from reasoning chains. We weight each step by how many traces independently attest to it, to return "Consensus Reasoning". Across six benchmarks spanning statutory interpretation, graduate-level science, narrative multi-hop reasoning, and first-order logic, our ensemble outperforms a matched-budget majority-vote baseline, with a maximum accuracy gain of 3.1% on MuSR-MM (narrative multi-hop reasoning). On a single model, the framework matches or exceeds self-consistency at the same trace budget while additionally exposing an inspectable consensus reasoning graph. Ensemble weights correlate with LLM-judge rankings of reasoning quality at Spearman $ρ= 0.30$-$0.51$, and consensus subgraphs are preferred over alternatives leading to the majority-vote answer in 54.4-65.4% of head-to-head comparisons across five of six datasets. We observe that our framework can also be used to analyze diverse reasoning perspectives for a problem.

</details>


### [27] [Semantic-Aligned Structural Abstraction for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27790)
*Wei Chen,Junkai Li,Tongguan Wang,Hui Liu,Feiyue Xue,Chuanxiang Ma,Ying Sha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes SentiLLM, a framework that converts non-verbal multimodal signals into text-like tokens via structural abstraction so they can be effectively interpreted by large language models for multimodal sentiment analysis, achieving state-of-the-art results with few trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based multimodal sentiment analysis models mainly capture low-level superficial correlations between text and non-verbal signals (audio, visual), but struggle to model deeper affective semantics that arise from temporal structure, salient shifts, and contextual interactions in these signals. Since non-verbal modalities can be viewed as time-evolving feature sequences structurally similar to language, there is a need for a principled way to abstract continuous signals into semantically meaningful, discrete units that LLMs can naturally understand and reason over.

Method: The authors introduce SentiLLM, a unified framework based on Semantic-Aligned Structural Abstraction. It first transforms continuous non-verbal feature sequences (e.g., facial expressions, prosody) into compact, semantically meaningful tokens. Central to this is a Dual-Stream Salience-Context Calibration Mechanism that disentangles each non-verbal sequence into: (1) a focus stream that captures salient, text-guided sentiment shifts; and (2) an ambient stream that models relatively stable background states. By calibrating dynamic shifts in the focus stream against the ambient background stream and aligning them with textual priors, the method projects non-verbal modalities into a unified semantic space compatible with LLM input. SentiLLM is designed as a plug-and-play module that adds only a small set of trainable parameters on top of pre-trained LLMs.

Result: On four benchmark multimodal sentiment analysis datasets (MOSI, MOSEI, CH-SIMS, and CH-SIMS v2), SentiLLM achieves superior discriminative performance compared with prior methods, demonstrating improved ability to capture affective semantics from multimodal data. The framework yields performance gains while requiring only a small number of additional trainable parameters, validating the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed structural abstraction and dual-stream calibration.

Conclusion: The study concludes that viewing non-verbal modalities as structurally isomorphic to language and abstracting them into semantically aligned, text-like tokens is a powerful paradigm for multimodal sentiment analysis. The Dual-Stream Salience-Context Calibration Mechanism effectively separates and calibrates salient sentiment shifts against stable background states, enabling LLMs to understand and reason over affective sequences more naturally. SentiLLM, as a lightweight, plug-and-play module, significantly boosts multimodal sentiment analysis performance across diverse datasets, suggesting that structural abstraction is a promising direction for future LLM-based multimodal understanding systems.

Abstract: Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA) aims to interpret complex human emotions by integrating natural language with non-verbal modalities. Non-verbal modalities share a structural isomorphism with natural language, as both can be viewed as feature sequences evolving over time. This isomorphism enables the transformation of non-verbal modalities into text-like tokens for unified semantic reasoning. Large Language Models (LLMs), designed to understand and generate sequential data, can thus be utilized to interpret complex affective sequences. However, existing LLM-based methods primarily capture low-level superficial features, failing to model affective semantics arising from structural variations and contextual interactions. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{SentiLLM}, a unified framework that leverages \textit{Semantic-Aligned Structural Abstraction} to distill continuous raw signals into compact, semantically meaningful tokens. Specifically, we introduce a \textit{Dual-Stream Salience-Context Calibration Mechanism}, which disentangles non-verbal feature sequences into a focus stream and an ambient stream. The focus stream captures salient sentiment shifts (e.g., facial expressions) guided by textual priors, while the ambient stream characterizes stable background states. Through calibrating these dynamic sentiment shifts against background states, SentiLLM effectively projects non-verbal modalities into a unified semantic space, making them naturally understandable for LLMs. Serving as a plug-and-play module, SentiLLM significantly improves discriminative performance with only a small number of trainable parameters. Our method achieves superior performance on four datasets, MOSI, MOSEI, CH-SIMS, and CH-SIMS v2, demonstrating the effectiveness of the structural abstraction paradigm in MSA. Our code is available at: \href{https://github.com/especiallyW/SentiLLM}.

</details>


### [28] [Beyond Borrowed Histories: Person-Aligned User Simulation for Interactive Role-Playing Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27816)
*Yuhang Zhu,Mingxuan Du,Benfeng Xu,Jie Gao,Lingyun Yu,Hongtao Xie*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes PALATE, a new benchmark for evaluating role‑playing agents (RPAs) using simulated users and personalized rubrics instead of fixed, one-size-fits-all evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RPA benchmarks evaluate models by asking them to continue a fixed dialogue and grading them with a static rubric. This fails to capture two key aspects of real-world RPA use: (1) responses depend heavily on the preceding multi‑turn conversation, so fixed histories do not fairly assess an agent’s true role‑playing ability; and (2) user satisfaction is highly personal, but existing rubrics ignore individual preferences and apply generic criteria. The authors want an evaluation framework that more faithfully reflects interactive, long-horizon conversations and individual user experiences.

Method: They build PALATE, a benchmark centered on LLM-based user simulators and character profiles. First, they create a pool of 300 character profiles. For each character, they train five separate user simulators, each representing a distinct simulated user. Candidate RPAs then engage in open-ended, multi-turn conversations with these simulators over the fixed panel of characters. Evaluation uses two types of rubrics: a general quality rubric and personalized rubrics tailored to each simulated user. These personalized rubrics are learned/constructed from held-out annotated data to better align with human judgments of satisfaction. PALATE computes multiple metrics: per-turn quality, long-horizon session performance, and per-user experience across the co-constructed conversation trajectories.

Result: On held-out annotated data, personalized rubrics correlate better with human satisfaction judgments than a single general rubric, indicating they more accurately capture user experience. Applying PALATE to 16 RPA systems, the benchmark can distinguish among them along several dimensions: it identifies differences in generic response quality, robustness and coherence over long multi-turn sessions, and variation in performance across different simulated users. Instead of yielding just one overall ranking, PALATE produces detailed, user-specific evaluation profiles for each RPA.

Conclusion: PALATE offers a more realistic and informative way to evaluate role‑playing agents by leveraging LLM-based user simulators and person-aligned rubrics. It addresses limitations of fixed-history, one-size-fits-all benchmarks, enabling nuanced assessments of RPA behavior over full multi-turn interactions and for different user types. This yields interpretable evaluations at the level of specific user–RPA pairs rather than collapsing performance into a single global score.

Abstract: Role-playing agents (RPAs) have become one of the most important consumer applications of large language models. Users engage in multi-turn conversations with RPAs for experiences such as emotional comfort, making reliable evaluation essential for measuring capability, comparing systems, and guiding further improvement. Existing benchmarks, however, typically require an RPA to continue a fixed dialogue history and then evaluate the continuation using a fixed rubric detached from the user. We identify and empirically demonstrate two limitations of this design. First, an RPA's output is shaped by the preceding dialogue history, preventing a scientifically grounded assessment of its role-playing ability in real multi-turn settings. Second, user experience varies substantially across individuals, and conventional fixed rubrics need not align with user satisfaction. We therefore introduce PALATE (Person-Aligned LLM-Simulated-User Assessment with Tailored Evaluation), a scalable RPA benchmark built on user simulators. PALATE is accompanied by a pool of 300 character profiles. Its main evaluation trains five per-user simulators and lets them engage candidate RPAs in free-form, multi-turn conversations over a pre-frozen panel of character profiles. Alongside a general quality rubric, we construct personalized rubrics to measure user satisfaction; on held-out annotated data, the personalized rubrics show higher agreement with human judgments than the general rubric. In the main evaluation of 16 candidates, PALATE separately characterizes generic turn quality, long-horizon session capability, and per-user experience on multi-turn trajectories co-constructed by each candidate. It thereby produces interpretable evaluations of specific user-RPA pairs rather than compressing systems into a single user-independent ranking.

</details>


### [29] [AutoSupervision: Closing the Feedback Loop in Scientific Workflows with Grounded Revision Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27845)
*Haobo Li,Eunseo Jung,Wenxiao Zhao,Feng Liu,Jiong Wang,Kaiyi Xu,Zijie Guo,Zixin Chen,Ben Fei,Fenghua Ling,Lei Bai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces AutoSupervision, a framework and dataset to automatically evaluate whether revisions to scientific manuscripts genuinely address peer-reviewer concerns using grounded evidence from transparent peer-review records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While large language models are increasingly used to assist scientific writing and peer review, there is little work on checking whether author revisions truly and evidentially resolve reviewer comments, which is critical for trustworthy AI-assisted scientific workflows. Existing systems can help generate or summarize feedback, but they lack mechanisms to verify if manuscript changes are meaningful and supported by the text. The authors aim to fill this gap by using real peer-review histories as supervision to train and evaluate models on this verification task.

Method: The authors build AutoSupervision from about 56,000 Nature Communications articles with transparent peer-review records, including reviewer comments, author responses, and revised manuscripts. They define a task where models must: (1) extract and characterize reviewer concerns, (2) judge whether each concern has been addressed in the revision, and (3) locate and cite specific evidence from the revised manuscript that supports the resolution. They use these triplets (comments, responses, revisions) as naturally supervised data, and evaluate multiple LLMs, along with ablation and case studies, on the defined subtasks.

Result: Experiments show that current LLMs are relatively strong at identifying and characterizing reviewer concerns, with the best model (GPT-5.5) achieving a score of 0.754 on this subtask. However, when it comes to evidence-based verification—deciding if concerns are actually addressed and grounding that decision in manuscript text—performance drops substantially, with the best model reaching only 0.501, revealing this as the main performance bottleneck.

Conclusion: The study concludes that although LLMs can understand and summarize reviewer concerns fairly well, they struggle to reliably verify, with explicit textual grounding, whether revisions resolve those concerns. AutoSupervision provides a large-scale benchmark and supervision source to study and improve this underexplored but crucial capability. The authors position their framework as a foundation for more trustworthy AI tools in scientific editing and peer review, highlighting the need for better evidence-based reasoning over long, complex scientific documents.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled AI systems to assist scientific research and peer review. However, an essential capability for reliable AI-assisted scientific workflows remains underexplored: verifying whether reviewer feedback leads to meaningful and evidence-supported manuscript improvements. We introduce AutoSupervision, which evaluates whether scientific manuscript revisions genuinely address reviewer concerns through grounded evidence. AutoSupervision leverages transparent peer-review records as a natural source of supervision, where reviewer comments specify scientific concerns, author responses describe claimed resolutions, and revised manuscripts provide evidence of changes. Given reviewer comments, author responses, and revised manuscripts, models must characterize reviewer concerns, determine whether concerns have been addressed, and identify supporting manuscript evidence. We construct AutoSupervision from 56,000 Nature Communications articles and corresponding review records. Then we conducted experiments on LLMs, the ablation study, and the case study. Our results show that while LLMs perform well in characterizing reviewer concerns, with GPT-5.5 achieving a score of 0.754, evidence-based verification remains the primary bottleneck, with the best-performing model reaching only 0.501.

</details>


### [30] [Beyond Feeling Better: Capability-Sustaining Emotional Dialogue as a Longitudinal Research Paradigm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27851)
*Ming Wang,Jiaqi Wu Young,Wenfang Wu,Daling Wang,Shi Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new paradigm, Capability-Sustaining Emotional Dialogue (CSED), for emotional support systems that focuses on maintaining and strengthening users’ long-term capacities rather than only providing short-term emotional relief.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current emotional dialogue and support systems prioritize immediate emotional relief and empathy but overlook long-term user capabilities such as emotion regulation, coping, autonomy, and healthy relationship with the system. There is a risk of dependence, lack of attention to termination, and inadequate evaluation of longitudinal outcomes. The authors identify a gap between existing practices and the need for support that helps users sustain and build their own capacities over time.

Method: The authors conduct a targeted literature and corpus audit. Using a PRISMA-ScR–guided sample of 60 system-building papers, they analyze goals, evaluation metrics, and attention to issues like autonomy or termination. They also analyze 300 supporter turns from the ESConv corpus, coding them for capability-relevant functions (e.g., reappraisal, self-efficacy support, boundary behaviors) versus more generic suggestions. Based on these analyses, they propose a conceptual and process model for CSED, including design commitments, evaluation timescales, lifecycle constraints, and a protocol for extending the audit to model behavior.

Result: The literature audit shows that 95% of 60 examined papers focus on relief-oriented goals, none evaluates capability or long-term outcomes, and only 1 paper addresses dependency, autonomy, or termination risk. In the ESConv data, 43.0% of supporter turns include capability-relevant functions, but 22.0% are generic suggestions, and only small fractions correspond to specific capability-support behaviors (4.0% reappraisal, 6.7% self-efficacy support, 0.3% boundary behavior). The authors also produce and release a protocol for auditing model behavior within this new paradigm and outline a process model linking user capabilities to design and evaluation.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current emotional dialogue systems are predominantly oriented toward short-term relief and pay little attention to sustaining user capabilities or managing longitudinal risks like dependency and unhealthy engagement. The authors propose Capability-Sustaining Emotional Dialogue (CSED) as a new longitudinal paradigm and provide an initial agenda, conceptual model, and protocol to make CSED empirically testable across data collection, system and policy design, model training, evaluation, and governance.

Abstract: Emotional dialogue research includes two influential strategy traditions. Empathetic dialogue prioritizes understanding a speaker's emotional experience. Emotional support conversation selects and sequences support for the seeker's current needs. Sustained use introduces a further goal. Effective support should sustain users' capacities for emotion regulation, coping, self-endorsed decisions, and social connection across the interaction lifecycle. We propose capability-sustaining emotional dialogue (CSED) as a longitudinal research paradigm that aligns supportive strategy with this goal and organizes data, models, system design, evaluation, and governance around repeated use, non-use, transition, and termination. A targeted literature-and-corpus audit motivates this position. In a PRISMA-ScR-guided sample, 95% of 60 system-building papers pursue relief-oriented goals. None evaluates capability or longitudinal outcomes, and only 1 considers dependency, autonomy, or termination risk. In 300 ESConv supporter turns, capability-relevant functions appear in 43.0%, while generic suggestions account for 22.0%, compared with 4.0% reappraisal, 6.7% self-efficacy support, and 0.3% boundary behavior. We release a protocol for extending the audit to model behavior. An illustrative process model connects latent user capability to six design commitments, four evaluation timescales, and lifecycle constraints. The resulting agenda makes CSED testable across data, policy design, training, evaluation, and governance.

</details>


### [31] [FinanceHarness: Autonomous Financial Deep Research Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27853)
*Yijia Xiao,Rujun Han,Yanfei Chen,Zifeng Wang,Ke Jiang,Zhongying CuiZhu,Vishy Tirumalashetty,Wei Wang,Burak Gokturk,Tomas Pfister,Chen-Yu Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces FinanceHarness, an end-to-end system and benchmark for automating financial deep research with LLM-based agents, showing current models perform poorly and that their harness yields measurable gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep research agents produce general-purpose reports that are not suitable for the highly specialized, time-sensitive needs of financial research, which requires historical pattern analysis, forecasting, and strict prevention of future-information leakage. There is a need for a domain-specific harness and a rigorous, verifiable benchmark to evaluate and improve financial research agents.

Method: The authors design FinanceHarness, a layered system that orchestrates finance-oriented tools and practitioner-designed workflows to automate financial deep research. It covers environment and data setup, an agent execution loop, and reward modeling tailored to finance. They also build FinanceGym, a benchmark consisting of thesis-driven research questions with rubrics that explicitly separate pre-cutoff and post-cutoff evaluation criteria, enabling point-in-time, leakage-safe assessment. They validate the benchmark with professional experts and evaluate multiple LLMs and agents within this harness.

Result: Professional experts achieve an 82% pass rate on the FinanceGym rubrics, confirming that the benchmark is solvable yet demanding. In contrast, state-of-the-art LLMs and agentic systems achieve under 40% rubric scores, indicating substantial headroom for improvement. Using the same open-weight model backbone, integrating it into FinanceHarness improves performance from 25.3% to 32.4% on the FinanceGym rubric.

Conclusion: FinanceHarness and FinanceGym together provide a practical, rigorously evaluated framework for automating and benchmarking financial deep research with LLM-based agents. The large gap between expert and model performance, and the measurable gains from the harness, show that specialized orchestration and evaluation are crucial and that there remains significant room for advancing financial research agents. The system and code are released publicly to support further research and development.

Abstract: Powered by advances in LLMs and autonomous agents, deep research has become one of the most widely adopted agentic products. However, most deep research systems write general-purpose reports, which are inadequate for financial deep research. Financial research demands specialized knowledge to analyze historical patterns and forecast upcoming events. Automating financial deep research therefore requires both a layered harness to drive the research agent and a verifiable, point-in-time benchmark that prevents leakage of future information. We present FinanceHarness, a harness that runs finance-oriented tools and practitioner-guided workflows, automating financial deep research end to end: environment and data construction, the agent execution loop, and reward modeling. We further propose FinanceGym, comprising thesis-driven research questions and rubrics that combine pre-cutoff and post-cutoff criteria. Professional expert validation yields an 82% pass rate. Even leading LLMs and agents score below 40% on the rubrics, showing that FinanceGym is challenging and leaves substantial headroom. With the same open-weight backbone, FinanceHarness improves the overall rubric score from 25.3% to 32.4%. FinanceHarness is available at https://github.com/Yijia-Xiao/FinanceHarness.

</details>


### [32] [Memory Decoder at Scale: A Pretrained, Parametric Long-Term Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27919)
*Rubin Wei,Jiaqi Cao,Jiarui Wang,Junming Zhang,Qipeng Guo,Bowen Zhou,Zhouhan Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper scales up Memory Decoder models, showing that adding a large, separately trained memory module to smaller base LMs can outperform much larger monolithic models with fewer total parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decoder-only LMs mix long-term knowledge storage and reasoning in the same parameters, so increasing knowledge capacity requires scaling the entire model, which is expensive. Prior Memory Decoder work showed promise but only at small scales. There is a need to test whether independently scaling a dedicated memory module can be a more parameter-efficient way to improve performance at realistic data and model sizes, and to develop infrastructure that makes large-scale kNN-style memory retrieval practical.

Method: The authors scale Memory Decoder models up to 6.9B parameters and pretrain them on 300B tokens. They build a distributed Faiss-based indexing and retrieval system to overcome the cost of indexing and searching at this data scale, including sparse, batch-wise loading of kNN distributions. They construct configurations where a relatively small base LM (e.g., Pythia-410M or various Qwen3 Base sizes) is paired with a large, separately pretrained memory module (general or domain-specific) and compare performance and parameter efficiency against larger baseline models without such memory.

Result: The distributed Faiss pipeline makes large-scale memory indexing and retrieval feasible at 300B tokens. Empirically, allocating more parameters to the memory module rather than the base model yields a better parameter–performance tradeoff across scales. For example, combining a 6.9B general memory with Pythia-410M increases the average score from 29.86 to 37.34 on 17 benchmarks, surpassing Pythia-12B’s 37.24 with 39% fewer parameters overall. For Qwen3 Base models from 0.6B to 14B, adding 1.7B domain memories improves the average score across three domains by over 9 points at every scale.

Conclusion: Independently scaling a pretrained memory module is an effective and more parameter-efficient strategy for boosting language model performance than scaling the base model alone. With appropriate distributed retrieval infrastructure, large parametric memory can be practically deployed, delivering superior performance–parameter tradeoffs across multiple model families and domains.

Abstract: Decoder-only language models entangle long-term memory and reasoning in a single parameter set, making it difficult to scale memory capacity independently. Memory Decoder introduces a parametric long-term memory module but only studies it at a relatively small scale. In this work, we present Memory Decoder at Scale, scaling memory models up to 6.9B parameters and pretraining them on 300B tokens. At this data scale, the combined cost of indexing and search makes a standard Faiss pipeline infeasible. We address this bottleneck with a distributed pipeline for Faiss indexing and retrieval, together with sparse, batch-wise loading of kNN distributions. Across model scales, we find that allocating more parameters to memory yields a better parameter-performance tradeoff than scaling the base model alone. On 17 benchmarks, pairing a 6.9B general memory with Pythia-410M raises its average score from 29.86 to 37.34, surpassing Pythia-12B (37.24) with 39% fewer total parameters. For Qwen3 Base models ranging from 0.6B to 14B, 1.7B domain memories improve the average score across the three domains by more than 9 points at every scale. Overall, our results demonstrate that independently scaling pretrained memory offers a more parameter efficient path to improving language model performance.

</details>


### [33] [RepBench: Compiling Benchmarks into Capability Representations for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28008)
*Yanshi Li,Xueru Bai,Shuman Liu,Long Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RepBench is a benchmark-grounded framework and dataset for systematically probing and steering capability directions in large language model representations, using many real benchmarks instead of ad-hoc synthetic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing representation engineering methods are usually evaluated on small, paper-specific synthetic datasets, which makes results hard to compare, reproduce, or trust as genuine measures of model capabilities rather than artefacts of dataset design. There is a need for a standardized, benchmark-grounded, capability-aligned data layer so that representation probing and steering can be evaluated in a robust and comparable way across models and methods.

Method: The authors construct RepBench by: (1) crawling 13,427 benchmark-related papers to derive a taxonomy of 182 capability clusters in 13 broader capability families; (2) collecting 353 public benchmark datasets and curating 46,149 probe texts; (3) auditing and aligning these texts to 94 capabilities, each backed by at least two independent benchmarks; (4) representing capabilities as pooled vectors over multiple benchmark instances; (5) evaluating different readout methods (e.g., difference-in-means, logistic regression) and aggregation criteria on 12 large language models in cross-benchmark transfer settings; and (6) analyzing the clustering structure of raw per-text vs benchmark-pooled capability vectors.

Result: They find that raw per-text representation vectors do not exhibit a clear or stable clustering granularity, whereas benchmark-pooled capability vectors show a clear interior optimum at a small number of clusters across all 12 evaluated models. However, these model-induced clusters have low agreement with the human-designed capability taxonomy, indicating a mismatch between human categories and model internal organization. In cross-benchmark transfer experiments, the simple difference-in-means readout achieves the highest average performance at the model level on ten out of twelve models, while logistic regression achieves the best performance on the largest number of individual capability–model pairs. This reveals that different readout methods favor different evaluation views. The authors release the full pipeline, corpus, and evaluation code.

Conclusion: Benchmark-grounded, multi-benchmark capability probing exposes important structure in model representations that is invisible when using single benchmarks or synthetic datasets. The internal clustering of capabilities in LLMs does not neatly align with human-designed taxonomies, and evaluation outcomes depend substantially on the choice of readout and aggregation method. RepBench provides a reusable, closed-loop workflow and data layer that enables more robust, comparable, and capability-aligned evaluation of representation engineering methods across models and tasks.

Abstract: Representation engineering reads and steers capability directions in large language models, yet methods are typically evaluated on paper-specific synthetic data. The resulting measurements are difficult to compare or reproduce and may reflect surface patterns rather than capabilities. We present RepBench, a benchmark-grounded data layer for capability-aligned representation probing. Crawling 13,427 benchmark papers yields a taxonomy of 182 capability clusters in 13 families; harvesting 353 public benchmark datasets yields 46,149 audited probe texts covering 94 capabilities, each supported by at least two independent benchmarks. This multi-benchmark design reduces dependence on any single source: raw per-text vectors exhibit no natural cluster granularity, whereas benchmark-pooled capability vectors show an interior clustering optimum at a small number of clusters on all 12 evaluated models, with low agreement to the human taxonomy. Under cross-benchmark transfer evaluation across twelve models completed by all four readouts, difference-in-means attains the highest model-level mean on ten models, while logistic regression wins the most capability-model cells. This disagreement shows that the readout method and aggregation criterion are meaningful evaluation dimensions. The pipeline, corpus, and evaluation code are released as a reusable closed-loop workflow.

</details>


### [34] [LEEPS: Latent-Guided Explore-Exploit Prompt Sampling for Efficient RLVR in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28077)
*Shuang Liang,Haoyang Zhou,Yifan Gong,Guowei Wang,Xiting Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces LEEPS, a latent-guided sampling strategy that improves training efficiency in RL with verifiable rewards by better balancing exploration and exploitation of prompts, leading to higher reasoning performance with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for language models, many prompts produce identical or uninformative rollout rewards, wasting generation budget and providing weak learning signals. Existing pre-rollout prompt selection methods face a trade-off: focusing too much on historically informative prompts narrows coverage and may overfit, while broad exploration lowers the proportion of informative prompts and reduces training efficiency. A more principled way to choose which prompts to roll out is needed to make RLVR more sample-efficient and broadly effective.

Method: The authors propose LEEPS (Latent-Guided Explore–Exploit Prompt Sampler). LEEPS first partitions candidate prompts into two portfolios: an exploit set of historically informative prompts and an explore set of uncertain prompts. It then adaptively allocates rollout budget between these sets based on their recent fractions of non-trivial (informative) rewards. To make exploration targeted, LEEPS uses representation-space similarity (latent neighbors) and historical rollout outcomes to prioritize uncertain prompts that are likely to exhibit non-zero reward variance, i.e., those most likely to be informative. This selection happens pre-rollout, adding only small online overhead and not requiring extra rollouts.

Result: On six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, using Qwen2.5-Math models at 1.5B and 7B scales, LEEPS achieves the highest average performance among compared methods, with relative improvements of 2.6% and 3.7% over the strongest baselines, respectively. It also learns faster over the course of training. Additionally, on three out-of-distribution general reasoning benchmarks, LEEPS again obtains the best average scores at both model sizes. The method adds only about 2 seconds of sampling overhead per training step, indicating good practical efficiency.

Conclusion: LEEPS effectively addresses the exploration–exploitation trade-off in pre-rollout prompt selection for RL with verifiable rewards. By adaptively reusing informative prompts and guiding exploration with latent-space similarity and historical outcomes, it improves sample efficiency and overall reasoning performance of language models with minimal computational overhead, and generalizes well to out-of-distribution reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but prompt groups with identical rollout rewards consume generation budget without effective learning signals. Pre-rollout prompt selection can reduce this waste by screening prompts before rollout generation. However, existing pre-rollout methods struggle to balance exploitation and exploration: repeatedly exploiting historically informative prompts can narrow training coverage, whereas broader exploration can lower the fraction of informative prompts. To address these limitations, we introduce LEEPS, a Latent-Guided Explore--Exploit Prompt Sampler that adaptively balances the reuse of previously observed informative prompts with continued exploration of uncertain ones. LEEPS partitions candidates into exploit and explore portfolios and adaptively allocates rollout budget according to their recent non-trivial ratios. It further uses representation-space neighbors and historical rollout outcomes to prioritize uncertain prompts likely to yield non-zero reward variance, thereby making exploration more targeted without additional rollouts. Across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, LEEPS achieves the highest average score at both model scales, with relative gains of 2.6\% and 3.7\% over the strongest baseline for Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B and 7B, respectively, and generally improves faster during the training process. It also achieves the highest average score across the three evaluated OOD general-reasoning benchmarks at both model scales and adds only about 2 seconds of online sampling overhead per training step. Code is available at https://github.com/ShuangLiangX/LEEPS.

</details>


### [35] [GGC: Selective Query Correction for Reliable Text-to-SPARQL Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28082)
*Ziyi Yang,Thanh-Son Nguyen,Tuan Anh Nguyen,Lihui Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a three-stage Generator-Gate-Corrector (GGC) framework that selectively fixes risky LLM-generated SPARQL queries, greatly improving accuracy and efficiency for Text-to-SPARQL tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are good at generating structured queries but often produce SPARQL that is syntactically valid yet semantically misaligned with the natural language question, causing wrong answers. Existing approaches that blindly correct or post-process all queries add overhead and can even harm already-correct queries. There is a need for a mechanism that makes LLM-based Text-to-SPARQL more reliable and efficient by focusing effort only where it is most needed.

Method: The authors design GGC, a pipeline with three components: (1) a Generator LLM that produces an initial SPARQL query from a natural language question; (2) a Gate module that predicts whether this query is likely incorrect and requires correction; and (3) a Corrector model that is invoked only for queries flagged as high risk to revise them. They evaluate GGC on a multiple-choice QA (MCQA) Text-to-SPARQL benchmark, perform ablation studies on the Gate threshold behavior, and investigate how the data used to train the Corrector affects performance and stability.

Result: On the MCQA benchmark, GGC increases query-level accuracy from 90.23% to 98.33%. Compared with a baseline that corrects all generated queries, GGC reduces inference overhead by 45%. Ablations show that the Gate performs robustly across different decision thresholds, and that varying the composition of the Corrector’s training data leads to notable differences in correction effectiveness and stability.

Conclusion: Selective correction via the Generator-Gate-Corrector framework significantly boosts the accuracy, reliability, and computational efficiency of LLM-based Text-to-SPARQL systems. By only invoking correction when the Gate predicts a high risk of error, GGC avoids unnecessary modifications to already-correct queries while still fixing many erroneous ones, offering a practical path toward more dependable query generation over knowledge graphs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in structured query generation, making them a natural choice for Text-to-SPARQL, which translates natural language questions into executable SPARQL queries over knowledge graphs. However, their initial outputs remain unreliable: generated queries may be executable yet semantically misaligned with input questions, leading to incorrect retrieval. To address this issue, we propose Generator-Gate-Corrector (GGC), a framework for reliable LLM-based Text-to-SPARQL generation. GGC first uses a Generator to produce an initial query, then applies a Gate to predict whether correction is needed, and finally invokes a Corrector only for selected high-risk queries. This selective correction mechanism avoids unnecessary modifications and reduces the risk of degrading originally correct queries. Experiments on MCQA show that GGC improves query-level accuracy from 90.23\% to 98.33\% while reducing inference overhead by 45\% compared with correcting all generated queries. Ablation studies show that the Gate is robust across thresholds and that Corrector training data composition affects correction effectiveness and stability. Overall, the results demonstrate that selective correction enhances the accuracy, reliability, and efficiency of LLM-based text-to-SPARQL generation.

</details>


### [36] [Challenges in annotations by humans and LLMs: A case study of evaluative language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28119)
*Mirela Imamovic,Aenne Cecilia Kristine Knierim,Khushi Pitroda,Ekaterina Lapshinova-Koltunski*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares how linguistics students, an expert linguist, and LLMs handle a highly subjective annotation task (Appraisal theory categories in TED talks) and finds that LLMs, especially when fine-tuned, can match or outperform non-expert humans, achieving an F1 of 0.77 and showing promise as annotation aids in digital humanities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex linguistic annotation tasks, especially those involving subjective judgements like evaluative language and Appraisal theory, are difficult, expensive, and inconsistent even among trained annotators. With the emergence of LLMs, it is important to know whether they exhibit similar struggles to humans or can help stabilize and scale such annotations for digital humanities research.

Method: The authors use a corpus of English TED talk transcripts as spoken popular science discourse and annotate evaluative language following Appraisal theory’s Attitude subsystem (Affect, Judgement, Appreciation) at sentence level in specific scientific domains. They compare three groups: linguistics students (linguists in training), a trained linguist, and several LLMs. They (1) collect and evaluate human annotations and inter-annotator agreement, (2) design and test three different prompts for automatic Appraisal class classification, (3) select the best-performing prompt, (4) evaluate three different LLMs with this prompt, and (5) fine-tune an LLM on the task to measure its performance using F1-score.

Result: Human results show that the trained linguist’s annotations are the most consistent reference, whereas linguists in training have relatively low agreement. Among the LLM approaches, one prompting strategy yields better performance than the others. Using this best prompt, the tested LLMs achieve solid classification accuracy, and a fine-tuned LLM reaches an F1-score of 0.77, performing closest to or better than non-expert human annotators when compared against the expert linguist’s annotations.

Conclusion: LLMs can handle complex, subjective linguistic annotation tasks in evaluative language reasonably well and, with suitable prompting and fine-tuning, can rival or surpass non-expert human annotators while aligning well with expert annotations. This suggests LLMs are valuable tools to support or scale complex annotation work in digital humanities, making it more feasible to apply rich theoretical frameworks like Appraisal theory to large corpora.

Abstract: In this paper, we draw a comparison between linguists in training, a trained linguist, and annotations generated by large language models (LLMs) to find out if they struggle with complex linguistic phenomena in a similar way. For this purpose, we analyse evaluative language in spoken popular science discourse, with the example of a corpus of English TED talk transcripts. We focus on the Appraisal theory and its Attitude subsystem, including the categories (classes) of Affect, Judgement, and Appreciation. In this context, Appraisal theory is an example of a highly subjective annotation task, making it a suitable example for the study of complex annotation challenges. First, we assess human annotations on a sentence level in specific scientific domains. Then, we develop three prompts and compare them for model performance for the automatic classification of Appraisal classes. We assess the performance of three LLMs using the best-performing prompt and finetune the model, reaching an F1-score of 0.77. We find that models perform best compared to annotations conducted by the trained linguist, while linguists in training do not reach high agreement scores. We conclude that LLMs can aid in complex annotation task resolution, opening new pathways for the complex theories annotated and analyzed in digital humanities studies.

</details>


### [37] [FinSMART: Financial Sentiment Analysis for Algorithmic Trading through Market-Aligned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28127)
*Giorgos Iacovides,Wuyang Zhou,Danilo Mandic*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes FinSMART, a reinforcement-learning-based framework that aligns financial sentiment analysis models with real market outcomes, achieving large improvements in trading performance and enabling continual, market-aware retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial sentiment analysis with LLMs relies on supervised learning over small, static, human-labeled datasets and is market-agnostic, making models slow to adapt to evolving market conditions and potentially misaligned with actual profitability. The authors want a method that directly ties sentiment signals to realized market performance and can continuously adapt as markets change.

Method: FinSMART uses a market-aligned reinforcement learning framework where the financial LLM’s sentiment outputs are optimized using realized market outcomes as feedback. It includes a signal extraction pipeline with market-aware data filtering to select informative events and a discrete asymmetric trading reward that maps sentiment decisions to economic gains or losses. This structure is designed to handle noise, non-stationarity, and multifactor influences in markets while enabling stable RL training. The framework also supports periodic retraining using new financial articles and their realized outcomes instead of manual sentiment labels.

Result: In experiments, FinSMART substantially outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on metrics such as profitability, risk-adjusted returns, and sentiment quality. The paper reports a 220% improvement in cumulative trading returns over the strongest baseline, along with more robust performance over time when the model is periodically retrained with fresh market-aligned data.

Conclusion: FinSMART demonstrates that market-aligned reinforcement learning can effectively improve financial sentiment analysis by directly optimizing for economic outcomes and enabling continual adaptation to market changes. The approach reduces dependence on static, manually labeled datasets and shows promise as a next-generation paradigm for building adaptive, financially grounded LLMs.

Abstract: Recent advances in Generative AI have substantially improved financial sentiment analysis through post-trained financial large language models (LLMs). However, existing approaches remain confined to a market-agnostic, supervised learning paradigm that relies on limited, static and human-annotated datasets, and thus are incapable of adapting to evolving market conditions. To address this limitation, we introduce FinSMART, the first market-aligned reinforcement learning framework for financial sentiment analysis, which directly optimizes sentiment signals using realized market outcomes. To deal with the noisy, non-stationary, and multifactorial nature of financial markets, FinSMART incorporates a signal extraction pipeline that combines market-aware data filtering with a discrete asymmetric trading reward, enabling stable reinforcement learning from economically meaningful market feedback. Experimental results demonstrate that FinSMART significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in profitability, risk-adjusted performance, and sentiment signal quality, improving cumulative trading returns by 220% over the strongest baseline. Uniquely, the FinSMART framework naturally supports market-aware retraining, at any point in time, by replacing costly manual annotation with newly observed financial articles and their realized market outcomes. Such a retraining strategy enables the model to continuously adapt to changing market dynamics, resulting in consistent performance gains over its static counterpart. These findings demonstrate the practical applicability of market-aligned reinforcement learning and highlight its potential as a next-generation paradigm for developing adaptive financial LLMs.

</details>


### [38] [Rethinking LLM-Judged Helpfulness as a Pedagogy Signal: A Pre-Registered Audit Across Tutor Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28128)
*Shuyi Fan,Boyuan Deng,Mengyu Xu,Jiale Liu,Hongyang Zhang,Qiaoxin Yang,Chongyang Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether general-purpose helpfulness scores from large language models can reliably indicate good tutoring behavior and finds they cannot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used as tutors, it is important to distinguish responses that simply give answers from those that promote student learning through guidance and scaffolding. Many current evaluation schemes rely on generic helpfulness rubrics, which may reward answer-giving instead of pedagogy. The authors want to test whether these general helpfulness measures can actually capture pedagogical quality and to understand how tutor behavior affects student independence.

Method: The authors run a pre-registered audit using three different tutor policy bases, each with conversational vs explicitly pedagogical variants built on the same underlying model. These tutors interact with a fixed, weak, simulated student in controlled settings. They measure two main process signals: (1) answer leakage (whether the tutor reveals the solution) and (2) next-turn independent work by the student. Claude Opus 4.8 acts as a frozen, condition-blind primary judge, scoring tutor turns using both a general helpfulness rubric and a pedagogy-specific rubric. After fixing Opus scores, GPT-5.6 Sol is prospectively chosen as a secondary judge to rescore the same 1,179 tutor turns under the same frozen rubrics, providing a robustness check across judges. Additional ablations test multiple policies on the primary base to explore how pedagogy and helpfulness scores vary together.

Result: Under the primary tutor base with Opus as judge, conversational and pedagogical policies show no significant difference in judged helpfulness, but are perfectly separated by the pedagogy rubric, with a small effect size for helpfulness (Cliff's |δ|=0.10) versus maximal separation for pedagogy (|δ|=1.0). Across the two LLM judges, the direction of pedagogy contrasts is stable wherever an effect is detected, but the helpfulness ranking is judge-dependent and reverses for two of the three tutor bases. In the Opus-only ablation, seven policies vary widely in pedagogy scores (a spread of 2.3 points on average) while remaining clustered in a narrow 0.25-point band of helpfulness scores. Additionally, turns where tutors reveal the answer consistently lead to reduced independent work by the student on the next turn across all bases, a deterministic and judge-invariant finding.

Conclusion: Generic helpfulness judgements from LLMs do not reliably track pedagogical quality in tutoring contexts and are sensitive to the choice of judge model. In contrast, pedagogy-focused rubrics and deterministic behavioral measures (such as answer leakage and subsequent student independence) more robustly distinguish tutoring strategies. Effective evaluation of LLM tutors should therefore combine pedagogy-specific rubrics with process-based metrics rather than relying on general-purpose helpfulness scores.

Abstract: LLM tutoring poses a measurement problem: can a general-purpose helpfulness rubric distinguish direct answer-giving from pedagogical guidance? We audit this signal in a pre-registered study. Within each of three tutor bases, we compare conversational and pedagogical policies instantiated with the same underlying model and paired with one fixed weak simulated student. Deterministic detectors measure answer leakage and next-turn independent work. Claude Opus 4.8 is the frozen, condition-blind primary judge. After the Opus scores were fixed, GPT-5.6 Sol was prospectively specified for a post hoc robustness audit of the same 1,179 confirmatory answer-phase tutor turns under the frozen helpfulness and pedagogy rubrics. On the primary base under Opus, the policies do not differ significantly in helpfulness but are perfectly rank-separated under the pedagogy rubric (Cliff's $|δ|{=}0.10$ vs. $1.0$). Across the two judges, pedagogy contrasts retain their direction where detected, whereas the helpfulness ordering is judge-contingent, reversing between judges on two of three bases. In an Opus-only ablation, seven primary-base policies span $2.3$ points in mean judged pedagogy within a $0.25$-point band of mean judged helpfulness. Separately, answer-revealing turns are followed by less independent student work on every base, a result that is judge-invariant by construction. In this controlled setting, general-purpose helpfulness is not a reliable pedagogy signal. Tutor evaluation should pair pedagogy-targeted rubrics with deterministic process measures.

</details>
