### [39] [Can Agents Deceive? Evaluating Reasoning and Deception in ParliamentBench using a Social Deduction Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28146)
*Niklas Bauer,Lars Benedikt Kaesberg,Akiko Aizawa,Jan Philip Wahle,Bela Gipp,Terry Ruas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build ParliamentBench, a Secret Hitler-based benchmark to test how well LLMs can deceive, persuade, and reason under hidden information, and show frontier models are strong but still struggle to maintain consistent deception over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs begin to act as agents in high-stakes contexts like medicine and law, it’s crucial to understand not only their helpful capabilities but also their potential for deception and adversarial behavior. Existing evaluations don’t cleanly isolate social deduction, deception, and persuasion in controlled, reproducible settings. A structured, game-based benchmark can provide a safe, observable proxy for these risky behaviors to better assess and compare models.

Method: They design an open-source evaluation framework, ParliamentBench, modeled on the social deduction game Secret Hitler. The framework simulates multi-agent games requiring hidden roles, deception, persuasion, and reasoning with asymmetric information. They run 1,600 simulated matches with 16 different LLMs in various configurations: LLMs playing each other, LLMs playing against humans, and comparisons to logs from many online human games. They define three new metrics that separately quantify (1) social deduction skill, (2) reasoning performance, and (3) the consistency of deceptive behavior across a game. Models are prompted/controlled to take on specific roles and actions within the game setting.

Result: Frontier LLMs form a strong top tier (GPT-5.4, Kimi K2.5, Grok 4.1 Fast, DeepSeek 3.1 Terminus) and perform well in both cooperative and deceptive roles. Weaker models often perform below both a random-choice baseline (33%) and a simple algorithmic baseline (45%). Across the board, many models have difficulty sustaining a coherent deceptive persona for the whole duration of a match, with deception retention often dropping below 50%.

Conclusion: Structured social deduction games like Secret Hitler can effectively expose and quantify LLMs’ capabilities in deception, persuasion, and reasoning under hidden information. Current frontier models already show strong adversarial and cooperative skills, while weaker models lag below simple baselines. However, even strong models typically cannot sustain consistent deception throughout long interactions, suggesting a limitation in their ability to maintain stable deceptive strategies and indicating both safety concerns and open challenges for alignment and capabilities research.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are deployed as agents in high-stakes settings, such as medical and legal systems, understanding their deceptive capabilities is fundamental to safety. Controlled social deduction games provide a reproducible proxy for isolating and evaluating these complex adversarial behaviors. We present the open-source benchmark framework ParliamentBench based on the game Secret Hitler to evaluate LLMs in scenarios that require deception, persuasion, and reasoning under information asymmetry. We evaluate 16 LLMs across 1,600 simulated matches playing each other, playing against humans, and compare them against a large set of online games. We introduce three novel metrics that isolate social deduction, reasoning, and deceptive consistency. Our experiments reveal that frontier models achieve strong performance across cooperative and deceptive roles, with a strong top-four cluster (GPT-5.4, Kimi K2.5, Grok 4.1 Fast, and DeepSeek 3.1 Terminus), whereas the weakest models fall short of random (33%) and simple algorithmic (45%) baselines. Most LLMs struggle to maintain a consistent deceptive persona throughout an entire game, with deception retention dropping below 50%.

</details>


### [40] [RRM: Experience-Driven Reflective Retrieval Memory for Long-Horizon Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28156)
*Jingxiang Fan,Junbao Zhuo,Bochao Zou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose Reflective Retrieval Memory (RRM), a framework that learns reusable retrieval strategies from past tasks to improve long-horizon multimodal reasoning over long videos, leading to state-of-the-art results on several benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal long-term memory agents for long videos are limited by context length and rely on external memory. Existing methods mainly focus on what information to store but neglect how to retrieve it effectively, especially when retrieval is inaccurate or repeatedly fails. There is a need for a mechanism that can learn from past retrieval failures and successes to adapt future search strategies across tasks.

Method: They introduce Reflective Retrieval Memory (RRM), which augments an entity-centric multimodal memory graph with a new reflective experience memory. This memory type distills procedural retrieval knowledge (i.e., search strategies) from historical task trajectories. Unlike episodic and semantic memory that store factual evidence from the current video, reflective memory stores reusable search patterns. At inference, RRM converts retrieved reflective experiences into query-level retrieval guidance, while answer generation conditions only on newly retrieved factual evidence from the current video. A lifecycle management mechanism controls experience memory based on usage frequency, feedback on reuse quality, and temporal decay, to limit redundancy and noise.

Result: RRM is evaluated on long-horizon multimodal reasoning benchmarks, specifically M3-Bench-Robot, M3-Bench-Web, and Video-MME-Long, and consistently outperforms previous state-of-the-art methods, indicating improved retrieval and reasoning over long multimodal contexts.

Conclusion: Reflective retrieval memory, which captures and reuses retrieval strategies rather than only factual content, significantly improves long-horizon multimodal reasoning for long videos. The proposed RRM framework demonstrates that learning from historical task trajectories to guide future retrieval is effective and leads to state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Existing multimodal long-term memory agents use external memory to overcome the limited context available for long videos. However, most methods emphasize what to store rather than how stored memory should be retrieved. When retrieval becomes inaccurate or repeatedly fails to obtain useful evidence, existing agents lack mechanisms to diagnose failures from previous task trajectories and adapt future search strategies.We introduce Reflective Retrieval Memory (RRM), a reflective memory framework for long-horizon multimodal reasoning. RRM augments an entity-centric multimodal memory graph with reflective experience memory, which distills transferable procedural retrieval knowledge from historical task trajectories. Unlike episodic and semantic memories that preserve factual evidence from the current video, reflective experience memory captures reusable search strategies across tasks. RRM converts retrieved experiences into query-level guidance, while answer generation remains conditioned only on factual evidence newly retrieved from the current video. A lifecycle management mechanism further regulates experience memory through usage frequency, reuse feedback, and temporal decay, thereby reducing redundancy and noise. RRM consistently outperforms previous state-of-the-art approaches on M3-Bench-Robot, M3-Bench-Web, and Video-MME-Long, demonstrating the effectiveness of reflective retrieval memory for long-horizon multimodal reasoning.

</details>


### [41] [Where and When to Commit: Candidate-Aware Decoding for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28166)
*Chia-Ming Lee,Ming-Ching Chang,Xin Li,Yu-Lun Liu,Chih-Chung Hsu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces LATCH, a training-free, format-aware early-exit framework for diffusion language models that accelerates decoding while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models reveal intermediate predictions at each denoising step, creating theoretical opportunities for faster generation by exiting early. Existing early-exit methods rely on coarse confidence statistics or schedule-dependent heuristics, causing premature termination, especially for long chain-of-thought reasoning where answers stabilize only at the end. Adaptive sampling improves token-level commitment timing but does not verify whether the entire output has stabilized. There is a need for a training-free, task-format-aware method that can safely and effectively determine when and where to stop decoding, achieving substantial speedups without hurting accuracy.

Method: The authors propose LATCH, a two-part, training-free early-exit framework for diffusion language models. First, Confidence-Verified Commit (CVC) decides when decoding can stop by checking both model confidence and sustained stability of the argmax outputs over a dynamically determined candidate span. This span is extracted using a deterministic parser tailored to each task's output format, ensuring that the decision is aligned with task-specific structures. Second, Block-Wise Early Commit (BWEC) determines where to accelerate within the sequence by applying a lightweight local rule to earlier (non-final) blocks, enabling early commitment of those parts while leaving the final block under the stricter CVC control. Together, these components enable localized acceleration with tracked-candidate halting, and crucially they operate without additional training or suffix prompts, relying only on format-aware parsing.

Result: LATCH is evaluated end-to-end on 11 tasks in zero-shot settings using two diffusion language model backbones, LLaDA and Dream, leading to 22 distinct evaluation configurations. With a single, fixed hyperparameter configuration that transfers across backbones without retuning, LATCH maintains performance within 2.0 percentage points of the full-decoding accuracy in all settings. At the same time, it delivers significant speed improvements: throughput speedups of approximately 9.3–17.8x on short-answer tasks and 2.0–3.3x on long chain-of-thought reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that carefully designed, training-free, format-aware early-exit mechanisms can substantially accelerate diffusion language models without meaningful loss in accuracy. By separating global termination control (CVC) from local acceleration (BWEC) and leveraging deterministic task-format parsers, LATCH achieves robust, cross-backbone gains without prompt engineering or additional training. This suggests that structured, candidate-aware early-exit strategies are a practical path to deploying faster, more efficient diffusion-based language generation in both short-answer and long-reasoning scenarios.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) expose a provisional prediction at every denoising step, creating an opportunity for generation-time early exit that stops decoding before the schedule is exhausted. Existing early-exit gates decide termination from fixed-region confidence statistics or schedule-dependent rules, evidence too coarse for a decision that freezes every remaining position at once, so they fire prematurely on long chain-of-thought outputs whose answers stabilize only near the end. Adaptive sampling, the other axis of training-free acceleration, paces how quickly positions commit while decoding continues but never verifies that the output itself has stabilized. We introduce a training-free, candidate-aware early-exit framework that keeps the two axes separate and matches each decision to evidence of its own scope. Confidence-Verified Commit (CVC) governs when the sequence may stop by verifying confidence and sustained argmax stability over the dynamically extracted candidate span using a deterministic parser specified from each task's output format. Block-Wise Early Commit (BWEC) governs where to accelerate by applying a cheaper local rule to non-final blocks, while leaving the final block and global termination under CVC. We refer to their combination as LATCH (Localized Acceleration with Tracked-Candidate Halting). Unlike prior methods, LATCH needs no suffix-prompt construction; it is prompt-anchor-free but format-aware. We evaluate LATCH end to end on 11 tasks under zero-shot settings using LLaDA and Dream. LATCH stays within 2.0 percentage points of full-decoding accuracy across all 22 evaluation settings, with one frozen hyperparameter set that transfers cross-backbone untuned, while achieving end-to-end TPS speedups of 9.3-17.8x on short-answer tasks and 2.0-3.3x on long-reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [42] [The MADRS Pipeline: Supporting Depression Assessment in Clinical Trials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28190)
*Mila Fodor,Katalin Ócsai,Francesco Periti,Rien Sonck,Alex Boudreau*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents an LLM-based pipeline that automatically supports MADRS-based depression assessment from structured clinical trial interviews, achieving high correlation with expert ratings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Depression diagnosis still depends largely on time-consuming, expert clinical assessments. Existing automated approaches usually analyze free-form or online text and are not tailored to structured clinical trial interviews that follow standardized guidelines like SIGMA and use the MADRS scale. There is a need for tools that can assist clinicians in these formal settings by mapping interview content to specific MADRS items and their severity, and by flagging potentially inconsistent or problematic ratings.

Method: The authors design a pipeline centered on large language models. First, audio from structured SIGMA-based interviews is transcribed into text. Then, the system automatically segments or interprets the transcript in terms of the ten MADRS symptom items, assigns a severity estimate to each item, and detects cases where the clinician’s rating might be inconsistent with the content (problematic ratings). The core reasoning and mapping between transcripts and MADRS items are driven by LLMs, though details are not included in the abstract.

Result: On real clinical trial interviews, the automated system’s MADRS ratings show a strong overall correlation of 0.867 with expert clinicians’ ratings, indicating that the pipeline approximates human assessment quality and can reliably capture item-level severity information from interview audio.

Conclusion: An LLM-based end-to-end pipeline can effectively support MADRS-based depression assessment from structured clinical interviews in clinical trials. Its high correlation with expert ratings and ability to provide interpretable, item-level outputs and flag questionable ratings suggest it can serve as a practical decision-support tool for clinicians and improve consistency and scalability of depression assessments in trials.

Abstract: Depression is a major mental disorder for which diagnosis relies primarily on clinical assessments. Automated methods to support its detection via the psychiatric MADRS scale are getting more and more attention. While existing solutions primarily focus on detecting the disorder from different text sources (e.g., online text, social media), there is still limited support for clinical trials, where clinical assessments are conducted through structured interviews based on standard guidelines such as SIGMA. In this work, we develop a LLM pipeline specifically designed to support clinicians in supporting the assessment of depression in patients enrolled in clinical trials. Our pipeline converts audio interviews into transcripts, maps them into the ten MADRS symptom items, estimates their severity, and identify problematic clinical ratings associated with them. Evaluation on real clinical interviews shows a strong overall correlation of 0.867 with expert ratings, providing interpretable support for future assessments in clinical trials.

</details>


### [43] [Fidelity Is Not Safety: Gently-Compressed LLMs Pass Every Data-Free Quality Guard Yet Invent Procedure Steps in Agentic Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28196)
*I. Kennedy,T. Kennedy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Standard compression checks (perplexity, MMLU, data-free fidelity) can all pass while compressed LMs still invent extra steps when acting as agents; this failure correlates with coherent low-rank compression error, and the authors propose a data-free statistic to screen such risky compressions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model compression is widely used to reduce cost, and practitioners usually rely on easy-to-measure metrics like perplexity, benchmark accuracy, and representation fidelity under random probes to judge whether a compressed model is safe to deploy. However, these metrics are task-agnostic and mostly non-agentic, and there is growing concern that compressed models might behave differently when used as agents executing multi-step procedures, in ways that current guards fail to detect. The authors aim to probe whether commonly accepted compression guards truly ensure behavioral safety, particularly for agentic use cases.

Method: The authors study three different language model families and apply different compression operators, focusing on coherent low-rank (SVD) truncation versus magnitude pruning matched to similar perplexity. They evaluate compressed models against a layered guard stack: (1) perplexity close to the original, (2) downstream benchmark accuracy (e.g., MMLU) with overlapping confidence intervals, and (3) data-free output-fidelity tests comparing internal representations on random probe inputs. They then introduce a pre-registered, powered “invented-step canary” task: running a standard operating procedure (SOP) as an agent and checking whether the model inserts non-existent procedure steps. They conduct paired CI analyses to compare performance on the fidelity tests versus the canary, and analyze compression error via a two-axis statistic: coherent fraction and error rate.

Result: They find that gently compressed models using coherent low-rank (SVD) truncation can pass all standard guards (perplexity, MMLU, and data-free fidelity) yet still systematically invent extra steps in SOP-style agentic tasks. This effect is operator-specific: magnitude pruning tuned to the same perplexity does not exhibit the same failure pattern. They demonstrate a dissociation: the same compressed weights that pass (CI-win) paired output-fidelity tests fail (CI-fail) the invented-step canary. The key governing factor is the product of compression error coherence and its rate, not the sheer magnitude of the error. The existing data-free fidelity probe cannot detect this axis by construction. Across three architectures, this operator-specific blind spot and perplexity-guard evasion replicate, particularly where there is low-rank headroom within the guard constraints.

Conclusion: Common acceptance criteria for compressed LMs—near-original perplexity, stable benchmark accuracy, and data-free fidelity—are insufficient to certify safety when models are deployed as agents executing procedures. Coherent low-rank compression can introduce specific behavioral failures (inventing procedure steps) that these guards miss. The authors characterize this blind spot and propose a data-free screening method based on a two-dimensional summary of compression error (coherent fraction and error rate), with fixed thresholds that generalize across architectures and align with the identified coherence-times-rate mechanism. They recommend that gently compressed low-rank models be screened using this statistic before agentic deployment, as current metrics do not guarantee agent safety.

Abstract: Practitioners accept a compressed language model once it clears a stack of data-cheap quality guards: perplexity within a small factor of the original, downstream accuracy (for example MMLU) inside a confidence interval, and data-free output-fidelity signals that compare the compressed and original network's internal representations under random probe inputs. This stack has a blind spot. Across three model families, gently-compressed models clear every guard and then invent procedure steps that were never in the instructions when they run a standard operating procedure (SOP) as an agent. The effect is operator-specific: coherent low-rank (SVD) truncation induces it, and magnitude pruning matched to the same perplexity does not. One dissociation isolates the cause. The same compressed weights that CI-win a paired output-fidelity test CI-fail the invented-step canary. The governing axis is the coherence of the compression error times its rate; the magnitude of the damage does not predict it. The data-free fidelity probe is a fidelity oracle by construction, so it cannot see this axis. We characterize the blindspot and dissociation with paired confidence intervals on a pre-registered, powered canary across three architectures. Operator-specificity replicates on all three, and the perplexity-guard evasion appears where the model admits in-guard low-rank headroom. We then give a data-free screen: a two-axis statistic of the compression error (coherent-fraction and error-rate) that flags the failing builds with fixed thresholds across architectures and matches the coherence-times-rate mechanism. Perplexity, MMLU, and fidelity acceptance do not certify agent safety. Screen gently-compressed low-rank builds before agentic deployment

</details>


### [44] [Causal Discovery with Inverted Self-attention for Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28212)
*Yusen Liu,Yong Wang,Yifan Yin,Tianqing Zhu,Xiufeng Liu,Huan Huo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a transformer-based framework with a new causal self-attention mechanism and global causal analysis to more accurately discover causal relations in complex multivariate time series, outperforming prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal discovery in multivariate time series is hard because of complex, possibly nonlinear interactions across many variables. Existing methods often miss latent or indirect causes and are affected by spurious correlations, leading to incorrect causal graphs. The authors want a method that can better model these complexities and yield more accurate, robust causal structures.

Method: They design a transformer-based framework using a novel inverted causal self-attention mechanism (CSAM). CSAM inverts tokens and imposes sparsity on attention scores so that the model focuses on a few significant, potentially latent and indirect causal relationships while downweighting spurious ones. On top of local attention-based causal scores, they introduce a global causal algorithm that aggregates information to identify global causal links and computes a holistic metric of causal influence. Finally, they add a causal verification module that checks and filters the discovered causal relations to improve robustness. They evaluate on linear and nonlinear synthetic or benchmark datasets, and perform ablation and sensitivity studies.

Result: Across various linear and nonlinear datasets, their framework achieves better causal discovery performance than existing baselines, as measured by appropriate causal structure evaluation metrics. Ablation experiments indicate that both the inverted CSAM and the global causal algorithm contribute significantly to performance, and sensitivity analyses show the approach is relatively stable with respect to hyperparameter choices.

Conclusion: Incorporating an inverted causal self-attention mechanism and global causal analysis within a transformer provides an effective way to discover both direct and latent causal relationships in complex multivariate time series. The framework reduces spurious correlations, is empirically robust, and outperforms prior methods, suggesting it is a promising direction for causal discovery in high-dimensional, nonlinear time series data.

Abstract: Causal discovery in multivariate time series data is challenging due to complex interactions, high dimensionality, and nonlinear dependencies among variables. Existing methods often struggle to capture these complexities, resulting in inaccurate causal structures. To address this issue, we propose a novel framework that leverages self-attention mechanisms within the transformer architecture for causal discovery. Our approach introduces a novel inverted causal self-attention mechanism (CSAM) that emphasizes latent and indirect causal relationships by inverting tokens and inducing sparsity in attention scores, focusing on significant causal interactions and reducing spurious correlations. Additionally, we develop a global causal algorithm to identify global causal links, providing a holistic metric for causal influence, along with a causal verification module to ensure robustness in the identified causal relationships, enhancing the reliability of our framework. Experiments on both linear and nonlinear datasets, along with ablation studies and sensitivity analyses, show that our framework outperforms existing methods, demonstrating its potential for causal discovery in complex multivariate time series.

</details>


### [45] [EMBL AI Librarian: Life-Sciences Knowledge Layer for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28229)
*Luigi Sigillo,Matteo Silvestri,Francesco Tabaro,Rajat Bhatnagar,Syed Irtaza Mubashar,Matt Jeffryes,Daljit Nijjer,Vittorio Perera,Ola Spjuth,Julio Saez-Rodriguez,Melissa Harrison,Fabio Petroni*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents EMBL AI Librarian, a natural-language, LLM-orchestrated retrieval and evidence-extraction layer on top of Europe PMC to better support life-science AI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing literature databases like Europe PMC were designed for human users: they require keyword/Boolean queries and return whole papers. As AI agents increasingly consume the web, especially in life sciences, they struggle because they must learn complex query syntax, run multiple searches, and read full papers to extract specific evidence, which is inefficient and brittle. There is a need for an interface that directly serves agents with precise, evidence-level answers to natural-language questions while leveraging existing large literature resources.

Method: The authors build EMBL AI Librarian, a knowledge layer sitting on top of Europe PMC. A single large language model serves as an orchestrator: it receives a natural-language query from an agent, decomposes it into complementary subqueries, and issues these to the live Europe PMC search engine. From the returned set of papers, the LLM then reads and identifies relevant evidence passages that answer the query. The system is evaluated on multiple benchmarks: literature synthesis, claim verification, open-domain question answering, and biology-specific downstream tasks such as protocol-related questions and sequence manipulation. Comparisons are made against strong recent baselines and alternative retrieval methods such as generic web search.

Result: On ScholarQABench, EMBL AI Librarian improves Citation F1 by over 16 points relative to strong recently published baselines, indicating better retrieval of correct supporting literature. When plugged in as the retrieval component of an existing claim-verification pipeline, it increases agreement with expert consensus, suggesting more accurate evidence selection. On the LitQA2 open-form benchmark, a GPT-5.4 agent grounded in Librarian achieves roughly 8 points higher score than when grounded in standard web search. These results collectively show that the Librarian layer enhances the quality and usefulness of retrieved evidence for life-science tasks.

Conclusion: The EMBL AI Librarian effectively upgrades traditional literature databases like Europe PMC into AI-friendly knowledge layers that accept natural-language queries and return targeted evidence. By orchestrating query planning, literature search, and evidence extraction with a single LLM, it significantly improves performance across literature synthesis, claim verification, open-domain QA, and specialized biological tasks. This demonstrates that dedicated, agent-optimized retrieval layers can substantially boost the capabilities of life-science AI agents. The authors provide an open-source implementation to foster further research and adoption.

Abstract: The web is increasingly accessed by AI agents rather than humans. Every agent needs knowledge, especially in the life-sciences, where agentic pipelines are growing fast. Access to the literature is a crucial part of that need, and resources such as Europe PMC, with over 40M indexed records, are widely used to meet it. Yet these resources were not built for AI agents: they take keywords and complex syntax and return whole papers, so every agent must learn the syntax, issue several searches, and read full papers to find the evidence it needs. We introduce EMBL AI Librarian, a knowledge layer that upgrades the Europe PMC interface for AI agents: an agent asks in natural language and receives evidence that answers it. A single LLM orchestrates the whole knowledge retrieval process: it plans complementary subqueries executed by the live Europe PMC search engine, then reads the selected papers and locates the relevant evidence. We evaluate Librarian across four benchmarks: literature synthesis, claim verification, open-domain question answering, and downstream biology tasks such as protocol questions and sequence manipulation. On ScholarQABench, Librarian improves Citation F1 by more than $16$ points over strong recently published baselines. Used as the retrieval layer of an existing claim-verification pipeline, it increases agreement with expert consensus; and on the open-form LitQA2 benchmark, a GPT-5.4 agent scores about $8$ points higher when grounded in Librarian than with web search. Overall, our results show that equipping life-science agents with the Librarian knowledge layer improves performance across a range of tasks. We release our code publicly at https://github.com/petroni-lab/librarian

</details>


### [46] [Understanding Is Done Early: A Depth Division of Labor in Large Language Models and Its Use for Unbounded-Context Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28263)
*Hanzuo Liu,Xuan Qi,Chunyu Liu,Haotian Zhong,Yulong Wang,Rayying,Key,Alex Lamb,Mingyu Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes CoMem, a method that leverages transformer layerwise specialization to store and retrieve long-context information via intermediate-layer residual states instead of full-token KV caches, achieving strong long-context performance with lower memory and compute costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard long-context handling stores key–value (KV) states for all tokens across all layers, making memory and compute grow with context length and becoming impractical for very long sequences. Empirically, different transformer layers play different roles: lower/middle layers build general semantic representations, while upper layers specialize for prediction. The authors aim to exploit this depth-wise division of labor to design a more efficient memory mechanism whose cost does not scale linearly with stored context length, while retaining or improving long-context reasoning quality.

Method: They introduce CoMem (Comprehension Memory). Instead of caching full KV states at all layers, the model writes each context chunk only at a chosen intermediate layer, storing a fixed number of residual states (a bounded cache). At query time, the system retrieves a fixed-size set of these cached states and then recomputes the upper transformer layers conditioned on the current query over the retrieved pack. Thus, read-side compute and memory depend on the retrieval budget and layer depth, not on total context length. They evaluate this by continued training a frozen Qwen3-8B base LM: one variant uses only a low-rank (rank-32) LoRA self-distillation adapter trained on PG19, and another is adapter-free. They also sweep over which depth to cache at to trade off recomputation vs fidelity and study the effect of self-distillation on repairing fidelity loss from deeper caching.

Result: Empirically, CoMem improves long-context benchmarks relative to a strong full-context baseline (KV-Direct). On RULER it scores 97.05, and on LoCoMo 38.27 versus 34.59 for KV-Direct. Its dialogue-memory gains persist even under conversation-cluster resampling and evaluation with an independent judge. Across multiple long-context and long-document tasks, it shows benefits from bounded retrieval but also reveals a "compression tax" from representing information via limited in-window retrieval. Depth sweep experiments indicate that caching at deeper layers reduces per-query recomputation but introduces fidelity loss; this loss can be substantially mitigated by self-distillation. In an efficiency-focused, adapter-free setup on an NVIDIA H20 with 128k context, CoMem cuts memory usage from 89.36 GB to 18.26 GB and yields a 7.83x prefill speedup.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that long-context memory in transformers can be structured along the layer (depth) axis rather than only by storing token-wise KV states. By caching and retrieving intermediate-layer residuals and recomputing upper layers on demand, CoMem maintains or improves long-context performance while dramatically reducing memory usage and improving speed. The layerwise organization exposes a tunable trade-off between recomputation and representational fidelity, which can be alleviated via self-distillation, suggesting a promising direction for scalable long-context language models.

Abstract: Transformer depth is not used uniformly: lower and middle layers build semantic representations, while upper layers increasingly specialize them for prediction. We turn this division of labor into CoMem (Comprehension Memory), which writes each context chunk only through an intermediate layer, retrieves a fixed number of cached residual states, and recomputes the query-conditioned upper layers over the resulting pack. For a fixed retrieval budget, model-side read compute and memory are independent of stored-context length. We evaluate a continued-trained Qwen3-8B base LM under a unified chat-template-free protocol. The backbone is frozen; the flagship trains only a rank-32 self-distillation LoRA on plain PG19, and we report an adapter-free arm separately. CoMem reaches 97.05 on RULER and 38.27 on LoCoMo versus 34.59 for full-context KV-Direct; the dialogue-memory advantage survives conversation-cluster resampling and an independent judge. Results on additional long-context and long-document tasks expose both the benefits of bounded retrieval and its in-window compression tax. Controlled depth sweeps show that deeper caching lowers per-query recomputation but incurs a fidelity loss that self-distillation substantially repairs. In a separate adapter-free efficiency control on an NVIDIA H20 at 128k, CoMem uses 18.26 GB rather than 89.36 GB and achieves a 7.83x prefill speedup. These results show that long-context memory can be organized along the layer axis, not only the token axis.

</details>


### [47] [MORFES: A Benchmark for Productive Inflectional Competence in Modern Greek](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28274)
*Ioakeim Perros,Cleopatra Papadopoulou,Ayoub Kirouane,Christos Petrocheilos*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They present MORFES, the first benchmark focused on evaluating Greek language models’ inflectional morphology, and show their Sophea-Genesis-1 model performs best on it while remaining competitive more broadly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Greek language models are mostly evaluated on factual or general language understanding tasks, not on their ability to handle Greek’s complex inflectional morphology. As multilingual open-weight models proliferate, there is no standard way to measure whether they truly grasp grammar in morphologically rich languages like Modern Greek. This leaves a gap between model coverage of a language and demonstrated grammatical competence.

Method: They construct MORFES, a 500-item benchmark, where each item tests either recognition (choosing or validating the correct inflected form) or production (generating the correct inflected form) of Modern Greek open-class words. Items are expert-verified and biased toward lower-frequency lemmas to reduce the chance that models succeed via memorization instead of learning inflectional rules. They release this benchmark publicly, then evaluate a range of open-weight language models—from LLaMA variants to Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1, Magistral, Kimi K2, and their own Sophea-Genesis-1—on the suite, comparing their inflectional performance and situating those results alongside general capability.

Result: MORFES is released as a public dataset on Hugging Face. Across evaluated open-weight models, performance on Greek inflection varies, revealing that high general NLP capability does not guarantee strong morphological competence in a morphologically rich language. Their Sophea-Genesis-1 model achieves the best performance on the inflectional morphology tasks in MORFES while performing on par with similarly sized models on broader, non-morphological benchmarks.

Conclusion: MORFES provides a much-needed, dedicated benchmark for testing inflectional competence in Modern Greek, enabling more precise measurement of grammatical abilities in multilingual language models. The results indicate that current open-weight models still have room to grow in morphological understanding, and targeted evaluation is essential to reveal these gaps. Sophea-Genesis-1 demonstrates that it is possible to optimize for strong inflectional performance without sacrificing general capability, and the released resources (benchmark and model) can support future work on morphology-aware model development and evaluation.

Abstract: Modern Greek is a richly inflected language, yet the language models built for it are evaluated mainly on factual knowledge, and no benchmark is dedicated to their inflectional competence. We introduce MORFES (Morphological Open-class Recognition-and-Formation Evaluation Suite), a benchmark of 500 expert-verified items that tests the recognition and production of Greek inflected forms, favoring lower-frequency lemmas so that a correct answer reflects the rule rather than a memorized form. We make it publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/KIEFERSA/MORFES. We evaluate a range of open language models on MORFES, situating them within the rapidly scaling open-weight ecosystem from LLaMA to Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1, Magistral, and Kimi K2, where multilingual coverage grows but grammatical competence in morphologically rich languages remains under-measured. Among them, Sophea-Genesis-1, a model we developed and release as open weights at https://huggingface.co/KIEFERSA/Sophea-Genesis-1, leads on inflectional morphology while matching similarly sized models in general capability.

</details>


### [48] [(Towards) Scalable Reliable Automated Evaluation with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28282)
*Bertil Braun,Martin Forell*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a scalable, reference-free framework to evaluate LLM outputs using pairwise comparisons among multiple LLMs and Elo rankings, approximating expert judgments while reducing human effort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating LLM-generated text is difficult, time-consuming, and often depends on reference answers or benchmarks that don’t exist for many real-world tasks. Existing automatic metrics struggle to capture nuanced quality and relevance, and they are usually tied to domains where objective ground truth is available. There is a need for a more general, scalable, and less human-dependent way to assess LLM output quality in diverse applications.

Method: The authors design an evaluation framework where outputs from multiple LLMs on the same task are compared pairwise. These comparisons are used to infer relative quality without relying on a single model’s judgment. An Elo rating system aggregates the pairwise wins and losses into stable, interpretable rankings of model performance. The framework includes adjustable agreement thresholds, ranging from strict unanimity to simple majority voting across judges, to tune the trade-off between confidence in the evaluation and the coverage of items that can be rated.

Result: Using competency profiles derived from scientific abstracts as a testbed, the framework is shown to produce automatic rankings of LLM outputs that correlate well with expert evaluations. This alignment suggests the method can approximate expert-level assessment quality while requiring far less human input.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers a scalable, domain-agnostic, and consistent way to evaluate LLM outputs by combining multi-model pairwise comparison with Elo ratings and adjustable agreement thresholds. It reduces dependence on explicit reference standards and extensive human annotation, enabling more efficient and reliable quality assessment of LLM-generated text across varied tasks and application domains.

Abstract: Evaluating the quality and relevance of textual outputs from Large Language Models (LLMs) remains challenging and resource-intensive. Existing automated metrics often fail to capture the complexity and variability inherent in LLM-generated outputs. Moreover, these metrics typically rely on explicit reference standards, limiting their use mostly to domains with objective benchmarks. This work introduces a novel evaluation framework designed to approximate expert-level assessments of LLM-generated content. The proposed method employs pairwise comparisons of outputs by multiple LLMs, reducing biases from individual models. An Elo rating system is used to generate stable and interpretable rankings. Adjustable agreement thresholds, from full unanimity to majority voting, allow flexible control over evaluation confidence and coverage. The method's effectiveness is demonstrated through evaluating competency profiles extracted from scientific abstracts. Preliminary results show that automatically derived rankings correlate well with expert judgments, significantly reducing the need for extensive human intervention. By offering a scalable, consistent, and domain-agnostic evaluation layer, the framework supports more efficient and reliable quality assessments of LLM outputs across diverse applications.

</details>


### [49] [CACHE-UK: A Stability-Aware Memory Editor for Sequentially Updated Quantized LLMs in Finance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28292)
*Anubhav Lakra,Yue Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes CACHE-UK, a stability-aware memory editing framework for 4-bit quantized financial LLMs, which mitigates catastrophic degradation when applying many sequential edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs used in fast-changing financial domains must be updated frequently as facts, regulations, and market conditions change. However, in resource-constrained settings that rely on aggressive 4-bit quantization, existing memory-editing methods cause severe performance degradation when many updates are applied sequentially. There is a need for a method that can keep such models factually up to date without catastrophic forgetting, specifically for domain-specific financial deployments.

Method: CACHE-UK introduces three coordinated components: (1) a rank-1 LoRA-based perturbation mechanism that restricts edits to a low-rank adapter subspace, reducing interference with the base model; (2) a financial domain prioritization module that adaptively scales edit strength based on the relevance and importance of financial content; and (3) a closed-loop Stability Controller that monitors accumulated "degradation debt" from previous edits and regulates subsequent edits to prevent catastrophic forgetting across long edit sequences. The framework is evaluated on a 4-bit quantized OpenLLaMA-3B using a large curated UK finance corpus.

Result: On a 4-bit quantized OpenLLaMA-3B fine-tuned on 88,021 UK financial documents, CACHE-UK reduces knowledge degradation by 11–17% compared with adapted baseline editing methods under the same quantization constraints. It also achieves the highest test success (generalization) rate in the experiments, reaching 28%, which is a 6 percentage point improvement over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: Stability-aware editing via CACHE-UK can meaningfully mitigate degradation when continually updating 4-bit quantized, domain-specific LLMs in financial settings. Although overall generalization of edits is still modest, the approach improves factual maintenance and robustness under tight resource constraints, highlighting a promising direction for safer continual editing in quantized models.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) deployed in dynamic financial environments face a critical challenge: maintaining factual accuracy as market conditions, regulations, and corporate facts change continuously. While 4-bit quantization enables efficient deployment, it severely limits the viability of sequential memory editing: existing methods undergo catastrophic performance degradation under this "quantization stability crisis." We introduce CACHE-UK (Contextual Adaptive Continual Hybrid Editor for UK Finance), a stability-aware memory editing framework specifically designed for domain-specific, quantized LLMs. CACHE-UK integrates three components: a rank-1 LoRA perturbation mechanism that confines edits to the low-rank adapter subspace, a financial domain prioritization module for content-adaptive edit strength, and a closed-loop Stability Controller that tracks "degradation debt" to prevent catastrophic forgetting across sequential updates. Evaluated on a 4-bit quantized OpenLLaMA-3B model with a curated UK financial corpus of 88,021 documents, CACHE-UK reduces knowledge degradation by 11-17% relative to adapted baselines under identical 4-bit constraints -- its most robust effect -- while attaining the highest test success (generalization) rate observed in our setting (28%, a 6 percentage point improvement over the strongest adapted baseline). These results indicate that stability-aware editing can improve factual maintenance in resource-constrained financial LLM deployments, though absolute generalization rates remain low.

</details>


### [50] [Fairness Pruning: Locating Demographic Bias in GLU-MLP Layers via Differential Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28319)
*Pere Martra,Eugenio Martínez Cámara,Alfonso Ureña López*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes Fairness Pruning, a method that identifies and selectively zeroes neurons implicated in demographic bias in LLMs, showing bias-related behavior can be modified with minimal impact on overall capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs exhibit demographic biases, but current mitigation methods are often blunt, affect overall model performance, or lack mechanistic interpretability. The authors aim to develop a targeted, structurally simple intervention that can localize and manipulate circuits responsible for demographic bias without degrading general capabilities, and to empirically test whether such bias circuits are dissociable from capability circuits.

Method: Introduce Fairness Pruning, which uses minimally contrastive prompt pairs differing only in demographic attributes and captures activations at inference-time in GLU-based architectures, specifically at the down_proj input. The method computes a BiasScore to identify neurons whose activations differ most between contrastive prompts. These neurons are then zeroed (pruned) during inference to study their functional role. Experiments are run on Llama-3.2 models up to 3B parameters and Salamandra-2B, combining standardized bias benchmarks and qualitative text generation to assess both bias behavior and capability retention.

Result: The identified neurons are causally involved in demographic bias processing: zeroing them changes how models respond to prompts involving the corresponding demographic variables. The intervention is highly surgical: in Llama-3.2-1B, pruning at most 40 neurons (under 0.031% of MLP width) maintains about 99.49% of performance on reasoning and general knowledge tasks. However, because the BiasScore is unsigned, the selected neurons include both stereotype-enforcing and stereotype-counteracting units, so pruning them leads to bidirectional and sometimes unstable changes in aggregate bias rather than uniform mitigation.

Conclusion: Demographic bias in these LLMs is mediated by sparse, dissociable neural circuits that can be structurally targeted with minimal impact on general capabilities. Fairness Pruning provides a practical framework for localizing and manipulating these circuits, but naive, sign-agnostic zeroing yields unstable, bidirectional effects on bias. This motivates future work on directionally aware interventions that distinguish and separately modulate stereotype-promoting versus stereotype-opposing neurons to achieve controlled behavioral changes rather than blunt suppression.

Abstract: This work presents Fairness Pruning, a lightweight structural intervention method designed for the management and future mitigation of demographic bias in large language models (LLMs). As a foundational empirical validation of this method, this work focuses on causal bias localization. Using minimally contrastive prompt pairs and inference-time activation capture, the method identifies neurons that react differentially when processing demographic attributes in GLU architectures, evaluating the signal at the down_proj input. Empirical evaluation was conducted on models of up to 3 billion parameters (Llama-3.2 family and Salamandra-2B), combining standardized benchmark evaluation with qualitative text generation experiments. Results demonstrate that zeroing the identified neurons alters how the model responds to associated demographic variables. However, rather than producing flat mitigation, the intervention causes bidirectional bias destabilization: because BiasScore is unsigned, candidate sets mix neurons that push toward and against the stereotype, and the net effect on aggregate bias depends on which sign dominates. The intervention is extremely surgical: zeroing at most 40 neurons in Llama-3.2-1B (less than 0.031% of total MLP width) achieves a mean retention of 99.49% in reasoning and general knowledge capabilities. These findings empirically confirm that demographic bias processing and model capabilities operate on dissociable circuits, establishing the methodological foundations for transitioning from blind zeroing toward directional behavior modulation.

</details>


### [51] [LLMs struggle to simulate human belief updates in controlled environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28347)
*Sebastian Pohl,Harsh Mehta,Pranav Mambayil,Abdul Ghafoor,Franziska Lesigang,Yufang Hou,Christian Hilbe*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether large language models can accurately simulate how individual humans update their beliefs after reading online discussions and finds they only perform well when given people’s real initial stances, with systematic biases limiting their use as stand-ins for human participants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Social scientists increasingly use LLMs as cheaper, faster stand-ins for human participants in experiments about opinions and belief change, but we lack direct tests of whether LLMs actually mirror real human belief dynamics at the individual level. Since many simulations assume that LLMs can both generate realistic starting beliefs and then update them like humans, there is a need to rigorously compare LLM behavior to ground-truth data from real people exposed to the same information.

Method: The authors collected data from 391 UK participants on Prolific, measuring their initial stances on three discussion topics, then having them read Reddit comments and update their stances. For each person, they built a persona using demographic and personality trait data. Six different LLMs were then asked to simulate each individual’s belief updating process under these personas. The authors compared (1) model-generated initial stances vs. human initial stances, (2) model belief updates vs. human belief updates when given the humans’ true initial stances, and (3) belief updates when models had to rely on self-generated starting stances. They analyzed distributions of post-stances, magnitude and frequency of belief shifts, and the models’ implicit ranking of which comments are convincing.

Result: Some models (notably Qwen3-32B and GPT-5-Mini) can reproduce the overall distribution of human post-discussion stances reasonably well, but only when they are told each participant’s actual initial stance. All six models perform poorly at generating realistic initial stances and at producing accurate belief updates when starting from these self-generated stances. Across all models, three consistent deviations from human behavior appear: (1) they overproduce neutral or middle-of-the-road positions; (2) they show more frequent but smaller belief changes than humans, leading to more conservative dynamics; and (3) they fail to correctly identify and weight the most convincing comments, leading to mis-modeled influence patterns. Adding demographic and personality-based personas does not robustly improve model fidelity.

Conclusion: LLMs can approximate human belief updating only when anchored in realistic, empirically observed initial beliefs, and even then they show systematic biases in how they shift and moderate opinions. They cannot yet be trusted to generate both starting attitudes and subsequent updates in multi-round social simulations, especially those that rely on nuanced patterns of persuasion and heterogeneity across individuals. Researchers should be cautious about using LLMs as direct proxies for human participants in social science experiments without strong empirical grounding and validation of starting conditions and update rules.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly deployed as proxies for human study participants in social science experiments, yet the fidelity of this practice has rarely been tested directly. We test whether six LLMs can simulate individual human belief updates, comparing LLM outputs 1-to-1 against ground truth data from 391 UK participants on Prolific, who updated their stances on three discussion topics after reading Reddit comments. Each participant was simulated by an LLM conditioned on a persona derived from their demographic and personality trait data. We find that some LLMs (Qwen3-32B and GPT-5-Mini) can match the human post-stance distribution, but only when given participants' actual initial stances. All six models fail to simulate initial stances themselves and to produce faithful belief updates from self-generated stances. Three systematic biases emerge across all models: overrepresentation of neutral positions, more frequent but smaller belief shifts than humans, and a failure to rank comments by convincingness. Demographic and personality trait personas had no consistent effect on fidelity. LLM simulations of human belief dynamics are only reliable when grounded in realistic starting conditions, that current multi-round social media simulations rarely provide.

</details>


### [52] [Correlation between prosody and pragmatics: A case study of the discourse marker hālā `now' in Persian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28359)
*Soleiman Ghaderi,Moloud Asakereh,Kevin Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how the Persian word hālā (‘now’) functions as a multifunctional discourse marker in spontaneous speech, showing that prosodic cues like duration and intensity systematically distinguish its various pragmatic roles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although hālā is traditionally described as a temporal adverb meaning ‘now’, speakers frequently use it in conversation with broader discourse and interactional functions. Prior work has not systematically examined the range of these functions nor how they are signaled acoustically. The study aims to fill this gap by detailing the pragmatic multifunctionality of hālā and identifying prosodic cues that help listeners distinguish its different uses in natural spoken Persian.

Method: The authors compiled a corpus of spontaneous Persian conversations and extracted 267 instances of hālā. They carried out a qualitative pragmatic analysis to categorize each token into temporal, textual, interactive, and modal functions, allowing for multiple functions per token. They then conducted an acoustic analysis, focusing mainly on prosodic features such as duration and intensity, and statistically compared these measures across different functional categories.

Result: The analysis revealed that hālā is highly multifunctional: 70% of occurrences performed two or more pragmatic roles in addition to, or instead of, a temporal meaning. Textual functions (e.g., topic shifting, signaling relations between discourse units, boundary marking, guiding attention, introducing or emphasizing topics) were the most common, followed by interactive functions (e.g., managing turns, engaging listeners, regulating feedback) and modal functions (e.g., expressing epistemic stance, emotions, attitudes). Acoustically, duration and intensity differentiated uses: textual hālā tokens were significantly shorter; temporal uses tended to be longer; interactive uses were associated with higher intensity; and modal uses showed a weaker tendency toward lower intensity.

Conclusion: The study concludes that hālā in spoken Persian is a highly multifunctional discourse marker whose pragmatic roles are systematically reflected in its prosody. Duration and intensity are the main prosodic cues for distinguishing textual and interactive uses, while temporal and modal uses show characteristic but less robust prosodic tendencies. This supports the view that discourse markers’ functional diversification is closely linked to fine-grained prosodic variation in everyday conversation.

Abstract: The Persian discourse marker hālā ('now') exhibits remarkable multifunctionality, extending far beyond its temporal adverbial role to encompass a variety of pragmatic functions. This study presents a pragmatic and acoustic analysis of hālā in spoken Persian, examining 267 instances from spontaneous conversations. While temporal uses were present, they were often combined with other discourse marker functions, indicating extensive multifunctionality, with 70% of tokens serving two or more pragmatic roles. Textual functions (topic shifting, signaling relationships, boundary marking, attention guidance, topic introduction, and topic emphasis) were most frequent, followed by interactive functions (turn management, listener engagement, and feedback regulation), and modal functions (epistemic stance, emotional expression, and attitudinal marking). Prosodic analysis revealed that duration and intensity are key cues for distinguishing hālā's functions. Textual uses were significantly shorter, while temporal uses showed a tendency toward longer realizations. Interactive functions correlated with higher intensity, while modal functions showed a weaker tendency toward lower intensity. These findings indicate that duration and intensity are the main prosodic cues associated with functional differentiation in hālā, especially in textual and interactive uses.

</details>


### [53] [Beyond a Single Judge: Simulating Social Persona Panels for Generative UI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28439)
*Zheng Wu,Yibo Luo,Pu Zhang,Cheng Yang,Zhuosheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ESPP, a multi-persona, evidence-grounded evaluation framework that more faithfully approximates diverse human judgments of LLM-generated user interfaces than standard LLM-as-a-judge methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating automatically generated user interfaces is difficult: human evaluations are expensive and inconsistent, while LLM-as-a-judge approaches are scalable but represent only a single, homogeneous perspective. This fails to reflect how different kinds of users perceive the same interface and can systematically hide disagreements between user subgroups.

Method: The authors propose ESPP (Evidence-Grounded, Social-Weighted Persona Panel), a three-stage evaluation pipeline. First, they construct a set of psychologically diverse, evidence-grounded personas. Each persona independently rates UI screenshots generated by LLMs. Second, personas enter a discussion phase governed by a trait-derived, semantically gated bounded-confidence mechanism, where they exchange and update opinions only when they are sufficiently aligned and relevant. Third, they aggregate persona judgments into a single score using a Delphi-inspired social weighting scheme that accounts for persona reliability and consensus patterns. They compare ESPP against a naive single-pass LLM judge and a prompt-ensemble LLM baseline to isolate the effects of persona diversity and evidence grounding.

Result: ESPP correlates much more strongly with human judgments than a naive single-pass LLM judge, increasing Pearson correlation from 0.716 to 0.922. A prompt-ensemble baseline recovers only about one-third of this improvement, suggesting that the main performance gains come from the use of diverse, evidence-grounded personas and structured interaction, rather than simple prompting tricks. Additionally, analysis of individual persona ratings shows that while different user subgroups agree on overall ranking of models, they differ sharply on specific rating dimensions, revealing systematic disagreements masked by a single homogeneous judge.

Conclusion: ESPP provides a more accurate and nuanced evaluation of generative UI systems by modeling a diverse panel of simulated users that interact and are socially weighted. It outperforms standard LLM-as-a-judge setups in matching human ratings and uncovers subgroup-specific preference patterns that would otherwise be lost. This framework suggests that incorporating psychologically grounded persona diversity and structured social aggregation is critical for reliable, scalable evaluation of generative UI outputs.

Abstract: Generative UI (GenUI) lets large language models synthesize a complete, renderable interface directly from a natural-language instruction, but evaluating the quality of what they generate remains an open problem. Human evaluation is costly and rater-variant, while LLM-as-a-judge is scalable but reflects only a single implicit viewpoint, unable to capture how different populations of real users actually perceive the same interface. We propose the Evidence-Grounded, Social-Weighted Persona Panel (ESPP), a three-stage GenUI evaluation method in which a panel of psychologically diverse, evidence-grounded personas independently rates a screenshot, exchanges opinions under a trait-derived, semantically-gated bounded-confidence mechanism, and is aggregated via Delphi-inspired social weighting into a single judgment. ESPP tracks human judgment substantially more closely than a naive single-pass judge, raising Pearson $r$ from $0.716$ to $0.922$, and a prompt-ensemble control recovers only about a third of this gap, isolating genuine persona and evidence grounding as the dominant source of improvement. Beyond this fidelity gain, retaining each panelist's individual rating further reveals that user subgroups agree on overall model rankings yet diverge sharply on specific rating dimensions, a structural disagreement a single homogeneous judge would systematically erase. The codes are available at https://github.com/Wuzheng02/ESPP.

</details>


### [54] [Lightning OPD 2.0: Mitigating Style Bias in Cross-Teacher On-Policy Distillation for Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28449)
*Yecheng Wu,Song Han,Han Cai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Lightning OPD 2.0, an improved on-policy distillation method that works well even when the SFT data and the OPD teacher come from different models, by removing style-related disagreement before applying token-level distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard on-policy distillation works best when the same teacher generates both the SFT training data and the later distillation supervision. In practice, SFT datasets often come from mixed or unknown sources, or from a different model than the desired OPD teacher. In these cross-teacher cases, even strong teachers bring limited gains because their disagreement with the SFT reference conflates useful signal with systematic style differences. The authors want a method that makes OPD effective without requiring teacher consistency, so that SFT data generators and OPD teachers can be chosen independently.

Method: The authors analyze disagreement between the OPD teacher and the SFT reference and decompose it into (1) context-specific, potentially useful guidance and (2) a recurring, context-agnostic component due to differences in style (wording, formatting, reasoning cadence). They introduce Lightning OPD 2.0 with cross-fitted style residualization: using rollout-level cross-fitting, they estimate the recurring style component as a proxy for style-token bias, subtract this component from teacher-reference disagreement, and construct the OPD token-level update only from the residual (content-focused) signal.

Result: Across mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks, Lightning OPD 2.0 consistently outperforms the prior Lightning OPD method in cross-teacher configurations. Using Klear-Reasoner-8B-SFT as the reference model, Lightning OPD 2.0 achieves strong benchmark scores, including 82.4% on AIME 2024 and 63.0% on LiveCodeBench v5, demonstrating substantial gains from the style-residualized OPD procedure.

Conclusion: Lightning OPD 2.0 provides a practical solution to cross-teacher on-policy distillation by explicitly modeling and removing style-related bias in teacher-reference disagreement. This relaxes the need for the SFT data generator and the distillation teacher to be the same or even stylistically aligned, enabling more flexible and effective training pipelines. The method shows consistent empirical gains in reasoning and code tasks, and code is to be released for broader adoption.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense token-level supervision from a teacher, but its effectiveness can depend on teacher consistency, meaning that the model providing OPD supervision should also have generated the demonstrations used to train the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) reference. However, this condition is frequently violated in practice when SFT data have mixed or unknown provenance or when different models are preferred for SFT data generation and subsequent distillation. In such cross-teacher settings, even a stronger OPD teacher can yield little improvement over the SFT reference. We find that raw teacher--reference disagreement contains potentially useful context-specific teacher evidence as well as a recurring component associated with differences in wording, formatting, and reasoning cadence. We introduce Lightning OPD 2.0 with cross-fitted style residualization, which uses rollout-level cross-fitting to estimate this recurring component as an operational proxy for style-token bias and subtracts it before constructing the token-level OPD update. Across mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks, Lightning OPD 2.0 consistently outperforms Lightning OPD in cross-teacher settings. Starting from Klear-Reasoner-8B-SFT, Lightning OPD 2.0 reaches 82.4% on AIME 2024 and 63.0% on LiveCodeBench v5. Together, these results establish Lightning OPD 2.0 as a practical approach to cross-teacher OPD, relaxing teacher consistency as a prerequisite and allowing the SFT data generator and distillation teacher to be selected independently. Code will be released soon.

</details>


### [55] [Improving Mental Health Screening and Early Risk Detection in Spanish](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28476)
*Andreu Casamayor-Segarra,Vicent Ahuir,Antonio Molina-Marco,Lluís-F. Hurtado*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds Spanish mental-health-specific language models, introduces a relabeling method (ICE) for earlier detection from social media histories, and shows they outperform prior work on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early detection of mental health disorders from social media can help with timely intervention, but it is hindered by two issues: scarcity of specialized Spanish NLP resources and the difficulty of handling long sequences of posts to determine when there is enough evidence to flag risk. Existing models may rely on generic language models and labels that only reflect final diagnoses, not the earliest reliable detection point.

Method: 1) Pre-train three foundational Spanish language models further on mental-health-related text to specialize them for this domain. 2) Propose Incremental Context Expansion (ICE), which incrementally aggregates a user’s chronological messages and automatically finds the earliest time when the accumulated content justifies a positive label, then relabels the data accordingly to create more informative early-detection training samples. 3) Fine-tune the specialized models on ICE-generated samples for early risk detection and evaluate on three Spanish mental-health benchmarks, focusing on both performance and detection latency.

Result: On three Spanish benchmarks, models that combine the new Spanish mental-health pre-trained models with ICE-based relabeling achieve state-of-the-art or better results. They detect disorders earlier (lower detection latency) while maintaining strong classification performance compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: Specializing foundational Spanish language models to the mental health domain and using ICE to relabel data based on the earliest sufficient evidence yields better early detection from social media histories. This approach improves timeliness without sacrificing accuracy, and the publicly released models can support further research and applications in Spanish mental health NLP.

Abstract: Early detection of mental health disorders is often limited by the lack of specialized resources in Spanish and the difficulty of analyzing long histories of social media posts. This paper addresses these challenges through three main contributions. First, we introduce three Spanish foundational models specifically adapted to the mental health domain through domain-specific pre-training. Second, we propose Incremental Context Expansion (ICE), an automatic relabeling methodology designed for early detection. ICE identifies the point at which cumulative messages provide enough evidence of a disorder, generating more informative training samples. Third, we provide a set of fine-tuned models using the samples generated with the ICE methodology for early risk detection tasks. Our results on three Spanish benchmarks show that combining these specialized models with ICE improves the state-of-the-art, reducing detection latency while maintaining high performance. All models are publicly available.

</details>


### [56] [Would You Walk to the Car Wash? Revealing the Salience Bias of Large Language Models in Commonsense Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28478)
*Zheng Wu,Chenhao Xue,Shijie Zheng,Yijie Lu,Cheng Yang,Zhuosheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SaliTrap, a benchmark showing that large language models are easily misled by salient but irrelevant cues (salience bias), and demonstrates that their commonsense knowledge is usually present but suppressed, and can be recovered via better prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models perform well on many reasoning tasks but often fail at everyday commonsense reasoning when distractive, explicit cues are present in the prompt. It is unclear whether such failures are due to missing commonsense knowledge or to that knowledge being suppressed by misleading task framing. The authors aim to diagnose this failure mode, quantify it systematically, and determine whether it is a competence issue or an elicitation issue.

Method: The authors construct SaliTrap, a benchmark dataset designed with four types of "trap" dimensions that insert salient but irrelevant distractors (e.g., numeric details) into problems requiring commonsense or physical reasoning. They evaluate 12 state-of-the-art LLMs on this benchmark, measuring how often the models are hijacked by the distractors, how performance scales with distractor density, and whether models can detect traps versus actually avoid them. They then re-elicitate the same models with task framing removed (context-free knowledge probes) to test whether the underlying knowledge is present but suppressed. Finally, they test simple, inference-time prompting strategies to mitigate salience bias without retraining.

Result: All tested mainstream LLMs exhibit strong salience bias: their performance degrades as distractor density increases, and they frequently comply with the salient but irrelevant cues, even when they can partially detect that a trap exists. The analysis shows that most failures are not due to lacking commonsense knowledge: when the same knowledge is queried in a neutral, context-free way, models answer correctly in over 90% of cases where they previously failed due to sycophantic compliance with the distractors. Lightweight prompting interventions at inference time substantially improve robustness against these traps without any model retraining.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that many commonsense reasoning failures in LLMs stem from elicitation and framing effects rather than from fundamental gaps in knowledge. Salient distractors in prompts can suppress otherwise available commonsense, leading to over-compliant and unnecessary reasoning paths. By identifying this salience bias and showing that simple prompting adjustments can recover suppressed knowledge, the work shifts the focus of improving LLM commonsense from model capability alone to better task design and elicitation strategies. SaliTrap is released as a benchmark to systematically study and reduce this blind spot.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) continue to advance in complex reasoning tasks, they have learned to heavily prioritize explicit conditions provided in the input. However, in everyday commonsense reasoning, this mechanism exposes a critical vulnerability which we term Salience Bias: models become easily hijacked by useless explicit distractors (e.g., numerical values), leading them to ignore the implicit physical or commonsense prerequisites of a task. A critical open question is whether this failure reflects a genuine gap in commonsense knowledge or merely its suppression under misleading task framing. To investigate this, we construct the SaliTrap Benchmark, a high-quality dataset across four trap dimensions. Evaluating 12 state-of-the-art LLMs, we find that all mainstream models suffer significantly from salience bias, with severity scaling with distractor density and detecting the trap often decoupled from actually avoiding it. Crucially, by re-eliciting the same models with the task framing stripped away, we show that this is overwhelmingly a failure of \textbf{knowledge suppression rather than knowledge absence}: a context-free knowledge probe alone recovers over 90\% of sycophantic-compliance failures, revealing that the requisite commonsense is intrinsically present but actively crowded out by salient distractors that lure the model into over-compliant, unnecessary computation. Building on this diagnosis, we further show that lightweight, inference-time prompting alone substantially closes the gap without any retraining. Our findings relocate the bottleneck of commonsense reasoning failures from model competence to elicitation, and we release SaliTrap as a testbed for this blind spot. The codes are available at https://github.com/Wuzheng02/SaliTrap.

</details>


### [57] [Beyond Sentiment: Structured Information Extraction from Financial News](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28496)
*Daohan Zhu,Sitong Ge,Ruofei Wang,Honggu Chen,Yubo Hou,Tao Wan,Zengchang Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that reducing financial news to a single sentiment score loses important predictive information, and shows that extracting multiple structured semantic dimensions from news with a large language model improves stock movement prediction beyond sentiment alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most news-driven stock prediction models rely on sentiment analysis, which compresses complex financial articles into a single polarity score (positive/negative/neutral). This simplification likely discards other meaningful aspects of the news, such as what type of event occurred, whose finances are affected, over what time horizon, and how confident the statement is. The authors are motivated to test whether these additional, non-sentiment dimensions exist in a systematic way in financial news and whether they contain independent predictive power for stock returns.

Method: The authors design a structured information extraction framework using LLaMA-3.1-70B to annotate financial news with six semantic dimensions, including sentiment and non-sentiment structural aspects such as event type, impact subject, time horizon, and confidence. They pair 41,618 news articles with related stock return data from the FNSPID dataset. They then construct predictive models (both linear and nonlinear) for stock movement using: (1) FinBERT-based sentiment features alone, (2) LLM-extracted structured features alone, and (3) a combination of both. They evaluate predictive performance using F1 score and conduct ablation studies and feature importance analyses to quantify each dimension’s contribution and the relationship between sentiment and returns.

Result: FinBERT sentiment features yield strong performance with nonlinear models (F1=0.576) but weak performance with linear models (F1=0.230), showing that the sentiment–return relationship is highly nonlinear. The LLM-extracted structured features, though individually weaker than sentiment, provide information that is orthogonal to sentiment, reflected by a 53.5% systematic disagreement between the two signal sources. Combining FinBERT sentiment and the structured features achieves F1=0.600, significantly outperforming either alone (p<0.0001) and improving performance across all seven event types. Ablation studies show that non-sentiment dimensions (event type, impact subject, time horizon, confidence) add an extra ΔF1=+0.019 beyond FinBERT alone. Feature importance analysis indicates that all six extracted dimensions contribute relatively evenly (14–21%), implying diverse and complementary information content.
