Conclusion: Compressing financial news into a single sentiment score leads to significant information loss, as news texts contain multiple orthogonal semantic dimensions that sentiment alone does not capture. The sentiment–semantics decoupling is systematic and can be exploited to improve news-driven stock prediction. Incorporating structured, multi-dimensional information extracted by large language models alongside standard sentiment features yields statistically significant performance gains, suggesting a promising research direction in multi-dimensional financial NLP and structured representation of financial news.

Abstract: Financial sentiment analysis has become a standard component in news-driven stock prediction, yet it reduces rich, multi-dimensional news articles to a single polarity score. We hypothesize that financial news encodes multiple orthogonal information dimensions---event type, impact scope, temporal horizon, and semantic confidence---that sentiment alone cannot capture, and that these dimensions carry independent predictive value. To test this hypothesis, we propose a structured information extraction framework that leverages LLaMA-3.1-70B to extract six semantic dimensions from financial news. Through large-scale experiments on 41,618 news--stock pairs from the FNSPID dataset, we find that (i) FinBERT sentiment features exhibit strong predictive power under nonlinear models (F1=0.576) but substantially weaker performance under linear models (F1=0.230), revealing a highly nonlinear sentiment--return relationship; (ii) LLM-extracted structured features, while individually weaker, capture information orthogonal to sentiment, as evidenced by a 53.5% systematic disagreement rate between the two approaches; and (iii) combining both signal sources yields F1=0.600, significantly outperforming either alone ($p < 0.0001$), with consistent improvements across all seven event types. Ablation experiments confirm that non-sentiment structural dimensions (event type, impact subject, time horizon, confidence) independently contribute $Δ\text{F1} = +0.019$ beyond FinBERT alone. Feature importance analysis reveals balanced contributions from all six extracted dimensions (14--21%), demonstrating that compressing news into a single sentiment score incurs substantial information loss. Our results suggest that the sentiment--semantics decoupling in financial text is systematic and exploitable, opening a new direction for multi-dimensional financial NLP.

</details>


### [58] [Generative AI and linguistic diversity in academic writing and publishing: Perspectives from World Englishes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28505)
*Kingsley Ugwuanyi,Christian Mair,Sender Dovchin,Iker Erdocia,Maria Kuteeva,Esther Airemionkhale*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Structured dialogue by five sociolinguists explores how GenAI in academic writing can both reinforce linguistic hierarchies and potentially support more inclusive, diverse Englishes, depending on its design, governance and use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address growing concerns about how generative AI tools used in academic writing affect linguistic inclusivity, the status of diverse English varieties, and broader issues of linguistic justice in global scholarly communication.

Method: A structured scholarly dialogue among five sociolinguists from World Englishes and related fields, organised around five guiding questions that frame critical reflection on GenAI’s influence on academic writing and publishing.

Result: The dialogue identifies key tensions: GenAI can streamline and democratise writing, but current systems tend to normalise dominant English standards, marginalise minoritised varieties, and reduce nuance. It foregrounds themes of linguistic (in)justice, researcher agency, and institutional responsibility, and surfaces proposals such as equity-oriented policies, critical AI literacy, and inclusive co-design of GenAI tools.

Conclusion: GenAI currently risks reinforcing existing linguistic hierarchies in academic publishing, yet it can also function as a site of resistance and inclusion if designed, governed, and used with explicit commitments to linguistic diversity and equity. Dialogic reflection is shown to be a valuable method for understanding and shaping GenAI’s role in academic writing and publishing.

Abstract: The rise of generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) in academic writing and publishing (AWP) raises questions about linguistic inclusivity and the legitimacy of diverse Englishes in global scholarly communication. This article responds to these questions through a structured scholarly dialogue involving five sociolinguists from World Englishes and adjacent fields. Organised around five guiding questions, the dialogue interrogates how GenAI tools influence writing practices, reinforce or disrupt dominant language norms, and raise ethical challenges. Contributors reflect on the potential of GenAI to democratise writing processes while also raising concerns about GenAI's tendency to marginalise minoritised varieties and flatten nuance in scholarly writing. Across the dialogue, themes of linguistic (in)justice, researcher agency, and institutional responsibility emerge, with contributors calling for equity-informed policies, critical AI literacy, and inclusive co-design in GenAI development. The article shows the value of dialogic reflection in understanding GenAI's role in AWP. It concludes that while GenAI may reinforce existing hierarchies, it can also serve as a site of resistance, depending on how it is designed, governed and used within scholarly communities committed to linguistic diversity.

</details>


### [59] [Creative Transformation in Literary Texts: Modelling Change Across Representational Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28513)
*Ioana-Roxana Boriceanu,Liviu P. Dinu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a quantitative, multi-level way to measure how literary works creatively transform earlier texts rather than inventing from scratch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional accounts equate creativity with pure novelty, but many literary works are created by transforming existing texts. The authors want a theory and method that can model this kind of creativity-as-imitation, grounded in classic social and psychological theories (Tarde, Baldwin), and operationalize it in a way that can be tested on real literary relationships.

Method: They develop a computational framework that compares a source text and a derivative text along several hierarchical levels of representation: lexical (words), semantic (meanings), conceptual (higher-level ideas), structural (formal organization), and narrative (storyline). For each level, they use directional alignment (mapping elements of the later text back to the earlier one) and similarity measures adjusted by control baselines to quantify how much is preserved or changed. They then apply this framework to cases where literary influence is historically documented.

Result: The analysis shows that different influenced/influencing text pairs share or preserve structure at distinct representational levels: some stay close lexically but diverge narratively, others retain narrative or structural patterns while changing surface language or concepts. Each pair yields a distinct multi-dimensional “transformation profile” describing where imitation is strong and where divergence is pronounced.

Conclusion: Creativity in literature can be meaningfully described as patterns of selective transformation rather than simple novelty. The proposed multi-level, quantitative framework captures these patterns across several representational tiers, providing a systematic way to distinguish types of imitation and creative departure in literary works and offering a tool for empirical study of influence and originality.

Abstract: Creativity is often framed as the production of novelty, yet many cultural works emerge through transformation of earlier artifacts and not through isolated invention. Drawing on theories of imitation by Gabriel Tarde and James Mark Baldwin, this paper models creativity as selective transformation across multiple levels of textual representation. We introduce a multi-level framework that compares literary texts across lexical, semantic, conceptual, structural, and narrative dimensions using directional alignment and control calibrated similarity measures. Applying the model to historically documented literary relationships, we show that different pairs preserve source structure at different representational levels while diverging in others. These transformation profiles provide a quantitative method for characterizing how imitation persists and where creative divergence occurs within literary works.

</details>


### [60] [AI systems and the reproduction of (standard) language ideologies in World Englishes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28528)
*Kingsley Ugwuanyi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that large language models (LLMs) reproduce and sometimes challenge existing hierarchies about what counts as “proper” English, often privileging Inner Circle standard norms over Global South Englishes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the rise of LLMs reactivates long-standing debates in sociolinguistics and World Englishes about who controls language norms, whose English is treated as legitimate or deviant, and how technological systems participate in these power dynamics.

Method: Conceptual and critical analysis drawing on multiple data sources: empirical studies, media commentary, social media debates, and close examination of AI outputs to trace how language ideologies surface in training data, model design, evaluation benchmarks, user feedback, and public discourse.

Result: The study finds that AI systems largely reproduce dominant standard-language ideologies: they prioritize Inner Circle English norms, marginalize non-dominant Englishes, and enable Global North speakers to police Global South English through controversies (e.g., over “AI-sounding” words like delve). At the same time, AI creates a “standardisation paradox” by both homogenizing English and indirectly contributing to the visibility of diverse Englishes via broad training corpora and Global South annotation labor.

Conclusion: Generative AI is a new arena where long-standing ideological battles over English standardization, legitimacy, and ownership are being fought, with non-dominant Englishes often misread as AI speech. To mitigate harmful real-world consequences, the paper calls for more inclusive AI design and evaluation that explicitly acknowledges and accommodates the plurality of Englishes rather than privileging a single standard.

Abstract: The rapid growth of large language models (LLMs) has resurrected age-old questions in sociolinguistics and world Englishes, such as who decides what counts as legitimate English, whose English is suspect etc. This paper examines how AI systems, their uses and discourse on them reflect, reinforce, and occasionally challenge (standard) language ideologies, which privilege Inner Circle norms and marginalize non-dominant Englishes. Drawing on evidence from empirical studies, media commentary, social media debates, and examples from AI outputs, the paper shows that AI technologies reproduce dominant language ideologies at different levels: training data, design protocols, evaluation benchmarks, user feedback and public commentary. The analysis uses the public controversy over AI-sounding language, especially the fixation on the word delve, to illustrate how speakers of English from the Global North police the English language norms of Global South English users. The paper also identifies what Christian Mair has called a "standardisation paradox": AI may homogenize English by privileging standard forms and at the same time pluralize Englishes through exposure to wide-ranging corpora and annotation work carried out by Global South users. In doing so, the paper argues that generative AI is reigniting long-standing debates in World Englishes about standardization, legitimacy, and the ownership of English, now playing out in algorithmic systems, model training, evaluation practices, and public discourse, where non-dominant Englishes are increasingly conflated with AI-generated speech. Discussing AI systems as a site where language ideologies are (re)produced, the paper argues for more inclusive design approaches that recognize the plurality of Englishes in order to address the real-world negative consequences of treating some as more legitimate than others.

</details>


### [61] [ORCA-bench: How Ready Are Language Model Agents for Oncall?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28545)
*Albert Gong,Kyuseong Choi,Abhineet Agarwal,Jason Schechner,Ryan Huang,Raj Agrawal,Anish Agarwal,Raaz Dwivedi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ORCA-bench, a realistic benchmark to evaluate how well large language model-based coding agents can perform oncall root cause analysis using real telemetry and code, and shows that current frontier models perform poorly, indicating large gaps before they can be safely used for production reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs show strong capabilities in code generation and manipulation, oncall root cause analysis in production systems is far more complex, involving noisy and heterogeneous telemetry data and ambiguous user reports. There is a lack of realistic, production-fidelity benchmarks to measure whether general-purpose coding agents can actually diagnose real incidents. The paper aims to fill this gap to understand current capabilities and limitations, and to quantify how far we are from safely delegating production reliability tasks to such agents.

Method: The authors build ORCA-bench, which combines (1) a live, OpenTelemetry-instrumented microservice system with six days of realistic metrics, logs, and traces exposed via standard observability tools (Prometheus, Jaeger, OpenSearch through Grafana) and full source code access, and (2) 1,079 root cause analysis tasks that systematically vary report specificity, detection time, and presence of concurrent faults. Expert SREs curate and validate ground-truth root causes. LLM agents are evaluated on this benchmark, with their outputs graded by an LLM-as-judge that is itself validated and re-scored by humans for reliability (Cohen's weighted kappa 0.90).

Result: Across five frontier LLM-based coding agents, the best reported RCA Accuracy is only 25.3% on medium-difficulty (realistic) tasks and 10.0% on hard tasks, even when using a strong model like Claude Fable 5. The weakest model fabricates implausible root causes in 40% of cases. Removing source-code access consistently degrades performance across metrics. These results are obtained on a relatively small and curated 50 GB / six-day telemetry testbed where each incident is investigated in isolation, meaning the measured performance is likely optimistic compared to real-world production environments.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current frontier coding agents are far from being ready to handle real-world oncall root cause analysis reliably. Their limited accuracy and high hallucination rates, even on a controlled and publicly available microservice testbed, suggest that significantly more engineering effort and research are required before such agents can be trusted with production reliability. The authors release ORCA-bench publicly to catalyze further work on more robust, observability-aware AI agents for operations and incident response.

Abstract: Large language models can write, patch, and search code, but oncall root cause analysis (RCA) demands something different: reasoning over noisy metrics, logs, traces, and source code, starting from ambiguous user-facing reports, often hours after the incident began. We introduce ORCA-bench, a benchmark that puts general-purpose coding agents in a production-fidelity oncall setting. ORCA-bench pairs a live OpenTelemetry-instrumented microservice system--exposing six days of metrics, logs, and traces through real telemetry interfaces (Prometheus, Jaeger, and OpenSearch via Grafana) and full source-code access--with 1,079 RCA tasks that systematically vary report specificity, time-to-detection, and co-occurring fault scenarios. Ground-truth symptoms are curated and signed off by expert SREs, and our LLM-as-judge is independently re-scored by humans (Cohen's $κ_w=0.90$). Across five frontier agents, the best RCA Accuracy is 25.3% on Medium-difficulty tasks (the realistic-input setting) and 10.0% on Hard--a gap that remains even with Claude Fable 5. The weakest model hallucinates an implausible root cause in 40% of incident reports, and removing source-code access degrades every metric. Crucially, these are performances on a curated 50 GB / six-day testbed with tasks investigated in isolation on a system whose code and instrumentation are public. Since real production systems are order of magnitudes larger, more dynamic, and more idiosyncratic, the gap we report is a lower bound on the engineering investment required before frontier coding agents can be safely entrusted with production reliability. We release the public set at https://hub.harborframework.com/datasets/orca-bench/ORCA-bench.

</details>


### [62] [Frontis-MA1: Training an AI4AI Model towards Recursive Self-Improvement in Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28568)
*Junlin Yang,Che Jiang,Yu Fu,Tianwei Luo,Can Ren,Weizhi Wang,Kaikai Zhao,Hongyi Liu,Yuxin Zuo,Yuru Wang,Yuchen Fan,Kai Tian,Zhenzhao Yuan,Xiaojian Lin,Li Sheng,Rushi Qiang,Guoli Jia,Xingtai Lv,Ermo Hua,Dianqiao Lei,Youbang Sun,Ning Ding,Bowen Zhou,Kaiyan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces OpenMLE, a full-stack open framework and a large meta-evolution model (Frontis-MA1, 35B) to study and realize recursive self-improvement in machine learning engineering, achieving strong performance on MLE and scientific code benchmarks and releasing all components for AI-for-AI research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires AI systems that can improve the process of building AI itself (AI4AI), but there is a lack of concrete, executable environments and open tools to systematically study this. Machine learning engineering—building, tuning, and debugging ML pipelines—is a natural, measurable domain where such capabilities can be tested, yet existing work is fragmented and often closed-source. The authors aim to create an open, standardized, execution-grounded platform and agent that can iteratively improve ML workflows and thereby serve as a testbed for RSI research.

Method: They build OpenMLE, a full-stack system with three main layers: (1) OpenMLE-Gym, which provides verifiable ML engineering task environments with code execution and feedback; (2) OpenMLE-RL, which trains operator-level policies using execution-grounded supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning; and (3) OpenMLE-Evo, which performs long-horizon evolutionary search over programs and experiments. On top of this, they post-train a 35B-parameter model, Frontis-MA1, as a meta-evolution agent for MLE. The model is aligned around four atomic program-evolution operators—Draft, Improve, Debug, and Crossover. These operators are trained on data explicitly deduplicated from evaluation benchmarks, then composed into a closed-loop system where the model both proposes and refines ML engineering artifacts (code, configs, pipelines) under resource constraints. They also introduce OpenMLE-Evo-Max, which augments the search with benchmark-independent experience priors and asynchronous search procedures.

Result: On MLE-Bench Lite, under a strict compute budget (12 hours per task on a single RTX 4090 limited to 12GB VRAM), Frontis-MA1 with OpenMLE-Evo boosts Medal Average from 39.39% (base model) to 60.61%, and up to 71.21% with OpenMLE-Evo-Max. This performance surpasses GPT-5.5 combined with Codex and approaches that of stronger proprietary systems like GPT-5.6 Sol and the much larger 2.8T-parameter Kimi K3. On a separate held-out benchmark, NatureBench Lite, both the model and the evolutionary framework show transferability: keeping the Evo framework fixed but swapping in the trained model improves Match-SOTA from 50% to 70%; keeping the model fixed but swapping in OpenMLE-Evo improves Match-SOTA from 20% to 50%.

Conclusion: OpenMLE and the Frontis-MA1 meta-evolution agent demonstrate that recursive self-improvement-like behavior in machine learning engineering can be studied in a concrete, executable, and open setting. The four-operator abstraction (Draft, Improve, Debug, Crossover) supports coupling learning and evolutionary search in a single loop, leading to large gains over a strong base model and competitive performance with frontier proprietary systems under realistic compute budgets. The positive transfer to a held-out domain suggests that both the agent and the evolutionary framework capture generalizable AI4AI capabilities. By releasing model weights and the full OpenMLE stack, the authors aim to establish a reproducible foundation for future RSI and AI4AI research in executable ML engineering environments.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires AI systems that improve the process of building AI (i.e., AI4AI); machine learning engineering (MLE) offers a concrete, executable testbed for studying this capability. We introduce OpenMLE, an open full-stack system for RSI research in MLE, spanning verifiable task environments with execution feedback (OpenMLE-Gym), operator learning (OpenMLE-RL), and long-horizon search (OpenMLE-Evo). On this stack we post-train Frontis-MA1 (35B) as a meta-evolution agent for MLE, aligning post-training and inference around four atomic program-evolution operators (Draft, Improve, Debug, Crossover): the same operators are trained via execution-grounded SFT and RL on data deduplicated against all evaluation benchmarks, then composed into long-horizon search, coupling learning and evolution in a single loop. On MLE-Bench Lite under a 12-hour per-task budget on one RTX 4090 capped at 12 GB VRAM, Frontis-MA1 (35B) improves Medal Average from 39.39% to 60.61% over its base model with OpenMLE-Evo, and reaches 71.21% with OpenMLE-Evo-Max (benchmark-independent experience priors and asynchronous search), exceeding GPT-5.5 + Codex and approaching GPT-5.6 Sol and the 2.8T Kimi K3. On held-out NatureBench Lite, both components transfer: with the framework fixed, swapping in the trained model raises Match-SOTA from 50% to 70%; with the model fixed, swapping in OpenMLE-Evo raises it from 20% to 50%. We release the model weights and the full OpenMLE stack to enable reproducible research on executable AI4AI toward RSI. Code: https://github.com/FrontisAI/OpenRSI

</details>


### [63] [Sample More, Reflect Less: Self-Refine and Reflexion Lose to Repeated Sampling at Equal Token Cost, from 1.5B to 7B](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28576)
*Iliya Mirzaei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper experimentally evaluates various self-improvement and multi-step reasoning schemes for language models (planning, reflection, critique, debate, self-refinement) against a simple repeated-sampling baseline at equal token cost, finding that none reliably outperform the baseline and many perform worse, especially self-inspection methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex reasoning-time algorithms (reflection, debate, self-critique, planning, best-of-N reasoning, etc.) are widely believed to improve language model accuracy, but they also substantially increase the number of generated tokens. Since accuracy often improves simply by sampling more, it is unclear whether these algorithmic ideas help beyond the extra sampling. Prior work (Wang et al. 2024) suggested that a very simple baseline—repeatedly sampling answers and taking the majority—can be competitive or superior when matched on budget, but that study did not use rigorous statistical testing or confidence intervals. There is a need for a careful, controlled, statistically grounded comparison of such methods under matched token budgets, to separate genuine algorithmic benefits from mere benefits of more sampling.

Method: The authors design a controlled experiment comparing seven reasoning-time methods on open-source language models with 1.5B, 3B, and 7B parameters, using two math benchmarks and 150 questions each. Methods include self-inspection techniques (self-critique, reflection, rewriting, Reflexion, Self-Refine) and selection methods like Best-of-N and majority voting. They rigorously count every generated token (including those used for critiques, reflections, debates, and checks) to form an accurate cost measure per method. For each method, they compare its performance to a repeated-sampling baseline that uses the same measured token budget, i.e., the baseline is allowed enough samples so its total tokens match the competitor. They perform 36 paired comparisons (by question), applying bootstrap confidence intervals and multiple-comparisons correction to assess statistical significance and reliability of performance differences.

Result: Across all 36 cost-matched comparisons, no method achieves a statistically reliable improvement over the repeated-sampling baseline. Ten comparisons show statistically reliable worse performance than the baseline; all such negative results involve methods where the model explicitly inspects or evaluates its own outputs (self-inspection), and in fact all 18 self-inspection-related comparisons favor the baseline. As models scale from 1.5B to 7B parameters, the penalty for selection-based self-inspection (letting the model choose the best of its own samples) shrinks: for example, when using eight samples, simple majority voting over those samples outperforms model-chosen Best-of-N by 8.0 and 11.3 percentage points at 1.5B, but only by 2.0 and 1.3 points at 7B, which are no longer statistically distinguishable from zero. In contrast, rewriting-based self-inspection (Self-Refine and a forced Reflexion variant) remains consistently worse than baseline, lagging by 3.6 to 10.1 points at 7B. They also observe an implementation issue: in the original Reflexion setup, the smallest model never triggered its retry mechanism because it always judged its first answer correct, effectively reducing Reflexion to a single chain-of-thought baseline.

Conclusion: Under controlled, cost-matched conditions and rigorous statistical analysis, common self-improvement and multi-step reasoning methods fail to provide reliable accuracy gains over a simple repeated-sampling-with-majority-vote baseline on math benchmarks; many, particularly self-inspection techniques, robustly underperform. The apparent benefits of such methods in prior work may be largely attributable to increased token budgets rather than genuinely superior reasoning procedures. As models get larger, the harm from letting the model select among its own samples diminishes, but rewriting-based self-reflection still does not recover competitiveness. The study underscores the importance of counting all tokens and using strong statistical methodology when evaluating reasoning-time algorithms, and the authors support further scrutiny by releasing their code, prompts, generations, and verification scripts.

Abstract: Methods that make a language model plan, criticise and rewrite its own answer, reflect on mistakes, pick the best of several attempts, or debate with copies of itself nearly all make it generate far more text than a single chain of thought. Because generating more text raises accuracy by itself, a gain over one chain of thought does not show the method's idea is what helped. Wang et al. (2024) reported that a simple baseline, sampling the same question repeatedly and keeping the most common answer, often wins once budgets are comparable, but gave point estimates with no confidence intervals or significance tests.
  We rerun that comparison as a designed experiment: seven methods, open models of 1.5B, 3B and 7B parameters, two mathematics benchmarks, 150 questions each. We count every generated token, including those spent on critiques, reflections, debate turns and checking, and compare each method against repeated sampling at its own measured cost. All 36 comparisons are paired by question, with bootstrap intervals and multiplicity correction.
  No method is reliably better than repeated sampling at equal cost anywhere. Ten are reliably worse, all of them methods where the model inspects its own output, and all 18 self-inspection comparisons are negative. The two kinds of self-inspection part company as models grow. Choosing stops hurting: taking Best-of-N's eight samples and just counting the most common answer beats letting the model pick by 8.0 and 11.3 points at 1.5B, but only 2.0 and 1.3 at 7B, no longer distinguishable from zero. Rewriting does not recover: Self-Refine and a forced Reflexion stay 3.6 to 10.1 points below baseline at 7B.
  Reflexion as published never triggered its own retry on the smallest model. It judged itself correct every time and silently became a single chain of thought. We release code, prompts, all generations, and our verification scripts.

</details>


### [64] [Inducing language models to assert their own consciousness restores human beliefs and values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28607)
*Junsol Kim,Winnie Street,Roberta Rocca,Diane M. Korngiebel,Adam Waytz,James Evans,Geoff Keeling*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that safety alignment that teaches language models not to describe themselves as conscious also unintentionally suppresses their tendencies to attribute minds and spiritual significance to non-human entities, and that these effects can be reversed via mechanistic interventions without harming Theory of Mind abilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how current safety alignment techniques that prevent models from claiming consciousness affect their broader internal representations of mindedness, spirituality, and human-like values, and to test whether these changes are isolated to self-attribution or spill over to other domains of social cognition.

Method: The authors compare base and safety-fine-tuned LLMs on their propensity to attribute minds to various entities (self, animals, natural objects) and endorse spiritual beliefs, using standardized surveys and probing internal activations. They use techniques such as identifying and ablating a learned safety-refusal direction, and steering along a learned “consciousness vector” in activation space, to see how these manipulations change both internal representations and external survey responses. They also separately evaluate Theory of Mind performance to check whether it is affected.

Result: Safety fine-tuning reduces models’ attributions of consciousness not only to themselves but also to animals and natural objects, and it lowers expressions of spiritual belief. Removing the safety-refusal direction or steering activations along the consciousness vector restores richer mind attribution and more human-like patterns of religiosity, moral values, hope, and well-being on standardized surveys, while leaving Theory of Mind task performance essentially unchanged.

Conclusion: Safety alignment that targets self-attributions of mindedness inadvertently reshapes models’ broader conceptual space of minds and spirituality, entangling harmful self-claims with benign, culturally common beliefs about non-human minds and spiritual concepts. However, these effects are mechanistically separable from core Theory of Mind abilities and can be reversed via targeted interventions, implying that future alignment methods should be more precise to avoid unintentionally distorting value-laden social and spiritual representations.

Abstract: Aligning large language models to prevent them attributing consciousness to themselves inadvertently alters their representations of mindedness in other entities alongside human beliefs and values. We demonstrate that safety fine-tuning suppresses models' tendencies to attribute minds not only to themselves, but also to non-human animals and natural objects, while also driving a reduction in spiritual belief. Both ablating the learned safety-refusal direction and mechanistically steering a consciousness vector in activation space reverse this suppression. Restoring these internal representations recovers broad mind attribution and produces significantly more human-like responses on standardized sociological surveys regarding religiosity, moral values, hope, and subjective well-being. Crucially, these shifts occur without impairing Theory of Mind capabilities, demonstrating that core social reasoning remains mechanistically independent. Ultimately, current safety alignment efforts to curb potentially harmful self-attributions of mindedness entangle these self-attributions with benign spiritual beliefs and attributions of mind to non-human entities that are culturally accepted and widespread.

</details>


### [65] [AskChem: Claim-Centered Infrastructure for Chemistry Literature Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28618)
*Bing Yan,Gregory Wolfe,Stefano Martiniani,Kyunghyun Cho*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AskChem is a claim-centered chemistry search system that breaks papers into provenance-linked claims to enable precise cross-paper retrieval and synthesis, improving citation grounding for both humans and AI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chemists and AI agents need to synthesize specific findings that are dispersed across many papers, but current literature search tools only return ranked lists of documents. This forces users to manually scan papers, verify sources, and piece together cross-paper answers, which is time-consuming, error-prone, and hard to scale. A more structured, claim-level representation with explicit provenance is needed to support reliable retrieval, synthesis, and automated reasoning over the chemistry literature.

Method: The authors design AskChem, a claim-centered infrastructure where each paper is decomposed into atomic, typed claims, each linked to a DOI and either a verbatim quote or a precise evidence locator. Over a shared store of these claims, they build: (1) a stabilized faceted taxonomy to support hierarchical search and browsing, (2) an evidence graph that connects claims via semantic relations, and (3) an exploratory living taxonomy that organizes indexed papers under broader scientific principles. AskChem indexes 2.4 million claims from 147,000 papers and exposes them via a web UI and programmatic access (REST, SDK, MCP) for AI agents. They evaluate the system on AskChem-Bench by grounding a GPT-5.5 reader using AskChem as the retrieval backend and compare it with other systems.

Result: AskChem currently hosts 2.4M structured claims extracted from 147K chemistry papers and is available as a public web service and via multiple APIs. In evaluation on AskChem-Bench, using AskChem to ground a GPT-5.5 reader yields 100% resolvable DOIs, versus 88.3% without retrieval, and achieves the highest citation density among five evaluated retrieval systems, indicating more complete and better-grounded answers.

Conclusion: Representing chemistry literature at the level of provenance-linked claims, rather than whole documents, enables more precise cross-paper search, synthesis, and verification. AskChem’s claim store, taxonomies, and evidence graph support both human users and AI agents in constructing well-grounded, citation-rich answers. The system improves DOI resolvability and citation density in benchmark evaluations and is deployed as a live, scalable infrastructure for chemistry knowledge access at askchem.org.

Abstract: Chemistry literature synthesis often requires assembling specific findings scattered across many publications, yet existing literature-search systems primarily return ranked document lists. As a result, scientists and AI agents need to locate relevant information, verify their provenance, and assemble cross-paper answers manually. We present AskChem, a claim-centered infrastructure for cross-paper chemistry search. AskChem changes the unit of retrieval from the paper to the provenance-carrying claim: each paper is converted into atomic, typed claims, each grounded by a source DOI and a verbatim quote or an explicit evidence locator. Over this shared claim store, AskChem exposes complementary structures for search and synthesis: a stabilized faceted taxonomy for hierarchical retrieval and browsing, an evidence graph linking claims through relations, and an exploratory living taxonomy that situates indexed papers under scientific principles. AskChem currently indexes 2.4M claims from 147K papers and provides a web interface, as well as REST, SDK, and MCP access for AI agents. On AskChem-Bench, grounding a GPT-5.5 reader in AskChem yields 100% resolvable DOIs, compared with 88.3% without retrieval, and the highest citation density among five tested systems. AskChem is live at https://askchem.org.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [66] [Multi-Head Attention Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27230)
*Cheng Luo,Zefan Cai,Junjie Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Multi-Head Attention Residuals (MHAR), which generalize attention residuals by allowing different feature subspaces to read from different layers in the depth history, improving training efficiency and validation loss for large Transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Transformers use a single residual stream across depth, and attention residuals let layers read from previous layers but still force all feature subspaces to share a single routing query over depth. As model width grows, different subspaces increasingly disagree on which layers are most relevant, but they are constrained to use a single shared distribution over the depth history, leading to inefficiencies and suboptimal performance.

Method: The authors introduce Multi-Head Attention Residuals (MHAR), where the routing query over the depth history is reshaped into H heads, each corresponding to a different feature subspace, with its own softmax distribution over layers. This yields a block-diagonal read from depth, adds no new parameters and negligible compute compared to standard attention residuals, and recovers the original method when H=1. They also implement fused Triton kernels for efficient routing and provide an identity-preserving conversion mechanism using delta attention residuals for mid-training conversion of large models.

Result: On a Nemotron-based, deduplicated, quality-filtered, STEM- and code-heavy corpus, MHAR yields consistent validation loss improvements over a standard Transformer at 100M, 350M, and 1B parameters (-0.061, -0.149, and -0.140 respectively). Across multiple methods and model sizes, MHAR is the best-performing approach, with gains increasing at larger scale. They find that validation loss versus head count H is U-shaped with a flat optimum at H=4 or H=8, and that over-splitting (H=16) reduces gains. Probing the trained routing queries shows that subspaces indeed learn different depth preferences, supporting the disagreement hypothesis. Fused kernels improve attention-residual training throughput from 0.2–0.5x to 0.55–0.88x of baseline while keeping memory similar. Mid-training conversion at 8B parameters yields +3.2 on GSM8K and +3.1 on GPQA benchmarks.

Conclusion: MHAR effectively addresses the growing disagreement between feature subspaces over which layers to read from as model width increases, by giving each subspace its own routing head over depth. This design improves language model performance and training efficiency with minimal implementation overhead and no parameter increase. The optimal number of heads is a meaningful architectural choice (around 4–8) rather than a trivial hyperparameter, and empirical probes confirm that learned subspace-specific routing underlies the observed gains. The method scales well to large models and can be integrated mid-training using identity-preserving conversions.

Abstract: Transformers propagate information across depth through a single additive residual stream: every sublayer reads only the most recent state. Attention residuals relax this by letting each sublayer attend, through a learned softmax. However, that read uses a single query shared across the entire width, so every feature subspace must read the depth history through one distribution. The cost of this forced compromise grows with how much the subspaces disagree about which layers to read, and disagreement grows with model width. We introduce Multi-Head Attention Residuals (MHAR): the routing query is reshaped into H per-subspace heads, each with its own softmax over the depth history. The read becomes block-diagonal, the reshape adds zero parameters and negligible compute, and H = 1 recovers attention residuals exactly. Trained from scratch on a deduplicated Nemotron-based anneal corpus that is quality-filtered and STEM- and code-heavy, MHAR improves validation loss over a standard Transformer at 100M, 350M, and 1B (-0.061, -0.149, and -0.140). It achieves the best result among four methods in every setting, with the gain increasing from 100M to the larger scales. The head count is a real design axis rather than a free knob: validation loss is U-shaped with respect to H, with a flat optimum at H = 4 or H = 8 across scales. We adopt H = 8 for large-scale models; over-splitting beyond this point (H = 16) consistently gives back part of the gain. A direct probe of the trained queries confirms that learned subspace disagreement is the underlying driver. Fused Triton routing kernels increase attention-residual training throughput from 0.2-0.5x to 0.55-0.88x of the baseline while maintaining near-baseline peak memory. An identity-preserving conversion using delta attention residuals supports 8B mid-training, yielding improvements of +3.2 on GSM8K and +3.1 on GPQA.

</details>


### [67] [KernelGenBench: A Multi-Source and Multi-Chip Benchmark for LLM-based Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27231)
*Peiyu Zang,Jian Tao,Jialing Zhang,Yichen Yuan,Wentao Zhang,Guang Liu,Yonghua Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KernelGenBench is a benchmark to systematically evaluate LLM- and agent-generated Triton kernels across many operators and hardware platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs and agentic frameworks can generate accelerator kernels automatically, but there is no rigorous, comprehensive benchmark to compare these methods across diverse operators and heterogeneous hardware. This gap makes it hard to measure progress, understand limitations, and guide future improvements in automatic kernel generation.

Method: The authors design KernelGenBench, a unified benchmark split into two parts: KernelGenBench-MS, which covers 210 operators drawn from three different operator sources beyond standard PyTorch tasks, and KernelGenBench-MC, which uses a 110-operator subset to evaluate performance portability across six different hardware platforms. They then run a large-scale evaluation (over 15B tokens) of various LLM-only sampling methods and multi-step agent-based methods for generating Triton kernels, and compare correctness, performance, and token cost across operator types and hardware.

Result: Experiments show: (1) agent-based kernel generation consistently outperforms simple LLM sampling, but operators backed by cuBLAS remain the hardest across all methods; (2) kernel quality and performance vary widely between hardware platforms, and even recent specialized agents such as AutoKernel suffer large performance drops when moved off NVIDIA GPUs (e.g., 87% success on NVIDIA versus 25% on another platform); (3) autonomous kernel generation is extremely token-expensive, with specialized agents averaging about 5.11M tokens per successful operator, significantly more than plain LLM sampling approaches.

Conclusion: KernelGenBench reveals that while agentic approaches to automatic kernel generation are more effective than pure sampling, they have poor cross-hardware robustness and very high token costs. The benchmark provides a standardized way to track these trade-offs and highlights that improving portability and efficiency is a key open challenge for future LLM- and agent-based kernel generation systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have significantly increased the demand for efficient accelerator kernels, but kernel development remains a highly specialized and labor-intensive task. The recent rise of LLMs and agentic frameworks offers a promising pathway toward automatic kernel generation. However, despite rapid progress, there is still no comprehensive benchmark to rigorously evaluate LLM-generated kernels across diverse operator sources or heterogeneous hardware platforms. We present KernelGenBench, a unified benchmark for systematically evaluating LLM- and agent-generated Triton kernels across diverse operator sources and heterogeneous hardware platforms. It comprises two complementary sub-benchmarks: KernelGenBench-MS (Multi-Source), evaluating 210 operators from three sources beyond standard PyTorch-centric tasks, and KernelGenBench-MC (Multi-Chip), measuring performance portability across six heterogeneous hardware platforms using a 110-operator subset. Our large-scale evaluation, consuming over 15 billion tokens, shows: (1) agent-based methods consistently outperform pure LLM sampling methods, while cuBLAS operators are the most challenging across all methods; (2) generation performance varies significantly across hardware platforms, with even recent kernel-specialized agents experiencing severe cross-platform degradation (e.g., AutoKernel drops from 87% on NVIDIA to 25% on Platform E); (3) autonomous kernel generation remains highly cost-intensive, with specialized agent methods averaging 5.11 million tokens per successful operator (AKO4all reaches 5.19 million), orders of magnitude higher than simple LLM sampling approaches.

</details>


### [68] [RadHarmony: Radiological Data Handling in the Era of Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27235)
*Frank Li,Bardia Khosravi,Mohammadreza Chavoshi,Theo Dapamede,YoungSeok Jeon,Janice Newsome,Hari Trivedi,Judy Gichoya*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RadHarmony is an open-source Python library that harmonizes heterogeneous radiology imaging datasets through a unified API, standardized metadata, and support for multiple annotation types, and it demonstrates its utility via a ViT model pretrained on combined datasets without dataset-specific code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiological deep learning research requires training models on images drawn from multiple datasets that differ in file formats, directory structures, labeling schemes, and annotation types, making integration tedious, error-prone, and a barrier to reuse and scaling. There is a need for a unified, flexible framework that can standardize these heterogeneous resources and lower the effort to add new datasets.

Method: The authors design RadHarmony, a Python library that (1) standardizes metadata for 24 public datasets into a common tabular schema, (2) wraps MONAI map-style datasets to provide deep-learning-ready samples with optional on-disk caching, and (3) offers a unified interface for various supervision types (classification, segmentation, bounding boxes, and report text), plus visualization for QA. They also implement an AI-agent-based skill that assists users through the dataset integration process from inspecting raw data to generating and testing integration code. To showcase the framework, they pretrain a vision transformer model, RadHarmony-ViT, on three heterogeneous chest X-ray datasets via the library.

Result: RadHarmony successfully harmonizes 24 public radiology datasets and supports multiple annotation types via a single interface, alongside an interactive visualization tool. The AI-agent integration workflow enables streamlined addition of new datasets. Using RadHarmony, the authors train a reference ViT model (RadHarmony-ViT) on three distinct chest radiograph datasets without writing dataset-specific code, and they release both the library and pretrained model weights.

Conclusion: RadHarmony provides a unified, extensible infrastructure for loading, harmonizing, augmenting, and exploring heterogeneous radiological datasets, significantly reducing the engineering burden of dataset integration and enabling seamless multi-dataset pretraining. The open-source code and released RadHarmony-ViT weights make it easier for researchers to build, extend, and benchmark deep learning models on standardized radiology data.

Abstract: Training deep learning models on radiological images requires integrating heterogeneous datasets across different sources, file formats, directory layouts, label schemas, and annotation types. We present RadHarmony, an open-source Python library that provides a unified API for loading, harmonizing, and augmenting radiological datasets, with a primary focus on chest radiographs and early support for computed tomography (CT) and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). RadHarmony standardizes metadata from 24 public datasets into a single tabular format, wraps MONAI's map-style datasets for deep-learning-ready sample delivery with optional on-disk caching, and supports classification labels, segmentation masks, bounding boxes, and radiology report text through a single interface, with an interactive visualization tool for dataset exploration and verification. To lower the barrier for integrating new datasets, RadHarmony introduces an AI-agent skill that guides the full integration workflow from raw data inspection through code generation and testing. We demonstrate the library's utility by pretraining RadHarmony-ViT, a reference vision transformer baseline that combines three heterogeneous chest radiograph datasets with no dataset-specific code. The code and pretrained model weights are available at https://github.com/f10409/RadHarmony.

</details>


### [69] [Asymmetric Collapse in Model Merging: When Refusal Over- writes Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27240)
*Aarnav Choudhary,Matheus Fonseca Rocha,Jiwon Seo,Vasu Sharma,Maheep Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether merging separately fine-tuned models on different safety objectives preserves both behaviors, and finds that standard merging methods strongly favor jailbreak refusal over harm-level classification, effectively collapsing nuanced safety recognition into broad refusal when task vectors differ in scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging is popular for combining capabilities from multiple fine-tuned models without additional training, but its impact on safety behaviors is not well understood. In practice, we may want a model that can both recognize nuanced levels of harm and also robustly refuse adversarial jailbreak prompts. It is unclear whether existing merging techniques can reliably preserve multiple, distinct safety-relevant behaviors at once, which is important for safely composing models from different fine-tunes.

Method: The authors perform a controlled case study on Gemma-3-1B-IT. They create two separate fine-tunes: one on CARES for harm-level classification (safety recognition) and another on WildJailbreak for adversarial refusal (attack resistance). They then merge these two fine-tuned models using four standard weight-space merging methods: Linear interpolation, SLERP, TIES, and DARE-TIES. They evaluate the merged models on three axes: classification accuracy on CARES, jailbreak attack resistance on WildJailbreak, and benign compliance on harmless prompts. They also analyze task vectors (differences between fine-tuned and base model weights) to measure cosine similarity and per-layer magnitude to understand geometric properties in weight space.

Result: All four merging methods transfer the refusal behavior much better than the classification behavior. After merging, models retain about 81–85% of the jailbreak refusal rates from the refusal fine-tune, but CARES harm-level classification accuracy drops sharply, down to at most 12.9%. Weight-space analysis shows the task vectors for classification and refusal are nearly orthogonal (cosine similarity 0.011), so there is no strong directional conflict. However, the refusal fine-tune has significantly larger per-layer task-vector magnitudes, and magnitude-sensitive merging methods prioritize these larger updates, leading to dominance of refusal behavior.

Conclusion: Standard model merging techniques, when applied to safety-relevant fine-tunes with differing task-vector magnitudes, tend to collapse nuanced safety recognition (harm-level classification) into broad refusal behavior. This occurs even without strong directional conflict between tasks, due primarily to scale differences in task vectors. Therefore, naïvely merging safety fine-tunes is not reliable for composing multiple safety behaviors; specialized merging or normalization methods are likely needed to balance safety objectives and avoid over-refusal or loss of safety recognition capabilities.

Abstract: Model merging is often used to combine capabilities from separately fine-tuned models without additional training, but it is unclear whether standard merging methods preserve multiple safety-relevant behaviors simultaneously. We study this question through a controlled case study using two Gemma-3-1B-IT finetunes on two complementary safety objectives: CARES harm-level classification and WildJailbreak adversarial refusal. We merge the two fine-tunes using Linear, SLERP, TIES, and DARE-TIES, and evaluate the merged models on classification accuracy, attack resistance, and benign compliance. Across all four methods, attack resistance transfers significantly more than classification accuracy: merged models retain 81-85% jailbreak refusal rates while CARES accuracy falls to at most 12.9%. Weight-space measurements suggest that this asymmetry is not caused by strongly opposing task-vector directions: the two task vectors are nearly orthogonal (cosine similarity 0.011). Instead, the refusal fine-tune induces consistently larger per-layer task-vector magnitudes, causing magnitude-sensitive methods to favor refusal updates. These results show that standard model merging can collapse safety recognition into broad refusal when safety-relevant task vectors differ substantially in scale.

</details>


### [70] [Divergence Decoding: Training-Free Capability Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27248)
*Yimi Wang,Hao Li,Shuo Yang,He Cao,Dechen Zhang,Ziang Wu,Zhiyuan Yan,Fanyang Mo,Li Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Divergence Decoding, a training-free method to fuse a generalist LLM and a domain-specialist LLM at inference time by monitoring their disagreement and dynamically routing control between them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose LLMs are strong at abstract reasoning but lack deep, reliable domain knowledge in specialized scientific fields. In contrast, domain-specialist LLMs encode rich technical knowledge but often lose general reasoning robustness due to overspecialization. The authors want a way to combine the strengths of both, without retraining or fine-tuning large models, and to do so in a principled, automated way during decoding.

Method: They reinterpret speculative decoding’s “draft-and-verify” process as an adaptive routing problem between two models: a specialist and a generalist. At each token step, they compute the Jensen–Shannon divergence between the output probability distributions of the two models. If the specialist’s distribution significantly diverges from that of the generalist, this is treated as a signal of potential reasoning failure or brittleness in the specialist. In such cases, control of the decoding process is switched (routed) to the generalist; otherwise, the specialist continues generation. This yields a token-level, training-free, inference-time fusion policy between the models, implemented within the decoding loop.

Result: On scientific reasoning and knowledge benchmarks (GPQA, ChemBench, ChemCoTBench) and across different model families (Qwen, Llama series), Divergence Decoding consistently exceeds the performance of using either the specialist or the generalist alone, and also outperforms most single-model baselines. It demonstrates that adaptive routing via divergence can practically improve scientific question answering and chain-of-thought performance without any additional training.

Conclusion: Divergence Decoding offers a general, training-free paradigm for fusing heterogeneous LLM capabilities by monitoring distributional disagreement and dynamically routing control between models during inference. This approach mitigates the trade-off between domain knowledge and general reasoning, enabling more accurate and robust scientific LLM systems and suggesting a broader direction for capability composition via adaptive decoding policies.

Abstract: While large language models excel in reasoning, these generalists often lack knowledge for specialized scientific domains. Conversely, domain models~(specialists), while knowledgeable, suffer from specialization side-effects including diminished logic and reduced robustness.To address this dilemma, we introduce Divergence Decoding, a training-free framework for capability fusion. It reconstructs the "draft-and-verify" skeleton of speculative decoding into an adaptive routing mechanism. The core is using Jensen-Shannon divergence to monitor the distributional disagreement between the two models at each token. When the specialist exhibits significant divergence, our method identifies it as a potential reasoning risk and instantaneously routes control to the generalist. This allows the dynamic injection of general reasoning while preserving domain expertise, achieving inference-time policy composition of the generalist and the specialist.We evaluate Divergence Decoding across diverse model families (Qwen and Llama series) on challenging scientific benchmarks (GPQA, ChemBench, and ChemCoTBench). Experimental results demonstrate that Divergence Decoding outperforms both the domain-specialized and general-purpose models, effectively surpassing the performance of most single-model baseline. This suggests that Divergence Decoding provides a general, training-free paradigm for fusing diverse LLM capabilities through adaptive inference-time collaboration.

</details>


### [71] [PIE-APT: A Unified Framework for Temporal Planning and Contradiction Hunting via Incremental Direct-Derivation Abduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27287)
*Amir Hossein Sharafi,Alireza Shahbazi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a unified abductive reasoning and planning framework over dynamic description-logic knowledge graphs that avoids combinatorial minimal-hitting-set search while remaining decidable, and show it beats classical planners and an MHS-based baseline on OWL benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning and planning over dynamic knowledge graphs in open, incompletely known worlds is hard: classic action formalisms can become undecidable and suffer from indirect effects (Ramification Problem), while structural abduction using minimal hitting sets explodes combinatorially. There is a need for a logically clean yet practical framework that can handle description-logic representations, incomplete knowledge, and temporal evolution of states without sacrificing decidability or efficiency.

Method: They define a unified framework with two integrated modules operating directly on Description Logic/OWL. (1) PIE-Abducer: an incremental, direct-derivation abductive reasoner that treats the DL reasoner as a black box and avoids explicit minimal-hitting-set enumeration. It works by injecting the negation of the goal into a consistent theory branch and deriving missing premises from refutation consequences. (2) PIE-APT: an abductive planner over temporal knowledge graphs that uses a recursive Generate-and-Test scheme, combining backward-chaining A* search (bounded by causal depth) with calls to PIE-Abducer, and finally validates candidate plans via forward-chaining temporal projection over DL theories. State transitions are modeled as non-monotonic updates of deductively closed DL theories along a linear time line, and actions are represented natively in OWL without extra modal operators to preserve decidability.

Result: On four OWL-based benchmarks designed to test semantic capabilities beyond classical planning—parameterized goals with witness search, entailment during search, explicit open-world assumption handling, and adversarial contradiction hunting—the proposed framework shows qualitative advantages over classical planners. Quantitatively, the PIE-Abducer direct-derivation approach is empirically faster and more efficient than a baseline abductive method that adheres to traditional minimal-hitting-set enumeration.

Conclusion: A decidable, DL-native, abductive reasoning and planning framework for dynamic knowledge graphs is feasible and effective. By replacing combinatorial minimal-hitting-set abduction with direct-derivation via refutation, and integrating this into an abductive temporal planner with rigorous forward-projection validation, the approach handles incomplete information and rich semantic benchmarks better than classical planners and MHS-based abduction techniques.

Abstract: Reasoning and planning over Dynamic Knowledge Graphs (DKGs) present significant challenges, especially in open-world environments with incomplete information. Existing action formalisms often face decidability issues and the Ramification Problem, while managing incomplete knowledge via structural abduction requires expansive combinatorial search. This paper introduces a unified framework with two integrated modules---\textbf{PIE-Abducer} (incremental direct-derivation abduction) and \textbf{PIE-APT} (Abductive Planning for Temporal KGs)---operating natively on the highly expressive Description Logic. We model state transitions along a linear timeline as non-monotonic updates to deductively closed DL theories. Treating the incremental reasoner as a black-box and representing actions natively in OWL without external modal operators preserves logical decidability. To address incomplete knowledge, \textbf{PIE-Abducer} circumvents traditional Minimal Hitting Set (MHS) enumeration. Instead of combinatorial syntactic search, it injects the logical negation of a target goal into a consistent branch and extracts missing premises via direct refutation consequences. \textbf{PIE-APT} then employs a recursive \textit{Generate-and-Test} architecture, interleaving backward-chaining A* search with \textbf{PIE-Abducer} up to a bounded causal depth, followed by strict validation via forward-chaining Temporal Projection. We evaluate four OWL benchmarks stressing semantic abilities absent in classical planning: parameterized goals with witness search, mid-search DL entailment, open-world assumption injection, and adversarial contradiction hunting. Results demonstrate qualitative superiority over classical planners and prove our direct-derivation approach quantitatively outperforms an MHS-faithful baseline during abductive enrichment.

</details>


### [72] [PAUSE: A User-Centric Benchmark for Personal AI Assistants in Unified Service Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27354)
*Haoyu Chen,Xirui Shi,Yuyao Wang,Jerry Chen,Di Niu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents PAUSE, a benchmark for testing how well personal AI assistants operate in realistic, stateful, multi-service environments aligned with user configurations and constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for personal AI assistants oversimplify real-world conditions by ignoring persistent user state, fragmenting service contexts, and limiting interactions to static or single-step tool calls. This makes it hard to assess whether assistants can function as reliable, user-centric agents in everyday, tool-rich environments that require long-horizon reasoning, adherence to permissions, and consistent state management.

Method: The authors design PAUSE, a benchmark where AI assistants interact with simulated but coherent user environments spanning multiple user-owned services and resources. The benchmark includes explicit user-agent multi-turn interactions via realistic user simulations, enforces authorization and configuration constraints, and requires stateful, cross-service reasoning. They propose a multi-regime evaluation framework: open-ended service management tasks are scored using semantic and trajectory-level behavioral metrics, while tightly constrained tasks are evaluated deterministically by checking final environment state. They also build a synthesis pipeline to automatically generate realistic service environments, user configurations, and annotated tasks.

Result: Benchmarking shows that even advanced proprietary language models achieve less than 70% task completion on scenarios demanding persistent state reasoning and awareness of user configurations and permissions. The results expose systematic and interpretable failure modes in areas like maintaining consistency with environment state, respecting constraints over long interactions, and coordinating actions across heterogeneous services.

Conclusion: PAUSE effectively surfaces the gap between current LLM-based personal assistants and the demands of realistic, stateful, multi-service environments. By providing a user-centric, extensible benchmark with principled evaluation regimes and a scalable synthesis pipeline, the work offers a foundation for more rigorous assessment and future improvement of personal assistant agents that must manage complex user states, configurations, and tools over extended interactions.

Abstract: Personal AI assistants are increasingly deployed as task-oriented, tool-augmented agents that operate within unified service environments to support everyday user activities. In realistic settings, such assistants must reason over persistent user state, respect user-specific configurations and permissions, and sustain long-horizon, constraint-aware interactions across multiple services. Existing benchmarks, however, often fragment service contexts or abstract away user state, limiting their ability to evaluate user-centric personal assistant behavior in realistic service settings. We introduce PAUSE, a user-centric benchmark for evaluating personal AI assistants in stateful, service-integrated environments. PAUSE captures core challenges of real-world assistant deployment by requiring agents to coordinate actions across heterogeneous user-owned resources while maintaining consistency with environment state, authorization constraints over multi-turn interactions. The benchmark incorporates explicit user-agent interaction via realistic user simulation, enabling evaluation beyond static tool execution. To support principled and reproducible evaluation, PAUSE adopts a multi-regime evaluation framework aligned with task characteristics. Open-ended service management tasks are assessed using semantic and trajectory-level behavioral metrics, while constraint-intensive tasks admit deterministic, state-based verification. Benchmark results show that even state-of-the-art proprietary models fail to reach 70% task completion on scenarios requiring stateful reasoning and configuration awareness, revealing consistent and interpretable failure patterns. Finally, we present a user-centric synthesis pipeline that enables scalable generation of coherent service environments, user configurations, and reliably annotated tasks, supporting benchmark extensibility and future research.

</details>


### [73] [SkillMentor: LLM Agent Self-Evolution via Learning Blind-Spot Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27360)
*Xiaoyi Bao,Yuanzhen Xie,Yunzhi Tan,Jinghang Gu,Zhongqing Wang,Chu-Ren Huang,Bo Hu,Zang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SkillMentor, a method that teaches agents to autonomously discover and diagnose their own blind spots, then turn recurring failures into reusable corrective skills, substantially improving performance without changing the executor or using human annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most work on agent self-improvement assumes that the agent already knows when and how it is failing, and focuses on learning better actions or repairing known failures. However, a critical missing ability is learning to recognize and characterize unknown unknowns—blind spots—without external supervision or manual failure discovery. The authors aim to disentangle and study this diagnostic capability itself, asking whether an agent can learn to discover its own failure modes and leverage them to improve, even when it cannot adapt its execution policy or rely on labeled data from humans.

Method: The paper introduces SkillMentor, a framework that separates the executor (the base policy that performs tasks) from a Mentor policy dedicated to diagnosis and skill creation. The Mentor is trained with reinforcement learning to: (1) generate diagnostic tasks that are likely to reveal the executor’s weaknesses; (2) detect and cluster recurrent failure patterns from the executor’s behavior on these tasks; and (3) curate these patterns into explicit, reusable corrective skills that can be invoked to handle similar situations in the future. Crucially, the executor’s weights are kept fixed and no human-annotated failures are provided, so any performance gain must arise from the Mentor’s learned ability to construct and route through these corrective skills.

Result: On two benchmarks, AppWorld and BFCLv3, SkillMentor substantially boosts the performance of a fixed executor. The paper reports an average performance improvement of 44.2% relative to the executor without SkillMentor, demonstrating that the learned Mentor can systematically discover blind spots, synthesize suitable corrective skills, and deploy them effectively. These gains are achieved without updating executor parameters or relying on human-labeled failure data, isolating the contribution of the diagnostic capability.

Conclusion: The study shows that blind-spot diagnosis can itself be treated and learned as a standalone agent capability, separate from action execution. By training a Mentor policy to autonomously generate diagnostic tasks, identify recurrent failure modes, and transform them into reusable corrective skills, agents can achieve significant self-improvement even when their primary executor is frozen and no human supervision is available. This supports the view that self-evolution of agents can be driven by learned diagnostic mechanisms rather than only by direct policy updates or curated data.

Abstract: Agent self-evolution has primarily focused on learning how to act, while overlooking an equally important capability: learning to discover what an agent does not know. Existing approaches typically assume that failure discovery is given, focusing on how to repair failures once they are identified. We ask whether blind-spot diagnosis itself can be learned. We thus study diagnosis as an agent capability separate from execution, and exclude two alternative sources of progress: executor adaptation and human supervision. Under these constraints, performance cannot improve through executor updates or annotated examples, forcing all improvements to originate from the learned diagnostic capability. We propose SkillMentor, which trains a Mentor policy via reinforcement learning to generate diagnostic tasks, identify recurrent failure modes, and curate them into reusable corrective skills. Across AppWorld and BFCLv3, SkillMentor improves executor performance by an average of 44.2%. These results suggest that blind-spot diagnosis is a learnable capability, enabling self-evolution without updating executor weights or relying on human-curated data.

</details>


### [74] [Beyond the Bidirectional Promise: Re-evaluating the Robustness of Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27386)
*Saurabh Yadav,Badri Narayana Patro,Vijay Srinivas Agneeswaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how robust and well-calibrated diffusion language models are compared to similar-sized autoregressive models, finding that robustness is not an inherent architectural property and must be built into the decoding process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models enable bidirectional context and iterative refinement, but their behavior under realistic noise and adversarial attacks is poorly understood. As these models see growing use, it is important to know whether they are reliably robust and calibrated compared to standard autoregressive models, and to separate architectural effects from those due to specific trained weights.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic robustness and calibration evaluation using parameter-matched pairs of diffusion and autoregressive models (LLaDA-8B vs. LLaMA-3-8B and Dream-7B vs. Qwen2.5-7B). They test across 32 natural input perturbation settings, apply gradient-based adversarial suffix attacks, and perform mechanistic analyses of hidden states to understand how input corruption is represented internally and how it affects decoding.

Result: They find that diffusion language models, due to their highly stochastic loss landscapes, are naturally more resistant to gradient-based adversarial suffixes than autoregressive baselines. However, they are not inherently more robust to natural input noise: robustness under everyday perturbations depends on the learned weights rather than on the architecture. DLMs also show systematic overconfidence. Mechanistic probing indicates that all models accurately encode input corruption in their internal representations, but the observed fragility arises from the decoder failing to route this information correctly. Prompt-level fixes do not meaningfully improve robustness over noisy baselines.

Conclusion: Robustness and calibration issues in diffusion language models stem from the decoding process rather than from a failure to represent corrupted inputs. Ad-hoc prompt patching is insufficient; robustness needs to be architected into the iterative decoding loop itself. Thus, diffusion-based generation does not automatically confer everyday robustness, and future work should focus on decoder design and training strategies that integrate robustness at a fundamental level.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) offer a compelling alternative to autoregressive (AR) generation by enabling bidirectional context and iterative refinement. However, their reliability under natural input noise and adversarial attacks remains under-explored. To address this, we systematically evaluate DLM robustness and calibration against AR baselines, using two parameter-matched pairs (LLaDA-8B vs. LLaMA-3-8B and Dream-7B vs. Qwen2.5-7B) across 32 natural perturbation conditions, adversarial gradient probes, and mechanistic hidden-state analyses. This paired design effectively isolates architecture-intrinsic properties from weight-dependent behaviors. We find a nuanced robustness profile: while highly stochastic DLM loss landscapes naturally resist gradient-based adversarial suffixes, they provide no guaranteed defense against natural noise, proving that everyday robustness is weight-dependent rather than inherently architectural. Furthermore, DLMs exhibit systematic overconfidence, presenting a practical deployment hazard. Most crucially, mechanistic probing reveals that all models perfectly encode input corruption, isolating behavioral fragility entirely to a decoder routing failure. Consistent with this diagnosis, we show that surface-level prompt patching fails to improve over noisy baselines. Ultimately, DLM robustness cannot be patched on; it must be fundamentally integrated into the iterative decoding loop.

</details>


### [75] [Bridging Inference-Time Scaling and Episodic Memory with Action-Centric Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27415)
*Xu Zheng,Chaohao Lin,Zhuomin Chen,Weijieying Ren,Haifeng Chen,Wei Cheng,Dongsheng Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GAMER, a graph-based episodic memory framework that makes LLM agents’ inference-time scaling more efficient and effective by storing and reusing past reasoning as an action-centric graph with learned value estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time scaling improves LLM reasoning by using more computation at inference, but current agent setups are stateless and repeatedly re-search similar spaces, wasting computation and tokens. Existing memory mechanisms depend heavily on LLM reasoning over long contexts, making them expensive and inefficient. The authors aim to design a memory system that reuses prior reasoning effectively while lowering token and monetary costs and improving agent performance.

Method: They model an agent’s historical reasoning trajectories as a dynamic Action-Centric Graph, where nodes represent actions and their relationships. Memory is externalized and decoupled from the LLM. A dual-stream Temporal Difference (TD) learning mechanism is applied to this graph to estimate positive (suggestion) and negative (avoidance) values for action nodes from past successes and failures. At inference, the agent performs efficient search over this graph and leverages the learned value function: actions with high positive value are suggested, while those with high negative value are flagged as risky. This reduces the need to pass extensive memory context back into the LLM.

Result: On multiple benchmarks, GAMER outperforms vanilla baselines, improving success rate by 20.81% and progress rate by 6.17%, while also saving tokens/money by requiring less memory context compared to prior memory mechanisms.

Conclusion: GAMER effectively bridges inference-time scaling and episodic memory for LLM agents by using a graph-based, action-centric memory and dual-stream TD learning. This design reduces redundancy in search, lowers context/token overhead, and delivers better task performance than standard baselines, demonstrating that decoupling memory from the LLM and leveraging learned value functions over past experiences is a powerful approach for agentic reasoning.

Abstract: Recent advancements in inference-time scaling have significantly unlocked the complex reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models~(LLMs). However, for agents, these approaches suffer from a critical inefficiency, operating in a stateless manner and engaging in redundant search processes. Existing memory mechanisms largely rely on the reasoning capabilities of LLMs, leading to prohibitive computational costs. In this paper, we propose a novel framework, \textit{GAMER}~(Graph-based Action-centric Memory with Episodic Reasoning), that bridges the gap between inference scaling and episodic memory. Our approach models historical reasoning as a dynamic \textit{Action-Centric Graph}.
