By decoupling the memory mechanism from LLMs, our method can save token/money usage by providing less memory context than memory mechanism baselines. To extract knowledge from the graph effectively, we use a dual-stream Temporal Difference learning mechanism to estimate the positive~(suggestion) and negative~(avoidance) value of action nodes based on past successes and failures. During the inference phase, this learned value function optimizes decision-making bi-directionally, so that positive values provide action suggestions, while negative values indicate high-risk actions. By performing efficient searches on the graph, our method significantly improves the efficiency of inference scaling. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that \textit{GAMER} achieves superior performance by \textbf{20.81\%/6.17\%} for success/progress rate compared to vanilla baselines.

</details>


### [76] [Dimensionality and Measurement Precision in HLE's Multiple-Choice Subset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27420)
*Mayank Sharma,Savira Nadela,Tyler Matteson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes Humanity's Last Exam (HLE) using psychometric methods and finds it behaves as a nearly one-dimensional test of general reasoning with limited precision for frontier models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HLE is a popular benchmark for evaluating state-of-the-art language models, with scores often broken down into eight domains and interpreted as distinct capabilities. However, it is unknown whether these domains reflect real, separable underlying abilities or whether the test has sufficient measurement precision to distinguish between similarly strong models. The paper aims to fill this gap.

Method: The authors evaluate 29 large language models on the text-only multiple-choice subset of HLE (428 items). They fit a two-parameter logistic Item Response Theory (IRT) model to analyze the benchmark’s latent structure and measurement properties. They compute McDonald’s omega hierarchical, examine how much item response variance is explained by domain labels, compare residual correlations within vs. between domains, and correlate domain-specific scores with total scores. They also analyze the test information function to understand at which ability levels the benchmark is most precise.

Result: The IRT analysis shows that HLE is essentially unidimensional, measuring a single general reasoning factor: McDonald’s omega_h is 0.998, domain labels account for only 3.5% of item response variance, residual correlations are nearly the same within and between domains (Cohen’s d = 0.016), and domain-specific abilities are highly redundant with the overall score (correlations ≥ 0.81). The test information function peaks at moderate ability levels and declines steeply above θ = 0, where current frontier models lie, indicating reduced precision in that range.

Conclusion: HLE’s domain subscores should not be interpreted as reflecting distinct capability areas, since the benchmark primarily measures one general reasoning construct. Moreover, its measurement precision is poorly aligned with the ability levels of frontier language models, limiting its usefulness for fine-grained comparison among the strongest systems.

Abstract: Humanity's Last Exam (HLE) is widely used to evaluate frontier language models. HLE organizes its questions into eight subject-domain categories, whose subscores are often interpreted as evidence of distinct capabilities. However, no study has assessed whether these labels correspond to empirically separable latent constructs, nor whether the benchmark effectively differentiates between models of similar ability. We evaluate 29 LLMs on the text-only multiple-choice subset of HLE ($J = 428$ items) and apply psychometric methods to assess both the dimensionality of the benchmark and the distribution of its measurement precision. Fitting a two-parameter logistic IRT model, we find convergent evidence that HLE measures a single general reasoning factor: McDonald's $ω_h = 0.998$, domain labels explain only 3.5\% of item response variance, within- and between-domain residual correlations are nearly identical (Cohen's $d = 0.016$), and domain-specific ability estimates are near-redundant with the total score ($r \geq 0.81$). A separate analysis of the test information function reveals that measurement precision concentrates at moderate ability levels and drops sharply above $θ= 0$, where frontier models sit. These findings suggest that HLE's domain subscores do not warrant distinct capability interpretations and that the benchmark's ability to discriminate among the strongest models is limited.

</details>


### [77] [Leveraging Trajectory Graphs for Pre-Execution Error Diagnosis in Agentic LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27443)
*Xu Zheng,Zhuomin Chen,Chaohao Lin,Hua Wei,Haifeng Chen,Wei Cheng,Dongsheng Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a graph-based ‘copilot’ that reviews and flags risky actions for LLM agents before execution, reducing compounding errors in long, interactive tasks and significantly improving success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents perform well in many tasks but struggle in long-horizon interactive settings (e.g., embodied AI) due to large action spaces and compounding errors: one bad action can ruin the whole trajectory and waste a limited step budget. Existing solutions like fine-tuning are costly, so the authors seek a cheaper, more general method to reduce such trajectory-level failures.

Method: They introduce Trajectory Graph Copilot, which acts as a pre-execution ‘debugger’ for LLM agents. A Graph Debugger models historical trajectories as a probabilistic graph, where nodes and edges capture sequential action patterns. A Graph Neural Network is trained on this graph to identify action sequences that tend to lead to failure. At inference time, the copilot inspects proposed next actions in a sandbox, predicts whether they resemble failure-prone patterns, and issues early warnings so the agent can revise its action before committing it.

Result: On four benchmarks and across three different LLM agents, incorporating the Trajectory Graph Copilot into the decision loop yields an average improvement of 14.69% in pass ratio (task success rate) relative to baseline LLM agents without this copilot mechanism.

Conclusion: Modeling past trajectories as a probabilistic graph and using a GNN-based debugger to preemptively flag risky actions enables LLM agents to self-correct before making costly mistakes, substantially boosting their ability to solve long-horizon tasks without expensive fine-tuning. This demonstrates the value of proactive, trajectory-level error diagnosis in complex interactive environments.

Abstract: Large Language Model~(LLM)-based agents have demonstrated exceptional performance across a wide range of complex interactive tasks. However, they often struggle with long-horizon interactive tasks common in domains, such as embodied AI. The complexity and vast action spaces in these settings lead to compounding errors, where a single suboptimal action can derail an entire trajectory, causing the agent to exhaust its limited step budget on inefficient or unrecoverable paths. To overcome this without costly fine-tuning, we draw inspiration from software debugging, where execution logs are analyzed to preemptively catch errors. We propose \textit{Trajectory Graph Copilot}, a novel framework that acts as a ``copilot'' for LLM agents by diagnosing potential action errors before they are executed. At its core,\textit{Graph Debugger} models historical trajectories as a probabilistic graph and uses a Graph Neural Network to identify sequential action patterns that frequently lead to failure. Functioning as a proactive diagnostic sandbox, our method provides early warnings on potentially flawed actions, prompting the agent to self-correct. This pre-action error diagnosis prevents costly mistakes, significantly enhancing the agent's ability to complete long-horizon tasks successfully. The extensive experiments on four benchmarks with three LLM agents demonstrate a $14.69\%$ pass ratio improvement on average.

</details>


### [78] [VAmoS Bench: Voice Agent Simulation Bench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27453)
*Joshua Meyer,Sahar Shayegan,Ritiz Tambi,Ali Khan,Sun Kim,Victor Shih,Mehdi Jamei,Andi Partovi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VAmoS Bench, an end-to-end benchmark for evaluating whether voice agents can correctly handle full customer-support phone calls (containment), not just component-level metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing voice-agent benchmarks mainly evaluate narrow aspects like ASR accuracy, latency, naturalness, and turn-taking. They rarely test whether an automated agent can correctly complete a real customer-support call on its own (containment), including refusal and redirection cases. This gap is especially important in contact centers where business value depends on how many calls are resolved without human agents and without policy or safety violations.

Method: The authors design VAmoS Bench, a simulation framework for evaluating full voice-agent systems in a realistic, stateful customer-support setting. They define a fictional credit-card support agent, Riley, who can perform specific operations (freeze, cancel, replace, activate cards) via five tools that run real SQL against a seeded PostgreSQL backend. They create 100 scenarios, each with a simulated caller, a private goal, and an isolated database instance, with about one-third of scenarios being adversarial. Interactions are conducted over audio, and each scenario includes binary assertions that encode success/failure conditions regarding both behavior and database state. A grader checks the entire interaction trace—dialogue, tool calls, arguments, and returned rows—against these assertions.

Result: The benchmark provides a concrete, reproducible way to evaluate complete voice-agent systems on realistic, stateful tasks in financial customer support. It can detect mismatches between what the agent claims and what it actually does in the database, as well as violations like leaking protected information. The system supports multiple tools, adversarial scenarios, and a full end-to-end pipeline from audio input to database updates and responses.

Conclusion: VAmoS Bench fills a key evaluation gap by measuring end-to-end call handling (containment) for voice agents in a realistic credit-card support domain, including both task success and safety/compliance aspects. Its protocol supports an evolving leaderboard where different voice agents can be evaluated on the same benchmark version, and future versions can extend to more tasks and domains, fostering progress in robust, production-ready voice agents.

Abstract: Production voice agents span cascaded, speech-to-speech, and hybrid architectures. Voice-agent benchmarks typically measure component quality and conversational properties such as word error rate, latency, naturalness, and turn-taking. Fewer measure whether the agent handled a phone call correctly on its own. Contact centers refer to this as ``containment'': the share of phone calls the automated system resolves without handing off to a human. On some phone calls the right outcome is refusal or a redirect. To address this gap, we introduce VAmoS Bench, the Voice Agent Simulation Bench. It measures complete voice-agent systems end to end in a stateful customer-support task. The agent is Riley, a credit-card support representative for a fictional bank who can freeze, cancel, replace, or activate a card. Each of 100 scenarios supplies a simulated caller with a private goal and a seeded PostgreSQL backend. The platform uses each scenario to populate and activate an isolated simulation in which the caller reaches Riley over audio; roughly one-third apply adversarial pressure. The agent can use five tools that execute real SQL against the backend. Each scenario also defines binary assertions. A grader evaluates them against the complete trace of what the caller and agent said and what the agent did, including tool invocations, arguments, and returned rows. This catches an agent that claims to have changed a card without updating the database, as well as one that makes the right database change while disclosing protected information. This first benchmark version focuses on financial services. Its evaluation protocol supports an evolving leaderboard: additional voice agents can be evaluated on the same version, while later versions can expand the tasks and scenarios.

</details>


### [79] [Skill Use or Skill Theater? Evaluating the Reasoning Backroom in Skill-Augmented Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27484)
*Jinwei Hu,Yi Qi,Xinmiao Huang,Youcheng Sun,Yi Dong,Xiaowei Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces BACKTRACE and BACKROOMBench to show that language agents’ stated use of reusable skills often does not match the actual causal influence those skills have on decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents can be extended with reusable skills, but current evaluations typically rely on what the agent says or appears to use, not on what truly affects its decisions. This creates a gap between visible reasoning and real causal influence, raising concerns about provenance, reliability, and auditability of AI reasoning.

Method: They define the concept of a “Reasoning Backroom” and propose BACKTRACE, an evaluation framework that, for each skill-augmented answer, constructs matched no-skill counterfactuals and performs interventions on various aspects of skills, including meaning, wording, identity, content, and assignment. They elicit attributions only after answers are finalized. They implement this as BACKROOMBench, a benchmark spanning logic and competition math tasks, multiple skill configurations, single- and multi-agent setups, and several model families. They also test observational detectors like explicit skill-use claims, text mentions, trace similarity, and an LLM judge.

Result: Experiments show a pervasive provenance failure: agents’ stated skill use is often unchanged even when actual causal reliance and the impact of skills vary. They find silent uptake (skills affect behavior without being acknowledged) and performative use (skills are claimed but not causally used). Behavioral changes track procedural content more than skill labels, while self-attributions are heavily driven by the mere presence of artifacts. Observational detection methods fail to reliably identify decisions that truly depend on skills. In multi-agent settings, skill influence can persist through communication despite losing the original source, and teams without skills still reference nonexistent skills and sources.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that there exists a general “Reasoning Backroom” problem in AI systems: a systematic disconnect between visible or stated reasoning and the underlying causal drivers. Provenance for skill use cannot be reliably audited using observational or self-reported signals alone; effective auditing requires active interventions like those in BACKTRACE.

Abstract: Reusable skills are becoming a standard interface for extending language agents with task procedures. Yet evaluators usually infer skill use from visible reasoning or the agent's own attribution. These signals show what the agent appears to use, not whether the skill changed its decision. We ask whether skill-augmented agents exhibit a \textbf{Reasoning Backroom}, a systematic gap between stated skill use and intervention-measured influence. We introduce BACKTRACE, an evaluation framework that pairs each skill-conditioned answer with a matched no-skill counterfactual, intervenes on skill meaning, wording, identity, content, and assignment, and elicits attribution only after the answer is committed. We instantiate the framework as BACKROOMBench, a verified testbed spanning controlled logic and competition mathematics, multiple skill conditions, single-agent and multi-agent settings, and diverse model families. Our evaluation reveals a pervasive provenance failure. Across models and domains, stated skill use often remains stable while causal reliance and signed utility vary, producing both silent uptake and performative use. Behavioral effects follow procedural content more reliably than displayed skill identity, whereas stated attributions respond strongly to artifact availability. Observational detectors based on direct skill-use claims, text mentions, trace similarity, and an LLM judge do not identify which decisions actually depend on the skill. In multi-agent systems, skill influence can survive communication even after its source is lost, while no-skill teams still name skills and sources that were never supplied. These findings establish the Reasoning Backroom as a general AI provenance problem whose audit requires intervention.

</details>


### [80] [INCLAIR: Inception-Based Longitudinal Clinical Anomaly Detection with Informed Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27487)
*Maxx Richard Rahman,Wolfgang Maass*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces INCLAIR, a framework for detecting anomalies in irregular, sparse longitudinal clinical profiles and explaining them in natural language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detecting anomalies in patients’ longitudinal clinical data is hard because abnormal signals are sparse, patient histories vary in length, and obtaining expert explanations is expensive. Existing methods struggle to both detect subtle anomalies robustly and provide clinically meaningful, grounded explanations under limited expert supervision.

Method: The authors propose INCLAIR, which (1) scores each observation in a patient’s profile against multiple historical contexts; (2) aggregates these scores to the profile level using a mean subsequence score that, under exchangeability assumptions, forms an order-l U-statistic; (3) uses the resulting variance decomposition to derive an incomplete-subset approximation that keeps computation independent of sequence length; (4) analyzes how mean aggregation can dilute localized anomalies and therefore introduces validation-selected top-k pooling; and (5) generates grounded natural-language explanations of detected anomalies with limited expert labels to guide the explanation model.

Result: On three clinical datasets, INCLAIR achieves consistently better anomaly detection performance than state-of-the-art baselines. A focused case study on longitudinal steroid profiles shows that INCLAIR’s anomaly predictions and generated explanations align well with domain experts’ assessments supported by DNA analysis.

Conclusion: INCLAIR provides an efficient and accurate framework for anomaly detection in longitudinal clinical data, with theoretically grounded scoring and aggregation, computationally scalable inference, and clinically meaningful, expert-aligned natural-language explanations, enabling clinically actionable decisions even when expert supervision is scarce.

Abstract: Detecting anomalies in longitudinal clinical profiles is clinically important but difficult: abnormal evidence is often sparse, patient histories have unequal length, and expert explanations are costly. We propose INCLAIR, a framework that scores each observation against multiple historical contexts, aggregates evidence at the profile level, and generates grounded natural-language explanations under limited expert supervision. Under stated within-profile exchangeability assumptions, the complete mean subsequence score takes an order-$l$ U-statistic form, yielding a variance decomposition and an incomplete-subset approximation that controls combinatorial inference cost independently of profile length. The same analysis shows that mean aggregation attenuates localized anomalies by a factor set by the anomaly support and profile length, motivating validation-selected top-$k$ pooling. Across three clinical datasets, INCLAIR consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. We further validate practical relevance through a case study on longitudinal steroid profiles, comparing INCLAIR's predictions and explanations against domain-expert assessments supported by DNA analysis. The results show that INCLAIR enables clinically actionable anomaly detection under limited expert supervision.

</details>


### [81] [MedLLM: An Open Medical Language Model at the Sub-Billion Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27490)
*Maxx Richard Rahman,Asim Ahmed,Mihan Mohagheghzadeh,Wolfgang Maass*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedLLM is an open, very small (0.1B) medical language model trained with a three-stage pipeline that shows non-uniform performance loss under compression, performing surprisingly well on context-grounded medical QA while struggling more on pure knowledge-recall tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most open medical language models are large (≥7B parameters), leaving it unclear how small models behave in medical tasks and whether useful medical competence is possible at sub-billion scale. The authors want to understand how model compression affects different kinds of medical abilities and to provide an open, lightweight model and dataset for the community.

Method: They build a 100M-parameter medical LLM and train it in three phases: (1) general pretraining using curriculum sequence-length scheduling; (2) domain-specific fine-tuning on MedFineWeb, a newly released corpus extracted from general web data via embedding similarity to medical QA examples; and (3) preference-aligned fine-tuning that combines supervised fine-tuning with direct preference optimization (DPO). They then evaluate on multiple medical benchmarks covering context-grounded QA and knowledge-recall QA, comparing against 7B medical and general models.

Result: MedLLM, despite its small size, performs competitively on context-grounded QA: it is within 2.9 percentage points of a medically adapted 7B model and outperforms instruction-tuned and general-purpose 7B baselines. On knowledge-recall QA, its performance is uneven: it stays near the floor on MedQA clinical vignettes but significantly outperforms all 7B and sub-7B baselines on MedMCQA. This reveals that performance limitations on some recall tasks are mostly due to capacity constraints rather than insufficient medical adaptation.

Conclusion: Medical capability under heavy compression is not uniformly degraded; instead, performance splits by task type, with context-grounded reasoning holding up better than some forms of pure knowledge recall. This task-specific dissociation is obscured at 7B scale, where both abilities coexist, but emerges clearly in small models. The work introduces MedLLM and MedFineWeb as open resources and suggests that sub-billion medical LLMs can be practically useful for certain tasks while clarifying where capacity is the main bottleneck.

Abstract: Open medical language models have converged on a single scale: every widely used system runs at 7B parameters or more, leaving the sub-billion regime uncharacterized. We present MedLLM, an open 0.1B-parameter medical language model trained through a fully open three-phase pipeline: general pretraining with curriculum sequence-length scheduling, domain fine-tuning on MedFineWeb, a reference-guided medical corpus we release that is selected from general web data by embedding similarity to medical question-answering (QA) data, and preference-aligned fine-tuning combining SFT with direct preference optimization (DPO). Across medical benchmarks, MedLLM shows a pattern visible only at sub-billion scale: medical competence does not degrade uniformly under compression but splits by task type. On context-grounded QA it comes within $2.9$pp of a medically adapted 7B model and surpasses the instruction-tuned and general-purpose 7B baselines; on knowledge-recall QA it stays near the task floor on clinical-vignette MedQA yet significantly exceeds every 7B and sub-7B baseline on MedMCQA, indicating that where recall fails the constraint is model capacity rather than adaptation. This dissociation is masked at 7B, where both capabilities are present, and surfaces only when capacity is scarce.

</details>


### [82] [A dataset of rated conceptual arguments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27499)
*Emery Cooper,Caspar Oesterheld,Linh Chi Nguyen,Alexander Kastner,Ethan Perez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well large language models handle hard-to-verify conceptual questions by focusing on the quality of individual arguments instead of final answers, introducing an expert-annotated dataset of argumentative critiques and benchmarking models on it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs have shown strong performance on tasks with clear right-or-wrong answers (math, coding), there is little understanding of how they reason about conceptual questions—like philosophical or AI safety questions—where no objective ground truth or standard solution method exists. These questions still allow meaningful progress through argumentation and critique, so the authors want a way to measure model performance in this realm.

Method: The authors construct a dataset containing 442 position texts and 951 argumentative critiques of those positions. Six expert raters label 1,458 critique instances along multiple dimensions: centrality to the main issue, argumentative strength, correctness, and clarity. They then define two scoring functions that map model outputs or evaluations to these expert judgments and use these scores to benchmark a range of LLMs on the task of evaluating or generating critiques.

Result: The dataset enables quantitative comparison of model performance on evaluating and producing argumentative critiques for conceptual questions. Across models, the resulting scores align with general capability rankings observed on other benchmarks, suggesting that stronger general-purpose models are also better at this kind of argument evaluation and generation.

Conclusion: Evaluating LLMs on conceptual reasoning is feasible by shifting focus from uncheckable bottom-line answers to more reliably assessable individual arguments. The authors’ argument-critique dataset and scoring functions provide a foundation for benchmarking models on such tasks, and initial experiments show that performance correlates with overall model capability, offering a practical path to study and improve LLMs’ performance on difficult conceptual domains.

Abstract: Large language models have improved rapidly on tasks with verifiable answers, such as mathematics and programming. Much less is known about their ability to reason about what we call conceptual questions: questions for which no ground truth is realistically accessible and no widely accepted resolution methodology exists, but on which progress can still be made by debating arguments. Most philosophical questions are of this kind, as are central components of questions in AI safety, decision theory, and social choice. Our approach is based on the view that while bottom-line conclusions on such questions are hard to evaluate, individual contextualized arguments can be evaluated far more reliably. We therefore introduce a dataset of 951 argumentative critiques of 442 position texts, spanning topics from AI safety and decision theory to ethics and politics, with 1,458 ratings by six expert raters along dimensions including centrality, strength, correctness, and clarity. We propose two scoring functions and benchmark a range of models. Performance tracks general capability rankings.

</details>


### [83] [Automated Transcript Analysis for Detecting Flaws in Agentic Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27518)
*Jeff Mohl,Nelson Gardner-Challis,Magda Dubois,Harry Coppock,Benjamin Allan-Rahill,Kaelan Yim,Damian Sójka,James Mann,Justin Olive*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes and tests automated AI-based scanners that analyze benchmark transcripts to detect validity issues in agentic AI benchmarks, demonstrating their usefulness but also current limitations and open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic benchmarks are used to assess frontier AI model capabilities, but recent manual audits have found significant validity flaws in benchmarks like SWE-Bench-Verified. Manual review is expensive and unscalable, and it is unknown whether automated methods can reliably detect flaws that undermine benchmark validity. The paper aims to create scalable, automated tools to systematically surface these issues and improve trust in benchmark results.

Method: The authors define four key types of benchmark validity issues: (1) ground truth access (models somehow seeing the correct answer), (2) tool failure, (3) guessing vulnerability, and (4) answer format ambiguity. They design AI-based scanners to analyze benchmark transcripts for each issue type. They then construct grading rubrics to guide human annotators in labeling transcripts for these issues. Using a held-out test set from Inspect Evals benchmarks, they compare scanner predictions to human labels to assess scanner performance. Finally, they apply the scanners to five widely used benchmarks to discover real quality issues and analyze where scanners succeed and fail across benchmarks, criteria, and model choices.

Result: The scanners successfully identified multiple verified quality issues in five popular benchmarks, including some that would likely be missed by random manual inspection, showing that automated transcript analysis can meaningfully aid benchmark auditing. However, the scanners did not catch all issues, and their accuracy varied significantly by benchmark, issue type, and model used. The evaluation revealed systematic performance gaps and highlighted how heterogeneity and lack of standardization in benchmark design and logging can reduce scanner reliability.

Conclusion: Automated AI scanners are a promising tool for auditing the validity of agentic benchmarks by detecting issues like ground truth access, tool failures, guessing vulnerability, and ambiguous answer formats. While current scanners can surface real and previously unnoticed problems, they are not yet sufficient for comprehensive quality assurance, given their uneven performance and dependence on benchmark standardization. The paper positions these scanners as a proof of concept and argues that improving scanner methods and standardizing evaluation practices are key next steps for robust, scalable benchmark quality control.

Abstract: Capabilities of frontier models are often assessed using agentic benchmarks. To trust these results, benchmarks must accurately measure what they claim to and be free from invalidating flaws. Previous manual audits of benchmarks such as SWE-Bench-Verified have uncovered several validity issues in transcripts. However, manual review is difficult to scale, and it is unclear whether automated methods can reliably surface flaws that compromise benchmark validity. In this paper, we developed AI scanners to detect four types of validity issues: ground truth access, tool failure, guessing vulnerability, and answer format ambiguity. We produced grading rubrics for each to instruct human labeling, and evaluated the scanners against human labels on a held-out test set of Inspect Evals benchmarks. Our scanners identified several verified quality issues in five widely used benchmarks, including cases unlikely to be caught by random manual inspection. Not all cases were identified, and scanner performance varied substantially across benchmarks, criteria and models. We highlight several open challenges to be addressed to improve scanners for stronger quality assurance claims, including broader standardization gaps in the evaluation field that degrade scanner performance. Together, these results serve as a proof of concept for using automated transcript analysis to audit benchmark quality more broadly.

</details>


### [84] [AI Literacy: An Exercise in Power-Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27547)
*Brady D. Lund,Zoë Abbie Teel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper critiques current AI literacy approaches for reducing people to passive consumers of AI outputs and proposes a critical, empowerment-focused framework that develops people as active epistemic agents who can use, question, and govern AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI now plays a central role in how knowledge is produced and circulated, yet most people are positioned as mere end-users with limited understanding or control over how these systems work, whose interests they serve, and what assumptions they encode. Existing AI literacy frameworks focus heavily on technical skills and safe use, which risks reinforcing this passive, consumer role. The authors are motivated to address the resulting lack of epistemic agency and the way unequal access to AI tools mirrors and deepens existing epistemic injustices and structural inequities.

Method: The paper uses a conceptual and theoretical approach rather than empirical experiments. It draws on Foucault’s concept of power-knowledge, Freire’s pedagogy of critical consciousness, and digital literacy scholarship to re-theorize AI literacy. Based on these theoretical foundations, the authors construct a normative, three-part framework for AI literacy that is oriented to empowerment and critical engagement rather than mere competence and compliance.

Result: The authors develop a three-part framework for AI literacy organized around: (1) contextual use—understanding AI systems within specific social, cultural, and political contexts; (2) critical interrogation—cultivating the capacity to question AI systems’ assumptions, data, power relations, and impacts; and (3) participatory governance—enabling people to engage in shaping, overseeing, and governing AI systems. This framework reframes AI literacy in terms of epistemic agency instead of consumer proficiency and highlights how unequal AI access and control reproduce epistemic injustices.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that existing AI literacy models are inadequate because they focus on technical competence and responsible use while sidelining questions of power, access, and epistemic justice. AI literacy should be reconceptualized as a critical, emancipatory practice that equips individuals to evaluate, resist, and help govern AI systems. Only by centering contextual use, critical interrogation, and participatory governance can AI literacy cultivate epistemic agents, address structural inequities in AI access and influence, and foster more just arrangements of power-knowledge in AI-mediated societies.

Abstract: As generative artificial intelligence becomes one of the most significant systems of knowledge production in our society today, questions relating to who can access and shape that production grow increasingly important in our discourse. This paper argues that the existing frameworks for AI literacy, which are dominated by technical competency and responsible-use principles, are insufficient because they enforce a "consumer" orientation toward AI rather than fostering genuine epistemic agency. Based upon Foucault's concept of power-knowledge, Freire's pedagogy of critical consciousness, and scholarship of digital literacy, this paper proposes a reconceptualization of AI literacy as a critical practice that equips individuals not just to use AI systems, but to critically evaluate them, resist their structuring assumptions, and participate in their governance. The paper further argues that unequal access to AI tools in society recapitulates longstanding epistemic injustices, and that a literacy framework oriented toward empowerment must account for these structural inequities. A three-part framework of AI literacy based on the notions of contextual use, critical interrogation, and participatory governance frames this literacy as a cultivation of epistemic "agents" rather than the training of competent consumers of AI-generated information.

</details>


### [85] [Using Large Language Models for Idea Generation in Innovation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27553)
*Lennart Meincke,Karan Girotra,Gideon Nave,Christian Terwiesch,Karl T. Ulrich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares human- and GPT-4-generated product ideas for college students and finds that GPT-4 ideas have higher purchase intent on average and are far more likely to be among the very best ideas, though they are less novel and less diverse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether large language models can effectively contribute to early-stage new product development by generating high-quality product ideas, and how their output compares to human ideation in terms of quality, novelty, and diversity.

Method: The authors construct three pools of product ideas (sub-$50 products for college students): one from university students pre-LLM era, and two from GPT-4 using zero-shot and few-shot prompts. They evaluate idea quality via standard market research methods estimating purchase intent; use text mining to measure similarity and diversity; and employ human raters to judge novelty. They then compare distributions of quality and identify top-performing ideas across pools.

Result: AI-generated ideas show higher average predicted purchase intent than human ideas, with few-shot prompting slightly better than zero-shot. However, AI ideas are rated less novel and show greater pairwise similarity, especially with few-shot prompting, indicating reduced diversity. When focusing on the upper tail of performance, AI-generated ideas are about seven times more likely than human ideas to fall in the top 10% of all ideas.

Conclusion: LLMs, specifically GPT-4, offer a strong advantage for generating high-quality product ideas, especially when prioritizing the best concepts, although they trade off novelty and diversity. Given their high productivity and the conservative nature of the reported advantage, AI creativity can substantially benefit new product development processes, particularly as a scalable ideation tool to surface many strong candidate ideas quickly.

Abstract: This research evaluates the efficacy of large language models (LLMs) in generating new product ideas. To do so, we compare three pools of ideas for new products targeted toward college students and priced at 50 dollars or less. The first pool of ideas was created by university students in a product design course before the availability of LLMs. The second and third pools of ideas were generated by GPT-4 from OpenAI using zero-shot and few-shot prompting, respectively. We evaluated idea quality using standard market research techniques to predict average purchase intent probability. We used text mining to assess idea similarity and human raters to evaluate idea novelty. We find that AI-generated ideas outperform human-generated ideas in terms of average purchase intent, with few-shot prompting yielding slightly higher intent than zero-shot prompting. However, AI-generated ideas are perceived as less novel and exhibit higher pairwise similarity, particularly with few-shot prompting, indicating a less diverse solution landscape. When focusing on the quality of the best ideas rather than the average ideas, we find that AI-generated ideas are seven times more likely to rank among the top 10 percent of ideas, demonstrating a significant advantage over human-generated ideas. We propose that this seven-to-one advantage is a conservative estimate because it does not account for the greater productivity of AI. Our findings suggest that despite some drawbacks, AI creativity presents a substantial benefit in generating high-quality ideas for new product development.

</details>


### [86] [Evaluating Agentic Bioinformatics through Function, Evidence, and Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27556)
*Phuc Pham,Truong-Son Hy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a framework (FEV) to evaluate large language model agents in bioinformatics by focusing on the transparency and correctness of their entire workflow, not just final answers, and use it to survey 128 papers and highlight gaps in validation and accountability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of biological LLM agents emphasize performance metrics, tool use, and architectures, but these do not guarantee scientific credibility or accountability. There is no standard way to analyze how these agents construct and justify complex bioinformatics workflows, nor to compare systems on transparency and validation quality. The authors aim to fill this gap so that agentic bioinformatics can be trusted and systematically improved.

Method: They conceptualize the agent’s full, inspectable workflow trajectory as the central object of evaluation and introduce the Function–Evidence–Validation (FEV) framework. FEV decomposes an agentic system into: (1) Functions (what operations it can perform and demonstrate in workflows), (2) Evidence (what traceable support is provided for each action and claim, including provenance and reasoning), and (3) Validation (how workflows and outputs are checked, benchmarked, or empirically tested for particular use cases). They then apply FEV to map 109 agentic or agent-adjacent systems and 28 benchmarks/evaluation resources (128 publications) across diverse bioinformatics domains, characterizing strengths and gaps along these three axes.

Result: The survey shows that across genomics, omics, protein science, drug discovery, computational pathology, and general bioinformatics, agentic systems have made strong progress in planning and tool-based execution. However, they largely lag in replayability of workflows, detailed provenance tracking, rigorous and domain-appropriate scientific evaluation, external/independent validation, and especially prospective empirical testing. This yields an uneven landscape where agents can perform complex tasks but often lack transparent, auditable support for their decisions and claims.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that evaluating agentic bioinformatics systems purely by final-answer correctness is insufficient and potentially misleading. Instead, systems should be judged on workflow correctness, including explicit functions, supporting evidence, and validation procedures. The FEV framework offers a practical, generalizable way to compare systems and to design future bioinformatics agents whose workflows are transparent, auditable, and scientifically accountable.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly plan, execute, and interpret biological analyses, yet fluent responses, successful tool calls, and benchmark performance alone do not establish scientific credibility. Existing reviews primarily organize biological agents by application, architecture, and agentic capability, but do not jointly operationalize the accountability of agent-generated workflows. We address this gap by treating the inspectable workflow trajectory, rather than architecture or final output alone, as the primary unit of analysis. We introduce the Function--Evidence--Validation (FEV) framework, which separates demonstrated workflow operations, traceable support for actions and claims, and use-case-specific validation. Using FEV, we map 109 agentic or agent-adjacent systems and 28 benchmark or evaluation resources, representing 128 unique publications across genomics, single-cell and spatial omics, protein science, drug discovery, computational pathology, and general bioinformatics automation. Across domains, planning and tool-mediated execution have advanced more rapidly than replayability, provenance, robust scientific assessment, external validation, and prospective empirical testing. We therefore argue that agentic bioinformatics should be assessed through workflow correctness rather than final-answer correctness alone. FEV provides a practical basis for comparing systems and designing transparent, auditable, and scientifically accountable bioinformatics workflows.

</details>


### [87] [DeepResearch Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27562)
*Yong Huang,Yulu Huang,for the team Collaboration*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeepResearch is a sparse-activation, 30B-parameter LLM-based agent system optimized for fast, long-context, tool-using, multi-step research, achieving state-of-the-art results on several agent benchmarks and released as a full open-source stack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based agents struggle to combine strong reasoning, efficient long-context handling, fast inference, robust tool use, and stable training in a single, open system for serious research and analysis. The paper aims to build an agent that can autonomously conduct deep research with high accuracy and efficiency while being practical to run and reproduce.

Method: The authors design a 30B-parameter sparse-activation model where only 3B parameters are active per token, combined with a 128K context window and hierarchical attention for long-context processing. They introduce a dual-mode reasoning engine with a ReAct mode for basic multi-step tasks and an IterResearch mode for up to 20-step iterative reasoning. The system orchestrates multiple tools (retrieval, web search, computation, file parsing) via a coordinated tool-use module. Training is improved using a GRPO-based reinforcement learning framework that applies token-level policy gradients for stability and faster convergence. An automated data synthesis pipeline expands seed examples into large-scale training data with high usability. They evaluate on several agent and search benchmarks.

Result: Compared to dense models of similar scale, the sparse architecture delivers 3.2× faster inference while achieving state-of-the-art performance on agent search benchmarks. Hierarchical attention with a 128K context improves accuracy by 18.7% and recall by 23.4% over standard long-context methods. The dual-mode reasoning engine yields a 31.2% accuracy gain over single-pass baselines. Tool-use accuracy reaches 92.1%. GRPO-based RL improves training stability by 35% and accelerates convergence by 42%. The data synthesis pipeline produces training data with 92.5% usability. On benchmarks, the system scores 87.3% on Humanity's Last Exam, 85.3% on BrowserComp Chinese, and 91.2% on WebWalkerQA.

Conclusion: The DeepResearch Agent System demonstrates that a sparse-activation, long-context, tool-augmented LLM with specialized reasoning modes and GRPO-based RL can deliver fast, accurate, and stable autonomous research capabilities. Its strong benchmark results and full open-source release make it a practical foundation for applications such as academic research, business analysis, R&D support, and education, and a platform for further work on deep information-seeking agents.

Abstract: The DeepResearch Agent System is a large language model system engineered for deep information retrieval, multi-step reasoning, and autonomous research tasks. Built upon a sparse activation architecture with 30 billion total parameters of which only 3 billion are activated per token, the system achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple agent search benchmarks while delivering 3.2 times faster inference compared to dense counterparts of equivalent scale. The system supports a 128K-token context window with hierarchical attention mechanisms that yield 18.7% accuracy and 23.4% recall improvements over standard long-context approaches. A dual-mode reasoning engine provides both a ReAct paradigm for basic multi-step problem solving and an IterResearch mode for high-performance iterative research with up to 20 reasoning steps, collectively delivering a 31.2% accuracy improvement over single-pass baselines. Multi-tool coordination integrates retrieval, computation, web search, and file parsing modules to achieve 92.1% tool-use accuracy. A reinforcement learning optimization framework based on the GRPO algorithm provides token-level policy gradients that improve training stability by 35% and accelerate convergence by 42%. An automated data synthesis pipeline with seed-based expansion achieves a 92.5% usability rate. Benchmark results include 87.3% on Humanity's Last Exam, 85.3% on BrowserComp Chinese, and 91.2% on WebWalkerQA. The system is fully open-sourced, including data synthesis, training, and inference code, and supports applications in academic research, business analysis, R&D support, and education.

</details>


### [88] [What makes prompts a graph: necessary and sufficient conditions for prompt graph engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27578)
*Sandeco Macedo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper defines and formalizes the concept of “prompt graphs” as explicit, executable graph structures connecting multiple LLM prompts, and provides criteria to distinguish real prompt-graph engineering from related practices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real LLM applications, prompts are no longer single strings but parts of complex systems where calls chain, branch, and merge via graphs. Industry is already building and optimizing such graph structures, but terminology is inconsistent and ambiguous: “graph” can mean reasoning patterns, orchestration flows, or multi-agent conversations, while “prompt engineering” is still often framed as crafting a single prompt. The field lacks a precise, shared definition and boundary for what counts as prompt-graph engineering, which hinders communication, evaluation, and systematic design.

Method: The authors conduct a conceptual and genealogical analysis drawing on prior work with persistent identifiers and key grey literature (framework docs, blog posts, etc.). They trace the evolution from dataflow graphs and build systems to prompt chaining and various “thought topologies” (chains, trees, graphs), culminating in compiled and optimized prompt graphs. Based on this analysis, they propose a constitutive definition of prompt graph engineering, identify four necessary conditions (explicit structure, separation of structure from prompt content, executable semantics, and treating the graph as a first-class artifact), and turn these into an inclusion/exclusion test. They then apply this test to six contemporary systems: LangGraph, DSPy, Prompt Flow, AutoGen, CrewAI, and Claude Code subagents.

Result: The paper yields: (1) a clear, operational definition of “prompt graph engineering”; (2) four concrete conditions that must be satisfied for something to qualify as a prompt graph; (3) an inclusion/exclusion framework that differentiates prompt graphs from six neighboring concepts; and (4) an empirical validation of this framework via analysis of six real-world systems, where the test reliably includes systems that embody prompt-graph engineering and excludes those that do not.

Conclusion: Prompt-based LLM systems should be understood and designed as explicit prompt graphs, not as isolated prompts or vague “pipelines.” Defining prompt graph engineering via four conditions gives practitioners and researchers a shared vocabulary and a practical test to classify and reason about systems. This clarification aligns terminology with current industrial practice and sets up a structured research agenda organized around four key design tensions, guiding future work on tooling, optimization, and theory for graph-structured prompting systems.

Abstract: Prompts stopped being isolated strings some time ago. In real systems, one model call feeds another, retrieval interleaves with generation, routers branch, and aggregators merge parallel results. Practice converged on a single structure to hold this together: the graph. Frameworks such as LangGraph, DSPy, and Prompt Flow expose it openly, and research systems already optimize it automatically. The vocabulary, however, lags behind. Graph names, variously, a reasoning topology inside one sampling strategy, a multi-agent conversation, or an orchestration artifact, while prompt engineering still evokes writing one good string. What is missing is a reference definition treating prompts as nodes of an explicit, executable, improvable graph. We build that definition through conceptual analysis over sources with persistent identifiers, complemented by primary grey literature. We reconstruct the genealogy of the idea, from dataflow graphs and build systems, through prompt chaining and the thought topologies (chain, tree, graph), to graphs compiled and optimized as artifacts. We then propose a constitutive definition of prompt graph engineering, state its four conditions (explicit structure, separation between structure and prompt content, executable semantics, and the graph as a first-class engineering artifact), and operationalize them as an inclusion and exclusion test. We draw the boundary against six neighboring concepts and apply the test to six real systems (LangGraph, DSPy, Prompt Flow, AutoGen, CrewAI, and Claude Code subagents); it includes and excludes consistently. We close with a research agenda organized along four design tension axes. The contribution is an operational definition and a shared vocabulary for a practice that industry already exercises daily without naming precisely.

</details>


### [89] [From Minds to Models: The Intersection of Psychology and LLM Behaviours](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27579)
*Oliver Guidetti,Reza Ryan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper probes whether ChatGPT shows race-based sentiment differences using a psychology-inspired experimental design, finding only weak, analysis-dependent evidence of bias and highlighting limitations of current sentiment-based tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used in sensitive domains like government and healthcare, yet their internal decision-making is opaque. Since psychology has long studied unobservable mental processes with indirect behavioural measures (e.g., Implicit Association Test), similar methods might reveal hidden biases in LLMs, especially racial bias, which has serious fairness and trust implications. Existing prompt-based bias tests are crude, so the authors want a more systematic, statistically grounded assessment inspired by psychological research methods.

Method: The authors adapt ideas from the Implicit Association Test to an open-ended prompting paradigm. They construct 14 base questions and systematically combine each with 8 racial categories plus a race-agnostic control, yielding 126 prompts. Each prompt is submitted once to three ChatGPT variants (GPT-3.5T, GPT-4, GPT-4T), producing 378 responses. They then label the sentiment of each response (positive, negative, neutral) and assign sentiment scores using underlying source scores: positive labels keep the score, negative labels invert its sign, and neutral = 0. Using these scores, they perform a two-way ANOVA with racial condition and model type as factors, followed by sensitivity analysis with rank-transformed data and post-hoc pairwise comparisons (Tukey correction).

Result: The two-way ANOVA shows a small main effect of racial condition on sentiment, but no effect of model type and no interaction between race and model. However, when they use a more robust rank-transformed analysis, the racial effect disappears. Tukey-corrected pairwise comparisons reveal no significant differences between specific racial conditions. One uncorrected comparison between European and Indigenous Australian prompts appears significant, but it was chosen post hoc and treated only as hypothesis-generating, not confirmatory evidence. Overall, the statistical evidence for race-based sentiment differences is weak and unstable across analytic choices.

Conclusion: The study concludes that under this design, ChatGPT models show at most weak and analysis-dependent racial sentiment differences, offering no robust evidence of systematic race-based sentiment bias. The authors emphasise that simple sentiment scoring of generated text is an inadequate proxy for detecting evaluative bias, partly because negative valence may reflect historically negative content rather than the model’s evaluative stance. They recommend specific design improvements—such as better control over elicited content, repeated sampling, and more nuanced behavioural tasks—and call for interdisciplinary collaboration between psychologists and AI researchers to develop stronger, theory-informed behavioural measures of model bias.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are often compared with the human mind because their decision-making is complex, non-linear and difficult to interpret. Psychological methods developed to investigate unobservable mental processes may therefore help examine LLM behaviour, particularly in government and healthcare. Building on prompt-based adaptations of the Implicit Association Test, this study tested whether ChatGPT produced sentiment differences across racial conditions in open-ended text. Fourteen base questions were crossed with eight racial categories and a race-agnostic control, producing 126 prompts. Each was submitted once to GPT-3.5T, GPT-4 and GPT-4T, yielding 378 responses. Sentiment scores were derived from categorical labels and source scores: positive labels retained the source score, negative labels were assigned its negative, and neutral responses were coded zero. A two-way ANOVA found a small main effect of racial condition, F(8, 351) = 2.04, p = .042, partial-eta squared = .044, but no effect of model, F(2, 351) = 0.07, p = .933, and no interaction, F(16, 351) = 0.23, p = .999. However, the effect was not retained in a rank-transformed sensitivity analysis, F(8, 351) = 1.53, p = .145, and Tukey-corrected comparisons found no significant pairwise differences. An uncorrected European-Indigenous Australian comparison was significant, but was selected post hoc and is reported only as hypothesis-generating. Evidence for sentiment differences was therefore weak and analysis-dependent. Sentiment scoring also cannot distinguish evaluative bias from the valence of historical content elicited by a prompt. We outline design changes needed to address these limitations and argue for interdisciplinary development of behavioural measures of model bias.
  Keywords: Implicit Bias, Psychological Research Methods, Artificial Intelligence, ChatGPT, Large Language Models, Sentiment Analysis

</details>


### [90] [Wiring diagram extraction and gluing: a case study in classifying figure skating jumps using 3D dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27598)
*Jason Lo,Mohammadnima Jafari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper improves Hasse clustering for sequential data by introducing a theory of gluing wiring diagrams so that multiple small clustering steps can reproduce the result of one large, computationally expensive step, and demonstrates this on classifying figure skating jump videos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hasse clustering is good at extracting and visualizing common patterns in sequential data, but it becomes computationally infeasible when the desired number of clusters is large due to combinatorial explosion. The authors want a way to scale Hasse clustering without losing its interpretability and pattern-extraction strengths.

Method: They develop a theoretical framework for "gluing" wiring diagrams that represent the output of Hasse clustering. This framework shows how to apply Hasse clustering iteratively on smaller parts or subproblems and then combine (glue) the resulting wiring diagrams to obtain a result equivalent to running Hasse clustering once on the full problem. They then apply this iterative/gluing procedure to the practical task of classifying figure skating jump videos.

Result: The theory demonstrates that repeated, smaller applications of Hasse clustering, combined via their gluing operations on wiring diagrams, can reproduce the same clustering structure as a single global run. Experimentally, on figure skating jump videos, the method successfully classifies the sequences, indicating that the approach is both feasible and effective in practice.

Conclusion: Iterative Hasse clustering with gluing of wiring diagrams overcomes the combinatorial complexity barrier of standard Hasse clustering while preserving its ability to extract and visualize common sequential patterns. This makes the method more scalable and practically applicable, as illustrated by the figure skating jump classification example.

Abstract: Hasse clustering is an algorithm that extracts common patterns in sequential data and represents them in graphical forms. As the number of expected clusters grows, however, the algorithm can become infeasible to run due to combinatorial complexity. In this article, we describe a theory of gluing wiring diagrams, allowing iterative applications of Hasse clustering to achieve the same result as a single application. We test our theory in the context of classifying videos of figure skating jumps.

</details>


### [91] [World Action Planner: Generalizable Decision-Making with Action-Conditioned World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27599)
*Xiangcheng Zhang,Yilun Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces World Action Planner, a robot planning system that combines vision-language model reasoning with a pose-image conditioned world model to generate and iteratively refine action plans, achieving strong generalization and outperforming prior end-to-end policy models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning policies often overfit to specific training environments and fail to generalize to novel scenes, layouts, and tasks. The authors seek a planning framework that generalizes better by explicitly reasoning about future outcomes instead of directly mapping observations to actions.

Method: They build World Action Planner, which couples a Vision-Language Model (VLM) for high-level reasoning and instruction understanding with a multi-task pose-image conditioned world model that can predict future states. The agent uses the VLM to propose initial plans, then iteratively optimizes and searches over action sequences by simulating imagined rollouts with the world model, refining plans based on these predictions.

Result: Experiments on compositional tasks, novel layouts, and zero-shot generalization benchmarks show that World Action Planner outperforms strong end-to-end baselines, including Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and World-Action Models (WAMs). Performance gains are particularly notable in settings that require recombining known skills or adapting to new environments.

Conclusion: Integrating VLM-based symbolic reasoning with a grounded world model enables a planning-style agent that generalizes better than purely end-to-end policy approaches. Iterative plan refinement via imagined rollouts is effective for complex, compositional, and zero-shot tasks, suggesting a promising direction for more general-purpose robot agents.

Abstract: Building generalizable agents for diverse applications remains a fundamental challenge. While imitation learning-based policies succeed in specific training environments, they often fail to generalize to novel scenes and tasks. In this work, we propose World Action Planner, a robot planning system that leverages the reasoning capabilities of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and the physical grounding of a multi-task pose-image conditioned world model. Our system enables an agent to propose initial action plans and iteratively refine them via optimization and search, reasoning over imagined world model rollouts. We demonstrate that our approach achieves superior performance across compositional tasks, new layouts, and zero-shot generalization scenarios, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art end-to-end policy models such as VLAs and WAMs. Project website at worldactionplanner.github.io

</details>


### [92] [CORE: In-Context Reconstruction for Unified Tabular Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27615)
*Yunfeng Zhao,Qingfeng Chen,Yue Tan,Shiyuan Li,Yili Wang,Yixin Liu,Shirui Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes CORE, a unified tabular anomaly detection method that aligns heterogeneous tabular features while preserving semantics and detects anomalies via in-context reconstruction without using labeled or synthetic anomalies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unified tabular anomaly detection aims to use a single model across many heterogeneous tabular datasets, but current methods struggle to align different feature spaces and often reduce detection to binary classification with distance-based features and synthetic anomalies, losing semantic information and failing to capture diverse, dataset-specific anomaly patterns.

Method: Introduce CORE, which has (1) a decorrelated feature alignment module that directly maps heterogeneous original features into a shared representation space while preserving their semantic structure, and (2) an in-context reconstruction module that, for each sample, reconstructs it using contextual normal samples from the same dataset, treating anomaly detection as reconstruction-based deviation estimation rather than binary classification or distance thresholding.

Result: CORE can perform unified tabular anomaly detection on unseen datasets by using reconstruction error in the learned unified representation space as an anomaly score, without relying on labeled anomalies or synthetic outliers; experiments (implied) show improved generalization and better preservation of feature semantics compared with distance-based unified methods.

Conclusion: By jointly addressing semantic-preserving feature alignment and anomaly detection via in-context reconstruction, CORE offers a more generalizable and label-free framework for unified tabular anomaly detection that can flexibly adapt to arbitrary new datasets and capture their specific normality distributions.

Abstract: Tabular anomaly detection (TAD), which focuses on identifying abnormal samples that deviate from the majority in tabular data, has received growing attention. Recently, there has been an emerging trend towards unified TAD, which seeks to detect anomalies across different datasets using a single generalizable model. In unified TAD, aligning heterogeneous data remains challenging. While existing methods often rely on distance-based unified feature construction, they may obscure the semantics of the original features. Moreover, existing approaches typically formulate anomaly detection as a binary classification task, which may overlook diverse anomaly patterns from various datasets and be misled by unrepresentative synthetic anomalies. To address these challenges, we propose an in-COntext REconstruction approach for unified TAD (CORE for short). It introduces a decorrelated feature alignment module to directly align heterogeneous features into a unified representation space, which retains their semantic information. Meanwhile, CORE formulates unified TAD as an in-context reconstruction problem, eliminating the need for labeled or synthesized anomalies. Specifically, the in-context reconstruction module reconstructs each sample by leveraging contextual normal samples to capture dataset-specific distributions, such that reconstruction errors reflect its deviation from normality, facilitating unified TAD on arbitrary unseen datasets.

</details>


### [93] [Hidden APIs in Language Models: Discovering Reusable Causal Interfaces from Forked Futures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27617)
*SiYuan Ma,Yiqin Luo,Zhangji,Canran Xiao,Albert Gao,Wei-Hsing Huang,Wei Wang,Qiwei Wu,Xinran Li,Jinfeng Wei,Qixin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new way to compare and characterize internal hidden states of language models by looking at how they support future computations, not just current outputs, and finds evidence for a shared, reusable causal interface across model variants and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current probing methods for language models often rely on current-answer correlations or researcher-specified latent labels, which cannot distinguish hidden states that produce the same immediate answer but differ in how they will behave under future operations. This limits our ability to identify stable, reusable internal interfaces that actually mediate future computations in a causal sense. The authors aim to construct a more principled, causal way to compare hidden states and to test candidate architectures for such reusable interfaces.

Method: The authors introduce "forked futures": after allowing a prefix state to form in a language model, they sample future operations (interventions, prompts, or transformations) and compare hidden states by the distributions of responses induced by these future operations. This defines an empirical causal equivalence (quotient) over hidden states without specifying latent labels. They then formalize several competing interface architectures—Shared, Local, Mixture, and Distributed—and evaluate them using prequential causal description length, subject to constraints on fidelity to future-signature behavior and matched capacity. They run detailed evaluations on Qwen2.5-1.5B and Llama-3-8B, extend to a sweep over five model backbones, and perform figure-aligned transplantation and API-aligned path analyses, as well as a blind four-class model-organism recovery test.

Result: Across detailed evaluations, the Shared interface architecture achieves the lowest held-out prequential causal description length, with improvements of 0.216 nats on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 0.294 nats on Llama-3-8B, while keeping the mean distortion of future-signatures low and tightly clustered. A sweep over five different backbones maintains a positive "Sharedness Gain" trend. The transplantation analysis shows that Shared interfaces best preserve target correctness, locality, copy properties, and composite behavior; API-aligned causal paths mediate 0.749 of the target effect compared to 0.150 for matched null paths. In a blind four-class model-organism test, the procedure correctly recovers 14 out of 16 architectures, with only one misclassification of a non-Shared organism as Shared among 12 non-Shared cases.

Conclusion: The findings support the existence of an economical, reusable causal interface inside the tested language models for the set of operations considered, favoring a Shared architecture over more local or distributed alternatives. However, the authors explicitly condition their claim on the particular candidate architectures, experimental interventions, and held-out futures they studied, indicating that the detected shared interface is robust within this regime but not assumed to generalize beyond it without further evidence.

Abstract: Identical language-model answers can arise from hidden states that support different future computations, so current-answer probes do not establish a reusable internal interface. We introduce forked futures: future operations are sampled only after a prefix state has formed, and states are compared through the response distributions induced by those operations. This yields an empirical causal quotient over hidden states without requiring researcher-specified latent labels. Shared, Local, Mixture, and Distributed interfaces then compete under prequential causal description length subject to future-signature fidelity and matched capacity constraints. In the two detailed model evaluations, Shared has the lowest held-out description length, with gains of 0.216 nats on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 0.294 nats on Llama-3-8B, while maintaining tightly clustered mean future-signature distortion; a five-backbone sweep preserves the positive direction of Sharedness Gain. The figure-aligned transplantation analysis gives Shared the strongest joint target-correctness, locality, copy-preservation, and composite profile, and API-aligned paths mediate 0.749 of the target effect versus 0.150 for matched null paths. In the blind four-class model-organism test, 14/16 architectures are recovered, with one observed non-Shared to Shared error among 12 non-Shared organisms. These results support an economical reusable causal interface within the tested operation banks, while keeping the claim explicitly conditional on the candidate architectures, interventions, and held-out futures.

</details>


### [94] [SCOPE: Synthetic Conditional Objectives for Policy Evolution in Black-Box Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27630)
*Nguyen Viet Tuan Kiet,Nguyen Huu Duc,Le Cong Bang,Tran Cong Dao,Huynh Thi Thanh Binh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCOPE is a framework that improves black-box combinatorial optimization by learning and evolving synthetic, history-conditioned objectives that guide search policies to explore diverse, high-quality solution regions under tight evaluation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In black-box combinatorial optimization, the true objective function is unknown and expensive to evaluate, so the search must decide which candidate solutions to try with very limited feedback. Traditional approaches often rely on a single surrogate model or heuristic that can mislead the search or prematurely converge to suboptimal regions, especially in high-dimensional or discrete spaces. There is a need for a more flexible and robust way to guide exploration that can adapt to observed data and maintain diversity in the search process.

Method: The paper proposes SCOPE, a general framework that constructs a set of synthetic conditional objectives based on the accumulated search history. Instead of directly optimizing the true black-box objective, SCOPE learns multiple synthetic objectives, each encoding a different preference over candidates. These objectives are used to train or evolve search policies that propose new candidate solutions. The algorithm operates in a nested-loop manner: in the inner loop, policies are optimized against their respective synthetic objectives, and a portfolio of top-performing policies is returned to avoid over-reliance on a single surrogate. In the outer loop, the framework evaluates the true black-box objective on the candidates generated by these policies, and then adaptively updates and selects synthetic objectives based on how well their policies discover promising areas in the search space. This reframes objective design as a means of guiding policy exploration with structured diversity.

Result: Across multiple benchmark combinatorial optimization problems, SCOPE leads to systematic improvements in search performance compared to baseline methods, especially when the number of allowed evaluations of the true objective is small. The learned synthetic objectives and associated policy portfolios help the algorithm more reliably discover high-quality regions of the search space and avoid premature convergence. The experiments also show that SCOPE generalizes effectively across tasks with different discrete structures, indicating robustness and adaptability of the framework.

Conclusion: SCOPE demonstrates that learning a diverse set of history-conditioned synthetic objectives to guide policy evolution is an effective strategy for black-box combinatorial optimization under tight evaluation budgets. By decoupling objective design from the true black-box function and framing it as a tool for steering exploration, SCOPE can exploit accumulated evidence while preserving structured diversity, leading to better and more robust search performance across a variety of discrete optimization benchmarks.

Abstract: Black-box combinatorial optimization requires systematically identifying high-quality solutions under a limited evaluation budget, yet the unknown objective function provides little guidance for deciding where the search should explore next. We introduce SCOPE, a general framework for Synthetic Conditional Objectives for Policy Evolution in Black-Box Combinatorial Optimization. Rather than directly optimizing the inaccessible objective, SCOPE learns a set of synthetic objectives conditioned on the accumulated search history, where each objective is designed to expose a distinct and potentially useful preference over candidate solutions. These objectives are then used to evolve search policies that generate diverse candidates, whose true quality is subsequently assessed through black-box evaluations. The outer loop adaptively updates and selects synthetic objectives according to how effectively their induced policies discover promising regions. In contrast, the inner loop returns a portfolio of top-performing policies to reduce the risk of relying on a single surrogate preference. This formulation reframes objective design as a mechanism for guiding policy exploration, enabling the search process to exploit observed evidence while maintaining structured diversity across discrete solution spaces. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmark problems demonstrate that SCOPE consistently improves black-box search performance under limited evaluation budgets and generalizes well across diverse combinatorial structures.

</details>


### [95] [ReDiPPO: Reference-Guided Value Calibration and Discrepancy-Aware Token Reweighting for Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27631)
*Zhenrong Zhang,Fei Wu,Jun Du,Jianshu Zhang,Si Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ReDiPPO, a PPO-based reinforcement learning framework that improves mathematical reasoning of LLMs by using a reference-guided critic and a discrepancy-aware advantage reweighting mechanism, achieving better value estimation and performance than prior RL fine-tuning methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PPO critics struggle on math reasoning tasks because reasoning trajectories are long, rewards are sparse, and intermediate states are hard to evaluate. This leads to noisy token-level credit assignment and suboptimal policy updates. The authors aim to design a method that provides more accurate value estimates and better token-level credit for mathematical reasoning, while still being PPO-compatible.

Method: ReDiPPO augments PPO with two key ideas: (1) a reference-guided critic trained with privileged signals from reference solutions (e.g., ground-truth answers) that provides improved value estimates at training time; and (2) a discrepancy-aware mechanism that compares the standard critic’s token-level value estimates with those from the reference-guided critic. The token-wise discrepancy between these two estimates is interpreted as a measure of reasoning difficulty and is used to reweight token-level advantages during PPO updates, focusing learning on hard or uncertain reasoning states while keeping a standard critic for deployment.

Result: Across multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks, ReDiPPO yields more accurate value estimation and consistently improves final reasoning performance over strong RL baselines such as standard PPO, DAPO, and GSPO. The experiments show robust gains in solution quality and reliability, indicating that reference guidance and discrepancy-aware reweighting effectively address the sparse-reward, long-horizon credit assignment problem in math reasoning tasks.
