Conclusion: Leveraging reference solutions as privileged training signals in a reference-guided critic, together with a discrepancy-based advantage reweighting scheme, significantly enhances PPO’s token-level credit assignment for mathematical reasoning. ReDiPPO thus provides a more effective RL framework for improving LLM reasoning, outperforming existing PPO-based methods on diverse math benchmarks and demonstrating the value of discrepancy-aware, reference-informed value learning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has emerged as an effective paradigm for enhancing the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models. Among existing policy optimization methods, Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) remains particularly appealing because its learned critic can, in principle, provide token-level credit assignment. However, in mathematical reasoning tasks characterized by long reasoning horizons and sparse outcome rewards, reliable token-level credit assignment remains challenging. The standard critic often fails to accurately evaluate intermediate reasoning states, resulting in noisy advantage estimates and suboptimal policy updates. In this paper, we propose ReDiPPO, a Reference-guided and Discrepancy-aware PPO framework for mathematical reasoning. ReDiPPO introduces a reference-guided critic that uses reference answers as training-time privileged signals to provide more accurate value estimation. Meanwhile, it retains a standard critic and quantifies the token-level reference-standard discrepancy between the standard value estimate and the reference-guided value estimate. This discrepancy serves as an indicator of difficult reasoning states and is used to reweight the corresponding token-level advantages during PPO optimization. Extensive experiments on diverse mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ReDiPPO improves value-estimation accuracy and consistently outperforms strong policy optimization baselines, including PPO, DAPO, and GSPO, in final reasoning performance. Our code is available on https://github.com/cii030/ReDiPPO.

</details>


### [96] [HealthCAT: An Interpretable Encoder-only Transformer Framework for Health Indicator Prediction and Temporal Interpretation of Wearable Sensor Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27635)
*Xiaotong Yu,Joshua Y. Kim,HaeJin Lee,Kalina Yacef*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HealthCAT is a Transformer-based framework for wearable sensor time-series that improves prediction of health indicators while providing time-step-level, class-specific interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearable sensors generate rich multivariate time-series that can capture behaviour linked to health, but most deep learning models for such data focus on accuracy and offer poor interpretability, limiting their usefulness in health research and clinical decision-making.

Method: The authors propose HealthCAT, which combines an encoder-only Transformer with a specialized Attentive Class Activation Token (AttentiveCAT) that produces class-specific attention scores at each time step; these scores can be aligned with domain-relevant behavioural cycles (e.g., time-of-day). They evaluate HealthCAT on two real-world wearable datasets from 306 participants, benchmarking predictive performance and using masking experiments to test whether the identified important time steps truly carry predictive information.

Result: HealthCAT outperforms deep learning baselines by up to 17% in F1-score and 12% in accuracy on both datasets with statistically significant improvements (p<0.05). In masking tests, time steps flagged as important by HealthCAT retain significantly more predictive value than randomly selected time steps across all masking conditions (p<0.05), validating the informativeness of its interpretations.

Conclusion: HealthCAT simultaneously achieves strong predictive performance and validated time-step-level interpretability for wearable sensor time series. By mapping class-specific importance back to behavioural cycles, it enables detailed analysis of when and how physical activity patterns relate to health indicators, moving beyond coarse aggregated metrics and supporting more precise health monitoring, behavioural analysis, and intervention design.

Abstract: Wearable sensors continuously capture fine-grained multivariate time-series data, providing opportunities to model behavioural patterns associated with health outcomes. However, existing deep learning methods prioritise predictive accuracy over interpretability, limiting their application in health research. In this study, we present HealthCAT, a flexible framework that integrates an Encoder-only Transformer with an Attentive Class Activation Token (AttentiveCAT) to generate class-specific, time-step-level interpretations. These interpretations can be mapped back onto behavioural cycles that are relevant to the domain (e.g., time-of-day), supporting individual-level analysis of wearable sensor data. We evaluated HealthCAT using two real-world wearable sensor datasets (306 participants in total). HealthCAT outperformed deep learning baselines by up to 17\% in F1-score and 12\% in accuracy on both datasets ($p<0.05$). In masking experiments, the time steps identified by HealthCAT carried significantly more predictive value than random selection across all masking conditions ($p<0.05$), indicating that the identified time steps are predictively informative. By coupling predictive performance with validated time-step-level interpretability, HealthCAT moves wearable sensor analysis beyond aggregated metrics towards temporal patterns that support health monitoring, behavioural pattern analysis, and intervention design in health research. The significance of this work is that it enables accurate prediction of health indicators from wearable sensor data while providing insights into when and how physical activity patterns occur, rather than relying solely on aggregated summary measures.

</details>


### [97] [HALO: Heterogeneous Admission through Localized Obligations for Safe Agentic Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27636)
*Taewoo Park,Kyeonghyun Yoo,Kiseok Kim,Seunghyun Yoo,Hwangnam Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HALO is a runtime protocol for agentic AI systems that selectively preserves and rechecks supported components of heterogeneous responses based on their prerequisites, improving safety and usability versus all-or-nothing admission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems increasingly return complex, heterogeneous responses that mix notices, requests, handoffs, and concrete actions. Current runtime policies typically either accept or reject the entire response. This is problematic because environmental conditions can change after generation, making some parts of the response stale or unsafe while others remain valid. Whole-response rejection wastes still-useful parts, whereas independent per-component checks may allow execution of components that actually depend on now-unsupported prerequisites. There is a need for a principled, automated way to admit only those components that remain both individually supported and dependency-consistent at runtime.

Method: The authors introduce HALO (Heterogeneous Admission with Localized Obligations), a runtime protocol for structured, multi-component agent responses. HALO requires each component to declare its prerequisites and obligations. At admission time, it identifies which components are still supported, then retains only those whose prerequisites are also supported, ensuring dependency consistency. Before any concrete action is dispatched to the external environment, HALO rechecks that exact action against current conditions. If an action is blocked (e.g., becomes stale or unsafe), it can be replaced only by a freshly generated candidate that is revalidated under the same rules. The protocol is evaluated through admission-expectation checks, protocol tests, structured-response replay experiments, and cold-start PX4/Gazebo simulation sessions.

Result: HALO satisfied all 96 predefined admission expectations and passed all 20 protocol tests, indicating that it behaved as intended under a variety of designed scenarios. In structured-response replay experiments, HALO retained all 248 components that were still supported, including all 128 components that were unaffected by unrelated changes, whereas a naive whole-response policy retained none of them once any part became unsupported. In ten cold-start PX4/Gazebo simulation sessions, HALO blocked every tested stale route, did not observe any stale setpoint being matched or executed, and still completed all fresh recovery actions, demonstrating both safety against stale actions and preservation of needed behavior.

Conclusion: HALO provides a robust runtime mechanism for selectively admitting and executing parts of heterogeneous agent responses while respecting dependencies and adapting to changing conditions. By preserving only components whose prerequisites remain supported and by rechecking actions immediately before dispatch, HALO avoids both the over-conservatism of whole-response rejection and the inconsistency risks of naive per-component checks. Empirical results across synthetic tests and PX4/Gazebo simulations suggest that HALO can reliably block stale behaviors while retaining and enabling fresh, valid actions, improving both safety and effectiveness of agentic AI systems in dynamic environments.

Abstract: Recent agentic AI systems may return a heterogeneous response containing notices, requests, handoffs, and actions. Conditions can change before external use, so components from the same response need not remain supported together. Rejecting the whole response discards useful components, whereas checking components independently can leave a dependent without its prerequisite. We present Heterogeneous Admission with Localized Obligations (HALO), a runtime protocol that preserves supported components whose declared prerequisites also remain supported, rechecks each exact action before dispatch, and allows blocked actions to be replaced only by fresh candidates. HALO matched all 96 admission expectations and passed all 20 protocol tests. In structured-response replay, it retained 248/248 supported components, including 128/128 unaffected by unrelated changes, while a whole-response policy retained 0/248. Across ten cold-start PX4/Gazebo sessions, HALO blocked every tested stale route, observed no matching stale setpoint, and completed all fresh recoveries.

</details>


### [98] [Evaluating and Pricing Advertisements in AI-Generated Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27686)
*John L. Turner-Smith,Zimeng Huang,Yuhan Fu,Yihang Zhang,Tonghan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds a simulation-based evaluator to predict users' click-through intent on LLM-embedded ads, then uses this continuous intent signal to design truthful pricing mechanisms for ad allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As search shifts to LLM answer engines, ads are woven directly into generated responses, so their value depends on both usefulness and likelihood of user clicks. Traditional ways to estimate click-through intent (behavioural logs, human labels, or frontier LLM judges) are inadequate: logs don't exist yet for these new formats, human labels are hard to calibrate, and LLM judges confuse fluent wording with real user intent. Without a reliable, continuous estimate of click intent, platforms cannot price ads properly or use the signal to train ad-generation models.

Method: The authors create a psychologically grounded agent-based simulation framework that mimics how users might respond (click or not) to ads embedded in LLM answers. From the simulated supervision, they distil a parameter-efficient evaluation model that predicts click-through intent and three additional dimensions of ad quality. These predictions are smooth and differentiable, enabling downstream optimisation. They validate the evaluator using behavioural perturbation tests, content degradation experiments, generalisation checks on fictional products, and comparison with human pairwise preferences. Using the evaluator's continuous intent estimates, they then derive a mechanism-design-based pricing rule that makes truthful bidding optimal, demonstrate it for best-of-k ad allocation, and extend it to more complex, non-monotone allocation rules.

Result: The evaluator shows higher sensitivity to relevance changes than strong zero-shot LLM judges (79% vs. 60–67%), can detect graded degradation in ad content quality, generalises to 103 fictional products with no errors, and matches human pairwise preferences in 86% of cases across five annotators, with accuracy increasing when the evaluator is more confident. On top of this intent signal, they construct a pricing mechanism that yields truthful bidding in both standard best-of-k and extended non-monotone allocation settings.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that simulated, psychologically informed supervision can fill the current gap in behavioural data for LLM-embedded ads and can be distilled into a compact, differentiable evaluator of click-through intent and ad quality. This evaluator outperforms frontier LLM judges and aligns well with human judgments, making it suitable both for mechanism design (truthful pricing and allocation of ads) and as a training objective for future ad-generation systems within LLM-based answer engines.

Abstract: As search increasingly shifts toward LLM-driven answer engines, advertising is becoming embedded within the generated response itself and should therefore be evaluated for both user utility and commercial value. The key challenge is click-through intent: behavioural logs are unavailable, human annotation resists calibration, and frontier LLM judges conflate intent with linguistic fluency. These gaps compound, as principled pricing presupposes a continuous intent signal, while generating such a signal presupposes supervision that is currently unavailable. We construct the missing supervision through a psychologically grounded agent simulation framework, and distil it into a parameter-efficient evaluator that predicts click-through intent, together with the three companion dimensions of ad quality, as smooth, differentiable estimates. Validated through sign-certain behavioural perturbations, the evaluator surpasses frontier zero-shot judges on relevance sensitivity (79% versus 60-67%), tracks graded content degradation, generalises without error to 103 fictional products, and agrees with human preference in 86% of pairwise judgements across five annotators, with agreement rising in the evaluator's confidence. Upon its estimates we build the pricing layer directly, deriving the unique payment rule under which truthful bidding is optimal, demonstrating it on a best-of-k allocation, and extending the mechanism to non-monotone allocations. The same differentiable signal stands ready as a training objective for ad generation.

</details>


### [99] [Rehearse: Stepping Back from the Confidence Cliff in Self-Improving Autoresearch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27687)
*Jiazhen Ji,Shouhong Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how well an autoresearch agent can judge which code modifications are worth running, finds that judgment reliability degrades over time (a “confidence cliff”), and proposes Rehearse, a focused-memory judging skill that restores late-loop accuracy and improves final model performance under fixed training budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoresearch systems iterate on ML code by proposing, training, and keeping beneficial changes, but full training runs are expensive. The loop’s efficiency hinges not just on generating modifications but on accurately predicting, before executing, which changes are likely to help. Existing logs show that as the loop progresses, a smaller fraction of proposed changes are useful, suggesting that judgment becomes harder late in the trajectory. The authors want to understand and fix this degradation to make autoresearch more sample- and compute-efficient.

Method: The authors analyze public AutoSOTA autoresearch logs to quantify how the fraction of helpful modifications changes over iterations. They then construct a benchmark of 366 modification pairs from 39 AutoSOTA tasks, each pair having one helpful and one harmful modification to the same baseline, and hide the measured outcomes. They evaluate an LLM judge that, given the code diffs and rationales but no history of previous attempts, chooses which modification is better. They define and measure “selective accuracy” (accuracy conditioned on the judge giving a confident verdict) across early vs. late stages in the loop and observe a drop—a “confidence cliff.” To address this, they design Rehearse: a judging skill that uses a focused memory of similar past attempts and their outcomes when comparing multiple candidate ideas before executing any. They empirically test Rehearse across 4,000 budgeted training runs on tasks including nanochat, image classification, and time-series forecasting.

Result: From AutoSOTA logs, the proportion of helpful modifications declines from about 70% in the first two iterations to 43% by iteration 6 and beyond, indicating that late-stage proposals are more often unhelpful. On a curated subset of 296 modification pairs where the judge returns a strict-consensus verdict, an LLM judge achieves 79.5% accuracy overall and 82.8% selective accuracy early in the loop. However, on the full 366-pair benchmark, selective accuracy drops sharply to 56.9% in later iterations, even though the judge still issues confident decisions—this is the “confidence cliff.” With Rehearse’s focused outcome memory, late-stage selective accuracy rises to 83.5%. In large-scale experiments totaling 4,000 training runs across three autoresearch loops (nanochat, image classification, time-series forecasting), Rehearse improves the final performance reached under the same training-run budgets compared to baselines without this skill.

Conclusion: Pre-execution judgment in autoresearch loops degrades as more successful modifications accumulate; LLM-based judges become overconfident yet less accurate late in the trajectory, producing a “confidence cliff.” By structuring the loop to compare multiple ideas at once and leveraging a focused memory of similar past attempts and their outcomes, Rehearse restores high late-stage selective accuracy and yields better end performance under fixed compute budgets. This suggests that memory-aware judging and comparison are key components for making autoresearch loops robust and efficient over long horizons.

Abstract: Autoresearch improves machine-learning code by proposing changes, running full training jobs, and keeping changes that improve the metric. The efficiency of this loop depends not only on generating ideas, but also on the agent's ability to decide, before spending a training run, whether a proposed modification is likely to work. We study how the reliability of this pre-execution judgment changes over the course of an autoresearch trajectory. In public AutoSOTA logs (Li et al., 2026; Tsinghua FIB Lab, 2026), the fraction of helpful modifications falls from 70% in the first two iterations to 43% by iteration 6+. On 296 same-baseline modification pairs from 39 paper-derived AutoSOTA tasks, each containing one modification that improved the metric and one that did not, with measured outcomes hidden, an LLM judge given candidate rationales but no prior-attempt history reaches 79.5% accuracy on the pairs where strict consensus returns a verdict. On the full 366-pair benchmark, however, this ability weakens substantially late in the loop. As successful changes accumulate, selective accuracy - accuracy conditioned on a strict-consensus verdict - falls from 82.8% to 56.9%, while the judge remains willing to decide. We call this operational pattern the confidence cliff. Rehearse implements the loop change as a lightweight skill for autoresearch loops: propose several ideas, compare them before execution, run the most promising, and judge with a focused memory of similar past attempts and outcomes. This focused outcome memory raises late selective accuracy to 83.5%. Across 4,000 budgeted training runs over three loops, Rehearse improves the endpoint under the same training-run budget on nanochat, image classification, and time-series forecasting.

</details>


### [100] [Guiding Large Language Models with Genetic Programming-Evolved Heuristic Knowledge for Dynamic Multi-Mode Project Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27698)
*Yuan Tian,Yi Mei,Mengjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to use knowledge from genetic-programming-evolved scheduling rules to guide large language models in dynamic multi-mode project scheduling, improving performance, token efficiency, and decision stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic multi-mode project scheduling is complex because activities can be executed in different modes with uncertain durations, under precedence and resource constraints. Practitioners rely on heuristic priority rules for fast online decisions, but designing good rules needs substantial domain expertise. Genetic programming can evolve strong heuristic rules automatically, but such rules are often opaque and hard to deploy in interactive, explainable systems. Large language models can interpret rich scheduling contexts and provide human-readable rationales, yet zero-shot LLM decisions lack domain-specific scheduling knowledge, can be token-expensive, and show instability across repeated calls. There is a need to combine the structured, optimized knowledge from GP-evolved rules with the flexible reasoning and interface of LLMs to get better, more stable, and more efficient online scheduling decisions.

Method: The authors propose an LLM decision framework that is guided by knowledge extracted from high-quality genetic-programming-evolved priority rules for dynamic multi-mode project scheduling. They reverse the usual LLM–GP hybrid direction: instead of using LLMs to help evolve heuristics, they use GP-derived heuristics to guide an LLM that makes online scheduling decisions. They design four knowledge-injection mechanisms: (1) Feature Selection, which reduces the decision context to a subset of GP-identified important features; (2) Feature Hint, which explicitly tells the LLM which features are important; (3) Rule Reference, which gives the LLM access to the GP rule as a reference; and (4) Rule Follow, which instructs the LLM to explicitly follow the GP rule’s decision logic. They then experimentally evaluate these mechanisms on dynamic multi-mode project scheduling instances, comparing them to an unguided LLM and to each other in terms of schedule quality, token usage, decision stability, and the distribution of features mentioned in generated explanations.

Result: Experiments show that injecting GP-derived knowledge generally improves the performance of an unguided LLM scheduler. However, the effectiveness depends on how that knowledge is represented and injected. Methods that simplify the decision context or provide explicit decision logic (Feature Selection and Rule Follow) perform better than those that merely highlight important features (Feature Hint) or loosely reference the rule. Feature Selection is the most token-efficient method while still improving schedule quality, whereas Rule Follow achieves particularly strong scheduling performance but requires more tokens due to the explicit rule logic. All guidance methods tend to make LLM decisions more stable across repeated runs and alter the feature focus in the LLM’s textual rationales, indicating that the GP knowledge influences the LLM’s internal reasoning and explanations.

Conclusion: The study concludes that GP-evolved scheduling rules are a valuable source of domain knowledge for guiding LLM-based online decision makers in dynamic multi-mode project scheduling. By transferring knowledge from GP to LLM—rather than vice versa—one can obtain better scheduling performance, greater decision stability, and lower token costs, depending on the guidance mechanism adopted. Representations that either streamline the decision context (Feature Selection) or provide clear, explicit decision logic (Rule Follow) are more effective than simply flagging important features. This suggests that carefully structured rule-based guidance is key to making LLMs practical and efficient for complex operational decision-making tasks, and opens avenues for combining traditional hyper-heuristics with modern LLMs in other domains.

Abstract: In dynamic multi-mode project scheduling, activities have alternative execution modes and uncertain durations, while precedence relations and limited resources constrain their execution. Heuristic priority rules support fast online decisions, but their design requires substantial domain expertise. Genetic programming (GP) hyper-heuristics can automatically evolve such rules. Large language models (LLMs), meanwhile, provide a flexible interface for interpreting scheduling information and explaining decisions. However, zero-shot LLM decisions may lack domain knowledge, consume many tokens, and vary across repeated queries. GP-evolved rules therefore provide a potential source of scheduling knowledge for guiding LLM decisions. Unlike existing LLM--GP hybrids that use LLMs to support heuristic evolution, we transfer knowledge in the reverse direction, using knowledge extracted from high-quality GP rules to guide an online LLM decision maker. We extract knowledge from high-quality GP rules and inject it through Feature Selection, Feature Hint, Rule Reference, and Rule Follow. These mechanisms are evaluated in terms of scheduling performance, token consumption, decision stability, and the feature focus expressed in generated rationales. GP-derived guidance generally improves the unguided LLM, but its representation matters. Simplifying the decision context or supplying explicit decision logic is more effective than highlighting important features. Feature Selection offers the best token efficiency, whereas Rule Follow achieves strong performance at greater token cost. Guidance also improves decision stability and changes the features expressed in generated rationales.

</details>


### [101] [SpatialCLI: Learning to Reason With Spatial Tools, Then Without Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27703)
*Yang Zhou,Zixuan Huang,Sunzhu Li,Zhuo Yang,Chen Zhang,Shunian Chen,Caijun Yan,Jianyao Xu,Shunyu Liu,Weijie Fu,Peiliang Li,Xiaozhi Chen,Yuxiang Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpatialCLI is a framework that lets vision-language models (VLMs) use specialist spatial perception tools and then internalize those skills, greatly improving their performance on embodied, spatial reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose VLMs are good at high-level reasoning but often miss fine-grained visual details critical for success in embodied tasks. Conversely, specialist vision models capture detailed spatial information (e.g., precise localization, segmentation, depth, pose) but cannot make task-level decisions or express reasoning in language. There is a capability gap between task-level reasoning and low-level perception, limiting the effectiveness of embodied agents that must both understand instructions and act accurately in complex visual environments.

Method: The authors propose SpatialCLI, a three-stage framework to teach VLMs to reason with and then internalize specialist spatial tools. (1) Call: expose specialist vision models (for localization, segmentation, depth, pose, etc.) as callable spatial tools that the VLM can use to augment its perception when solving tasks. (2) Learn: improve the VLM’s tool usage via Cold-Start Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) combined with agentic Reinforcement Learning (RL), so the VLM learns when and how to invoke these tools effectively. (3) Internalize: convert successful tool-use trajectories into verbalized supervision, enabling the VLM to absorb specialist perceptual capabilities into its own parameters so it can perform better even without explicit tool calls. They also create SpatialCLI-Bench, a benchmark with 516 examples that stress compositional perception across localization, segmentation, depth, and pose tasks.

Result: On the MindCube benchmark, using SpatialCLI with tools boosts Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct’s accuracy from 29.3% to 84.6%, outperforming GPT-5.6-Sol with tools, which reaches 72.1%. After the internalization stage, the enhanced Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct still achieves 73.8% accuracy even without using tools, indicating substantial transfer of specialist perceptual capabilities into the base model.

Conclusion: SpatialCLI effectively bridges the gap between general vision-language reasoning and specialist spatial perception in embodied agents. By first teaching VLMs to call external spatial tools and then internalizing those skills through verbalized trajectories, the framework yields large performance gains on compositional perception tasks and preserves much of that improvement even in a tool-free setting. The accompanying SpatialCLI-Bench provides a focused evaluation suite for such compositional spatial perception abilities.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used in embodied agents to interpret visual inputs, reason about spatial relationships, and make task-level decisions based on that reasoning. However, a fundamental capability mismatch remains: general VLMs can reason about the overall task but often miss the visual details that determine success, while specialist vision models can capture those details but cannot translate them into task-level decisions. In this work, we propose SpatialCLI, a framework that teaches VLMs to reason with spatial tools and progressively internalize the specialist perceptual capabilities they provide. SpatialCLI proceeds in three stages: (1) Call exposes specialist vision models as spatial tools to augment the VLM's perception; (2) Learn uses Cold-Start SFT and agentic RL to improve tool use; and (3) Internalize verbalizes successful tool-use trajectories to internalize specialist perceptual capabilities. We further introduce SpatialCLI-Bench, a 516-example benchmark for compositional perception across localization, segmentation, depth, and pose. On MindCube, SpatialCLI raises Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct from 29.3% to 84.6% with tools, surpassing GPT-5.6 Sol with tools (72.1%), while retaining 73.8% without tools after internalization.

</details>


### [102] [Albilich: Steerable Proof-State Orchestration for LLM-Based Mathematical Research with CAS Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27705)
*Ting Gong,Michael Ruofan Zeng,Yong Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Albilich is an open-source agentic framework that coordinates long-horizon mathematical research with LLMs, CAS tools, and literature retrieval, achieving strong results on formal benchmarks and real open problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs can generate useful mathematical insights, scaling them to long, multi-step research problems is hard because attempts are difficult to coordinate, evaluate, and reproduce. There is a need for a structured, persistent, tool-using environment that allows LLMs to conduct sustained mathematical investigations in a way that is both efficient and human-steerable.

Method: The authors build Albilich, an agentic harness that integrates (1) long-horizon reasoning via an LLM, (2) computer algebra systems for symbolic and computational assistance, (3) literature retrieval to incorporate prior work, and (4) persistent SQLite-based context management for tracking hypotheses, partial results, and experiments over time. They evaluate it on the RealMath benchmark and on selected open problems from the Kourovka Notebook, and perform ablation studies removing CAS and an advisor agent to measure their roles in performance and efficiency.

Result: On the RealMath benchmark, Albilich solved all 10 problems when using CAS and 9 out of 10 without CAS. On selected Kourovka Notebook problems in group theory, it found a counterexample to Problem 21.142 and proved a strengthening of Problem 20.2. An ablation on Problem 17.91 shows a 32% reduction in token usage when CAS is enabled, and an ablation on Problem 21.142 indicates worse verifier acceptance and failure to find proof strategies without the advisor agent.

Conclusion: Albilich provides an effective, scalable environment for AI-assisted mathematical research, where LLMs can be steered by humans and augmented by CAS, literature tools, and persistent state. The empirical results suggest that such structured, tool-augmented agentic setups can both improve success rates on challenging math problems and reduce computational cost, highlighting a promising direction for future autoresearch systems in mathematics.

Abstract: Large language models can contribute useful ideas to mathematical research, yet long-horizon proof attempts remain difficult to coordinate, evaluate, and reproduce. We present Albilich, an open-source agentic harness for autoresearch in mathematics that combines long-horizon reasoning, computer algebra systems (CAS), literature retrieval, and persistent SQLite-based context management.
  We evaluate Albilich on the RealMath benchmark (Zhang et al. 2025) and on open problems in group theory from the Kourovka Notebook (Khukhro and Mazurov 2026). It solved 10/10 problems on RealMath with CAS and 9/10 with no CAS. On the Kourovka problems, Albilich produced a counterexample to Problem 21.142 and a proof of a strengthening of Problem20.2. Anablation on Problem 17.91 demonstrates 32.0% token reduction when CAS is enabled. An ablation on Problem 21.142 demonstrates higher verifier-rejection rate and failure to synthesize proof routes in the absence of the advisor agent. These results support Albilich as a human-steerable, CAS-boosted environment for scalable AI-assisted mathematical research.

</details>


### [103] [MECA: A Mechanism-Centered Agent for Constructing Well-Specified and Valuable Mathematical Conjectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27709)
*Wentao Long,Yunfei Zhang,Chenyi Li,Zaiwen Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MECA, a multi-agent system that automatically turns broad math research directions into precise, mechanism-backed conjectures that are still hard for current automated provers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatically generating useful, well-specified mathematical conjectures is hard: existing open problems are often too vague, overly broad, or disconnected from feasible proof strategies, making them unsuitable targets for AI tools. The authors want a way for AI to propose conjectures that are both mathematically meaningful and concretely attackable, bridging the gap between informal research ideas and formal conjectures with some internal structure.

Method: They design MECA, a multi-agent framework where different LLM-based agents collaborate. Explorer agents propose candidate conjectures together with explicit mathematical mechanisms (like inequalities, invariants, decompositions, or reductions) that might connect assumptions to conclusions. They test and refine these mechanisms and modify the conjecture accordingly. Critic agents then evaluate the proposed mechanisms and conjectures for correctness, coherence, and research value, and their feedback drives further refinement of assumptions, scope, and conclusions. This iterative dialogue centers on mechanisms rather than just surface-level rephrasing.

Result: In a reconstruction setting, MECA is given material related to a target paper (but not the paper itself) and asked to recover its main conclusion; it outperforms a generate-and-revise baseline on this task. In a second setting, MECA is used to generate 100 semi-open mathematical problems from seeds taken from the literature and existing open problems. These conjectures are then tested via independent proof/refutation attempts using automated theorem provers. The resulting conjectures are judged to be well-specified, of genuine research interest, and remain unsolved by current automated provers, indicating nontriviality.

Conclusion: Focusing conjecture generation around explicit mathematical mechanisms, and implementing this via a coordinated multi-agent framework, leads to conjectures that are both precisely formulated and meaningfully supported, while still leaving a real unresolved core. MECA demonstrates that mechanism-centered refinement is a promising direction for AI-assisted mathematical discovery, producing conjectures that are aligned with mathematical practice and that pose challenging yet structured targets for future automated reasoning systems.

Abstract: Automatically constructing well-specified and valuable mathematical conjectures remains a central challenge in AI-assisted mathematical discovery. Many existing open problems and conjectures are often too broad, underspecified, or difficult to connect to plausible proof or refutation strategies. We view a mathematical mechanism as a structure or reasoning principle that connects the assumptions of a candidate problem to its target conclusion, such as an inequality, invariant, decomposition, or reduction to an intermediate claim. We present MECA (MEchanism-centered Conjecture Agent), a multi-agent framework that constructs conjectures by jointly developing candidate statements and their supporting mechanisms. Explorer agents propose mechanisms, test how they apply, and revise the candidate conjecture accordingly, while critic agents assess their mathematical validity and research value. Their feedback guides changes to the assumptions, scope, and conclusion. Through this process, MECA transforms broad research directions into precise conjectures with substantive mathematical support while retaining a clearly identified unresolved core. We evaluate MECA in two complementary settings. First, we compare it with a generate-and-revise baseline on reconstructing preselected target-paper conclusions from target-conditioned but article-blind source materials. Second, we construct 100 semi-open problems from literature-derived seeds and existing open problems and evaluate them through independent proof and refutation attempts by automated provers. Our results indicate that mechanism-centered refinement produces well-specified and research-worthy conjectures that remain challenging for current automated provers.

</details>


### [104] [New Synchronous Computation Dynamics for Hopfield Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27720)
*Francisco Requena-Domínguez,Rafaela Benítez-Rochel,Ezequiel López-Rubio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a faster, convergent synchronous update rule for Hopfield networks using a new tool (Discrete Differential Filter) to choose neuron subsets that maximally decrease energy at each step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Hopfield networks use asynchronous (one-neuron-at-a-time) updates, which can be slow in practice. Fully synchronous updates can be faster but may not converge or may not optimally reduce the energy function at each step. The authors want a principled way to update multiple neurons at once that both guarantees convergence and minimizes total processing time.

Method: They formalize the task of moving from the current state to a next state that yields the largest possible decrease in the Hopfield energy while still guaranteeing convergence. This selection itself is a combinatorial optimization problem. To tackle it, they introduce a new computational tool called the Discrete Differential Filter (DDF) that evaluates candidate neuron updates. Based on DDF, they define a new synchronous dynamics, SD-DDF, which updates one or more neurons simultaneously according to the DDF’s recommendations. They then compare SD-DDF against the original asynchronous dynamics using four computational experiments, measuring processing time and convergence behavior.

Result: The SD-DDF dynamics, driven by the DDF tool, successfully identifies multi-neuron updates that significantly reduce the energy at each step while maintaining convergence. Experiments show empirical speedups in processing time compared with the standard asynchronous Hopfield updates, indicating fewer update steps or less total computation to reach stable states.

Conclusion: A new synchronous Hopfield network dynamics, SD-DDF, is introduced and theoretically justified. Using the Discrete Differential Filter, it selects neuron subsets that yield maximal energy decrease per step while preserving convergence. Empirical tests on several problems demonstrate that this approach can substantially reduce processing time relative to the traditional asynchronous Hopfield dynamics.

Abstract: The dynamics of the original Hopfield network is asynchronous (sequential) (updates the state of only one neuron per time step). In this paper, we propose a new tool and a new dynamics to reduce the processing time by updating one or more neurons simultaneously per instant while ensuring process convergence and aiming for the maximum energy decrease at each step, thus guaranteeing the shortest total processing time. From the point of view of synchronous dynamics, calculating the next network state at which energy decreases the most from the current state while ensuring convergence is itself a combinatorial optimization problem. We develop and use a new tool to solve it. We call this new tool Discrete Differential Filter (DDF) and, based upon it, we develop a new synchronous dynamics which we call SD-DDF (Synchronous Dynamics based upon Discrete Differential Filter). In this paper, we review the original asynchronous dynamics for Hopfield networks and present a new tool and a new synchronous dynamics with its theoretical justification and four computational experiments to assess the speed up in processing time empirically.

</details>


### [105] [Baikal: Structured Search for Deep Research over Data Lakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27726)
*Dhruv Agarwal,Rishitha Guttapalle Mohan,Aarti Kumari,Ashi Sinha,Athulya Anil,Kavitha Srinivas,Horst Samulowitz,Andrew McCallum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Baikal, a budgeted search framework that organizes a large heterogeneous data lake into semantic regions and adaptively explores them to generate higher‑quality research reports than prior LLM-based agents under a fixed subquestion budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM research agents working over massive, heterogeneous data lakes must gather and synthesize evidence from thousands of tables and passages. Existing iterative retrieve‑then‑generate systems tend to over-focus on locally promising evidence and neglect other semantic areas when operating under a fixed query or token budget, which hurts coverage and overall research quality. The paper aims to provide a more principled way to balance exploration of new evidence regions with exploitation of promising ones.

Method: The authors frame deep research over data lakes as a budgeted search problem. They first cluster heterogeneous evidence (tables and text passages) into semantic regions. Baikal then runs an adaptive search over these regions: for each selected region, it has the LLM generate region-grounded subquestions, retrieve and inspect evidence, and assess the quality of findings. These quality scores act as rewards that update region-level value estimates. Region selection is guided by different policies, including random, LLM-guided, Bayesian ε-greedy, and UCB strategies, which trade off exploration vs. exploitation under a fixed subquestion budget.

Result: On two large data lakes—HybridQA (10,993 tables + 227K Wikipedia passages) and TAT-QA (2,757 tables + 13K financial report passages)—and 15 evaluation queries each, Baikal is compared to strong baselines like DeepSearcher and an OpenCode research agent with retrieval and clustering variants. Using a new evaluation rubric (groundedness, relevance, diversity, utility) and GPT-5-mini as the scorer, Baikal’s best configuration improves report quality scores by 28% on HybridQA and 36% on TAT-QA relative to the strongest baselines.

Conclusion: Organizing evidence into semantic regions and performing structured, budgeted exploration over them substantially improves LLM-based research quality in large, heterogeneous data lakes. Semantic-region-based search helps balance exploration and exploitation, leading to better grounded, more diverse, and more useful findings under the same subquestion budget, highlighting the importance of structured semantic exploration for systematic research and discovery.

Abstract: Deep research over data lakes requires an LLM agent to investigate evidence across thousands of heterogeneous tables and passages to synthesize a report. Existing methods perform iterative retrieval and generation, letting accumulated context determine what to investigate next, which can overexploit locally promising evidence and fail to cover distinct semantic regions under a fixed budget. To address this, we cast deep research over data lakes as a budgeted search problem and present Baikal - a framework that clusters heterogeneous evidence into semantic regions, then searches over them adaptively to balance exploration and exploitation. Within each selected region, Baikal generates and investigates region-grounded subquestions, using finding quality as rewards to update region-level value estimates and guide search under policies ranging from random and LLM-guided selection to Bayesian $ε$-greedy and UCB. We evaluate Baikal on 15 queries each over HybridQA and TAT-QA data lakes containing 10,993 and 2,757 tables, respectively, together with 227K Wikipedia passages and 13K financial report passages. We assess research quality with a new rubric covering groundedness, relevance, diversity, and utility, and use GPT-5-mini to score Baikal and strong baselines, including DeepSearcher and an OpenCode research agent with retrieval and clustering variants. Across both data lakes, Baikal performs strongly under several region-selection policies; its best configuration improves report scores over the strongest baselines by 28% on HybridQA and 36% on TAT-QA. Our analyses attribute these gains to organizing and exploring semantic evidence regions, which improves groundedness and diversity and yields more useful findings under the same subquestion budget. These results demonstrate the value of structured semantic exploration for systematic research and discovery over heterogeneous data lakes.

</details>


### [106] [VeriSkill: A Self-Evolution Framework for Program Verification Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27733)
*Changguo Jia,Tianqi Zhao,Zhiyou Xiao,Weiming Zhang,Minghui Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VeriSkill, a framework that enables LLM-based agents to improve at program verification by automatically evolving reusable skills from past verification attempts and verifier feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Program verification with LLM agents requires not only code generation but also precise specifications, annotations, lemmas, and verification-tool calls. These components rely on reusable skills, but existing self-improvement methods cannot effectively learn and refine such skills because they fail to pinpoint which specific skills caused verification failures and cannot extract clear guidance from the often opaque feedback of verification tools. There is a need for a specialized self-evolution mechanism tailored to the structure and feedback characteristics of program verification tasks.

Method: The authors propose VeriSkill, a self-evolution framework specialized for program verification. VeriSkill analyzes verification trajectories and attributes failures to specific missing or flawed skills. It then (1) distills these failure attributions into diagnostic 'signatures' that capture what went wrong, (2) turns these signatures into reusable lessons or candidate revised skills, and (3) iteratively refines and filters these skills, accepting only revisions that both improve verification success and preserve program semantics. The system operates across different verification tools, agent frameworks, and LLM backends.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that VeriSkill leads to better verification performance than competing baselines on a range of settings. Across multiple verification tools, agent frameworks, and LLM models, VeriSkill consistently achieves higher success rates in verifying programs, confirming that its skill attribution and refinement approach yields practically useful improvements.

Conclusion: VeriSkill offers an effective approach to self-evolution for LLM agents in program verification. By systematically attributing failures to specific skill deficiencies, encoding them as diagnostic signatures, and refining skills while preserving semantics, it overcomes limitations of prior evolution methods and consistently improves verification outcomes across diverse tools and models.

Abstract: Automating program verification with LLM agents requires generating specifications, annotations, auxiliary lemmas, and tool invocations, all of which depend on reusable skills. A natural remedy is skill self-evolution: distilling skills from trajectories and refining them through feedback. However, existing evolution methods struggle with program verification tasks because they cannot reliably identify skill-specific failures or extract actionable signals from opaque verifier feedback. In this paper, we propose VeriSkill, a self-evolution framework built for program verification. It attributes verification failures to skill deficiencies, distills diagnostic signatures into reusable lessons, and iteratively refines candidate skills, admitting only revisions that improve verification performance while preserving program semantics. Experiments show that VeriSkill consistently outperforms all baselines across multiple verification tools, agent frameworks, and LLM backends.

</details>


### [107] [SpecCal: Ambiguity-Aware Candidate Calibration for Infrared Spectrum-Based Molecular Structure Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27788)
*Yixuan Chen,Bo Liu,Yusen Tan,Guokun Yang,Wenjie Du,Jun Xia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce SpecCal, a training-free, plug-and-play framework that recalibrates and augments candidate molecules proposed by existing IR-to-molecule models using spectral consistency, improving molecular structure reconstruction from IR spectra.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inferring full molecular structures from IR spectra is difficult because spectra encode limited and often ambiguous structural information: different molecules can share similar functional groups and vibrational patterns, leading to highly similar spectral signatures. Existing IR-to-molecule models cope by outputting ranked candidate sets, but these rankings are heavily influenced by model-specific generation biases and may miss structures that better satisfy the spectral constraints. There is a need for a method that can correct or refine these candidate sets without retraining or modifying base models, especially under spectral ambiguity.

Method: They propose SpecCal, a training-free, model-agnostic calibration framework that operates post hoc on the candidate molecules produced by any base IR-to-molecule model. SpecCal re-ranks existing candidates based on their consistency with the observed IR spectrum and additionally introduces new, structurally plausible candidate molecules guided by spectral alignment criteria. It is plug-and-play, requiring no parameter updates or retraining; instead, it uses spectral consistency to refine and expand the candidate set to better match the observed spectrum under ambiguity.

Result: Across multiple IR-to-molecule benchmarks and using diverse base models, SpecCal consistently improves top-k reconstruction accuracy at both the SMILES level (exact molecular structure) and scaffold level (core structural framework). This shows that its calibration and augmentation of candidate sets leads to more accurate inclusion and prioritization of the true structure among the predicted molecules.

Conclusion: Calibrating and expanding model-generated candidate sets using spectral consistency offers an effective, training-free strategy to mitigate ambiguity in IR-based molecular reconstruction. SpecCal, as a plug-and-play, model-agnostic framework, reliably enhances top-k reconstruction performance across models and benchmarks, demonstrating that post hoc candidate calibration is a practical complement to existing IR-to-molecule predictors.

Abstract: Inferring molecular structures from infrared (IR) spectra is a fundamental yet challenging problem. A key difficulty is that an IR spectrum provides limited structural information: different molecules may share similar functional groups and local vibrational patterns, leading to highly similar spectral responses. Thus, even when an observed spectrum has a unique underlying structure, reconstructing it from the spectrum remains ambiguous. Existing IR-to-molecule models usually generate a ranked set of candidate molecules, but this set is largely determined by the model's learned generation preference and may not fully capture the structures that best satisfy the observed spectral constraints. To address this limitation, we propose SpecCal, a training-free candidate calibration framework for IR-to-molecule prediction. SpecCal operates on the candidate outputs of existing base models and improves the prediction set by re-ranking current candidates while introducing additional structurally plausible alternatives guided by spectral consistency. The framework is plug-and-play and model-agnostic, requiring no parameter updates for integration with diverse base models. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that SpecCal consistently improves top-k reconstruction at both SMILES and scaffold levels across different base models. Further analyses demonstrate that calibrating candidate sets under spectral ambiguity provides a practical way to improve molecular reconstruction from IR spectra. The code is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/SpecCal-B18A.

</details>


### [108] [Annotating Topical Legal Insights from Case Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27792)
*Subinay Adhikary,Dwaipayan Roy,Debasis Ganguly,Shouvik Kumar Guha,Kripabandhu Ghosh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents LeDA, a web-based legal data annotation system that lets users dynamically define and annotate legal concepts in case proceedings to build structured, concept-based document representations for downstream legal AI tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal documents are usually processed as flat bag-of-words text, which limits advanced analysis and downstream tasks like case retrieval and judgment prediction. There is a need for structured, concept-level representations of legal case proceedings, but predefined ontologies for legal concepts are often missing or incomplete, especially in complex jurisdictions like the Indian Supreme Court. Therefore, a flexible annotation system is needed that can support discovering and defining new legal concepts on the fly while annotating documents.

Method: The authors design and implement LeDA, a web-based system for Legal Data Annotation. It provides generic functionality to annotate entities or concepts in legal documents, and to adjudicate between annotators. Its key innovation is allowing annotators to dynamically create new tags (concept types) during the annotation process, without requiring a pre-existing ontology. The system is applied to Indian Supreme Court case proceedings, where three assessors use LeDA to annotate and adjudicate legal concept names, thereby constructing semantic, concept-level representations (bags of concepts) for each document.

Result: LeDA is successfully deployed and used by three assessors to annotate legal concepts in Indian Supreme Court case proceedings. The system enables the creation of a concept-based semantic representation of legal documents that can serve as input for downstream tasks like prior case retrieval and judgment prediction. The abstract indicates the system works as a practical tool for collaborative annotation and adjudication, but does not provide numerical evaluation results in the abstract.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that LeDA effectively supports the discovery and annotation of legal concepts in case proceedings, even in the absence of a predefined ontology. By enabling dynamic tag creation and adjudication, it helps construct structured, bag-of-concepts representations of legal documents, which are more suitable than flat bag-of-words for advanced legal AI tasks such as retrieval and prediction. The demonstrated use on Indian Supreme Court cases validates its usefulness as a legal data annotation infrastructure.

Abstract: In this paper, we mainly concentrate on finding concepts or topics from the legal case proceedings, since adopting a structured representation for legal documents, as opposed to a mere bag-of-words flat text representation, can significantly enhance processing capabilities. To achieve this objective, we put forward a set of diverse concepts for legal case proceedings. With this motivation, we propose LeDA, a system for Legal Data Annotation. The system offers the generic functionality of annotating and adjudicating entities or concepts within documents via a web-based interface. A novel feature of our system is that it allows to dynamic create new tags for annotation, which is a particularly useful provision for situations where there exists no pre-defined ontology for the entities (concepts) that need to be annotated - these being rather discovered by annotators as they continue examining more documents. The system that we demonstrate is currently in use to annotate a set of concepts from legal documents to construct semantic representations of documents as bags of concepts that can then be used for several downstream tasks, such as prior case retrieval, judgment prediction, and so on. Along with the system features in general, we also describe how LeDA was used by 3 assessors to annotate and adjudicate legal concept names from Indian Supreme Court case proceedings.

</details>


### [109] [MemeBench: What LVLMs Miss When Interpreting Culture-Dependent Memes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27798)
*Weihang Wang,Kainan Tu,Jielei Zhang,Run Yang,Boheng Sheng,Yuchen He,Yu Xie,Pengyu Chen,Peiyi Li,Huyang Sun,Longwen Gao,Zhouhui Lian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MemeBench, a diagnostic benchmark and VIKR schema to analyze where large vision-language models (LVLMs) fail when interpreting memes, and proposes a retrieval-based method (KAR) that partially closes the gap between visual recognition and knowledge-based understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LVLMs can describe visible content but often fail to truly interpret memes, which require cultural knowledge and reasoning beyond pixels. Current meme benchmarks mostly use labels or coarse scores, making it hard to pinpoint which part of the explanation fails (visual perception, entity recognition, knowledge, or reasoning). The authors want a fine-grained diagnostic tool to measure and analyze these failures, especially in culturally rich domains like anime, comics, and games.

Method: The authors construct MemeBench, a dataset of 1,253 Chinese and English memes focused on anime, comics, games, and related subcultures. Each meme has human-written reference explanations and structured VIKR annotations (Visual clues, Identity links, Knowledge units, Reasoning mechanisms). They evaluate 26 LVLMs on this benchmark to measure coverage and gaps across VIKR dimensions. To test improvement strategies, they propose KAR, an entity-guided retrieval baseline that uses CultureBase to fetch relevant cultural knowledge based on detected entities, and compare it against a generic retrieval baseline across four controlled models.

Result: Across 26 LVLMs, all models describe visible content more reliably than they capture the knowledge needed to interpret memes, with even the strongest model showing a 22.6% gap between visual and knowledge coverage. The KAR retrieval method increases overall VIKR Success by 3.6–7.4% over the base models and performs better than generic retrieval in terms of fixing incorrect answers without introducing new errors. However, both KAR and generic retrieval improve Identity and Knowledge dimensions while consistently reducing Visual coverage, indicating a trade-off between knowledge augmentation and visual grounding.

Conclusion: MemeBench, with its VIKR schema, provides a fine-grained diagnostic benchmark that reveals not only whether LVLM meme interpretations succeed but also which components fail—visual perception, entity identification, knowledge, or reasoning. The study shows that targeted, entity-guided retrieval (KAR) can partially close the visual-knowledge gap but also introduces systematic trade-offs, especially reduced visual coverage. This suggests that improving LVLM meme understanding requires integrated approaches that jointly preserve visual grounding while augmenting cultural and commonsense knowledge, and that MemeBench can serve as a tool to evaluate such methods.

Abstract: Large vision-language models have improved at describing visual content, but accurate descriptions do not ensure interpretation when meaning depends on knowledge beyond the pixels. Memes expose this gap because they rely on cultural entities, background knowledge, and community conventions. Most meme benchmarks reduce interpretation to labels or holistic scores, obscuring where an explanation breaks down. We introduce MemeBench, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,253 Chinese and English memes with human-written references and quality-controlled VIKR annotations, centered on anime, comics, games, and adjacent online subcultures. Its VIKR schema decomposes explanations into Visual clues, Identity links, Knowledge units, and Reasoning mechanisms. Across 26 LVLMs, every model covers visible content more reliably than the knowledge needed to interpret it, and even the strongest retains a 22.6% Visual-Knowledge gap. To test whether this diagnosis can guide improvement, we introduce KAR, an entity-guided retrieval baseline built on CultureBase. Across four controlled models, KAR raises VIKR Success by 3.6-7.4% and, compared with generic retrieval, repairs more answers and breaks fewer. Yet both retrieval conditions improve Identity and Knowledge while reducing Visual coverage in every comparison. MemeBench reveals whether an interpretation succeeds, what is missing, and whether targeted evidence fills the diagnosed gap.

</details>


### [110] [STEREODISCO: Discovering Stereotypicality in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27824)
*Farane Jalali Farahani,Corina Dima,Mojtaba Nayyeri,Raphael H. Heiberger,Steffen Staab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes STEREODISCO, a framework to systematically discover and analyze stereotype-carrying semantic axes inside LLM activations, revealing many axes beyond those studied in social psychology and showing divergence between LLM and human stereotype structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on stereotypes in LLMs mainly looks at a small, predefined set of stereotype dimensions from social psychology and operates on surface-level word embeddings. This leaves unknown which other semantic dimensions encode stereotypes and how these dimensions are internally represented in modern LLMs. A more systematic, representation-level method is needed to uncover the broader structure of stereotypes in LLMs and compare it with human stereotype theory.

Method: Adapt the semantic differential method to neural activations. First, automatically construct about 2,000 antonym-based candidate semantic axes from WordNet antonym synsets. For each axis, learn a corresponding geometric direction (probe) in the hidden activation space of a target LLM, effectively mapping linguistic opposites to vectors in representation space. Then, project social group concepts onto these axes and use statistical testing to identify axes where projections show systematic, non-random group differences, which are interpreted as stereotypical axes. Apply this pipeline to two instruction-tuned LLMs (LLaMA-3-8B-Instruct and Mistral-7B-Instruct).

Result: The framework successfully recovers a large set of interpretable semantic axes in the internal activation space of both models and detects many axes that carry stereotypical associations for social groups. The two LLMs are more similar to each other in how they rate social groups along these axes than either is to human stereotype ratings from social psychology. The method also surfaces new or underexplored stereotype-relevant axes such as humble–proud, narrow-minded–broad-minded, and cowardly–brave, which independent human annotators validate as meaningful stereotypes.

Conclusion: STEREODISCO provides a scalable way to map and quantify stereotype-laden semantic axes in LLM internal representations, going far beyond the limited dimensions previously studied. The discovered divergences between LLM and human stereotype structures suggest that LLMs encode a distinct, model-internal stereotype space. The approach both broadens the empirical basis for studying social biases in LLMs and offers a tool to systematically uncover new stereotype dimensions that merit further scrutiny and potential mitigation.

Abstract: LLMs encode, convey, and perpetuate stereotypes. Prior computational research focuses on a small set of semantic axes investigated in social psychology, and operates on word embeddings produced by language models, leaving open which other semantic axes carry stereotypical associations in LLMs and how LLMs internally represent such axes. We introduce STEREODISCO, a framework that adapts the semantic differential method (Osgood et al., 1957) to the systematic study of stereotypes in LLM internal representations. STEREODISCO constructs approx. 2,000 candidate semantic axes from WordNet antonym synsets, recovers each as a geometric axis in the LLM's activation space via probing, and identifies stereotypical axes via a statistical test over concept projections. As a case study, we apply STEREODISCO to social group stereotypes with LLAMA-3-8B-INSTRUCT and MISTRAL-7B-INSTRUCT. We find that the two LLMs agree with each other on social group ratings more than with humans, suggesting that LLM-encoded stereotype content diverges from that documented in social psychology. We also discover stereotypical axes not investigated in prior work -- including humble vs. proud, narrow-minded vs. broad-minded, and cowardly vs. brave, which human annotators independently confirm.

</details>


### [111] [Sign Language Question Answering: A New Task, Benchmark, and Baseline for Sign Language Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27826)
*Shiwei Gan,Lichen Wang,Xiao Liu,Yafeng Yin,Kuizhuang Liu,Sanglu Lu,Lei Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Sign Language Question Answering (SLQA) as a new task to evaluate deep semantic understanding of sign language videos, builds two benchmarks (SignQA) on PHOENIX14T and CSL-Daily, and proposes a baseline model that outperforms existing vision-language models across multiple reasoning categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sign language understanding tasks (e.g., continuous recognition and translation) rely on fixed mappings from sign videos to glosses or sentences, so they only partially reflect whether models truly understand the semantics of sign language videos. There is a need for a more flexible, fine-grained evaluation that can probe diverse reasoning abilities over sign language content, not just recognition or translation performance.

Method: The authors define a new task, Sign Language Question Answering (SLQA), where a model observes sign language videos and answers arbitrary natural language questions about them. To support this, they automatically generate question-answer pairs from existing gloss and sentence annotations of PHOENIX14T and CSL-Daily using carefully crafted templates, resulting in two SignQA benchmarks covering five question types: position reasoning, structural reasoning, visual search, gloss recognition, and translation understanding. They further design a baseline model with a Question-Conditioned Modulated Temporal Downsampling module to model temporal video features conditioned on the question and an in-domain knowledge transfer strategy to leverage pretrained SLU models for the new QA task.

Result: On the constructed SignQA benchmarks, the proposed baseline model consistently surpasses representative general-purpose vision-language models across all five question categories, showing better alignment with sign language semantics and more effective question-aware temporal reasoning.

Conclusion: SLQA is an effective new task formulation for assessing semantic and reasoning abilities in sign language understanding beyond traditional recognition and translation. The two introduced SignQA benchmarks and the proposed baseline model together establish a strong foundation and point of comparison for future research on sign language question answering and richer SL understanding systems.

Abstract: Recent advances in sign language (SL) understanding (SLU) have led to remarkable progress in tasks such as continuous SL recognition and SL translation. However, these tasks are designed with predefined objectives, requiring models to learn a fixed mapping from sign videos to glosses or spoken-language sentences. As a result, they provide only a limited assessment of whether a model truly understands the semantic content of SL videos. To address this limitation, \textbf{we first propose a new task, Sign Language Question Answering (SLQA)}, which evaluates SL understanding by requiring models to answer arbitrary natural language questions about SL videos. Unlike previous SLU tasks, SLQA provides a more flexible and comprehensive evaluation framework that assesses multiple reasoning capabilities beyond recognition and translation. To facilitate this task, \textbf{we further construct two SignQA benchmarks} based on PHOENIX14T and CSL-Daily by automatically generating question-answer pairs from existing gloss and sentence annotations using carefully designed templates. The resulting datasets cover five complementary question categories, including position reasoning, structural reasoning, visual search, gloss recognition, and translation understanding. \textbf{Finally, we propose a simple yet effective baseline model} equipped with a Question-Conditioned Modulated Temporal Downsampling module and an in-domain knowledge transfer strategy, enabling effective knowledge transfer from existing SLU tasks while enhancing question-aware temporal feature modeling. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our baseline consistently outperforms representative vision-language models across all question categories, establishing a strong benchmark for future research on SLQA. Datasets are available at:{https://huggingface.co/datasets/hulala/SignQA-2026}.

</details>


### [112] [MemTxn: A Transaction Boundary for Source-Supported Updates and Complete-State Recovery in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27834)
*Hanshuai Cui,Zhiqing Tang,Zhi Yao,Fanshuai Meng,Qianli Ma,Weijia Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MemTxn, a transactional governance layer for persistent memory in LLM agents that ensures reliable, verifiable memory updates and robust recovery from memory errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent memory helps LLM agents keep and reuse knowledge across sessions, but any incorrect write can persist and harm future behavior. Existing memory systems focus on better storage and retrieval but lack transactional guarantees: they don't ensure that updates are actually supported by source evidence, they don’t resolve conflicting facts in a principled way, and they don't provide a clear mechanism to restore correct state after faults. This creates reliability and safety problems when agents autonomously read and write long-lived memory.

Method: The authors design MemTxn as a governance layer that sits outside the main answer model. It introduces three key components: (1) Ordered PatchTest, which validates proposed memory writes by checking that each update is actually supported by its source content; (2) a Temporal Resolver, which chooses which version of a fact should be visible to the agent when multiple, potentially conflicting versions exist (e.g., based on time and consistency rules); and (3) a durable snapshot journal that records memory states so the system can roll back to a consistent application-visible snapshot after errors, even when the underlying physical write set is unknown. They evaluate MemTxn on controlled audits and several benchmarks for persistent-memory agents.

Result: In an item-disjoint audit setting, MemTxn perfectly distinguished valid from invalid memory updates, accepting all 60 supported originals and rejecting all 179 hard negatives. Under scenarios with persistent multi-key faults on LongMemEval-S and LoCoMo benchmarks, MemTxn successfully restored the full declared active memory map without needing to know which keys were physically corrupted. On the MemoryAgentBench FactConsolidation task, MemTxn achieved the best average F1 score across twelve different answer-model configurations and surpassed the Dense baseline by 17.06–24.07 F1 points in five key settings.

Conclusion: MemTxn provides a transactional abstraction for LLM agent memory: it verifies that writes are evidence-backed, resolves conflicting facts, and supports robust recovery from memory faults. Empirical results show that this governance layer substantially improves reliability and factual consolidation in persistent-memory agents, outperforming existing dense-memory approaches across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Persistent memory lets long-running large language model agents reuse information across sessions and tasks. Yet errors in writable memory can persist and corrupt future behavior. Existing systems improve storage and retrieval, but they do not provide a transaction boundary for reliable updates and recovery. We therefore propose MemTxn, a governance layer outside the answer model. MemTxn verifies whether an update is supported by its source. It also selects the visible version when facts conflict and restores the application-visible state after a fault. The system uses Ordered PatchTest to validate writes, a Temporal Resolver to select versions, and a durable snapshot journal to recover state. On an item-disjoint audit, MemTxn accepts all 60 supported originals and rejects all 179 hard negatives. Under persistent multi-key faults on LongMemEval-S and LoCoMo states, it restores the complete declared active map without knowing the actual physical write set. On MemoryAgentBench FactConsolidation, MemTxn achieves the highest average F1 across all twelve answer-model configurations. It outperforms Dense by 17.06--24.07 points in five representative settings.

</details>


### [113] [Crossing the Margin Cliff: Toward Relearn-Robust LLM Unlearning via Margin Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27836)
*Xiangyu Yin,Jiaxu Liu,Zhen Chen,Chih-Hong Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies why existing methods for unlearning specific data from large language models are fragile to relearning attacks, identifies a geometric phenomenon called the “margin cliff” as a key cause, and proposes Margin Calibration (MC), a plug-in training objective that significantly improves robustness against relearning at some cost to utility on retained data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unlearning methods aim to remove the influence of specific training data (the “forget set”) from large language models while preserving performance on the remaining data (the “retain set”). However, in practice these methods often fail when the model is exposed to a small amount of the forgotten information again (relearn attacks), quickly regaining its ability to reproduce that data. The paper is motivated by the need to understand the root causes of this fragility, characterize it theoretically and empirically, and design an objective that makes unlearning more robust without extensive per-task tuning.

Method: The authors analyze unlearning through the lens of optimization geometry, focusing on token-level answer margins (logit differences between correct and competing tokens) for forget vs. retain content. They evaluate 14 post-hoc unlearning methods (gradient-based, preference-based, and distillation-based) across multiple model sizes and benchmarks (TOFU, MUSE-News, Phi-3.5 panel). They discover a pattern called the “margin cliff,” where post-unlearning per-token margins for forget content cluster in a narrow band just above those of a retain-trained reference model. They prove that this behavior arises whenever a retain coupling keeps the diagnostic log-odds for forget tokens above a floor, which is induced by standard token-saturating losses at stationarity, and they empirically verify this condition in most method-size combinations. Building on this, they propose Margin Calibration (MC), a plug-in objective that adds: (1) a non-saturating margin hinge loss anchored at the reference model’s per-token margin, to continue pushing down forget margins beyond the saturation point, and (2) a KL-divergence probe on a disjoint instruction-following corpus, to maintain some alignment with general behavior. They instrument training to measure a gradient-dominance condition under which MC’s stationary points lie on the “cliff-crossing” side, ensuring margins are pushed low enough to resist relearning attacks.

Result: Empirically, fine-tuning on as few as 20 forget examples in the TOFU benchmark substantially restores the ROUGE score on a held-out forget set for all evaluated baseline unlearning methods, confirming strong relearning fragility. The margin cliff phenomenon appears in 41 of 42 method–model-size cells, and the theoretical retain-coupling condition is directly validated in 34 of 42 cells. The proposed Margin Calibration method, using a single frozen hyperparameter configuration, outperforms all baselines across three Llama-3 sizes and three forget tiers on TOFU, as well as on MUSE-News with Llama-2-7B-hf and on a Phi-3.5 model panel. MC wins all 14 head-to-head aggregate forget metrics and all populated relearn settings, reducing average post-attack ROUGE-L from 0.41 to 0.18. It also lowers membership inference AUC (a privacy leakage measure) in 13 out of 14 settings. The main tradeoff is a reduction in utility on retain data. A deployment-oriented variant of MC achieves similar gains without relying on a retain-trained reference model.
