Conclusion: The paper concludes that the fragility of current LLM unlearning methods under relearn attacks is largely due to optimization dynamics that leave forget-related token margins hovering just above those of a retain reference—a regime termed the margin cliff—so that a small amount of relearning data can quickly restore forgotten behavior. By characterizing the conditions that create this cliff and introducing Margin Calibration, a non-saturating margin objective with an auxiliary KL probe, the authors demonstrate that unlearning can be made substantially more robust, with tighter guarantees on how much relearning can increase margins. While this robustness comes with some loss of performance on retained data, the work provides both a theoretical and practical framework for designing and evaluating stronger unlearning objectives, including variants suitable for deployment without specialized retain references.

Abstract: Large language model unlearning is consistently fragile under relearn attacks. On TOFU, fine-tuning on twenty forget examples substantially recovers held-out forget-set ROUGE for every method we evaluate, and we trace this fragility to optimization geometry. The per-token answer margin of fourteen post-hoc methods spanning gradient, preference, and distillation families converges into a narrow band above the retain reference in 41 of 42 method--size cells, a regularity we call the margin cliff. We prove that this cliff follows whenever the retain coupling holds the diagnostic log-odds of forget content above a floor, a condition that token-saturating losses induce at stationarity and that we verify directly on 34 of 42 cells. Margin Calibration (\textsc{MC}) is a plug-in polish adding a non-saturating margin hinge anchored at the reference's per-token margin plus a KL probe on a disjoint instruction corpus, restoring forget-side pressure where the native loss saturates. Under a stated gradient-dominance condition, whose on-trajectory gradient signature we measure by instrumenting the polish, its stationary set lies on the cliff-crossing side, yielding an attack-budget upper bound on the relearn margin lift. Across TOFU (three Llama-3 sizes, three forget tiers), MUSE-News on Llama-2-7B-hf, and a Phi-3.5 panel, a single frozen configuration wins all 14 head-to-head forget aggregates and all populated relearn cells (panel-mean post-attack ROUGE-L $0.41$ to $0.18$) and lowers raw membership AUC on 13/14, with reduced retain-side utility as the main cost. A deployment variant matches these gains without a retain-trained reference.

</details>


### [114] [Virtual Process Dossier: A Process-Aware Data Catalogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27840)
*Lukas Kubelka,Alexander Bott,Frank Döhner,Saksham Kiroriwal,Georg Zeeb,Julia Butte,Julius Pfrommer,Jürgen Beyerer,Tobias Käfer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents the Virtual Process Dossier (VPD), a knowledge-graph-based data catalog that captures both data and workflow provenance for multi-stage manufacturing, enabling FAIR and step-specific access to datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-stage manufacturing, many datasets are generated at different workflow steps. Downstream AI and optimization need to clearly distinguish and relate these step-specific datasets and understand how they were produced. Existing catalogs often lack fine-grained, explicit workflow provenance and FAIR exposure of such data, limiting reuse, traceability, and reliable AI-based optimization.

Method: The authors design a Knowledge Graph-based data catalog called Virtual Process Dossier (VPD). They define a VPD ontology as the semantic core to model workflow steps, datasets, and their relations; create a provenance framework that integrates ontology instantiation directly into the production environment to capture both prospective (planned) and retrospective (executed) provenance; and implement a user interface that allows human-centered interaction with the VPD Knowledge Graph. The ontology and implementation are made available as open-source artifacts.

Result: The result is a working VPD system comprising an ontology, provenance integration framework, and user interface. It can represent datasets and their provenance across multi-stage manufacturing workflows, expose them in a FAIR-compliant way, and distinguish data produced at different processing steps for downstream AI-based optimization. The artifacts are published on GitHub for reuse and extension.

Conclusion: The VPD demonstrates that a Knowledge Graph-based approach can effectively function as a FAIR data catalog that also captures detailed workflow provenance in multi-stage manufacturing. By making both planned and actual process information explicit and accessible, VPD supports traceable, step-specific data access for AI-based optimization and other advanced analytics, and offers reusable semantic and software components for similar industrial scenarios.

Abstract: We propose the Virtual Process Dossier (VPD), a Knowledge Graph-based data catalogue that also captures workflow provenance. We developed VPD for multi-stage manufacturing use-cases where downstream AI-based optimization tasks require to distinct between datasets generated during individual workflow steps. VPD provides these datasets in a FAIR manner and makes both prospective and retrospective workflow provenance explicit. Our contributions are: (1) the VPD ontology that serves as the catalogue's semantic core; (2) the VPD provenance framework that integrates ontology instantiation into the production environment; and (3) the VPD user interface that provides human-centered interaction with the VPD Knowledge Graph. The ontology and code are available at https://github.com/kubeluk/VirtualProcessDossier .

</details>


### [115] [Simplifying Neural Networks During Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27854)
*Lorenzo Sciandra,Samuele Fonio,Roberto Esposito*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a training-time method that detects when and where a deep network’s layers become redundant, then truncates the model and replaces the last part with a small classifier, keeping accuracy while greatly reducing parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overparameterized deep nets are expensive and hard to understand. Neural Collapse suggests that, near the end of training, class features and classifiers adopt a simple, highly structured geometry. The Tunnel Effect further indicates that only some early/mid layers are truly needed for feature extraction. The authors want to exploit these training dynamics to automatically simplify networks during training, rather than pruning or compressing them post‑hoc, while preserving performance.

Method: They introduce an NC-inspired framework that tracks representation dynamics using the Inverse Fisher Criterion, which acts as a stable, efficient proxy for the variability collapse associated with Neural Collapse. Using this signal, the method automatically determines: (1) a split point in the network between a feature-extraction part and a classification part, and (2) the training time when simplification is safe. At that point, they remove the deeper trailing layers beyond the split and replace them with a lightweight classification head, then continue training this reduced architecture end‑to‑end.

Result: On standard image-classification benchmarks and across several architectures (MLP, VGG, ResNet), the simplified models obtained by their procedure use substantially fewer parameters while reaching test accuracy that is comparable to the original full networks. This demonstrates that the detected split point and timing allow aggressive structural simplification without significant performance loss.

Conclusion: The paper shows that training dynamics associated with Neural Collapse and the Tunnel Effect can be turned into a practical, online model-simplification strategy. By monitoring an Inverse Fisher Criterion during training, one can automatically identify and discard superfluous layers, replacing them with a compact classification head, thereby cutting parameters with little to no accuracy degradation. This provides a principled way to exploit overparameterization during learning and then remove it when it becomes unnecessary.

Abstract: Understanding and exploiting the training dynamics of overparameterized deep neural networks remains a central challenge in modern machine learning. Recent evidence on Neural Collapse (NC) shows that class representations and classifiers exhibit highly structured geometry, while the Tunnel Effect suggests that only a subset of layers is essential for feature extraction. We combine these two perspectives and propose an NC-inspired training framework for simplifying deep networks during training. Our method monitors representation dynamics through the Inverse Fisher Criterion, a stable and efficient proxy for the variability collapse behavior, to identify both the split point between feature extraction and classification and the training stage at which simplification becomes viable. We then replace the trailing layers with a lightweight classification head and continue training the reduced model. Experiments on image-classification benchmarks across MLP, VGG, and ResNet architectures show that the proposed method achieves substantial parameter reductions while maintaining accuracy comparable to that of the full model. Code to reproduce the experiments can be found at: https://github.com/LorenzoSciandra/NNS.

</details>


### [116] [Back to All-Entity Ranking: Sampler-Dependent Evaluation in Continuous-Time Dynamic Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27861)
*Minwoo Yu,Young-guk Ha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that common next-destination prediction evaluations on continuous-time dynamic graphs (CTDGs) that rely on sampled negative destinations produce rankings and ablation conclusions that critically depend on the negative-sampling scheme and candidate set size, and recommends all-entity ranking over a fixed destination catalog instead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In CTDG next-destination prediction, models are typically evaluated by ranking the true (positive) destination among a small set of sampled negative destinations. However, this practice can make evaluation scores depend on arbitrary researcher choices: the distribution from which negatives are sampled and the number of candidates used. This dependence can distort which model appears superior and how architectural modules are judged, but this effect has not been systematically characterized for CTDG benchmarks.

Method: The authors analyze how the negative sampling distribution and candidate set size affect Bayes-optimal rankings and practical evaluations. They show theoretically that non-uniform negatives alter the optimal ranking rule and that finite candidate sets drawn uniformly can destabilize model comparisons. Empirically, they run factorial evaluations distinguishing repeated vs new positives and seen vs unseen negatives, design a minimal scorer that uses only pair-history membership, and apply controlled interventions on model representations. They test six CTDG models across four datasets (LastFM, MOOC, Reddit, Wikipedia), comparing metrics obtained with a typical sampled-negative setting (e.g., Uniform-20) to metrics from all-entity ranking over the full destination catalog.

Result: The study finds that evaluation with sampled negatives is highly sensitive to the negative sampling configuration. A simple pair-history-based scorer can perform surprisingly well under certain sampling regimes due to information leakage from the sampler. Across six models and four datasets, at least one pair of models changes relative ranking between the common Uniform-20 metric and the full-catalog all-entity evaluation in three of the four datasets. Similarly, the measured effect of a given architectural module (e.g., through ablation) changes in both magnitude and direction as the candidate-set size and training objective vary.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that model superiority claims and ablation-based module evaluations made using sampled-negative benchmarks in CTDGs are conditional on the specific negative-sampling and candidate-set configuration, and thus may not be reliable or comparable across studies. To remove this source of arbitrariness and variation, they advocate using all-entity ranking over a fixed, enumerable destination catalog as the primary basis for architecture comparison on CTDG benchmarks, while keeping the original CTDG scoring function unchanged.

Abstract: Next-destination prediction in continuous-time dynamic graphs (CTDGs) commonly ranks an observed interaction against sampled negative destinations. The resulting score is conditional on both the negative distribution and the number of candidates chosen by the researcher. We show that a non-uniform negative distribution changes the Bayes-optimal ranking, while even a finite candidate set drawn uniformly can destabilize model rankings and measured module effects.
  Time-varying source-destination history membership and model operations that use this information directly transmit the sampler's influence to the evaluation score. We examine this mechanism using a factorial evaluation of repeated and new positives against seen and unseen negatives, a minimal scorer based solely on pair-history membership, and controlled representation interventions. Across six models on LastFM, MOOC, Reddit, and Wikipedia, at least one model pair changes relative order between the expected Uniform-20 metric and the full catalog on three of the four datasets. The measured effect of the same module also changes in magnitude and direction with the candidate-set size and training objective.
  These results establish that model-superiority and ablation conclusions from sampled-negative benchmarks are conditional on the stated candidate configuration. All-entity ranking evaluates every destination in a fixed catalog, eliminating negative-selection freedom and sampling variation while retaining the original CTDG scorer. We therefore recommend all-entity ranking as the primary evidence for architecture comparisons on CTDG benchmarks with an enumerable, fixed destination catalog.

</details>


### [117] [Orca: Neural Operators for Causal Reasoning in Continuous Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27867)
*Gerrit Großmann,David A. Selby,Sebastian J. Vollmer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce Orca, a causal modeling framework that uses neural operators to handle continuous-time, feedback-rich systems and enable counterfactual reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical structural causal models assume static, acyclic variables observed once, which is unrealistic for many real-world systems (patients, climate, economies) that change continuously, have feedback loops, and are observed irregularly. There is a need for a causal framework that works directly with time-evolving processes and can still support interventions and counterfactuals.

Method: They propose Orca, where each node in the causal graph is a time-dependent function and each causal mechanism is a neural operator—a learned map between function spaces. They adapt neural operator architectures so that a mechanism: (1) takes entire parent functions as input, (2) respects temporal causality (arrow of time), and (3) incorporates exogenous noise as a latent function that can be inferred and reused for counterfactual queries. They formalize this model class mathematically and instantiate it using extended neural operator designs.

Result: They show, on synthetic continuous-time datasets, that Orca can perform counterfactual reasoning: given observed trajectories, it can infer latent noise functions and then predict what would have happened under alternative interventions. The experiments demonstrate the feasibility of using neural operators as structural causal mechanisms in continuous time with feedback.

Conclusion: Neural operator learning can serve as a foundation for causal reasoning in continuous-time, feedback-driven systems. Orca operationalizes this idea by representing variables and exogenous noise as functions over time and mechanisms as neural operators, enabling counterfactual analysis beyond traditional, static structural causal models.

Abstract: Structural causal models are the standard language for reasoning about interventions and counterfactuals, but they describe static variables, typically measured once, and usually forbid cyclic dependencies. Many systems we care about, such as patients, climates, and economies, instead evolve continuously in time, are observed at irregular time points, and contain feedback loops. We argue that neural operator learning provides a natural foundation for causal reasoning in this setting, and propose Orca, a framework in which each node of the causal graph is a function of time and each mechanism is a learned map between function spaces. We extend existing neural operator architectures to express causal mechanisms: a mechanism computes the function value of a node from its parent nodes by taking several parent functions as input, respects the arrow of time, and treats latent exogenous noise as a function that can be inferred and reused for counterfactuals. We formalize the model class and demonstrate counterfactual reasoning on synthetic continuous-time examples. Code is available at https://github.com/gerritgr/orca

</details>


### [118] [Search as Computation Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27871)
*Alexander Tuisov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to optimally allocate costly internal computations (like simulations or node expansions) when they only matter through the final decision, formalizing this as a terminal computation-allocation problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many decision-making algorithms (e.g., search, bandits, simulations) use internal computations that consume time or resources before outputting a final decision, but these algorithms are usually evaluated only by the quality of that final decision. There is no unified theory of how to optimally schedule and value these internal computations across different algorithmic settings or how to relate them to information-theoretic quantities.

Method: The authors formalize internal decision-making procedures as terminal computation-allocation problems, where computations generate observations about a latent environment, update beliefs, and influence only the terminal decision loss. They derive Bellman equations for optimal allocation of computation under various resource models (fixed budget, priced computation, exact certification). They introduce and analyze the value of computation (VOC), relating it to mutual information and knowledge-gradient under different loss functions (log loss vs. simple regret). They examine how information gain can mis-rank computations while still upper-bounding VOC. They instantiate the framework on several computation topologies, including multi-armed bandits (pulls), Monte Carlo tree search (tree simulations), and graph search (node expansions). Finally, they introduce a model of frontier resolution and heuristic error and show that maximizing an approximate VOC yields weighted A* as a solution, with A* and greedy best-first search as limiting cases.

Result: The paper shows that optimal internal computation allocation can be characterized via Bellman equations in a unified framework encompassing bandits, tree search, and graph search. It proves that under log loss, myopic VOC coincides with mutual information, while under simple regret the VOC becomes a knowledge-gradient measure. It also proves that information gain can arbitrarily mis-rank computations compared to true VOC, though it always serves as a one-sided upper bound. In the explicit search model, they derive that approximately maximizing VOC naturally leads to weighted A* search, bridging heuristic search algorithms and rational metareasoning about computation choice.

Conclusion: The work provides a unified decision-theoretic and dynamic programming framework for understanding and optimizing the allocation of costly internal computations in algorithms whose performance is judged only by a terminal decision. It clarifies how value of computation relates—but is not equivalent—to information-theoretic measures, and demonstrates that common search strategies like weighted A* can be justified as approximate VOC maximizers. Rather than promoting a single optimal acquisition rule, the theory highlights a shared underlying decision problem across diverse algorithms and shows how different rules emerge as approximations under different assumptions.

Abstract: Many algorithms spend an internal resource before returning a decision and are evaluated only by the quality of that terminal output. We formalize such procedures as terminal computation-allocation problems: costly computations produce observations, update beliefs about a latent environment, and matter only through terminal decision loss. Bellman equations characterize optimal allocation under fixed budgets, priced computation, and exact certification. We then relate value of computation (VOC) to information. Mutual information equals myopic VOC under log loss, whereas under simple regret VOC is a knowledge-gradient quantity; moreover, information gain can rank computations arbitrarily poorly, although it gives a one-sided upper bound on VOC. Bandit pulls, tree simulations, and node expansions illustrate the same model under different computation topologies. Finally, under an explicit frontier-resolution and heuristic-error model, maximizing approximate VOC recovers weighted A*, with A* and greedy best-first search as limiting cases. The theory identifies a shared decision problem without asserting that one acquisition rule is universally optimal.

</details>


### [119] [An Empirical Study of Coordination Mode as the First-Class Citizen in From-Scratch Multi-Agent Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27877)
*Yanyu Ren,Yunfeng Bai,Xizheng Wang,Li Chen,Dan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MSEval, a realistic multi-agent software engineering benchmark that evaluates how different agent collaboration topologies affect speed, cost, and quality on real-world coding projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent coding benchmarks mostly use synthetic tasks, overlook real time and monetary costs, mix up pure reasoning ability with communication patterns, and focus on whether something is merely completed instead of how well it is done. The authors want a rigorous, realistic way to measure how multi-agent AI teams actually build software in practice, capturing organizational structure effects and practical trade-offs.

Method: They design MSEval, a benchmark built on 10 real, full-stack software projects from diverse domains. They define hierarchical requirements and deterministic scoring rubrics. They implement an execution engine called LegoGent that runs 10 different collaboration topologies, where multiple agents coordinate at fixed sync intervals and deploy via real CI/CD pipelines. An automated grading agent, TAgent, dynamically tests implementations, collecting metrics on functional success, latency, and token cost (with prefix caching). They run 100 experiments varying organizational topology while controlling for task and model.

Result: The experiments show that the way agents are organized (their collaboration topology) can influence performance as much as, or more than, the underlying model quality. For the same tasks and models, changing the topology can alter scores by more than 30 points and can double wall-clock completion time. Structured, pipeline-like topologies converge faster and yield higher-quality solutions, while topologies with heavy managerial oversight perform worse on all key metrics.

Conclusion: MSEval provides a rigorous, reproducible benchmark to evaluate multi-agent coding systems on realistic software projects, making it possible to systematically study the trade-offs among speed, cost, and quality. The findings highlight that organizational design of agent teams is a crucial factor, sometimes as important as model capability, and that pipeline-style structures are generally superior to manager-heavy setups. The benchmark and tooling are publicly released for the community to use and extend.

Abstract: Multi-agent vibe coding promises to accelerate software development, yet existing benchmarks rely on synthetic environments that ignore practical time and monetary costs, conflate reasoning with communication, and reward only superficial completion. We introduce multi-agent from-scratch evaluation benchmark, MSEval, evaluating multi-agent coding on real-world tasks. Grounded in 10 authentic, full-stack projects across 10 domains, MSEval scores performance using hierarchical requirements and deterministic rubrics.
  Its execution engine, LegoGent, tests 10 collaboration topologies where agents coordinate via periodic sync intervals and deploy through native CI/CD pipelines. Concurrently, the automated grader TAgent dynamically probes implementations to jointly measure functional success, latency, and prefix-cached token cost. Across 100 runs, MSEval reveals that organizational topology rivals model capability in shaping the speed--cost--quality trade-off. For identical tasks and models, varying the topology shifts scores by over 30 points and doubles wall-clock time. Structured pipelines converge fastest with the highest quality, whereas heavy managerial oversight degrades performance. Ultimately, MSEval establishes a rigorous, reproducible standard for measuring how multi-agent teams actually build software. The benchmark is released at https://github.com/robinren03/MSEval.

</details>


### [120] [Not All Tokens Deserve Equal Credit: Counterfactual Sensitivity Credit Reallocation for Long-CoT Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27888)
*Qiangqiang He,Zhongheng Wu,ZiJian Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes why current RL with verifiable rewards approaches, especially on-policy self-distillation, misattribute credit across tokens in long chain-of-thought reasoning, and proposes a simple modification to GRPO that reallocates credit away from counterfactually sensitive but uninformative tokens, improving mathematical reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR methods for improving long chain-of-thought (CoT) rely either on critic-free response-level rewards broadcast uniformly across tokens (e.g., GRPO) or on on-policy self-distillation that assumes likelihood shifts from a privileged teacher are aligned with answer quality. However, these schemes ignore the fact that different tokens contribute unequally to final correctness, and it is unclear whether privilege-induced likelihood changes really point in answer-aligned directions at the token level. This can lead to inefficient or even misleading learning signals, especially in long reasoning traces.

Method: The authors empirically probe the reliability of on-policy self-distillation signals at token level. For a fixed sampled trajectory (CoT), they rescore it under two counterfactual outcome conditions: one where the verifier labels it as correct and one as incorrect. They then analyze how token log-probabilities shift under these opposing conditions, examining direction consistency, overlap of optimization signals, and which token types experience large shifts (surface-form vs. reasoning content tokens). Based on these diagnostics, they design Counterfactual Sensitivity Credit Reallocation (CSCR), an extension of GRPO that measures each token’s counterfactual sensitivity and proportionally reduces advantage (credit) assigned to highly sensitive tokens before renormalizing so that total reward and overall direction from the verifier are preserved.

Result: The analysis shows that likelihood shifts induced by outcome-privileged scoring are often not answer-aligned: most affected tokens move in the same direction when the same trajectory is labeled correct or incorrect, sign reversals are rare, and optimization signals from opposing labels heavily overlap. Large shifts tend to occur on easy-to-substitute surface-form tokens rather than on reasoning-critical tokens. Using these insights, CSCR improves upon standard GRPO across long-CoT mathematical reasoning benchmarks using the same number of policy updates. Ablation studies confirm that using the privilege-induced direction itself is unreliable, moderate downweighting of high-sensitivity tokens works best, and overly strong modulation harms optimization stability.

Conclusion: Privilege-induced token-level likelihood shifts in on-policy self-distillation do not reliably encode answer-aligned learning signals; they mostly track counterfactual sensitivity and overemphasize surface-form tokens. Treating these raw shifts as dense supervision can therefore misguide training. Instead, counterfactual sensitivity can be used as a diagnostic for how much credit a token should receive. The proposed CSCR method, which reallocates advantage away from highly sensitive tokens while preserving the verifier’s global reward signal, yields more effective and stable training for long-CoT reasoning than vanilla GRPO, as supported by benchmark improvements and targeted ablations.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is central to improving long-CoT reasoning in large language models. Critic-free methods such as GRPO convert response-level rewards into advantages and uniformly broadcast them across tokens, overlooking their unequal contributions to the final outcome. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) instead provides dense distributional supervision by minimizing the forward KL divergence between an unprivileged policy and a privileged self-teacher, implicitly assuming that the resulting likelihood shifts encode reliable answer-aligned information. We test this premise by fixing each sampled trajectory and re-scoring it under two opposing outcome conditions, one asserting correctness and the other incorrectness. Most affected tokens shift in the same direction under both conditions, with few sign reversals and substantial overlap in the induced optimization signals. Large shifts also concentrate on highly substitutable surface-form tokens, whereas tokens carrying problem-specific reasoning content are less sensitive. These findings show that privileged shifts fail to provide reliable answer-aligned directions, while their magnitudes primarily reflect counterfactual sensitivity rather than token-level learning value. Based on these observations, we propose Counterfactual Sensitivity Credit Reallocation (CSCR), a simple extension of GRPO that reduces credit for highly sensitive tokens and renormalizes token-level advantages to preserve both the original credit budget and verifier-determined direction. On long-CoT mathematical reasoning benchmarks, CSCR consistently outperforms GRPO baseline with the same number of policy updates. Targeted ablations further corroborate our diagnosis: privilege-induced directions are unreliable, moderate downweighting is most effective, and stronger modulation destabilizes optimization.

</details>


### [121] [Dynamic Spectral Filtering for Temporal Graph Learning: Learning Evolving Propagation Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27891)
*Yan Kong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Dynamic Spectral Filtering (DSF), a temporal graph learning method where the graph propagation operator itself evolves over time via a compact spectral parameterization, achieving DEFT-level accuracy with dramatically lower parameter count, memory, and runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most temporal graph learning methods focus on evolving node states or encoding interaction histories while assuming a fixed graph propagation operator. This neglects the possibility that the graph filter—the way information diffuses across the graph—may itself change over time, which could capture dynamic structural patterns in a more principled way. At the same time, existing powerful temporal models can be computationally heavy in terms of parameters and GPU memory, which is problematic for large or resource-constrained settings. The authors are motivated to design a temporal graph model that explicitly evolves the spectral propagation operator over time, while remaining highly parameter-, memory-, and time-efficient.

Method: The authors introduce Dynamic Spectral Filtering (DSF), where the propagation at each time step t is represented as a Chebyshev polynomial spectral filter whose coefficients are vector-valued and time-dependent. These coefficients form a compact multi-order representation of the propagation operator and are treated as recurrent temporal states. A recurrent update branch computes new candidate coefficients, and multiplicative gates—both global and filter-order-specific—control the magnitude and update of these coefficients over time. Because the temporal state lives in the space of filter coefficients rather than node embeddings, it is independent of the number of nodes, yielding parameter efficiency. DSF is evaluated on temporal link prediction tasks, compared especially to the DEFT baseline and other temporal GNN methods.

Result: On temporal link prediction benchmarks MOOC, Wikipedia, and Reddit, DSF achieves AP scores of 0.7851, 0.9088, and 0.9860, respectively. It uses only about 93K to 133K parameters, 68 to 182 MB peak GPU memory, and 1.6 to 2.1 seconds per training epoch. Compared to the closely related DEFT model, DSF performs better on MOOC, nearly matches performance on Reddit (within 0.001 AP), and is slightly worse on Wikipedia, while using 8.3–8.6× fewer parameters, 25–33× less GPU memory, and 5–19× less time per epoch. Across all compared methods, DSF reduces GPU memory consumption by 3.3–38.6×.

Conclusion: Evolving the spectral response of the graph propagation operator over time—rather than only evolving node states—constitutes a useful temporal inductive bias in temporal graph learning. DSF shows that such operator-centric temporal modeling can deliver competitive link prediction performance while being significantly more efficient in parameters, memory, and runtime than comparable methods like DEFT and other baselines. The approach is therefore promising for scenarios where computational efficiency is a primary concern.

Abstract: Temporal graph learning is commonly organized around the evolution of node states or the encoding of interaction histories. We study an underexplored, operator-centric question: should the graph propagation mechanism itself evolve over time? We introduce Dynamic Spectral Filtering (DSF), which represents propagation at snapshot t by a Chebyshev polynomial filter with vector-valued, time-dependent coefficients. DSF explicitly treats these compact multi-order coefficients as recurrent temporal states. A recurrent branch proposes updates, while multiplicative global and order-specific gates regulate their magnitude. The temporal state is independent of the number of nodes. On MOOC, Wikipedia, and Reddit temporal link-prediction benchmarks, converged DSF runs attain AP scores of 0.7851, 0.9088, and 0.9860, respectively, with 93K to 133K trainable parameters, 68 to 182 MB peak GPU memory, and 1.6 to 2.1 seconds of training per epoch. Against the closely related DEFT baseline, DSF is better on MOOC, within 0.001 AP on Reddit, and modestly lower on Wikipedia, while using 8.3 to 8.6 times fewer parameters, 25 to 33 times less GPU memory, and 5 to 19 times less time per epoch. Relative to all measured alternatives, it uses 3.3 to 38.6 times less GPU memory. These results support direct spectral-response evolution as a useful temporal inductive bias when computational efficiency is a first-class requirement.

</details>


### [122] [MMHBench: A Multi-Perspective Benchmark for Mental Health Understanding in Long-Form Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27895)
*Jinpeng Hu,Erqiang Wang,Shan Wang,Zhuo Li,Peipei Song,Xun Yang,Meng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a new multimodal benchmark (MMHBench) and a question-generation framework to rigorously test how well models understand mental health in long-form videos, and show current models struggle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mental-health-related video benchmarks reduce understanding to coarse labels (e.g., diagnosis or sentiment), making it unclear whether models actually reason about psychological states versus exploiting superficial patterns. There is a need for a finer-grained, multi-perspective benchmark that directly probes nuanced reasoning about behavior, context, and latent mental states, especially in long-form, multimodal settings.

Method: They construct MMHBench, a benchmark of 268 long-form videos with 2,184 curated questions divided into two settings: (1) third-person assessment questions that ask about observable behaviors and multimodal evidence; and (2) first-person perspective-taking questions that require reasoning conditioned on a character’s perspective to infer plausible mental states from evidence. To create high-quality, diverse questions, they design a Multi-Agent Question Generation (MAQG) framework where multiple simulated social roles (agents) propose questions from different perspectives. These questions are iteratively refined through feedback among the agents and then further filtered and corrected via expert-guided verification. They then evaluate 22 multimodal large language models (both open- and closed-source) on MMHBench to measure their capabilities and limitations.

Result: MMHBench is successfully built with a substantial number of long-form videos and carefully checked questions, structured into third-person and first-person reasoning tasks. Using MMHBench, the authors evaluate 22 representative MLLMs and find that performance across models is generally low, showing large gaps between current capabilities and the kind of nuanced mental health understanding the benchmark demands.

Conclusion: Long-form video-based mental health understanding is still a difficult open problem for current multimodal LLMs. MMHBench provides a more rigorous, fine-grained, and multi-perspective benchmark than prior work, and the MAQG framework offers a scalable way to generate high-quality, perspective-rich questions. The authors position MMHBench as a challenging testbed to spur future research on deeper multimodal psychological reasoning in AI systems.

Abstract: Mental health understanding in long-form videos requires nuanced reasoning over observable behavior, interpersonal context, and latent psychological states. Existing benchmarks largely reduce this task to coarse-grained classification, providing limited insight into whether models truly understand psychological phenomena or rely on superficial correlations. To address this limitation, we introduce MMHBench, a comprehensive multimodal benchmark for multi-perspective mental health understanding, comprising 268 long-form videos and 2,184 carefully curated questions. MMHBench organizes the evaluation into two complementary settings: (1) third-person assessment, consisting of 605 questions that focus on the interpretation of observable behaviors and multimodal evidence, and (2) first-person perspective-taking, comprising 1,579 questions that require perspective-conditioned reasoning to identify the interpretation of the mental state supported by the available multimodal evidence. We propose a Multi-Agent Question Generation (MAQG) framework that simulates diverse social roles to synthesize questions from multiple perspectives. The generated questions are refined through multi-role feedback and iterative optimization, followed by expert-guided verification to ensure quality and validity. Extensive evaluation of 22 representative multimodal large language models (MLLMs), spanning both open-source and leading closed-source models, demonstrates that long-form video mental health understanding remains highly challenging.

</details>


### [123] [A Cross-Architecture Audit of Direction-Based Inference-Time Defences in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27910)
*Xiangyu Yin,Tora Bodin,Rohan Menon,Chih-Hong Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically compares several inference-time, direction-based defences against jailbreaks in vision-language models, finding that no single method dominates across models, that an image-conditioning direction is often best for balancing safety and utility but is architecture-specific, and that refusal-related directions in text-only and multimodal settings are partially aligned, implying defences must be per-family calibrated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direction-based interventions in the residual stream are a popular practical defence against jailbreak prompts in vision-language models, but it is unclear which directions work best, how robust they are across architectures and layers, and how they relate to known refusal directions from text-only models. This paper aims to systematically compare leading candidates under controlled conditions and to study the geometric relationship between refusal-related directions in text and multimodal models.

Method: The authors evaluate five defence candidates that subtract a calibrated vector from the residual stream at a chosen decoder layer: (1) a mean image-conditioning shift, (2) a CMRM-style refusal direction, (3) a ShiftDC-style attack-specific residual, (4) a prompt-based instruction to ignore the image, and (5) a random control direction. They test these across 15 model×layer settings spanning four VLM families, using a magnitude-controlled protocol that matches intervention norms per prompt and pairs each direction with a random control of equal norm. They then measure refusal recovery, utility preservation, direction specificity/transfer, and geometric alignment (cosine similarity) between directions, with statistical testing (e.g., sign test).

Result: No defence consistently dominates on both improving refusal behavior and preserving normal-task utility. The mean image-conditioning shift performs best overall on LLaVA 1.5 and Pixtral 12B, uniquely maintaining utility loss at the noise floor in all model families tested. A prompt instruction to ignore the image is best on Qwen2.5-VL, while an attack-specific residual direction performs best on Qwen2-VL 2B. The image-conditioning direction is specific to each model-layer cell in 13/15 cases and does not transfer well even between dimension-compatible architectures like LLaVA 1.5 13B and Pixtral 12B. Geometrically, the CMRM refusal direction has consistently positive cosine similarity with the image-conditioning shift across all cells (mean ~0.35, range 0.17–0.65), far above random baselines (15–25×), with a sign-test p≈3e−5, indicating meaningful overlap between these directions.

Conclusion: Inference-time, direction-based defences against VLM jailbreaks are effective but highly model- and architecture-specific; no universal direction works best everywhere. Image-conditioning based shifts often offer the best trade-off between safety and utility, but must be tuned per decoder family and layer. The observed positive alignment between multimodal image-conditioning directions and text-only CMRM refusal directions suggests that different recipes capture overlapping refusal geometry, reinforcing that safety interventions should be calibrated separately for each model family rather than assumed to be broadly transferable.

Abstract: Inference time defences against vision language model jailbreaks often subtract a calibrated direction from the residual stream at a chosen decoder layer. We compare five defence candidates across 15 model and layer cells from four architectural families under a magnitude controlled protocol that matches the intervention size for each prompt and pairs every direction with a random control of the same norm. The candidates are the mean image conditioning shift, a CMRM style refusal direction, a ShiftDC style attack specific residual, a prompt instruction to ignore the image, and a random control. No single candidate dominates on both refusal recovery and utility preservation. The image conditioning shift leads on LLaVA 1.5 and Pixtral 12B and is the only candidate whose utility loss remains at the measurement noise floor in every family. The prompt instruction leads on Qwen2.5 VL, while the attack specific residual leads on Qwen2 VL 2B. The image conditioning direction is direction specific in 13 of 15 cells, but strongly architecture specific and nontransferable across the only dimension compatible pair, LLaVA 1.5 13B and Pixtral 12B. We also connect text only and multimodal refusal geometry. The CMRM direction has positive cosine alignment with the image conditioning shift in all 15 cells, with mean 0.35, range 0.17 to 0.65, 15 to 25 times the random vector null, and a sign test p value of about 3e-5. These results show that the two recipes recover partially overlapping geometry and that direction based defences should be calibrated separately for each language decoder family.

</details>


### [124] [IFHierBench: Hierarchical Instruction Following for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27912)
*Yuetian Mao,Chunyang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces IFHierBench, a benchmark to evaluate how well large language models follow hierarchical, nested instructions with constraints; results show that even top models struggle, especially as constraint depth increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world LLM applications increasingly require models to produce complex, structured outputs where different sections and nested fields must satisfy distinct, precise constraints. Existing instruction-following benchmarks only handle flat, global constraint lists and cannot test whether models correctly apply constraints scoped to specific parts of a hierarchical output. There is a need to systematically measure and understand models’ ability to follow such layered, nested instructions.

Method: The authors construct IFHierBench, a benchmark of 600 prompts that require hierarchical, layered outputs. Prompts are stratified across four levels of constraint-tree depth and use 35 distinct types of constraints. For each prompt, they design a deterministic checker that programmatically verifies whether constraints are satisfied at every level and scope of the output hierarchy. They then evaluate seven state-of-the-art proprietary and open-weight LLMs on this benchmark, computing prompt-level accuracy and analyzing performance as a function of constraint complexity and depth.

Result: Across the seven evaluated models, none achieve strong performance: even the best-performing model surpasses 50% prompt-level accuracy only slightly. Performance declines notably as the depth of the constraint tree increases, revealing specific difficulty in reliably honoring nested and scoped constraints within hierarchical outputs.

Conclusion: Current LLMs are significantly limited in their ability to follow hierarchical, nested instructions with scoped constraints, particularly as structural depth grows. This exposes a key gap between existing models and the needs of real-world applications that rely on complex, constrained outputs. The authors argue that future training and alignment methods should explicitly target fine-grained constraint adherence across different levels of an output’s hierarchy to improve instruction-following capabilities.

Abstract: Instruction-following ability is critical for deploying large language models in real-world applications, where downstream components depend on the output satisfying specific constraints. Modern deployments increasingly handle the full task in a single LLM call, with one prompt specifying a layered output whose overall artifact, structural sections, and nested fields must each satisfy concrete constraints. Existing instruction-following benchmarks treat the constraint set as a flat list applied uniformly to the response, so they cannot scope a check to a particular section of the output. We introduce IFHierBench, a hierarchical instruction-following benchmark of 600 prompts stratified across four constraint-tree depths and 35 distinct constraints, each prompt paired with a deterministic checker that verifies satisfaction at every scope. Evaluating seven leading proprietary and open-weight models, we find that even the strongest model only marginally exceeds 50% prompt-level accuracy and that accuracy degrades sharply as constraint depth grows. Reliably following nested constraints remains a substantial gap for current LLMs, motivating future training methods that consider constraint adherence at finer granularity to achieve better instruction-following ability.

</details>


### [125] [One Anchor for All: Unified Multilingual and Multimodal Safety Alignment for LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27917)
*Enyi Shi,Fei Shen,Chuancheng Shi,Linxia Zhu,Shuyi Miao,Jinhui Tang,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a neuron-level method to transfer safety alignment from English-only training data to multilingual, multimodal large vision-language models, by locating and editing a tiny shared set of safety-critical neurons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual, multimodal LVLMs face increasingly covert malicious attacks that mix images and instructions in many languages. Existing defenses are mostly trained monolingually or unimodally, require large safety datasets and costly full-parameter fine-tuning, and thus struggle with compound multilingual+multimodal attacks and poor scalability. The paper aims to find a more efficient, transferable, and fine-grained way to align safety across both languages and modalities.

Method: They analyze internal neuron activations of an LVLM on harmful vs. benign samples. First, within a single language and single modality, they identify "safety neurons" whose activations strongly differ between harmful and benign inputs and have large impact on model outputs (functional saliency). Next, within each language, they intersect the safety neurons found in text-only and image+text settings to obtain modality-shared safety neurons (MS-Neurons), which respond to safety risks in both visual and textual inputs. Then, using English as a pivot, they intersect MS-Neurons across multiple languages to find neurons that are both modality- and language-shared (MLS-Neurons). Finally, they fine-tune only this tiny set of MLS-Neurons (about 0.03% of all parameters), using English safety supervision, and evaluate the resulting model on multilingual and multimodal safety benchmarks.

Result: The proposed MLS-Neuron framework yields substantial gains in safety performance on a variety of multilingual and multimodal benchmarks, compared with prior state-of-the-art safety tuning approaches. Despite updating only around 0.03% of parameters, the method improves resistance to compound attacks that mix languages and images, while maintaining or minimally affecting the model’s general capabilities on non-safety tasks.

Conclusion: Neuron-level cross-dimensional alignment via MLS-Neurons is an effective and highly parameter-efficient strategy for safety-aligning large vision-language models. By locating a small set of neurons that are jointly responsible for safety behavior across languages and modalities and fine-tuning only them with English safety data, one can robustly enhance multilingual and multimodal safety without sacrificing general utility or incurring high fine-tuning costs.

Abstract: As large vision-language models (LVLMs) are deployed globally, the combination of multilingual instructions and visual information makes malicious attacks more covert and sophisticated than ever before. However, existing methods isolate language and modality defenses, which, coupled with the scarcity of safety data and high fine-tuning costs, makes it difficult for models to defend against compound attacks. To address this severe challenge, we propose a neuron-level cross-dimensional safety alignment framework driven by modality- and language-shared safety neurons (MLS-Neurons). First, we identify monolingual and unimodal safety neurons by comparing responses to harmful and benign samples, quantifying functional saliency through activation strength and downstream impact. Then, by intersecting these unimodal neurons within each language, we extract modality-shared safety neurons (MS-Neurons) responsive to both visual and textual risks, bridging the safety representation gap between modalities. Furthermore, using English as a semantic anchor, we intersect MS-Neurons across languages to identify modality- and language-shared safety neurons (MLS-Neurons), serving as key defenses against compound attacks. Finally, we update only this minimal subset of shared neurons (~0.03% of parameters), transferring English-only safety supervision to multilingual and multimodal scenarios. Extensive experiments show that our method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches across diverse multilingual and multimodal safety benchmarks while preserving general utility.

</details>


### [126] [Meta-Task: Turning Terminal Task Synthesis into a Terminal Task for Scalable Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27929)
*Zhihong Pan,Jiyuan He,Kai Zhang,Yupeng Han,Ze Liu,Yuze Zhao,Yongcong Ye,Zhaohua Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They present Meta-Task, a framework that treats the creation of terminal tasks as a terminal task itself, using an agent in a real container to iteratively generate, execute, and verify diverse, executable tasks for training terminal agents, yielding better performance with fewer trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training robust terminal agents needs many diverse, executable, and verified terminal tasks plus high-quality trajectories, but current synthetic data methods often produce unreliable tasks (never actually executed) and are constrained by existing code repositories, limiting diversity and scalability.

Method: Redefine terminal-task synthesis as a Terminal-Bench-format task: an agent runs in a real container, iteratively generating, executing, and verifying tasks so that executability and internal consistency are enforced in-loop. Decompose task requirements along multiple dimensions, use a multi-phase pipeline to first design high-level novel task specs then concretize them into tasks, optionally pull in external materials (e.g., files, repos) for realism and variety, and apply an LLM-as-judge filter at the end to keep only high-quality trajectories.

Result: Using only 3,221 synthesized trajectories from Meta-Task to fine-tune Qwen3-14B and Qwen3-32B on Terminal-Bench 2.0 yields Avg Pass@1 scores of 22.5% and 31.8%, respectively, which surpass competing approaches that rely on substantially larger training datasets.

Conclusion: Meta-Task can reliably and scalably synthesize diverse, executable terminal tasks and trajectories by tightly coupling generation with real execution and verification, leading to more data-efficient training of terminal agents and outperforming state-of-the-art baselines on Terminal-Bench 2.0 with far less data.

Abstract: Training terminal agents at scale requires diverse, verifiable terminal tasks and high-quality interaction trajectories, yet acquiring such data remains a significant challenge. Existing synthesis methods face two key limitations: (1) weak reliability caused by the disconnect between task generation and real execution, and (2) limited diversity and scalability due to dependence on existing repositories. We propose Meta-Task, a framework that redefines terminal task synthesis as a Terminal-Bench-format task itself: an agent operates within a real container environment to iteratively generate, execute, and verify tasks, so that synthesized components are checked for internal consistency and executability within the generation loop itself. Building upon this, we decouple the target task requirements along multiple dimensions, introduce a multi-phase mechanism that dynamically designs novel task specifications before producing the actual tasks, and incorporate optional external material support to enhance diversity and realism. We additionally apply LLM-as-Judge filtering to ensure the quality of the final training data. Experiments on Terminal-Bench 2.0 show that fine-tuning on only 3,221 Meta-Task synthesized trajectories achieves 22.5% and 31.8% Avg Pass@1 for Qwen3-14B and Qwen3-32B respectively, outperforming concurrent approaches with significantly less training data.

</details>


### [127] [The Geometric Nature and a Free Proxy for Flow-Matching Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27933)
*Ziyang Rao,Yiren Zhao,Weiyu Guo,Ben Fei,Yandong Guo,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a cheap, training‑free way to estimate uncertainty for flow-matching action policies by analyzing the geometry of their denoising trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow matching is popular for action generation in embodied control, but as a conditional generative model it can be confidently wrong, especially under misinterpretation or OOD inputs. Existing uncertainty estimation methods for real-time control need extra training, are computationally heavy, and don't generalize well. There is a need for a reliable, cheap, and general uncertainty signal to know when to trust FM-generated actions for safe deployment.

Method: They provide a geometric interpretation of uncertainty in flow-matching velocity fields, arguing that ideal low-uncertainty behavior resembles an affine-isotropic contraction field. Deviation from this ideal geometry is used as a signature of uncertainty. Based on this, they define "denoising acceleration" (accel), which measures the bending/curvature of the denoising trajectory. accel is computed from a single forward pass of the FM model, requiring no extra training, resampling, or additional evaluations. They give theoretical analysis showing that accel correlates with FM uncertainty and design experiments to measure this correlation and test accel for online failure detection during rollouts.

Result: They show both theoretically and empirically that accel is a faithful proxy for FM uncertainty. In experiments on online failure detection for real-time control, accel can detect failing rollouts well before episode termination. Under realistic deployment budgets, it matches or outperforms baseline uncertainty methods that rely on costly resampling or additional training, across various settings.

Conclusion: Geometric properties of the FM velocity field, especially the curvature of denoising trajectories, provide a powerful and practically useful signal for uncertainty. The proposed denoising acceleration metric offers a general, training- and compute-free uncertainty proxy for flow-matching action heads, enabling early failure detection and safer deployment, while competing with or surpassing existing, more expensive methods.

Abstract: Flow matching (FM) has become a popular action head paradigm for modern embodied models. However, as a conditional generative model, it does not explicitly expose its inherent uncertainty, producing faulty action chunks even when it misinterprets the scene or encounters out-of-distribution (OOD) inputs. Therefore, determining when an FM-generated action can be trusted is essential for safe deployment, yet existing uncertainty estimation methods on real-time control suffer from several issues: extra training budget, high computational overhead, and low generalization ability. In this work, we provide a geometric interpretation of FM uncertainty in the velocity field, showing that uncertainty manifests as deviation from an ideal affine-isotropic contraction field. Building on this observation, we introduce denoising acceleration ($\mathrm{accel}$), a highly-generalizable and cost-free uncertainty proxy that measures the bending of the denoising trajectory from a single forward pass, without additional model evaluations, training, or resampling. We theoretically and empirically demonstrate that $\mathrm{accel}$ is a faithful proxy for FM uncertainty and further test its utility in online failure detection. Results show that $\mathrm{accel}$ identifies failing rollouts well before termination, matching or even outperforming costly resampling- and training-based baselines across settings under realistic deployment budget. Code and demos available at: https://github.com/rrrrrrzy/fm-geometry.

</details>


### [128] [Shapes from Examples: Foundations of Shape Learning in Recursive SHACL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27934)
*Bente Gortworst,Cem Okulmus,Magdalena Ortiz,Anni-Yasmin Turhan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to automatically learn SHACL shapes from positive and negative node examples, focusing on a fragment equivalent to the Description Logic ELI, and analyzes existence, computation, and complexity of best-fitting shapes under several semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SHACL is widely used to validate RDF and knowledge graphs, but shapes are usually handcrafted, which is costly and error-prone. Automatic learning of shapes from example data would greatly streamline schema design and data quality tasks. The fitting approach—finding a shape that accepts all positives and rejects all negatives—is a natural learning paradigm whose formal properties in realistic SHACL fragments and with recursive shape catalogs were not well understood.

Method: The authors formalize the fitting problem for SHACL shapes, restricting the target language to a core fragment corresponding to Description Logic ELI and allowing the use of recursively defined shapes via a shape catalogue. They analyze three semantics for such catalogues: well-founded, stable, and supported. For each semantics, they study (i) whether a fitting shape exists and (ii) how to compute a most specific fitting shape, deriving algorithms and matching complexity bounds. They also identify structural restrictions and special cases where the problem becomes polynomial-time solvable.

Result: The paper proves that for the considered SHACL/ELI fragment and each of the three semantics, deciding the existence of a fitting shape and computing a most specific fitting shape are solvable in exponential time, and that these upper bounds are tight (i.e., the problems are ExpTime-complete). Moreover, for practically relevant restricted forms of shape catalogues and graph structures, they derive polynomial-time algorithms, showing that the general intractability can be avoided in common cases.

Conclusion: Automatic SHACL shape learning via the fitting paradigm is theoretically feasible but computationally expensive in the general case, with ExpTime-complete complexity for existence and most specific fitting under well-founded, stable, and supported semantics. However, by restricting to certain realistic subclasses of catalogs or graphs, the tasks become polynomial-time. This clarifies the trade-offs between expressivity, recursion, and computational cost, providing guidance for designing practical shape-learning tools for knowledge graphs.

Abstract: SHACL shapes enable data graph validation, making automatic shape learning essential for knowledge graph applications. We investigate the well-known fitting approach to this task: given sets P and N of positive and negative example nodes from an input graph, compute a shape expression C, possibly using shape names defined in a recursive shape catalogue, that validates at every node in P and none in N. We focus on the case where C is written in a core fragment of SHACL corresponding to the Description Logic ELI. For the catalogue, we consider the well-founded, stable, and supported semantics. We address fitting existence and most specific fitting computation, establish tight exponential-time upper bounds for both problems, and obtain polynomial bounds for relevant special cases.

</details>


### [129] [From Scoring to Acting: Outcome-Verified Comparative Self-Distillation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27937)
*Xu Xia,Jinghua Piao,Min Yang,Xiaochong Lan,Jiaju Chen,Yong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new self-distillation method, Outcome-Verified Comparative Self-Distillation (OVCSD), to better internalize skills in LLM agents by using outcome-verified teacher guidance and comparative learning between teacher and student trajectories, achieving strong gains on ALFWorld and WebShop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents often rely on external tools or retrieval to perform well at inference, and current on-policy self-distillation approaches supervise student policies with teacher scores on student-generated actions. This has two issues: teacher preferences are not grounded in actual task outcomes, and action-level scoring fails to fully exploit the rich information in both teacher and student rollouts and their divergences. The authors want a more outcome-grounded, data-efficient way to internalize capabilities into the base model.

Method: They introduce Outcome-Verified Comparative Self-Distillation (OVCSD). First, they collect failed student trajectories and organize them into a prefix tree. From states that the student actually reaches, they invoke a skill-conditioned teacher policy to generate continuations, but keep only those continuations that are verified to lead to successful task outcomes. For each student-teacher trajectory pair that shares a prefix and then diverges, OVCSD performs localized comparative learning at the first divergence: it trains the student to prefer the teacher’s successful action over its own failed one. It then distills the teacher’s successful suffix after the divergence to transfer completion behavior. This combines outcome-verified supervision and comparative learning over trajectory pairs, rather than unguided teacher scoring of student actions.

Result: On two interactive benchmarks, ALFWorld and WebShop, and across three model scales, OVCSD consistently outperforms both skill-free reinforcement learning baselines and prior self-distillation methods. It yields up to 29.7 percentage points absolute improvement in success rate on ALFWorld and 5.4 points on WebShop compared with the strongest existing baselines, while requiring less than 3% additional privileged (teacher) interactions during training.

Conclusion: Outcome-verified, trajectory-comparative self-distillation is more effective than conventional action-scoring-based on-policy self-distillation for internalizing skills in LLM agents. By selectively invoking a teacher from student-reached states, validating teacher continuations by actual task success, and training on localized divergences and suffixes, OVCSD efficiently transfers capabilities with modest extra supervision and yields substantial performance gains on complex interactive environments.

Abstract: Recent work on LLM agents is shifting from external capability elicitation to capability internalization, enabling agents to retain useful skills without retrieval at inference time. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers a promising direction, but many existing methods typically supervise students by scoring actions along student-generated trajectories. Such supervision has two limitations: teacher preferences are not validated by environment outcomes, and action-level scores underuse information from student rollouts, teacher rollouts, and their behavioral relationship. We therefore advocate outcome-verified teacher supervision and comparative learning over teacher-student trajectories. Based on this view, we propose Outcome-Verified Comparative Self-Distillation (OVCSD). OVCSD organizes failed student rollouts into a prefix tree, adaptively invokes a skill-conditioned teacher from student-reached states, and retains only outcome-verified successful continuations. It then applies localized comparative learning at the first state-aligned divergence and distills the post-divergence teacher suffix to transfer completion behavior. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop across three model scales show that OVCSD consistently outperforms skill-free RL and existing self-distillation baselines, achieving up to 29.7 and 5.4 absolute success-rate gains over the strongest baselines on ALFWorld and WebShop, respectively, while adding less than 3% privileged interaction during training.

</details>


### [130] [MARS-RA: Rank Aggregation for Credit Assignment via Multimodal Comparisons in Embodied Multi-Agent Cooperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27967)
*Dawei Wang,Di Zhao,Xinyuan Liu,Marci Chi Ma,Xiaoyang Liu,Chengming Zhou,Gary Ushaw,Richard Davison*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MARS-RA, a framework that tackles credit assignment in cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning by reframing it as a rank aggregation problem based on pairwise agent comparisons generated by large multimodal models, enabling robust reward shaping and improved cooperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning, especially in embodied AI environments, it is hard to determine how much each agent contributes to the team reward because feedback is sparse, delayed, noisy, and the set of active agents changes over time. Existing credit assignment methods often rely on absolute performance measures that are brittle under these conditions. There is a need for a robust way to infer individual contributions that can handle noise and a dynamic population of agents while still providing useful learning signals.

Method: The authors propose MARS-RA, which replaces absolute contribution estimation with a relative, ranking-based approach. Large multimodal models are used to generate contribution-based pairwise comparisons between agents (i.e., which of two agents contributed more). These comparisons are then aggregated through a rank aggregation procedure to produce contribution scores for all agents. The resulting scores are used in potential-based reward shaping, modifying each agent’s reward to better reflect its inferred contribution. The framework is theoretically analyzed for convergence and robustness, and Shapley values are leveraged as a conceptual/interpretive reference for understanding the contribution scores.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows that under suitable conditions, the rank-aggregation-based contribution scores lead to convergent and robust learning dynamics, even when the set of agents is dynamic and observations are noisy. Empirically, on a variety of challenging cooperative tasks involving different interaction patterns and task types, MARS-RA consistently improves the coordination and performance of agents compared to baselines, indicating that the relative-credit signals effectively guide cooperation.

Conclusion: Recasting credit assignment as a rank aggregation problem over pairwise contribution comparisons provides a robust and theoretically grounded way to perform reward shaping in cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning. By leveraging large multimodal models to generate comparisons and using Shapley values as an interpretive benchmark, MARS-RA improves cooperative behavior across diverse tasks, suggesting a promising direction for tackling credit assignment in complex embodied AI settings.

Abstract: Credit assignment is a fundamental challenge in cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning, particularly in embodied AI settings characterized by limited and delayed feedback as well as dynamically changing numbers of active agents. We propose MARS-RA, a framework that reformulates credit assignment as a rank aggregation problem using contribution-based pairwise comparisons among agents generated by large multimodal models. This shift from absolute to relative estimation ensures robustness against noise and dynamic agent participation, converting comparison results into contribution scores for potential-based reward shaping. We provide theoretical justification for the convergence and robustness of the proposed framework, and show that Shapley values can be used as an interpretive reference. Experimental results on challenging tasks of different types indicate that MARS-RA can guide agents toward effective cooperation.

</details>


### [131] [Share the Judge, Learn the Deferral: Where Specialization Helps LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27984)
*Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to best incorporate domain specialization into AI evaluators, comparing specialization in model weights vs. in decision rules for when to trust judgments, and finds that sharing judgment learning while specializing deferral policies yields better accuracy–compute tradeoffs and safer deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems generate many candidate outputs that must be evaluated, but it is unclear whether evaluators should be made domain-specialized by changing their model parameters or by adjusting how and when their judgments are trusted. The authors want a practical architectural rule for building reliable, efficient evaluators in multi-domain settings.

Method: They analyze 99,952 rubric-conditioned examples to test how supplying correct or incorrect rubrics affects an evaluator’s accuracy. They then compare different specialization schemes: (1) a single shared judge vs. multiple criterion-family LoRA adapters trained on splits of the same data, and (2) initializing those adapters from scratch vs. from a shared trained judge. Separately, they evaluate specialization at the deferral/policy level by building a 0.6B-4B-8B model cascade on RewardBench 2, using learned correctness heads to route examples, and compare this to margin-based routing. They conduct locked-test evaluations, compute accuracy, audited coverage under risk constraints, normalized parameter compute, and confidence intervals across multiple train/test repartitions.

Result: Providing the correct rubric improves locked-test accuracy by 2.11 points over a response-only baseline, while using an unrelated rubric decreases it by 2.66 points. Splitting the same corpus across eight criterion-family LoRA judges reduces accuracy by 10.05 points and drops audited coverage at 5% risk from 24.44% to 5.43%. This performance drop is not reproduced by a single rank-64 adapter with equivalent total capacity, nor explained by learning rate or optimizer step differences. Initializing the family-specific adapters from a shared, trained judge recovers test accuracy to 76.85%, about 19.94 points higher than training from scratch at the same learning rate. For specialization via deferral, a 0.6B-4B-8B cascade routed by learned correctness heads on RewardBench 2 reaches 89.40% accuracy vs. 84.75% for the standalone 8B model, using only 0.415 normalized parameter compute, and passes one-sided 95% risk audits across 20 locked repartitions. Margin-based rules stay around 84.8% accuracy while using ≥0.94 compute.

Conclusion: Domain specialization is better applied to how evaluators’ judgments are used (deferral/routing policies and audited boundaries) than by fragmenting judgment learning across many small, specialized models when data is limited. Shared training of a general judge, optionally followed by adapter-based specialization initialized from that shared judge, maintains accuracy and risk performance, while rule-based routing in model cascades yields better accuracy–compute tradeoffs and passes risk audits. A practical design rule is to keep judgment learning shared until there is enough domain data to justify splitting, and to locate domain-specific adaptations at an audited interface that governs when and how model judgments are trusted.

Abstract: Agentic systems have widened the gap between producing candidate outputs and reviewing them. This paper asks a practical architectural question: should domain specialization be built into an evaluator's weights, or into the rule that decides when its judgment can be trusted? We study 99,952 public, rubric-conditioned examples. Supplying the correct rubric improves locked-test accuracy by 2.11 points over a response-only control; replacing it with an unrelated rubric costs 2.66 points. Dividing the same training corpus among eight criterion-family LoRA judges, however, loses 10.05 points and cuts audited coverage at a 5% risk target from 24.44% to 5.43%. Matching the bank's stored capacity with one rank-64 adapter does not reproduce this loss. Nor is the result explained by learning rate or optimizer steps. Initializing the family adapters from a shared, trained judge recovers test accuracy to 76.85%, 19.94 points above scratch training at the same learning rate (95% interval 18.88-21.02). The result changes when specialization governs deferral rather than judgment. On RewardBench 2, learned correctness heads route examples through a 0.6B-4B-8B cascade without changing any reward score. Across 20 locked repartitions, the cascade attains 89.40% accuracy, compared with 84.75% for 8B alone, at 0.415 normalized parameter compute. Every run passes an exact one-sided 95% risk audit; margin-based rules remain near 84.8% accuracy while using at least 0.94 compute. These results suggest a qualified design rule: share the learning of judgment until there is enough data to justify a split, and place domain-specific adaptation in an audited release boundary.

</details>


### [132] [SKIMIX: Multi-Agent Harness-Time Scaling with Skill Mixture for Dynamic Harness Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27994)
*Jia Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SKIMIX is a multi-agent framework where agents with different skills iteratively refine answers, improving open-ended math reasoning but not multiple-choice tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents often depend on large libraries of skills, but it is hard to choose the right skills, combine them effectively, and keep them up to date. The paper aims to address the challenge of managing and exploiting rich skill libraries in a scalable, automated way.

Method: The authors design SKIMIX, a multi-agent system where each agent has its own portfolio of skills. For each task, SKIMIX retrieves relevant skills using embeddings, routes work across agents using a submodular anti-dilution strategy to avoid overloading with redundant skills, and updates skill portfolios adaptively over time. Agents collaborate via iterative refinement rounds, successively improving a solution.

Result: On six reasoning benchmarks, SKIMIX’s multi-agent collaboration significantly improves performance on open-ended mathematical reasoning tasks, while yielding small or even negative performance changes on multiple-choice benchmarks. Performance does not grow steadily with more agents; instead, gains peak at a certain agent count and most benefits appear in the first refinement round.
