Conclusion: Skill-level ensembles are beneficial mainly for open-ended reasoning tasks, and their effectiveness depends on task characteristics. Increasing agent count is not always better, and early refinement brings most of the gains. These findings give practical guidance for building scalable, multi-agent systems that leverage large skill libraries effectively.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly rely on large skill libraries, but selecting, combining, and maintaining skills remains difficult. We propose SKIMIX, a multi-agent framework in which agents with different skill portfolios collaborate through iterative refinement. SKIMIX combines embedding-based skill retrieval, submodular anti-dilution routing, and adaptive skill evolution. Across six reasoning benchmarks, multi-agent collaboration substantially improves open-ended mathematical reasoning but offers limited or negative gains on multiple-choice tasks. Agent-count scaling is non-monotonic, and most improvements arise during the first refinement round. These results show that task characteristics determine whether skill-level ensembles help and provide practical guidance for scalable agent design.

</details>


### [133] [MMLDSum-LLM: Multimodal Long-Document Summarization with Visual-Alignment and Keyword-Aware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28006)
*Xianpeng Zhang,Jiahua Yang,Dongyu Chen,Lei zhang,Jian Ma,Xu guohuan,Haonan Lu,Tianhuang Su,Chuangchuang Wang,Kai Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new benchmark and training framework to improve multimodal long-document summarization, focusing on better key-information coverage and cross-modal consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs often miss key information and hallucinate across modalities when summarizing long documents because important evidence is sparse and spread over text and images. These problems arise from attention drift over long contexts and poor alignment between visual and textual information, so better evaluation resources and training methods are needed.

Method: The authors build MMLDSum-Bench, a benchmark for multimodal long-document summarization covering diverse domains, context lengths, and visual-text distributions. They then propose MMLDSum-LLM, a two-stage training framework: (1) supervised fine-tuning with two customized weighted losses—visual-alignment weighted loss and keyword-aware weighted loss—to emphasize important visual and textual cues; (2) GRPO-based reinforcement learning with a multi-objective reward that balances keyword coverage, image-text alignment, ROUGE scores, and length control.

Result: On MMLDSum-Bench, the proposed method is evaluated under a unified protocol including LLM-as-a-judge scoring, atomic-claim precision/recall, image-text alignment metrics, and ROUGE. Compared with strong closed-source and open-source multimodal models, MMLDSum-LLM achieves significantly better coverage of key information and improved cross-modal consistency.

Conclusion: A dedicated benchmark plus a tailored two-stage training pipeline substantially mitigates key-information omission and cross-modal hallucinations in multimodal long-document summarization, leading to more faithful and better-aligned summaries than existing multimodal LLMs.

Abstract: Multimodal long documents are core carriers of professional knowledge, where critical evidence is sparsely distributed across paragraphs and modalities. This easily causes key information omission and cross-modal hallucinations in summarization by multimodal LLMs. These issues stem from attention drift in long-range dependency modeling and gaps in inter-modal alignment. To address this, we introduce MMLDSum-Bench, a high-quality benchmark for multimodal long-document summarization, covering multiple domains, context-length scales, and visual-textual modality distributions. We further propose MMLDSum-LLM, a reproducible two-stage training framework that combines supervised fine-tuning with visual-alignment weighted loss and keyword-aware weighted loss, followed by GRPO with a multi-objective reward (keyword coverage, image-text alignment, ROUGE, and length control). Extensive experiments on MMLDSum-Bench, comparing against leading closed-source and open-source multimodal models under a unified evaluation protocol - including LLM-as-a-judge scoring, atomic-claim precision/recall, image-text alignment (ITA), and ROUGE - demonstrate that our approach significantly improves key-information coverage and cross-modal consistency.

</details>


### [134] [MUL-T: Decoding Spatial Cellular Architecture in Multiplexed Tissue Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28030)
*Farzaneh Seyedshahi,Kai Rakovic,Adalberto Claudio Quiros,John LeQuesne,Ke Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose MUL-T, a lightweight transformer that models tissue architecture as a masked prediction task on discrete cell tokens, capturing spatial cellular interactions efficiently and improving performance on multiple clinical prediction tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handcrafted, cohort-specific features (e.g., marker statistics, cell-type proportions) used in multiplexed imaging often do not scale, are brittle to changes in marker panels, and struggle to generalise across heterogeneous datasets. There is a need for a more flexible, scalable, and data-driven way to model both cellular phenotypes and their spatial context that can transfer across cohorts and tasks.

Method: They design MUL-T, a compact transformer that treats tissue as a sequence/set of discrete cell tokens and learns contextualised representations via a masked contextual prediction objective, similar to masked language modeling. The model learns [CLS] embeddings that summarise tissue architecture without task-specific labels, aiming to encode higher-order cell–cell interactions while keeping the architecture and training computationally lightweight.

Result: On multiple downstream tasks—tumour pattern classification at core-level, patient-level grading, PD-L1 positivity, and cross-dataset treatment response prediction—MUL-T surpasses classical feature-engineering baselines and reaches performance close to a much larger foundation Vision Transformer, while using far fewer parameters and lower training cost.

Conclusion: Modelling tissues as tokenised cells within a masked-transformer framework provides a scalable and general way to learn rich, context-aware tissue representations. MUL-T captures complex cellular interactions efficiently, generalises across diverse clinical tasks and datasets, and offers a competitive alternative to large foundation models and brittle handcrafted feature pipelines.

Abstract: Understanding tissue organisation in multiplexed imaging requires modelling both cellular phenotypes and their spatial context. Existing approaches typically rely on handcrafted features, such as marker intensity statistics or cell-type proportions, which often fail to scale or generalise across cohorts with heterogeneous marker panels. We introduce MUL-T, a lightweight transformer framework that reframes tissue architecture as a masked contextual prediction task over discrete cell tokens. By learning contextualised [CLS] embeddings without task-specific supervision, the model captures higher-order cellular interactions while remaining computationally efficient. We evaluate MUL-T on several clinically relevant downstream tasks, including core-level tumour pattern classification, patient-level grading, PD-L1 positivity prediction, and cross-dataset treatment response prediction. Across tasks, MUL-T consistently outperforms classical feature-based baselines and achieves performance comparable to a foundation ViT model, despite substantially fewer parameters and lower training cost.

</details>


### [135] [DataClawEval: A Benchmark for Data Engineering Agents in Real Industrial Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28033)
*Debin Meng,Jiaming Yang,Zefang Zong,Tengyue Xu,Haining Xie,Yang Li,Peng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DataClawEval is a benchmark for testing how well autonomous LLM agents can complete real-world, end-to-end data engineering tasks across multiple execution engines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM benchmarks mostly test narrow tasks like Text-to-SQL or simple data analysis, which do not reflect the complexity of full data engineering workflows. There is a need to rigorously assess agents on realistic, production-style, end-to-end pipelines.

Method: The authors construct DataClawEval from production-grade code written by professional enterprise data engineers, yielding 100 end-to-end tasks spanning five execution engines: PySpark, MySQL, HiveSQL, PrestoSQL/Trino, and FlinkSQL. Each task is run in an isolated, case-specific sandbox and evaluated with deterministic, rule-based scripts instead of LLM-as-a-judge. They then benchmark 16 state-of-the-art LLM-based agents on these tasks.

Result: Across the benchmark, the best-performing agent achieves an overall score of only 74.9, and performance varies significantly by execution engine. No single model performs best across all engines, indicating strong domain specialization and a lack of general, robust capability.

Conclusion: End-to-end autonomous data engineering remains an unsolved and challenging problem for current LLM-based agents. DataClawEval provides a rigorous, reproducible benchmark—complete with dataset, containerized environments, and deterministic evaluation scripts—to drive progress in this area.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) and LLM-based agents are increasingly being deployed to automate complex workflows, promising to revolutionize data management and processing. However, existing benchmarks predominantly focus on simplified Text-to-SQL translation or data analysis, leaving the critical and complex domain of end-to-end data engineering largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce DataClawEval, the first comprehensive benchmark designed specifically to evaluate the end-to-end task completion capabilities of autonomous agents in real-world data engineering scenarios. Built upon production-grade code authored by professional enterprise data engineers, it comprises 100 rigorous, end-to-end tasks spanning five execution engines: PySpark, MySQL, HiveSQL, PrestoSQL/Trino, and FlinkSQL. Rather than non-deterministic LLM-as-a-judge scoring, each task is executed within a case-specific, isolated sandbox and graded by deterministic, rule-based scripts. Evaluating 16 frontier agents exposes critical limitations: The strongest model attains only 74.9 overall, and no single model dominates, as each excels on a different engine, revealing strict domain specialization rather than omnipotent proficiency. Thus, autonomous data engineering remains a formidable, unresolved challenge. We release our dataset, containerized environments, and deterministic evaluation scripts at https://github.com/Dicemy/DataClawEval/tree/master

</details>


### [136] [SKILL-KD: Contrastive Skill Distillation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28048)
*Qiming Shi,Yibo Dou,Jiawen Zhu,Yulong Tao,Linbo Jin,Zhaolu Kang,Yunfan Zhou,Di Weng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SKILL-KD, a contrastive skill distillation framework that creates explicit, reusable textual skills (“skill patches”) by comparing student failures with teacher trajectories, then iteratively refines and consolidates these patches to robustly improve frozen LLM agents across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill-based prompting methods often encode skills as summaries or memories of past experience. This is problematic for weaker student agents: their failed trajectories lack evidence of the missing behavior, and teacher trajectories alone are too implicit for the student to turn into concrete, reusable guidance. There is a need for a principled way to extract explicit, actionable skills that bridge the gap between strong teacher agents and weaker student agents.

Method: SKILL-KD frames skills as an explicit distillation medium between a strong teacher and a weaker student agent. For each task where the student fails, the method compares the student’s failing trajectory with the teacher’s successful trajectory and extracts a textual “skill patch” that captures the actionable discrepancy. The student is then re-run using this patch to test its effectiveness. If the student still fails, the patch is iteratively refined. To avoid skill drift from many local edits, SKILL-KD maintains trace-linked edit histories for skills and applies Drift-Aware Skill Consolidation, which decides whether each new patch should add a new rule, delete or modify an existing rule, or be skipped altogether.

Result: Across five different agent benchmarks and two different student configurations, SKILL-KD consistently improves the performance of frozen student agents, outperforming baselines that rely on fixed-model adaptation strategies. This demonstrates that explicit contrastive skill distillation and careful skill consolidation can more effectively transfer capabilities from teacher to student agents.

Conclusion: Treating skills as explicit, textual distillation artifacts—rather than vague summaries of experience—enables more effective transfer of abilities from strong to weak LLM agents. By contrastively deriving skill patches from teacher–student discrepancies and managing them with drift-aware consolidation, SKILL-KD robustly improves frozen student agents across multiple benchmarks, suggesting a promising direction for scalable, practical skill-based prompting and knowledge transfer in LLM agents.

Abstract: Skill-based prompting has become a practical mechanism for improving large language model (LLM) agents, yet existing skill acquisition methods often treat skills as experience summaries, memory entries, or direct summaries of successful demonstrations. This creates a mismatch for weaker student agents: when a student fails because it lacks task knowledge or operational strategy, its failed trajectory may not contain enough evidence to infer the missing behavior, while the teacher trajectory may be too implicit to be internalized as reusable guidance. We propose SKILL-KD, a contrastive skill distillation framework that treats skills as an explicit distillation medium between agents of different capabilities. Given a student failure and the teacher trajectory on the same task, SKILL-KD distills their actionable discrepancy into a textual skill patch, evaluates the patch by re-running the student, and iteratively refines the patch when the student still fails. To prevent repeated local updates from causing skill drift, SKILL-KD further maintains trace-linked edit histories and performs Drift-Aware Skill Consolidation, deciding whether each patch should add a new rule, delete or modify an existing rule, or be skipped. Across five agent benchmarks and two student settings, SKILL-KD consistently improves frozen student agents over fixed-model adaptation baselines.

</details>


### [137] [IndustryForge-27B: A Domain-Enhanced Multimodal Foundation Model for Industrial CAD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28050)
*Nianchen Deng,Jiaxin Ai,Tao Hu,Shu Zou,Yurui Dong,Siqi Li,Xinyu Cai,Xuemeng Yang,Licheng Wen,Hongbin Zhou,Hairong Zhang,Pinlong Cai,Botian Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build IndustryForge-27B, a 27B-parameter multimodal model specialized for industrial CAD, that significantly outperforms its base and a strong closed model on CAD tasks while preserving general ability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial CAD automation needs models that can understand engineering drawings and 3D geometry, write correct parametric CAD code and COM API code, and operate from single parts up to assemblies. Existing general multimodal models are too weak on these specialized tasks, and single-task fine-tuning is too narrow to support the diverse needs of higher-level industrial agents.

Method: Starting from Qwen3.5-VL-27B, they curate and integrate six CAD-focused multimodal datasets (~52k samples): CAD visual question answering (CAD-VQA), parametric CAD code generation for single parts (text2cadquery), assembly-level CAD code (text2cadquery-assembly), and three Windows COM programming datasets for Inventor/SolidWorks (com_2d, com_3d, com_assembly). They then apply a unified multi-task supervised fine-tuning (SFT) procedure over all these sub-corpora.

Result: On four CAD-domain benchmarks, IndustryForge-27B improves average performance over the base model by +33.65 percentage points and beats a strong closed-source baseline (GPT-5.4) on all four benchmarks. On eleven general capability benchmarks, it slightly improves over the base model with a +1.56 point mean gain and shows no catastrophic forgetting.

Conclusion: A single 27B multimodal model, IndustryForge-27B, can serve as a unified foundation for industrial CAD and related software-operation tasks, supporting everything from part-level to assembly-level design, script and API code generation, and both one-shot and iterative, closed-loop workflows, while retaining broad general capabilities.

Abstract: Automating industrial CAD design and manufacturing places distinctive demands on multimodal foundation models: the model must see engineering drawings and 3D geometry screenshots, write correct parametric-modelling scripts and Windows COM API code, and cover the full range from single parts to assemblies. General-purpose multimodal models fall short on these tasks, while single-task fine-tuning is too narrow to support the diverse calls that upper-layer agents issue. We build IndustryForge-27B on top of Qwen3.5-VL-27B by curating and integrating six industrial-CAD sub-corpora totalling $\sim$52k multimodal samples---CAD Visual QA (CAD-VQA), parametric CAD code (text2cadquery), assembly-level CAD code (text2cadquery-assembly), and three COM sub-corpora for Inventor / SolidWorks (com_2d / com_3d / com_assembly)---and training with a unified multi-task SFT recipe. Across four CAD-domain benchmarks IndustryForge-27B lifts the base model by $+33.65$~pp on average and outperforms the strong closed-source model GPT-5.4 on all four; across eleven general-capability benchmarks it retains, and slightly improves upon, the base model ($+1.56$~pp mean, no catastrophic forgetting). IndustryForge-27B will serve as the common substrate for downstream industrial-agent projects, providing a unified starting point for a full-stack industrial agent that spans from CAD design to industrial-software operation, from parts to assemblies, and from single-shot generation to closed-loop self-improvement.

</details>


### [138] [SemPIC: Learning Semantic Position-Independent KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28069)
*Hui Xie,Peng Xiao,Yutong Deng\textsuperscript,Shuoran Dou,Jian Yang,Jinyang Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SemPIC improves the reliability and quality of position‑independent KV caching for long‑context LMs by learning better document KV representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context caching methods either depend on strict prefix reuse or produce unreliable position‑independent KV states, leading to degraded attention and task performance when documents are reused under varying contexts and instructions.

Method: The authors analyze attention deviations in PIC and a boundary-conditioned baseline, then propose SemPIC: a LoRA-enabled Writer network that, via behavioral distillation, learns to generate per-layer document KV caches while the original decoder acts as an unchanged Reader. Training is done offline during cache construction, and they add KV Gradient Checkpointing to lower memory use while keeping gradients flowing through cached KVs.

Result: On three language models and four tasks, SemPIC improves mean micro-F1 from 0.53 (KV Packet baseline) to 0.60, narrowing the gap toward Full Recompute at 0.62.

Conclusion: Learning document-level KV representations with SemPIC significantly enhances position-independent caching for long-context retrieval and agentic workloads, approaching full recomputation quality while maintaining compatibility with standard KV interfaces and cache-hit decoding paths.

Abstract: Long-context retrieval and agentic workloads repeatedly reuse the same documents under changing instructions, histories, and document orders. Prefix caching cannot exploit this reuse, while position-independent caching (PIC) remains unreliable because independently compiled KV states lack the future context in which they will be consumed. Our diagnostics show that a learned boundary-conditioned baseline sharply reduces attention deviation near reusable-block boundaries but leaves interior and task-level residuals, motivating adaptation of the document representation itself. We present \emph{SemPIC}, which trains a LoRA-enabled Writer to compile native per-layer document KVs through behavioral distillation while retaining the pretrained decoder as an unchanged Reader. Adaptation is confined to offline cache construction, preserving the standard KV interface and cache-hit decoding path. We further introduce KV Gradient Checkpointing, which reduces peak training memory without severing gradients through cached KVs. Across three models and four tasks, SemPIC raises mean micro-F1 over KV Packet from 0.53 to 0.60, approaching Full Recompute at 0.62.

</details>


### [139] [Echoverse: Deep, Evolving Environments for Training Computer-Use Agents at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28074)
*Yash Pandya,Sahil Gupta,Sarthak Harne,Archana Yadav,Kavyansh Chourasia,Hussein Mozannar,Vibhav Vineet,Sara Abdali,Corby Rosset,Yash Lara,Ahmed Awadallah,Ece Kamar,Akshay Nambi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Echoverse is a framework of co-evolving, stateful synthetic applications for training and evaluating computer-use agents, showing that deep, targeted, and continuously repaired environments substantially improve smaller models toward frontier performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computer-use agents need to learn from real interactions with applications, but the important apps are login-gated and stateful, so they cannot easily be used at scale for training. Existing synthetic environments can be generated in bulk, but the main bottleneck is now their quality and structure rather than quantity. The authors aim to understand what environment properties most improve agent performance and to provide a system that can automatically create, evaluate, and refine such environments along with the model.

Method: The authors introduce Echoverse, which compiles formal task specifications into stateful applications backed by databases. Each environment includes tasks whose success is graded by grounded verifiers tied to the application state. They run a co-evolution loop where every rollout is used in two ways: (1) to repair and refine the environment, including its tasks and verifiers, based on observed failures and issues; and (2) to provide training data for the model. They train a 9B-parameter model across 12 such environments and systematically vary environment depth, task targeting, and co-evolution to isolate their effects. They also treat the same environments as RL environments with rewards combining the grounded verifier and a dense per-step judge.

Result: A 9B model trained on twelve Echoverse environments improves from 36.5% to 67.1% across fourteen evaluation splits, ending within 14 percentage points of the much larger teacher model. Environments with shallow behavioral depth reduce live-site performance (80.0% to 75.0%), while deep environments increase it substantially (80.0% to 85.0% and 48.0% to 65.0%). Focused practice on a single interface control across varied renderings generalizes to unseen widget families and to real web tasks. Repairing just one environment in the co-evolution loop boosts the model’s performance on that environment from 16.2% to 38.5%. Using the worlds as RL environments with a reward composed of grounded verification plus dense step-wise judging further raises held-out scores from 58.8% to 68.0%.

Conclusion: High-quality, stateful synthetic environments that are behaviorally deep, targeted to real failure modes, and co-evolved with the model provide substantial gains for computer-use agents, enabling smaller models to approach the performance of much larger ones. Echoverse operationalizes this through compiled applications with grounded grading and a dual-use rollout loop for both environment repair and model training. The same environments are useful for supervised training and RL, and the authors release several environments, including applications, seed data, and verifiers, as a benchmark for future work.

Abstract: Computer-use agents learn from what their actions change, so training one needs applications it can act on, break and reset. The applications that matter most are login-gated and stateful, so synthetic environments stand in for them. Recent pipelines generate such environments in bulk, which moves the bottleneck from how many exist to what is inside each one. The returns, we find, come from three properties: how much behavioural depth an environment carries, whether it targets the interaction an agent actually fails, and whether it improves alongside the model. We present Echoverse, which compiles specifications into stateful applications whose tasks are graded against the application's own database, and a co-evolution loop that reads every graded rollout twice: as repairs to the environment, its tasks and its verifier, and as training signal for the model. Trained on twelve such environments, a 9B model improves from $36.5\%$ to $67.1\%$ across fourteen evaluation splits, within fourteen points of the much larger frontier model that taught it. We examine each property in turn. On the same domains, shallow environments push live-site accuracy below the base model ($80.0 \to 75.0$) while deep ones raise it ($80.0 \to 85.0$ and $48.0 \to 65.0$); drilling one interface control across many renderings transfers to held-out widget families and to the open web; and repairing a single environment lifts the model trained on it from $16.2\%$ to $38.5\%$. The same worlds serve as reinforcement-learning environments, where a reward combining the grounded verifier with a dense per-step judge raises held-out score from $58.8\%$ to $68.0\%$. We release four environments as a benchmark, with their applications, seed data and grounded graders. Code: https://aka.ms/echoverse

</details>


### [140] [Group-Reflective Self-Distillation for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28076)
*Binbin Zheng,Zijun Xie,Guanqun Zhao,Enlei Gong,Xing Ma,Xiaoliang Fu,Zeyu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose Group-Reflective Self-Distillation (GRSD), a method that uses the model’s own verified trajectories to generate fine-grained, capability-aligned guidance that improves reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for language model agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR), rewards are usually terminal and trajectory-level, so they do not distinguish which specific behaviors in a trajectory were good, bad, or incidental. Existing self-distillation approaches add natural-language skills, but these are often extracted by stronger models from single trajectories or from external sources, leading to guidance that may not align with the current policy’s capabilities or may be overly specific to particular paths. There is a need for a way to derive richer, fine-grained, and policy-aligned supervision directly from the agent’s own experience while still leveraging verifiable rewards.

Method: They introduce Group-Reflective Self-Distillation (GRSD). For each prompt, the current policy generates a group of trajectories and a verifier labels them as successful or failed. The policy then ‘reflects’ in natural language on each verified trajectory (on-policy reflections). A stop-gradient snapshot of the policy is used to contrast reflections from successful versus failed trajectories to build group-level, privileged guidance that highlights patterns associated with success or failure, while staying aligned with the policy’s current capabilities. A self-teacher model then conditions on this group-level guidance to refine turn-level credit assignment: it modulates the outcome-based advantages for actions within trajectories, sharpening which steps get more or less credit, but it keeps the overall learning direction set by the verifier labels (success vs failure). This guidance is then used to update the policy.

Result: Across multiple agentic environments and various model sizes, GRSD leads to better performance than strong baselines in RLVR: it improves learning efficiency and final task success. Additionally, policies trained with GRSD generalize better to unseen tasks compared to those trained with prior agentic self-distillation or RLVR methods.

Conclusion: Using the agent’s own verified rollouts to create group-level reflective guidance enables more precise and capability-matched supervision in RLVR. GRSD disentangles successful behaviors from recurring mistakes within trajectory-level signals, improves credit assignment at the turn level while respecting verifier feedback, and yields more robust, generalizable language model agents than existing self-distillation and RLVR approaches.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is effective for training large language model agents. However, terminal rewards provide only coarse trajectory-level supervision, leaving successful behaviors, recurring mistakes, and incidental choices entangled in the same outcome signal. Existing agentic self-distillation methods enrich sparse supervision with natural-language skills, but skills retrieved externally or extracted from a single trajectory by stronger models may mismatch current experience, exceed the policy's capability, or remain path-specific. We propose Group-Reflective Self-Distillation (GRSD), which derives capability-aligned and outcome-discriminative guidance from the policy's own verified rollouts. For each prompt, the policy reflects on each verified trajectory in an on-policy group, and a stop-gradient snapshot contrasts the resulting reflections from successful and failed rollouts to construct group-level privileged guidance. Conditioned on this guidance, a self-teacher refines turn-level credit assignment by modulating outcome-based advantages while preserving the verifier-determined learning direction. Experiments across multiple agentic environments and model scales demonstrate that GRSD consistently outperforms competitive baselines and generalizes more effectively to unseen tasks.

</details>


### [141] [SciDataSailor: Deep Scientific Data Exploring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28098)
*Jiyong Rao,Yicheng Qiu,Chi Zhang,Chunfeng Song,Runkai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new agentic LLM paradigm and framework, SciDataSailor, for deep exploration and analysis of complex scientific data repositories, using MCTS-based tool-interactive trajectories and releasing both a training set and benchmark across many scientific domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific data are stored in complex hierarchical repositories with many heterogeneous, interdependent files, making them hard to explore and analyze without substantial domain expertise and manual effort. While LLM agents have improved at planning, reasoning, and tool use, their capability to operate directly on real scientific data via executable environments is underexplored. The paper aims to fill this gap by defining a task setting where agents systematically explore such repositories, run analyses, and synthesize evidence-based conclusions.

Method: The authors define the Deep Scientific Data Exploration task paradigm, where an LLM agent must navigate scientific repositories, read and interpret heterogeneous file types and schemas, execute analytical code, integrate evidence across files, and produce grounded conclusions. To support this, they propose SciDataSailor, a trajectory synthesis framework built on Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). It includes four task-specific mechanisms: (1) difficulty-stratified exploration seeds to expose agents to a range of task complexities, (2) dual-feedback first-play urgency to guide early search decisions using both environment and model signals, (3) hierarchical strategy-to-tool action generation to translate high-level plans into concrete tool calls, and (4) entropy-guided branching to focus search on promising yet uncertain paths. Using this framework, they automatically generate tool-interactive trajectories for training and evaluation.

Result: With SciDataSailor, the authors curate two main resources: (1) SciDataSailor-SFT-2K, a dataset of synthesized trajectories for supervised fine-tuning of LLM agents on deep scientific data exploration tasks; and (2) SciDataSailor-Bench, an evaluation benchmark containing 627 meta-information summarization tasks and 586 scientific QA tasks. These tasks span 27 real-world datasets from life, earth, and physical sciences, providing broad coverage of scientific data scenarios. The abstract implies that the framework succeeds in generating meaningful trajectories and tasks, although detailed performance metrics are not given here.

Conclusion: The paper establishes Deep Scientific Data Exploration as an important new agentic task paradigm and introduces SciDataSailor as a practical MCTS-based framework for synthesizing tool-interactive trajectories in complex scientific repositories. By releasing both a supervised fine-tuning dataset and a broad benchmark across multiple scientific domains, the work creates infrastructure for training and evaluating LLM agents that can operate directly on real scientific data, potentially reducing the need for manual, expert-driven data inspection and analysis.

Abstract: Scientific datasets are commonly organized as hierarchical repositories containing heterogeneous and interdependent files, making their inspection, integration, and analysis labor-intensive and reliant on domain expertise. Although large language model (LLM) agents have advanced substantially in planning, reasoning, and tool use, existing research has largely overlooked their ability to interact with real scientific data assets through executable environments. We introduce Deep Scientific Data Exploration, an agentic task paradigm in which agents navigate repositories, interpret heterogeneous files and schemas, execute analyses, integrate cross-file evidence, and produce conclusions grounded in executed observations. To operationalize this paradigm, we present SciDataSailor, a framework for synthesizing tool-interactive trajectories by balancing broad exploration with targeted exploitation. SciDataSailor instantiates trajectory synthesis as Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with four task-specific mechanisms: difficulty-stratified exploration seeds, dual-feedback first-play urgency, hierarchical strategy-to-tool action generation, and entropy-guided branching. Using this framework, we construct SciDataSailor-SFT-2K for supervised fine-tuning and SciDataSailor-Bench for evaluation, with the latter comprising 627 meta-information summarization tasks and 586 scientific question-answering tasks across 27 datasets spanning the life, earth, and physical sciences.

</details>


### [142] [Distilling Answer Set Programming Theories from Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28086)
*Nelson Higuera Ruiz,Markus Hofmarcher,Claudiu Leoveanu-Condrei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether large language models can automatically synthesize complete and correct Answer Set Programming (ASP) theories for visual question answering benchmarks, using an ASP solver in the loop and a fixed time budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Authoring ASP theories manually is complex and labor-intensive, particularly for non-trivial domains like visual question answering (VQA). With the rise of powerful language models and neurosymbolic methods, there is an open question whether these models can autonomously distill high-quality symbolic theories instead of just providing black-box solutions. The work aims to quantify how model scale and type affect the ability to generate such theories under a uniform, dataset-agnostic protocol.

Method: They use a neurosymbolic distillation protocol where a language model interacts with an ASP solver in the loop. Starting from an empty theory file and a single generic prompt, the model is given one hour to incrementally construct a full ASP theory that works with a fixed agent harness. The protocol is dataset-agnostic, and they apply it to three VQA benchmarks: CLEVR, GQA, and CLEVRER. They evaluate nine models of different scales and types—four frontier proprietary models, two mid-tier models, and three open-weight models—measuring final task accuracy achieved by the distilled theories. They also test how supplying handwritten reference theories from other datasets affects performance.

Result: Three of the four frontier models (Claude Sonnet 4.6, Claude Opus 4.7, DeepSeek V4 Pro) successfully reach 100% accuracy on CLEVR and between 92.8% and 98.8% on GQA, and achieve 92.7%–95.3% on CLEVRER. GPT-5 attains 98.7% on CLEVR but performs much worse on GQA (41.8%) and somewhat worse on CLEVRER (86.7%). When provided with additional handwritten reference theories from other datasets, the first three frontier models’ accuracies change by at most ±3.4 percentage points, whereas GPT-5’s accuracy drops by 3–19 percentage points depending on the dataset.

Conclusion: State-of-the-art language models can autonomously distill high-quality ASP theories for complex VQA benchmarks when coupled with a solver in the loop, but performance is highly model-dependent. Most frontier models generalize well across datasets and are relatively robust to the inclusion of external reference theories, while GPT-5 shows instability and sensitivity to such auxiliary information. The released code, prompts, and distilled theories provide a reproducible framework and resources for further research on neurosymbolic theory induction and on understanding model-specific behaviors in symbolic synthesis tasks.

Abstract: Writing Answer Set Programming (ASP) theories from scratch is a difficult and time-consuming task. We take a neurosymbolic approach to study whether a model can distill complete and correct theories, given a fixed agent harness with the solver in the loop. The protocol is dataset-agnostic: with a single prompt and an empty file as the starting point the model is given a 1-hour time limit to derive a complete theory. We chose VQA as the application domain, three benchmarks (CLEVR, GQA, CLEVRER), as these are publicly available and non-trivial. In order to study the model scale required for solving this task we nine different models: four frontier (Claude Sonnet 4.6, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, DeepSeek V4 Pro), two mid-tier (DeepSeek V4 Flash, gpt-oss-120b), and three open-weights (qwen3.6-27b, gpt-oss-20b, qwen3.5-9b). Three of four frontier models reach 100% on CLEVR and 92.8%-98.8% on GQA; on CLEVRER, Sonnet, Opus, DeepSeek V4 Pro score 92.7%-95.3%. GPT-5 reaches 98.7% on CLEVR but drops to 41.8% on GQA and to 86.7% on CLEVRER. Adding handwritten reference theories from other datasets moves the other three frontier models by at most +/-3.4 pp but reduces GPT-5's accuracy by 3-19 pp. We release the code, prompts, and theories distilled.

</details>


### [143] [CDAE: Enhancing Perturbation Robustness in Pretrained Language Models with Contrastive Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28236)
*Sina Heydari,Amirreza Abbasi,Mohsen Hooshmand,Majid Ramezani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Contrastive Denoising Autoencoder (CDAE) to make BERT sentence embeddings robust to meaning-preserving text perturbations, improving stability compared to BERT and SimCSE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-trained language models like BERT produce sentence embeddings that change notably under small, semantics-preserving text edits (e.g., synonym replacement, masking, word dropout). This instability harms downstream tasks that expect consistent representations for semantically equivalent sentences. The authors aim to make sentence embeddings more invariant to such perturbations without heavy models or expensive training.

Method: They propose CDAE, a lightweight model built on top of pre-trained BERT embeddings. CDAE jointly optimizes (1) a contrastive objective to pull together embeddings of perturbed versions of the same sentence and push apart different sentences, and (2) a denoising/reconstruction objective that reconstructs the original sentence (or its embedding) from its perturbed version. Various perturbation strategies (synonym substitution, masking, word dropout, with different intensities) are applied during training to learn perturbation-invariant representations.

Result: Across multiple perturbation strategies and strengths, CDAE embeddings remain more similar between an original sentence and its perturbed versions than baseline BERT embeddings and SimCSE. The gains in similarity become larger as perturbation strength increases, indicating stronger robustness and stability of the learned representations under noise.

Conclusion: CDAE effectively enhances the robustness of BERT sentence embeddings to semantic-preserving perturbations while maintaining semantic information. The findings suggest that explicitly learning perturbation-invariant representations via combined contrastive and denoising objectives is a promising path for improving sentence embeddings. The authors release their implementation for further research and applications.

Abstract: Pre-trained language models have significantly improved sentence representation learning, yet their embedding remain sensitive to semantic preserving textual perturbations such as synonym substitution, masking and word dropout. This work proposes a lightweight Contrastive Denoising Autoencoder (CDAE) that refines pre-trained BERT embedding by jointly optimizing contrastive and reconstruction objective to learn perturbation-invariant representation. We evaluate the proposed framework using multiple perturbation strategies with varying strengths and compare it against the original BERT embeddings and SimCSE. Experimental results show that CDAE consistently preserves higher embedding similarity under perturbations, with the improvements becoming more pronounced as framework effectively enhances representation stability while preserving semantic information, highlighting perturbation-invariant learning as a promising direction for improving sentence embeddings. The source code is publicly available at: https://github.com/ComputationIASBS/CDAE

</details>


### [144] [Diversifying Personalized Research Ideation against AI-Induced Homogenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28087)
*Rui Xu,Yunke Wang,Linwei Tao,Wenjie Xuan,Yong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents DivAlign, a pipeline that generates AI-assisted research ideas that both fit individual researchers and are diversified across a community, reducing redundancy without sacrificing researcher–idea alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI ideation tools generate research ideas mostly in isolation and based on coarse, high-level user profiles. This leads to two issues: (1) suggestions that sound reasonable but are not well grounded in a specific researcher’s skills, history, and context, and (2) many researchers being recommended very similar high-probability themes, causing homogenization of a field’s research portfolio. The authors aim to design a system that better personalizes ideas while also explicitly promoting diversity at the community level.

Method: The authors propose DivAlign, a four-stage pipeline. (1) It builds fine-grained researcher profiles, presumably from prior publications and other signals. (2) It uses these profiles to generate candidate research directions tailored to each researcher. (3) It scores each candidate along three alignment dimensions: Executability (how feasible it is for that researcher), Comprehensibility (how understandable/within their expertise it is), and Growth Potential (room for impact and development). (4) It then selects and surfaces a set of directions that are locally well aligned to each researcher but jointly chosen to reduce redundancy across the overall portfolio, effectively de-homogenizing suggestions at the community level. The system is evaluated on a benchmark built from 95 AI researchers across five AI subfields, comparing to baselines such as coarse, single-shot ideation and an independent top-choice selection variant.

Result: On the 95-researcher benchmark, DivAlign reduces redundancy in suggested research ideas while preserving alignment quality. Relative to coarse, single-shot ideation, it reduces the average pairwise similarity of recommendations from 0.331 to 0.294 and the nearest-neighbor similarity from 0.704 to 0.608. Compared to a variant that independently picks each researcher’s top candidate, DivAlign lowers nearest-neighbor similarity from 0.663 to 0.608, yet maintains 99.9% of the original researcher–direction fit score. These metrics empirically support that the method diversifies community-level suggestions without meaningfully sacrificing per-researcher fit.

Conclusion: DivAlign demonstrates that AI-assisted research ideation can be structured to both respect individual alignment (via fine-grained profiles and multi-dimensional alignment scoring) and mitigate community-level homogenization (via diversity-aware selection). The approach effectively de-homogenizes research directions across a community while almost fully retaining how well each direction matches its intended researcher. This suggests that future ideation tools should jointly optimize for person-level fit and portfolio-level diversity rather than treating each recommendation in isolation.

Abstract: AI-assisted research ideation has emerged as a promising paradigm for accelerating scientific discovery, with systems now capable of generating research directions conditioned on papers, topics, or lightweight researcher contexts. Yet current systems largely optimize individual suggestions in isolation. This leaves two blind spots. First, coarse researcher representations may elicit mainstream directions that appear broadly feasible, but lack sufficient researcher-specific grounding. Second, independent recommendations can concentrate a community's portfolio around recurring high-probability themes. To address these blind spots, we propose DivAlign, a four-stage pipeline for alignment-preserving de-homogenization. DivAlign extracts fine-grained researcher profiles, generates profile-conditioned candidate directions, scores them along three alignment dimensions (Executability, Comprehensibility, and Growth Potential), and surfaces researcher-local directions while reducing redundancy across the community portfolio. On a benchmark we construct from 95 AI researchers across five subfields, DivAlign reduces community-level redundancy while preserving researcher-direction fit. Compared with coarse single-shot ideation, it lowers average pairwise similarity from 0.331 to 0.294 and nearest-neighbor similarity from 0.704 to 0.608. Compared with the independent top-choice variant, DivAlign reduces nearest-neighbor similarity from 0.663 to 0.608 while retaining 99.9% of the researcher-direction fit score. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Ruixxxx/DivAlign.

</details>


### [145] [GLM-RAG: Graph Language Models for Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28397)
*Maya Arseven,Anette Frank,Beni Egressy,Johann Higl,Moritz Plenz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They compare three types of retrievers for retrieval-augmented generation over knowledge graphs and show that graph language model (GLM) retrievers transfer best to new domains and reach state-of-the-art on multi-hop benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG over knowledge graphs needs retrievers that understand both the graph’s structure and the meaning of nodes/relations. Existing GNN-based retrievers capture topology for multi-hop reasoning, while GLMs promise to combine graph reasoning with the semantic strengths of language models, but their relative strengths, especially in-domain vs out-of-domain and single- vs multi-hop settings, are not well understood.

Method: They build a retriever based on graph language models and systematically compare it against GNN-based retrievers and standard vector-search retrievers. The comparison spans single-hop and multi-hop RAG setups and explicitly tests transfer to unseen domains, while also analyzing effects of model scale and the size of the retrieved subgraph.

Result: Finetuned GLM-based retrievers generalize better to out-of-domain tasks and set new state-of-the-art results on two multi-hop benchmarks. On in-domain multi-hop QA, GLM retrievers perform on par with prior methods and improve further as model parameters and subgraph coverage grow. GNN-based retrievers provide better graph coverage with relatively efficient training, and simple vector search performs best on single-hop datasets.

Conclusion: GLM-based retrievers are particularly strong for out-of-domain and multi-hop reasoning over knowledge graphs, offering better transfer and competitive in-domain performance that improves with scale. GNN-based retrievers remain attractive for efficiently achieving high graph coverage, while traditional vector search is still a strong option for simpler, single-hop tasks, suggesting that the best retriever choice depends on task complexity and domain generalization needs.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) over knowledge graphs requires retrievers that can effectively capture both graph structure and semantic information. Recent approaches have explored graph neural network (GNN)-based retrievers to model graph topology in multi-hop reasoning tasks. In parallel, graph language models (GLMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm that integrates graph reasoning and the semantic capabilities of language models. In this work, we introduce a GLM-based retriever and investigate the comparative strengths of GLM-based, GNN-based, and traditional vector-search-based retrievers in single- and multi-hop RAG settings, and with a particular focus on transferability to unseen domains. Our findings suggest that finetuned GLM retrievers generalize better out of domain, achieving SOTA on two multi-hop benchmarks. On in-domain multi-hop QA datasets they remain comparable to prior work, with promising scaling as parameters and subgraph coverage increase. GNN-based retrievers achieve higher graph coverage with an efficient training setup, whereas the vector-search baseline excels at single-hop datasets.

</details>


### [146] [PerturbMap: Cross-Context Transfer of Single-Cell Perturbation Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28090)
*Panpan Cui,Yiqi Liu,Wenhao Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PerturbMap is a method to predict how a genetic or chemical perturbation will affect cells in an unobserved cellular context by combining a context-specific low-rank model with carefully transported effects learned from other contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In single-cell perturbation experiments, not every perturbation is profiled in every cellular context (e.g., different patients, conditions, or cell states). As a result, when we want to know the effect of a given perturbation in a new recipient context, we might only have measurements for that perturbation in other source contexts. Naively ignoring these data wastes useful information, while simply copying or lightly calibrating effects across contexts can mislead if context-specific responses differ. There is a need for a principled way to transfer perturbation effects between contexts that leverages cross-context data without over-transferring incorrect signals.

Method: PerturbMap predicts the missing effect of a query perturbation in a recipient context by (1) learning a recipient-local low-rank base model of perturbation effects within that context, and (2) augmenting this base with a set of proposals obtained by transporting the measured responses of the same perturbation from source contexts into the recipient context. The transport is done via source-to-recipient ridge-regression experts trained on perturbations that are observed in both contexts (paired training perturbations). For each source route, a reliability score is estimated using validation anchors (perturbations held out for validation), and these scores determine how much each transported proposal contributes to the final prediction. Only proposals judged reliable are ‘accepted’ and combined with the base prediction, forming a mixture-of-experts style model that is perturbation-specific and context-aware.

Result: On a Perturb-CITE-seq melanoma dataset, PerturbMap reduces the mean squared error (MSE) of predicted full perturbation effects by 4.1% compared to using the recipient-local low-rank base model alone. It also achieves lower MSE than alternative baselines including FedAvg (a federated averaging scheme), predicting zero effect, directly copying source responses, calibrated copying, and an identity-shuffled affine control. Despite using a weaker training interface than a centralized pooled reference model that has direct token-level matching across contexts, PerturbMap’s MSE is within 2.82×10^{-6} of that stronger reference. A diagnostic based on condition-mean specificity—retrieving the correct same-recipient counterpart in the top 10 neighbors by cosine similarity—improves from 74.5% with the low-rank base to 80.5% with PerturbMap, indicating better context-specific fidelity.

Conclusion: PerturbMap offers a principled, data-efficient way to predict perturbation effects in unmeasured cellular contexts by combining a local low-rank model with reliability-weighted transport of observed effects from other contexts. It outperforms naive and commonly used baselines, and approaches the performance of a stronger centralized pooled reference model while operating in a more constrained and realistic setting. This suggests that careful modeling of cross-context transfer routes, along with reliability estimation, can substantially improve generalization of perturbation-response maps in single-cell experiments.

Abstract: Single-cell perturbation atlases rarely measure every intervention in every cellular context: a query perturbation is often observed in one or more source contexts but missing in the recipient context where its effect is needed. Ignoring those measured responses discards query-specific experimental evidence, whereas copying or weakly calibrating them across contexts risks transferring the wrong signal. We propose PerturbMap, which predicts a missing recipient-context effect by combining a recipient-local low-rank base with accepted proposals that transport the same perturbation's measured source responses through source-to-recipient ridge experts fit on paired training perturbations, with proposal weights determined by route reliability estimated on validation anchors. On the Perturb-CITE-seq melanoma cohort, PerturbMap improves full-effect MSE by 4.1\% over a recipient-local low-rank base and achieves lower MSE than FedAvg, zero-response, raw-copy, calibrated-copy, and identity-shuffled affine controls. It remains within $2.82\times10^{-6}$ MSE of our centralized token-matched pooled reference, which uses a stronger training interface. A condition-mean specificity diagnostic shows the same direction: same-recipient top-10 counterpart retrieval by cosine increases from 74.5\% for the low-rank base to 80.5\% for PerturbMap.

</details>


### [147] [WIDE: Boosting Adaptive LLM Inference via Token-level Dynamic Width Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28418)
*Haozhe Hu,Hao Wu,Peiran Yin,Chao Han,Yunpu Ma,Xiaoyu Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WIDE is a differentiable, token-level dynamic width pruning framework for LLMs that enables fine-grained, hardware-efficient sparsity with high accuracy and substantial real-world speedups in both prefill and decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static structured pruning is hardware friendly but degrades accuracy under high sparsity due to input-agnostic computation allocation. Dynamic sparsity improves accuracy by adapting to inputs but is usually coarse-grained (layer-level or depth-level) and hard to accelerate efficiently in real deployment. The paper aims to get the quality benefits of fine-grained dynamic pruning while still achieving practical speedups on real hardware and workloads.

Method: The authors introduce WIDE, an end-to-end differentiable framework that performs token-level dynamic width pruning. For each token, WIDE learns to select subsets of attention-head groups and FFN-channel groups, effectively deciding a dynamic width at a neuron-block granularity rather than at coarse layer-level. They train this via a two-stage pipeline to learn token-wise sparse execution patterns. To make this deployable, they co-design pruning and kernels: (1) reorder masks, (2) perform hardware-agnostic block-level skipping, and (3) apply hardware-dependent intra-block skipping, so dynamic sparsity can be executed efficiently across multiple granularities and hardware types.

Result: At 50% sparsity, WIDE achieves a 55.1% performance boost over state-of-the-art dynamic depth pruning under calibration-only settings, with better quality retention. In real inference workloads, it reaches near-theoretical kernel-level speedups of up to 1.98× for prefill and 4.95× for decoding, with end-to-end accelerations of 1.68× and 1.55× respectively.

Conclusion: Fine-grained, token-level dynamic width pruning can significantly improve LLM efficiency without large accuracy loss when paired with a carefully co-designed execution kernel. WIDE demonstrates that such dynamic sparsity is not only effective at the algorithmic level but also practically accelerable in real prefill and decoding scenarios, outperforming prior dynamic depth pruning baselines.

Abstract: Pruning is a promising approach for improving the efficiency of LLMs. Existing static structured pruning methods are hardware-friendly and can deliver practical throughput gains, but their input-agnostic computation allocation often causes substantial accuracy degradation under aggressive sparsity. Recent dynamic sparsity methods improve quality retention by adapting computation to individual inputs, yet they remain largely limited to coarse-grained structural decisions and their practical acceleration under real-world inference scenarios remains challenging. To address these challenges, we present WIDE, the first end-to-end differentiable token-level dynamic width pruning framework designed for both prefill and decode scenarios. WIDE enables fine-grained computation allocation by allowing each token to dynamically select attention-head groups and FFN-channel groups, extending dynamic pruning beyond layer-level decisions to neuron-block-level granularity. Through a two-stage training pipeline, WIDE learns effective token-wise sparse execution patterns and achieves substantially better quality retention than existing approaches. To make such fine-grained dynamic pruning practical, we further propose a pruning--kernel co-design framework that decomposes dynamic sparsity acceleration into mask reordering, hardware-agnostic block-level skipping, and hardware-dependent intra-block skipping, enabling efficient execution across different granularities. At 50% sparsity, WIDE provides 55.1% performance boost when compared to the state-of-the-art dynamic depth pruning under calibration-only settings. Under prefill and decoding inference workloads, WIDE achieves close-to-theoretical kernel-level speedups of up to 1.98x for prefill and 4.95x for decoding, as well as 1.68x and 1.55x end-to-end acceleration. Our code is available at https://github.com/EIT-NLP/LLM-Pruning/tree/main/WIDE.

</details>


### [148] [An Instrument to Evaluate Governance Proposals: AI Policy Analysis at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28094)
*Paulo Carvao,Claudio Mayrink Verdun,Isabel Adler,Jeffrey Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for systematically and transparently analyzing AI governance policies using multidimensional rubrics, expert input, computational text analysis, and LLMs, focusing on relevance and alignment rather than policy desirability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI governance debates are polarized and often framed as binaries, which hides important tradeoffs, assumptions, and value choices embedded in policy proposals. Policymakers and analysts lack tools to systematically compare proposals across multiple objectives in a transparent way that makes underlying priorities visible without forcing a single normative judgment. There is also a need to understand how to reliably use LLMs for policy analysis while making their analytical assumptions explicit.

Method: The authors design a policy analysis framework organized around multiple policy attributes (e.g., objectives, tradeoffs) operationalized as empirically grounded rubrics. They develop these rubrics using a mixed-methods process: qualitative input from subject-matter experts plus computational text analysis of policy documents to validate and refine attributes and scoring criteria. They then encode these rubrics into models and test the use of commercial large language models for rubric-based assessments, benchmarking them against a domain-trained, rubric-calibrated model that has clearly specified analytical assumptions (attribute definitions, scoring logic, and weights). Comparative visualizations are used to show how different policies score across attributes and facilitate cross-policy comparison.

Result: The framework yields structured, multidimensional profiles of AI governance proposals, quantifying the relative emphasis on various policy objectives and surfacing tensions between them. The authors demonstrate that rubric-based analysis enables clearer comparison across policies and that a domain-trained rubric-calibrated model can serve as a more transparent reference point for evaluating the behavior of general-purpose LLMs on policy assessment tasks. The outputs include comparative visualizations and quantified attribute scores that support interpretability and highlight tradeoffs without forcing a singular evaluation of which policy is "best."

Conclusion: The paper concludes that rubric-based, multidimensional policy analysis can make AI governance debates more transparent by explicitly encoding and revealing analytical assumptions and normative priorities, rather than hiding them. By focusing on relevance and alignment across attributes, the framework helps users judge whether a given policy analysis tool matches their own value commitments, without prescribing substantive policy outcomes. The approach is designed to be jurisdiction-agnostic and practically useful for policymakers, analysts, and researchers, and it demonstrates how domain-trained, rubric-calibrated models can benchmark and complement commercial LLMs in structured policy analysis.

Abstract: This paper introduces a policy analysis framework for systematic, transparent assessment of AI governance proposals in an evolving and contested regulatory landscape. AI policy debates often collapse into binary positions that obscure underlying tradeoffs and normative assumptions. The framework structures policy analysis around multiple policy attributes, allowing users to surface priorities and tensions without prescribing outcomes. We use a mixed-methods approach that integrates qualitative insights from subject matter experts with computational text analysis to inform the design of policy attribute rubrics. This quantifies the relative emphasis of different policy objectives and presents them through comparative visualizations that support interpretability and cross-policy comparison. The paper also examines the use of commercial LLMs for rubric-based policy analysis, benchmarking their outputs against a domain-trained rubric-calibrated model with explicitly defined analytical assumptions. Rather than assessing policy effectiveness or desirability, the framework focuses on relevance and alignment across attributes. By making analytical assumptions explicit, including attribute selection, rubric construction, and weighting schemes, the framework enables users to evaluate whether its embedded priorities align with the users' own normative commitments. The approach is jurisdiction-agnostic and intended to support policymakers, analysts, and researchers navigating complex AI governance environments.
  Contributions: (1) multidimensional policy assessment through empirically grounded rubrics that surface tradeoffs rather than resolving them; (2) a transparent hybrid methodology combining feedback from subject-matter experts with computational validation; and (3) use of domain-trained rubric-calibrated models as a benchmark for comparing different general-purpose large language models.

</details>


### [149] [Metaphor Tracer: A Theory-Informed Analysis of Hidden States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28434)
*Marc Heimann,Roxana Assadi Moghaddam,Olga Brovkina,Mark Pettifor,Lutz Goetzmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes two measures derived from a language model’s hidden states—aggregator and differentiator—to characterize how individual tokens function structurally within a single text, showing that these are relational, text-specific properties not reducible to token vectors or type-level statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what language models’ hidden states reveal about the structure and organization of a single discourse, beyond traditional notions like surprisal, attention, or token salience, and to test whether token-level representations encode a token’s role in this specific text rather than just its lexical type.

Method: Using untrained, single forward passes of three different language models, the authors define two metrics computed from hidden states at each token position: the aggregator, indicating how much a position consolidates the whole text into a stable configuration, and the differentiator, indicating how other tokens are transiently carried into its subspace. They freeze the constants of these measures on one discovery text and then apply them to other texts. They compare aggregator scores against surprisal, attention, and various type-level and lexical controls; they validate against two independent ground truths: (1) an engineered register boundary that the aggregator tracks, and (2) a psychoanalyst’s prior annotations of clinical transcripts. They also conduct a transfer test on base vs. instruct-tuned model pairs to see how structural fidelity relates to lexical type-transfer properties.

Result: The aggregator behaves differently from standard information-theoretic or salience measures: as a signifier repeats, its surprisal and attention diminish but its aggregator score remains stable, indicating it tracks a token’s structural role rather than novelty. Aggregator scores align closely with independent ground truths: they follow an engineered register boundary in all evaluated cells (6/6) and strongly correlate with psychoanalyst annotations in most cells (34/36), with graded effects beyond lexical controls and dissociations that type-level metrics cannot capture. A transfer test shows that a model whose token structure is tightly tied to lexical types performs worst at reading singular discourse, and that instruction tuning improves discourse-reading fidelity without altering type-transfer behavior.

Conclusion: Hidden states encode structural value as a relational property of a token’s position within a specific text, not as an intrinsic property of its standalone vector or lexical type. The proposed aggregator (and differentiator) operationalize a non-essentialist, discourse-relative theory of meaning that predates neural LMs: structural roles emerge from a token’s relations within the current text, offering a new way to interpret and evaluate language model representations that is distinct from surprisal, attention, or type-level similarity.

Abstract: What do a language model's hidden states say about the organization of a single text? From one forward pass, without training, we score every token position on two properties. The *aggregator* measures whether the position consolidates the whole text into a stable configuration. The *differentiator*, whether other tokens are transiently carried into its subspace as the model reads: metaphor in its root sense, transport. Constants were frozen on one discovery text; every other is confirmatory.
  The aggregator is not, in the classic sense, an information measure, nor a measure of salience. Across three unrelated models, as a signifier repeats, its surprisal and its attention drain while its aggregator score holds: the channel marks a token's place in the text. That this tracks a reading rests on independent ground truth: an engineered register the aggregator follows across its boundaries (6/6 cells), and a psychoanalyst's marking of clinical transcripts, fixed before the instrument existed, in 34/36 cells, with a graded increment above lexical controls and dissociations no type-level measure reproduces. A transfer test gives the result its shape: the model whose token structure travels with lexical type reads the singular discourse worst, and in a matched base/instruct pair tuning raises fidelity without moving type-transfer. Structural value is a property of a token's place in *this* text, not of its vector alone: a relational rather than essentialist reading of hidden states, operationalizing theory that predated the instrument.

</details>


### [150] [SVR: Self-Verifying Refinement via Joint Verdict-Confidence Reinforcement Learning for Adaptive Test-Time Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28457)
*Hongyu Chen,Liang Lin,Guangrun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Self-Verifying Refinement (SVR), a reinforcement learning framework where a language model learns to verify its own answers and adaptively decide when to stop reasoning, improving accuracy and efficiency on math benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods that scale test-time computation either allocate a fixed, uniform compute budget to all inputs—wasting resources on easy problems—or rely on external verifiers/feedback, which limits practicality and generality. There is a need for an internal, learned mechanism that lets a model decide whether its current solution is correct and whether more computation is necessary, without oracle feedback at inference.

Method: The authors design Self-Verifying Refinement (SVR), a multi-turn RL framework where, at each turn, the model outputs: (1) a candidate solution, (2) a discrete correctness verdict (Correct/Incorrect), and (3) a confidence score. A simple rule-based controller keeps the current solution only when the model’s verdict is Correct and confidence passes a threshold; otherwise, the model continues refining the answer using its own previous outputs as context. Ground-truth labels are used only in the reward function during training. SVR is trained with GRPO on fixed-horizon trajectories, using rewards that simultaneously encourage final solution correctness, well-calibrated self-verification signals, and states that are “ready to stop.” At inference, the learned verification signal is used to adaptively stop reasoning, so that easy problems take fewer turns and harder ones get more compute.

Result: On seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks with the Qwen3.5-2B model, SVR attains a macro-average accuracy of 0.563 while using an average of only 2.99 inference turns per problem. In holistic comparisons, SVR outperforms standard GRPO and strong multi-turn baselines, as well as a fixed-budget, oracle-guided score-feedback method, and it achieves these accuracy gains with substantially fewer turns than a fixed ten-turn inference setting.

Conclusion: Learned self-verification can function as an effective internal control signal for when to retain an answer and when to continue refining it, enabling adaptive allocation of test-time compute without access to external or oracle feedback. SVR thus offers a practical framework for making multi-step reasoning more accurate and computationally efficient, especially on mathematical reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Scaling test-time computation can improve language-model reasoning, but uniform budgets waste computation on easy inputs, while verifier-guided refinement relies on external feedback. We introduce Self-Verifying Refinement (SVR), an oracle-free multi-turn reinforcement learning framework that learns to use self-verification as a compute-control policy. At each turn, the model produces a solution together with a discrete correctness verdict and a confidence score; it retains the current answer only when the verdict is Correct and confidence exceeds a threshold, and otherwise continues refinement using its own self-verification. Ground-truth correctness is used only to construct training rewards and is never exposed to the policy through refinement prompts or required at inference. SVR is trained with GRPO on fixed-horizon trajectories using rewards that promote solution correctness, calibration-aware self-verification, and stop-ready correct states; adaptive stopping is activated only at inference. On seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks with Qwen3.5-2B, SVR achieves a macro-average accuracy of 0.563 with only 2.99 inference turns on average. In the evaluated complete-system comparison, it exceeds standard GRPO, strong multi-turn baselines, and a fixed-budget oracle-guided score-feedback reference while requiring substantially fewer turns than fixed ten-turn inference. These results demonstrate that learned self-verification can serve as an effective internal control signal for answer retention and adaptive test-time compute allocation.

</details>


### [151] [MIND: Lightweight and Effective Memory Injection Defense for LLM Agents via Intent-Aware Information Bottleneck](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28103)
*Dongyi Liu,Haixing He,Xiaobao Wu,Jia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MIND, a lightweight, intent-aware denoising framework to defend memory-augmented LLM agents against memory injection attacks by detecting and filtering malicious memories while keeping task performance and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-augmented LLM agents are susceptible to memory injection, where attackers poison the memory store so that retrieved content diverts the agent away from the original user intent, causing task failures. Existing defenses are either computationally expensive (e.g., repeated LLM-based auditing) or ineffective in multi-turn settings due to information redundancy. There is a need for an efficient, robust defense that handles long, multi-turn contexts without heavy overhead.

Method: The authors first analyze benign versus poisoned interaction trajectories and find that they differ in how subsequent behavior aligns with the initial user intent. Based on this, they propose MIND (Memory Intent-Aware Neural Denoising), which leverages an intent-aware Information Bottleneck (IB). The IB jointly encodes the initial user intent and each turn-level behavior into compact, intent--behavior representations that preserve cross-turn attack signals but discard task-irrelevant and repetitive information. A lightweight detector is then trained on these representations to classify and filter malicious memories, avoiding the need for repeated full LLM audits.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that MIND substantially reduces attack success rates (ASR) under memory injection while maintaining task accuracy and efficiency. On the ReAct-StrategyQA benchmark, MIND reduces the mean ASR-r and ASR-a by 55.4% and 55.3%, respectively, while achieving comparable accuracy and latency to an undefended agent, indicating strong protection without noticeable performance degradation.

Conclusion: MIND offers an effective and efficient defense against memory injection attacks in memory-augmented LLM agents by focusing on intent-behavior relationships via an information-bottleneck-based denoising approach. It mitigates information redundancy in multi-turn contexts, significantly lowers attack success, and preserves both task performance and inference efficiency, making it a practical solution for securing LLM-based agents in real-world applications.

Abstract: Memory-augmented LLM-based agents are vulnerable to memory injection attacks: Agents may retrieve poisoned memory from attackers, which diverts their behavior from initial user intent and finally causes task failure. However, existing defense mechanisms either incur high computational cost or suffer from information redundancy in multi-turn contexts. To address these challenges, we propose Memory Intent-Aware Neural Denoising(MIND), a lightweight defense framework for memory injection attack. Our preliminary analysis reveals that benign and poisoned trajectories exhibit distinguishable relationships between the initial user intent and subsequent behavior. Building on this observation, MIND employs an intent-aware Information Bottleneck(IB) to extract compact intent--behavior representations from the initial intent and turn-level behavior. The IB preserves intent-relevant cross-turn attack signals while filtering task-irrelevant and repetitive information, and a lightweight detector identifies malicious memories from the resulting representations. As such, MIND mitigates information redundancy in multi-turn contexts while avoiding the overhead of repeated LLM auditing. Extensive experiments show that MIND reduces attack success rates while preserving task accuracy and inference efficiency. Notably, on ReAct-StrategyQA, MIND reduces mean ASR-r and ASR-a by 55.4% and 55.3%, respectively, while matching the undefended agent in average accuracy and latency.

</details>


### [152] [OSReward: Instituting Standardized Evaluation for Cross-Platform Computer-Use Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28609)
*Qiushi Sun,Kanzhi Cheng,Yian Wang,Bowen Yang,Hang Yan,Liheng Chen,Fangzhi Xu,Zichen Ding,Nuo Chen,Jialin Cao,Xingdong Gong,Zehao Li,Kaiming Jin,Xinfeng Yuan,Zhoumianze Liu,Jingyang Gong,Zhangyue Yin,Jiahui Gao,Zhiyong Wu,Tianbao Xie,Jianbing Zhang,Ben Kao,Lingpeng Kong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how reliable vision-language models are as automatic judges of computer-using agent (CUA) trajectories and introduces benchmarks and open reward models to make such judging cheaper and more accurate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
