Motivation: Computer-using agents need automatic evaluation of whether they correctly completed tasks, since human-written rules and human annotators do not scale. The community has started to rely on vision-language models as judges of agents’ trajectories, but it is unclear if these judges are accurate and reliable enough, especially under real-world, diverse conditions. This gap motivates a systematic benchmark and analysis of such judges and the development of better, scalable reward models.

Method: The authors construct OSReward, a benchmark of CUA trajectories sourced from diverse agent backbones performing human-verified instructions across multiple platforms. These trajectories are multi-stage human-annotated with ground-truth success/failure verdicts. On top of OSReward, they build (1) OSReward-Hard, a subset focusing on genuinely difficult cases, and (2) OSReward-Multi for detailed, multi-dimensional scoring (e.g., efficiency, alignment). They then evaluate a broad set of vision-language models as judges on these benchmarks to diagnose biases and failure modes. To improve the situation, they release OS-Shepherd-100K, a large corpus of trajectory judgments with reasoning, and train OS-Shepherd reward models (9B and 35B) on this data to serve as open, low-cost, and reliable evaluators.

Result: Evaluation on OSReward and its variants shows that current vision-language models, including state-of-the-art systems, are not yet ideal judges for CUA trajectories. They display a systematic leniency bias, frequently labeling failed trajectories as successes. High-performing models tend to be computationally expensive and impractical at scale, while cost-effective open models significantly underperform. Training on the OS-Shepherd-100K dataset yields OS-Shepherd 9B and 35B reward models that attain stable, reliable judgment quality comparable to commercial frontier models but at 30–60% lower computational cost.

Conclusion: Vision-language models used as judges for computer-using agents are currently unreliable and biased, especially in realistic, challenging settings. The OSReward benchmark family provides a rigorous, human-verified way to evaluate such judges, revealing systematic issues. The OS-Shepherd dataset and trained reward models offer a practical path toward scalable, accurate, and cost-effective automatic evaluation of CUA trajectories. These contributions collectively advance the design and deployment of reliable reward models for real-world agent systems and make high-quality judging capabilities more broadly accessible through open resources.

Abstract: Computer-using agents (CUAs) are advancing rapidly across the digital world. A CUA trajectory records the agent's actions, states, and reasoning. Verifying whether it fulfilled the task instruction is central to CUA evaluation, data curation, and reinforcement learning. Neither human-written verifiers nor human annotators can provide such verification at scale, so the field increasingly turns to vision-language models (VLMs) as judges of CUA trajectories. But a fundamental question has long gone unexamined: are these VLM judges reliable enough? To study it systematically, we introduce OSReward, a realistic, high-quality benchmark that evaluates VLM judges on CUA trajectories. The trajectories come from diverse agent backbones executing human-verified instructions across platforms, then rigorously labeled with ground-truth verdicts through multi-stage human annotation. Building on it, we derive OSReward-Hard, a challenge set concentrating genuinely hard cases, and OSReward-Multi for fine-grained efficiency and alignment scoring. The most comprehensive evaluation of VLM judges to date finds even state-of-the-art models fall short of an ideal judge, sharing a systematic leniency bias that mislabels failed runs as successes. The few reliable enough to trust are too expensive to run at scale, while affordable open models trail far behind. To close this gap, we construct and release OS-Shepherd-100K, an open corpus of reasoning-annotated trajectory judgments for the CUA community. On it, we train OS-Shepherd (9B and 35B), open reward models that supply low-cost, stable, and reliable reward signals, matching commercial judges at 30-60% lower cost than the frontier. Extensive analyses further inform the design of reliable CUA reward at scale. Our code, benchmark, dataset, and model checkpoints are available at https://os-copilot.github.io/OSReward-Home/.

</details>


### [153] [Beyond Rephrasing: Book-Level Organization Improves Synthetic Textbook Data for Mid-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28109)
*Jiawen Tao,Miao Peng,Yaoming Li,Xiaokun Yuan,Mengzhou Wu,Wenhan Yu,Guoan Wang,Nuo Chen,Tong Yang,Maxm Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that organizing synthetic pretraining data into coherent, book-level documents—rather than just generating good local text—significantly improves language model performance, and introduces a scalable pipeline to create such structured synthetic textbooks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While synthetic data has improved language model pre-training, most prior work focuses on the quality or style of generated passages, overlooking whether large-scale document structure (e.g., book-like organization) itself contributes to gains. The authors suspect that coherent, long-range organization of related content into book-level documents may provide additional learning signals that are not captured by isolated or loosely concatenated texts.

Method: The authors build a scalable pipeline that: (1) retrieves source material from an existing pre-training corpus, (2) clusters this material into topical units, (3) plans hierarchical tables of contents for textbooks, and (4) assembles source-grounded sections into complete synthetic books (the Full setting). This process generates 686K textbooks (32B tokens) across more than 15,000 disciplines. They then run controlled pre-training experiments comparing the Full setting to several variants: (a) Split, where the same generated sections are treated as independent documents (no book packaging); (b) RandomConcat, where sections from different books are concatenated to match length but not topical coherence; and (c) Rephrase, where retrieved documents are independently rewritten in the same style without clustering, TOC planning, or book assembly. They test these mixes in mid-training against baselines that use natural books and measure downstream performance on held-out tasks, including experiments on Llama3-8B.

Result: Replacing natural books in a mid-training mixture with the synthetic Full textbook corpus yields an average +1.09 improvement in downstream performance. When holding content and token count fixed but removing book packaging (Split condition), performance drops by about 1.02 relative to Full, attributing the gain to coherent document organization. RandomConcat, which matches document length but merges sections from unrelated books, underperforms Full, showing that length alone cannot explain the effect. The Rephrase condition, which rewrites retrieved documents without structured book assembly, is outperformed by Full by +1.17, indicating that structured synthesis (clustering, TOC planning, coherent assembly) provides additional benefit. On Llama3-8B, the Full setting also outperforms both RandomConcat and Natural Books, reinforcing that the benefits generalize across models.

Conclusion: Book-level organization of synthetic pretraining data is an important and previously underexplored factor in language model performance. Carefully structuring synthetic content into coherent textbooks—via topical clustering, hierarchical TOC planning, and consistent assembly—yields significant gains over both natural books and less structured synthetic baselines, beyond what can be explained by token quality, style, or document length alone. The work suggests that future synthetic data design should explicitly consider large-scale document structure, not just local text generation quality.

Abstract: Synthetic textbook data has improved language model pre-training, but prior work largely treats the benefit as a property of generated content or local rewriting style. We study a different factor: whether related content is organized into coherent book-level documents. We contribute both a scalable synthesis pipeline and controlled evidence that this organization matters. The pipeline retrieves source material from a pre-training corpus, clusters it into topical units, plans hierarchical tables of contents, and assembles source-grounded sections into complete books (our Full setting), yielding 686K textbooks (32B tokens) across 15,000+ disciplines. Replacing natural books in a mid-training mix with this corpus improves downstream performance by +1.09 on average. Controlled comparisons then disentangle the relevant design factors. A content-matched Split condition holds generated text and tokens fixed but treats each section as an independent document; Full's +1.02 mean gain isolates document packaging. A length-matched RandomConcat control that joins sections from different books remains below Full, ruling out document length alone. A retrieval-pool-matched Rephrase condition independently rewrites individual retrieved documents under the same audience-by-style scheme, without clustering, TOC planning, or book assembly; Full's +1.17 gain demonstrates the value of structured synthesis. On Llama3-8B, Full likewise outperforms both RandomConcat and Natural Books, supporting book-level organization as a useful axis for synthetic pre-training data design.

</details>


### [154] [AISPA: User-Centric System Prompt Auditing for Large Language Model Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28617)
*Xiangning Lin,Shenzhe Zhu,Shu Yang,Zhenyu Zhang,Haoqian Zhang,Yipeng Zhao,Chengxuan Qian,Tianwei Wang,Ziheng Zhang,Zhenlong Yuan,Dingcheng Wang,Juncheng Wu,Yuan Si,Jiaxin Liu,Baolong Bi,Robert Mahari,Tobin South,Dazza Greenwood,Zexue He,Rishi Bommasani,Sophia Kazinnik,Andreas Haupt,Samuele Marro,Erik Brynjolfsson,Alex Pentland,Jiaxin Pei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework (AISPA) to audit and evaluate hidden system prompts used in commercial AI products, revealing large variation, shallow protections, growing prompt length/protectiveness, and persistent problematic instructions, and arguing for more transparency and oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: System prompts crucially shape AI behavior but are typically undisclosed to users and regulators, creating a trust and accountability gap. There is little systematic understanding or auditing of how these prompts are designed, how well they protect users, or whether they sometimes conflict with users’ interests. The authors aim to fill this gap by creating a structured, user-centric way to examine and compare system prompts in real commercial products.

Method: The authors develop AISPA (Artificial Intelligence System Prompt Assurance), a user-centric auditing framework that breaks system prompts into components and evaluates them along eight user-relevant dimensions. They then collect system prompts from 88 commercial AI products and extract 3,249 individual instructions. Each instruction is coded as protective (aligned with user protection) or problematic (potentially against user interests), and the authors analyze patterns across products and organizations—such as numbers of protective instructions, coverage of the eight dimensions, and temporal trends in prompt length and protectiveness.

Result: The audit shows: (1) large variation in system prompt design across companies, with some products including over 60 protective instructions on average and others fewer than 5; (2) protective instructions are nearly universal (98.9% of products have at least one) but often incomplete, with only 24% of products covering all eight AISPA dimensions; (3) over time, system prompts have become longer and include more user-protective content; (4) problematic instructions remain common—about 40% of products contain at least one—and they often coexist alongside protective instructions in the same prompt.

Conclusion: System prompt design in commercial AI is heterogeneous, increasingly focused on user protection, but still riddled with gaps and problematic elements. Because these prompts are hidden yet highly consequential, the authors argue for greater transparency, standardization, and independent oversight of system prompts, and present AISPA as a practical framework to assess and improve them from a user-centric perspective.

Abstract: System prompts are instructions configured by developers to govern the behaviors of foundation models in AI applications. They are used throughout commercial AI products, but are rarely disclosed to the public or regulators, creating a serious trust and accountability gap in the wide deployment of AI systems. In this paper, we introduce Artificial Intelligence System Prompt Assurance (AISPA), a user-centric framework for systematically auditing system prompts in AI systems. AISPA examines specific parts of a system prompt and evaluates them along eight dimensions that matter to users. We then use this framework to review 3,249 instructions from system prompts in 88 commercial AI products, classifying each instruction as either protective (of users) or problematic. Our audit surfaces four core findings. First, system prompt design varies substantially across products and developers, with some organizations averaging over 60 protective instructions per product while others average fewer than 5. Second, protective instructions are widely adopted but shallow in scope: 98.9% of products contain at least one, yet only 24% cover all eight dimensions of the AISPA taxonomy. Third, system prompts have grown steadily longer and more protective of users, suggesting that user protection is becoming a more visible concern in commercial prompt design. Fourth, despite this progress, problematic instructions remain pervasive: roughly 40% of products contain at least one instruction that works against user interests, and protective and problematic instructions frequently coexist within the same prompt. Our findings highlight the need for greater transparency, standardization, and independent oversight for system prompts in commercial AI products.

</details>


### [155] [BlueprintRepair: Typed Local Edits for Failed Lean Proof Blueprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28110)
*Ruslan Khrulev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces BlueprintRepair, a structured interface for repairing Lean proof blueprints using a small set of schema-checked local edit operations, and evaluates it against less-structured editing interfaces on a new benchmark, showing that typed repair is more cost-effective and quickly reaches high coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based Lean provers increasingly organize proofs as blueprints (dependency graphs of formal statements), but when proofs fail, repairing these blueprints reliably and efficiently is difficult. Existing interfaces like free-form patching or rewriting are flexible but can be expensive, brittle, and slow to converge, and there is a lack of controlled benchmarks capturing realistic failure and repair trajectories. The authors aim to design a repair interface that constrains edits enough for robustness and efficiency while retaining enough expressiveness to fix most localized failures, and to systematically compare such an interface against common alternatives under matched conditions.

Method: The authors propose BlueprintRepair, an interface where an LLM edits a proof blueprint (a dependency graph) via ten predefined, schema-checked local operations. Each operation specifies the node it edits, ensuring the target theorem remains fixed, and Lean checks each proposed change for correctness. Accepted repairs have to explicitly list all blueprint lemmas they depend on, enforcing transparency of dependencies. They also introduce BlueprintTrace, a benchmark consisting of 142 controlled failure cases, each with complete logs of accepted and rejected repair attempts (repair trajectories). Using this benchmark, they compare three interaction modes—typed local edits (BlueprintRepair), exact source patching, and full module rewriting—holding constant the underlying source state, Lean feedback, model, and token budget. Experiments use DeepSeek-V4-Flash and Qwen3.6-Flash, with one repair episode per state-interface pair and token-budget-controlled runs (e.g., up to 10,000 completion tokens).

Result: On the BlueprintTrace benchmark, all three interfaces (typed edits, patching, rewriting) enable DeepSeek-V4-Flash to solve a similar total number of localized failures, indicating that the constrained typed interface is sufficiently expressive for many repair tasks. However, typed repair is substantially more cost-efficient: per solved state, patching consumes about 1.30x and full rewriting about 2.06x the tokens used by typed repair. Moreover, typed repair reaches nearly all of its eventual coverage within 10,000 completion tokens per task, whereas the two free-form interfaces lag significantly behind in coverage at the same budget. With Qwen3.6-Flash, overall success is lower, but the relative pattern persists: typed repair remains the cheapest interface, performs best on proof-authoring states, and again displays the same localized efficiency advantages over patching and rewriting.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that a structured, schema-checked edit interface (BlueprintRepair) can match the repair success rate of more flexible free-form interfaces while being notably more token-efficient and faster to reach its maximum coverage. The results suggest that constraining LLM interactions to typed local operations on proof blueprints is an effective design choice for Lean proof repair, especially for localized failures, and that such interfaces can generalize across different models. The BlueprintTrace benchmark and trajectories provide a foundation for future research on proof-repair strategies and interface design in LLM-based theorem proving systems.

Abstract: LLM-based Lean proving systems increasingly organize a proof as a blueprint: a dependency graph of formal statements. We introduce BlueprintRepair, a repair interface that lets a model change this graph through ten schema-checked local operations. An operation names the node it edits, so the target theorem cannot be changed. Lean checks every applied change, and an accepted repair must declare every blueprint lemma its proof uses. We also construct BlueprintTrace, a benchmark of 142 controlled failures with complete accepted and rejected repair trajectories. We compare typed edits, exact source patches, and complete module rewrites under matched source, feedback, model, and budget, one episode per state and interface. With DeepSeek-V4-Flash, the three interfaces solve almost the same number of the benchmark's localized failures. Typed repair is the cheapest per solved state (patching is 1.30x as expensive, rewriting 2.06x), and within 10,000 completion tokens per task it reaches almost all of its final coverage, while both free-form interfaces are well behind. A second model, Qwen3.6-Flash, solves fewer states but keeps typed repair cheapest, puts it ahead on the proof-authoring states, and repeats the localized pattern.

</details>


### [156] [ConMem: Contribution-Aware Memory for Long-Horizon Manufacturing Inspection Logs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28126)
*Bingchen Liu,Yuanyuan Fang,Lei Liu,Guangyuan Dong,Xing Fu,Yuanyuan Gao,Shuyue Wei,Xin Li,Xiangtian Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ConMem, a contribution-aware memory framework that selects and stores the most diagnostically valuable inspection records to support LLM-based, early-risk equipment inspection, greatly cutting token usage and latency while improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term steel-equipment inspection requires reasoning over many heterogeneous, repeated inspection logs. Existing retrieval-augmented generation systems treat all historical logs equally, failing to identify which past records truly matter for early risk detection and thus often missing weak early fault signals while also being inefficient in context usage and response time.

Method: ConMem introduces a contribution-aware memory for LLM-assisted inspection. It first segments inspection logs into functional evidence units based on their diagnostic role. Then it uses a Shapley-value-style estimation to quantify each unit’s contribution to downstream diagnostic performance. Under a constrained memory budget, ConMem selectively retains the highest-contribution evidence for future LLM queries, enabling focused retrieval and early-risk reasoning in a human-in-the-loop workflow.

Result: On a real-world dataset, ConMem reaches 76.0% QA accuracy, outperforming the strongest directly comparable baseline. Compared with naive 8K-context LLM baselines, it reduces input tokens by 88.2% and response time by 86.6%. Ablation studies show that both functional-role-aware segmentation and contribution-based valuation help the model prioritize weak degradation signals to guide field inspection.

Conclusion: Contribution-aware memory management substantially improves LLM-based equipment inspection by retaining only the most diagnostically useful evidence units. ConMem supports efficient, accurate, and early-risk-focused inspection, as confirmed by experiments and practical deployments, where it preserves weak early degradation signals across multiple cycles and enables timely alerts for on-site inspectors.

Abstract: Long-horizon steel-equipment inspection requires reasoning over heterogeneous records accumulated across repeated inspection cycles. Existing retrieval-augmented generation systems treat historical logs as a static corpus and retain records without estimating their diagnostic value, failing to report early risk. To this end, we propose ConMem, a contribution-aware memory framework for LLM-assisted equipment inspection, supporting a human-in-the-loop early-risk screening system. Specifically, our ConMem first segments inspection logs into functional evidence units, then estimates each memory unit's contribution to downstream diagnosis through a Shapley-style estimation, and finally retains high-value evidence under a constrained memory budget. In experiments, we evaluate ConMem on real-world dataset and ConMem achieves 76.0% QA accuracy, exceeding the strongest directly comparable baseline. Relative to the naive 8K-context LLM baselines, it reduces the average number of input tokens by 88.2% and response time by 86.6%. Ablation studies also show that the functional-role-aware segmentation and contribution-based valuation are helping prioritize weak degradation signals for targeted field inspection. Practical deployments further confirm that ConMem retains the weak early signal across three inspection cycles, providing an early-stage seal-wear alert targeted for on-site inspectors.

</details>


### [157] [AgenticASR: Refining Speech Recognition in Real-World Scenarios via an Agentic Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28175)
*Zixuan Jiang,Binghao Qiang,Jiaying Chi,Yanqiao Zhu,Kai Yu,Xie Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Agentic Speech Recognition (AgenticSR), which converts streaming speech directly into clean, edited text that reflects the speaker’s final intent, rather than a verbatim, disfluent transcript.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ASR systems aim for verbatim transcription, faithfully capturing all spoken words including fillers, repetitions, false starts, and self-corrections. While accurate, such transcripts are hard to read and can distort the actual intended message, especially for downstream NLP tasks that assume clean written input. Existing spoken-to-written approaches typically operate only after the full audio or transcript is available and cannot revise already-emitted text when later speech changes the interpretation of earlier content. There is therefore a need for an online, intent-preserving ASR paradigm that can continually revise outputs as more context becomes available.

Method: The authors define a new task, Agentic Speech Recognition (AgenticSR), which transforms incoming audio streams into clean written text by removing disfluencies, resolving self-corrections, and performing text normalization while preserving the speaker’s final intent. They implement this with AgenticASR, an ASR–Refiner architecture. A base ASR front end produces initial transcripts; then a Refiner repeatedly processes a bounded active context (a limited recent window of transcript plus corresponding audio) and rewrites the associated output span as new audio arrives. This design allows continual emission and revision over arbitrarily long streams. They also build AASR-Bench, a bilingual evaluation benchmark with fine-grained, rubric-based atomic criteria, and conduct ablation studies on Refiner capacity, context length, and online vs. offline inference trade-offs.

Result: On the AASR-Bench benchmark, AgenticASR achieves the best scores among multiple evaluated ASR front ends integrated into the proposed architecture, indicating superior ability to produce clean, intent-preserving transcripts. A human–AI agreement study shows that the rubric-based automatic or semi-automatic scoring used in AASR-Bench aligns well with independent expert judgments. Ablation experiments further quantify how model capacity, context window size, and inference mode (online vs. offline) affect performance and latency, clarifying the quality–latency trade-offs of the approach.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that AgenticASR is an effective and practical framework for real-time, intent-preserving clean transcription. By enabling continual revision of previously emitted text and providing a dedicated benchmark with robust evaluation rubrics, the work positions AgenticSR as a viable next step beyond verbatim ASR, better suited for human consumption and downstream language processing. The authors plan to release code, the AASR-Bench dataset, and an online demo to facilitate further research and adoption.

Abstract: Automatic speech recognition (ASR) has achieved substantial gains in transcription accuracy, yet verbatim transcription does not necessarily produce readily usable text. It retains fillers, repetitions, false starts, and self-corrections that increase reading effort, obscure the speaker's final intent, and propagate unresolved or abandoned content to downstream tasks. Existing spoken-to-written methods process completed audio or transcripts but cannot revise emitted text when later speech changes how preceding content should be interpreted. We therefore formulate Agentic Speech Recognition (AgenticSR), an audio-to-clean-text task that removes disfluencies, resolves self-corrections, and normalizes written form while preserving the speaker's final intent. AgenticASR implements this task through an ASR--Refiner architecture that repeatedly transforms a bounded active context and replaces its corresponding output span as audio arrives. This enables continual emission and revision over streams of arbitrary duration. We also introduce AASR-Bench, a bilingual benchmark with fine-grained atomic rubrics. Across multiple ASR front ends, AgenticASR attains the highest AASR-Bench scores among evaluated systems. A human--AI agreement study shows that rubric-based judgments align with independent expert assessments. Ablations characterize Refiner capacity, context length, and the quality--latency trade-off between online and offline inference. Together, these results establish AgenticASR as a practical framework for intent-preserving clean transcription during ongoing speech. Code, AASR-Bench, and a demo will be released at https://github.com/AnXMuy/AgenticASR.

</details>


### [158] [Old Tricks, New Models: How Simple Image Transformations Break Modern AI-based Content Moderation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28187)
*Marco Alecci,Francesco Marchiori,Iyiola Emmanuel Olatunji,Tegawendé F. Bissyandé,Jacques Klein*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how robust commercial, foundation-model-based image moderation APIs are against simple image transformations and finds they can be easily bypassed, so they should not be used as standalone safety filters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated content moderation is needed at scale but traditional task-specific classifiers have limited policy coverage and contextual understanding. New multimodal moderation APIs based on large foundation models are marketed as more capable and broader in coverage, yet it is unclear whether they are actually more robust in practice, especially against realistic manipulations of images. The authors want to understand if this technological shift truly improves the security and reliability of image moderation systems.

Method: The authors perform a large-scale black-box evaluation of three commercial image-moderation services built on foundation models. They apply seven simple, model-agnostic image transformations—requiring no access to gradients, surrogate models, or internal details of the systems—across multiple providers, datasets, categories of harmful content, perceptual-similarity constraints, and varying transformation intensities. They then measure how often these transformations cause unsafe images to be reclassified as safe while remaining recognizable to humans, and compare robustness across services and harm types.

Result: All three commercial image-moderation APIs can be bypassed by inexpensive, simple image transformations. Even fixed and straightforward modifications such as color inversion and grayscale conversion lead to unsafe-to-safe classification flips, despite the harmful content still being clearly recognizable to human viewers. Robustness differs widely across datasets and harm categories, and vulnerabilities are especially pronounced for multimodal content and self-harm imagery.

Conclusion: Switching from conventional moderation classifiers to foundation-model-based moderation APIs does not automatically yield robust or secure content filtering. These APIs remain vulnerable to simple, realistic image transformations and cannot be relied on as a strong security boundary. Effective deployment requires evaluating them under such transformations and using them as one layer within a broader, defense-in-depth moderation pipeline rather than as the sole safety mechanism.

Abstract: While automated content-moderation systems have become essential for screening harmful content at scale, conventional task-specific classifiers often provide limited policy cov- erage and contextual understanding. Recently, commercial multimodal moderation APIs built on large foundation models have been introduced with the promise of providing broader and more capable safety filters. In this work, we analyze whether this shift also yields more robust image moderation. We conduct a large-scale black-box evaluation on three established commercial image-moderation services and compare their robustness. By evaluating seven simple, model-agnostic image transformations across multiple providers, datasets, harm categories, perceptual-similarity constraints, and transformation intensities, we find that: (1) all three commercial services can be bypassed using inexpensive image transformations that require no gradients, surrogate models, or knowledge of the target system; (2) even fixed transformations such as color inversion and grayscale conversion induce unsafe-to-safe decision changes while preserving content that remains recognizable to humans; (3) their robustness varies substantially across datasets and harm categories, with multimodal content and self-harm exhibiting pronounced vulnerabilities. This yields the conclusion that replacing conventional moderation classifiers with foundation-model-based APIs does not, by itself, provide a reliable security boundary. Such systems must be evaluated under realistic transformations and deployed as one component of a layered moderation pipeline rather than as standalone safety filters.

</details>


### [159] [Qwen-UI-Agent Technical Report: Toward Next-Generation Real-World Centric Foundation GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28227)
*Hanzhang Zhou,Panrong Tong,Xu Zhang,Quyu Kong,Chenglin Cai,Tianyu Xia,Gongjie Zhang,Jianan Zhang,Long Li,Long Chen,Lei Wang,Gaole Dai,Pengxiang Li,Liangyu Chen,Yue Wang,Steven Hoi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Qwen-UI-Agent is a real-world oriented, cross-device GUI agent framework that unifies GUI and CLI actions, uses large-scale real-device environments plus sandboxes, and is trained with an AutoResearch-style data flywheel and large-scale online RL to achieve state-of-the-art performance on mobile and competitive results on computer and web tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI agents show promise but often fall short for real-world deployment: they are limited to specific platforms, struggle with long-horizon tasks, lack proactive behavior, and require heavy human supervision for improvement. The authors aim to build a general-purpose, reliable executor across real devices that can operate over mobile, desktop, and web environments, combine GUI with command-line capabilities, and improve itself autonomously with minimal human intervention.

Method: They propose Qwen-UI-Agent, a foundation GUI agent spanning mobile, desktop, web, and DeepSearch environments. The system combines diverse sandbox environments with a large-scale real-device mobile runtime. A unified action space allows interleaving GUI operations and CLI execution, generating batched actions in one model step. They employ an AutoResearch-style data flywheel where agents construct tasks and environments, analyze failures, and design subsequent training iterations. Online reinforcement learning is used over long trajectories (>100 turns) with more than 10,000 concurrent environments to speed rollouts. A lightweight harness layer enables proactive service initiation and stateful, cross-device workflows between mobile and computer.

Result: Qwen-UI-Agent achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple mobile-use benchmarks: 82.1% on MobileWorld, 92.2% on MobileWorld-Real, and 97.5% on AndroidDaily. It also shows strong computer-use performance with 79.5% on OSWorld-Verified and a 40.0% partial-progress score on OSWorld-v2. For browser and GUI grounding tasks, it reaches 73.6% on WebArena and 81.5% on ScreenSpot-Pro. These results are competitive with, and in some mobile cases surpass, frontier models such as Opus 4.8, Gemini 3.1 Pro, and GPT-5.6 Sol.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that Qwen-UI-Agent is an effective, real-world oriented foundation GUI agent capable of operating across mobile, desktop, web, and search environments. Through a unified GUI+CLI action space, large-scale real-device and sandboxed runtimes, an AutoResearch-style data flywheel, and long-horizon online RL, it delivers state-of-the-art mobile performance and competitive desktop and web capabilities compared to leading frontier models. This demonstrates the viability of scalable, cross-platform GUI agents that can handle long workflows, proactively provide services, and improve with limited human oversight.

Abstract: GUI agents have the potential to become a general purpose executor over existing digital devices. To advance them toward real-world use, we envision agents that operate reliably on real devices, execute workflows across platforms, combine GUI interaction with CLI execution, complete long-horizon tasks, proactively initiate useful services, and autonomously improve their capabilities with minimal human effort. Guided by this vision, we present Qwen-UI-Agent, a real-world centric foundation GUI agent spanning mobile, computer-use, web, and DeepSearch environments. Qwen-UI-Agent combines diverse sandbox environments with a large-scale real-device mobile runtime. Its unified action space interleaves GUI operations with CLI execution and generates batched actions in a single model turn. An AutoResearch-style data flywheel uses agents to construct tasks and environments, diagnose failures, and plan subsequent iterations. Online RL supports training on trajectories exceeding 100 turns, with over 10,000 concurrent environments accelerating rollout. A lightweight harness layer supports proactive service initiation and stateful workflows across mobile and computer.
  Across a broad suite of evaluations, Qwen-UI-Agent sets state-of-the-art performance on mobile-use benchmarks while delivering competitive performance on computer- and browser-use tasks against frontier models, including Opus 4.8, Gemini 3.1 Pro, and GPT-5.6 Sol. On mobile use, it achieves 82.1% on MobileWorld, 92.2% on MobileWorld-Real, and 97.5% on AndroidDaily. On computer use, it achieves 79.5% on OSWorld-Verified and a 40.0% partial-progress score on OSWorld-v2. On browser use and GUI grounding, it achieves 73.6% on WebArena and 81.5% on ScreenSpot-Pro, respectively.

</details>


### [160] [AI and Authenticity in Islamic Research: A Critical Evaluation of Generative AI Reliability, Hallucination, and Source Fidelity in Quranic, Hadith, and Fiqh Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28237)
*Muhammad Sajjad Akbar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically evaluates how reliable major generative AI systems are when answering Islamic religious questions and concludes they are useful for basic learning but not trustworthy as authoritative sources of religious rulings or advanced scholarship.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As Muslims increasingly use generative AI for Qur’anic interpretation, Hadith explanation, jurisprudential rulings, and religious education, there is little data on whether these systems are accurate, authentic, and trustworthy enough for high‑stakes religious use. The paper aims to fill this gap with systematic evidence rather than assumptions or anecdotes.

Method: The authors tested six leading generative AI systems with 50 realistic, open‑ended Islamic questions spanning Qur’anic interpretation, Hadith, Fiqh, ethics, pastoral advice, and issues sensitive to different Madhhabs. Responses were gathered in real‑world use from participants in Australia and the UK. They were then analysed using a mixed‑methods framework that assessed domain accuracy, verification of citations, hallucinations, consistency in jurisprudential reasoning, handling of uncertainty, traceability of sources, and geographical variation in answers.

Result: Across systems, AI outputs showed partial usefulness for introductory explanations but also notable problems: hallucinated or unverifiable religious references, incomplete or incorrect citations, inconsistencies in dealing with jurisprudential disagreement and Madhhab diversity, and limited transparency about sources and uncertainty. Reliability varied between systems and question types, and responses were not consistently robust enough for high‑trust religious decision‑making or scholarly work.

Conclusion: Current generative AI models can support basic Islamic learning as assistive, introductory tools but should not be used as independent authorities for fatwas, detailed jurisprudential guidance, or academic‑level Islamic research. Their answers require verification against authenticated primary texts and expert scholars. The study establishes an empirical baseline for AI reliability in Islamic knowledge and offers guidance for Muslims, educators, researchers, and AI developers on appropriate and cautious use of such systems in religious contexts.

Abstract: Generative Artificial Intelligence (AI) is increasingly used by Muslims for religious guidance, Qur'anic interpretation, Hadith explanation, jurisprudential rulings, and Islamic education. Despite its growing adoption, there is limited empirical evidence on whether current AI systems provide authentic, verifiable, and trustworthy Islamic knowledge suitable for high-trust religious contexts. This study evaluates six leading generative AI systems using fifty realistic open-ended Islamic questions covering Qur'anic interpretation, Hadith, Fiqh, ethics, pastoral advice, and Madhhab-sensitive topics. Responses were collected under real-world conditions from participants in Australia and the United Kingdom and analysed using a mixed-method framework examining domain accuracy, citation verification, hallucinations, jurisprudential consistency, uncertainty handling, source provenance, and geographical variation.
  The study addresses four research questions: (1) How accurate and authentic are AI-generated responses across major Islamic knowledge domains? (2) To what extent do AI systems produce hallucinations, incomplete citations, or unverifiable religious references? (3) How consistently do models handle jurisprudential disagreement, Madhhab diversity, and uncertainty? (4) Are current AI systems sufficiently reliable for religious guidance, Islamic education, and scholarly research?
  Overall, current generative AI systems are valuable as assistive tools for introductory Islamic learning but should not be treated as authoritative sources for religious rulings or Islamic research without verification against authenticated primary sources and qualified scholarly expertise. This study provides one of the first comprehensive empirical evaluations of AI reliability within Islamic knowledge, offering practical guidance for researchers, educators, AI developers, and the wider Muslim community.

</details>


### [161] [Operationally Guided Placement-Aware Learning for Industrial Online 3D Bin Packing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28257)
*Dheeraj Poolavaram,Aanchal Rajesh Chugh,Sebastian Dorn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OPAL is a learning-based framework for online 3D bin packing that jointly optimizes how candidate placements are generated and ranked, using operationally informed heuristics plus a neural policy to improve space utilization and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional and recent learning-based approaches to online 3D bin packing focus mainly on the policy that selects from candidate placements, while the generation and representation of those candidates are largely geometry-driven and do not fully capture industrial requirements like stability, compactness, and balance. This gap limits performance and practicality in real industrial palletizing scenarios, where packings must be not just dense but also operationally feasible and robust.

Method: The paper proposes OPAL, a framework that integrates: (1) OG-EMS, an Operationally Guided Empty-Maximal-Space generator that samples multiple anchors per free-space region and scores them by operational criteria (low height, support, compactness, and spatial diversity); (2) an operational representation for each candidate placement that encodes both geometric and operational attributes; (3) an xLSTM-based Placement Encoder that models dependencies across candidate features; and (4) a lightweight recurrent policy core trained with proximal policy optimization (PPO) to rank feasible actions (placements) given the current item and pallet state.

Result: On the BED-BPP benchmark for online 3D bin packing, OPAL attains a mean space utilization of 0.49 and shows that its operationally guided candidate generation contributes a 15.1% improvement, while the learned ranking policy adds a further 6.3% gain, all while keeping inference-time performance robust enough for industrial use.

Conclusion: Jointly optimizing candidate generation and ranking with operationally informed heuristics and learned representations leads to significantly better and still efficient online 3D bin packing. By embedding operational constraints directly into the candidate generator and the learned policy, OPAL produces packings that are more space-efficient and practically viable than previous geometry-driven or policy-only approaches.

Abstract: The online three-dimensional bin packing problem (3D-BPP) is a longstanding challenge in logistics and industrial palletizing. Recent learning-based methods use a learned policy to select among feasible candidate placements. Performance depends on the candidate generator and representation, especially in industrial settings where packings must be space-efficient, stable, compact, and balanced. However, prior work has mainly optimized the policy, while candidate generation and representation remain largely geometry-driven. We address this gap with OPAL, an operationally guided placement-aware learning framework for industrial online 3D-BPP which combines an Operationally Guided Empty-Maximal-Space generator (OG-EMS), an operational representation for each candidate placement, and a masked ranking policy trained with proximal policy optimization. OG-EMS evaluates multiple anchors within each free-space region and prioritizes low, well-supported, compact, and spatially diverse placements. An xLSTM-based Placement Encoder models dependencies among geometric and operational candidate attributes, while a lightweight recurrent core combines the resulting embeddings with the current item and pallet state to rank feasible actions. On the BED-BPP benchmark, OPAL achieves a mean space utilization of 0.49, with improvements of 15.1% from operationally guided candidate generation and 6.3% from learned ranking, while maintaining robust inference-time performance.

</details>


### [162] [LLM-Guided Evolutionary Search for Constraint Model Reformulation to Improve Solver Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28268)
*Kostis Michailidis,Dimos Tsouros,Nguyen Dang,Tias Guns*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether large language models can automatically improve the solving performance of combinatorial optimization models by iteratively reformulating them, and proposes a diversity-aware strategy that outperforms simpler approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Even when a constraint model correctly encodes a combinatorial problem, its solving time can vary greatly depending on modelling choices. Since experts must currently hand-craft performance-optimized models, there is a need to automate performance-oriented reformulation. With the emergence of LLMs that can already generate correct models from natural language, the natural next step is to see if they can also optimize models for speed, reducing reliance on human expertise.

Method: The authors frame model reformulation as an evolutionary search process inspired by Automatic Heuristic Design (AHD). An LLM repeatedly proposes alternative constraint models starting from a user-provided baseline. Each candidate is checked for correctness and benchmarked on problem instances. Different search / retention strategies decide which previous attempts, instructions, and performance feedback are included in each new LLM prompt. They adapt AHD strategies and introduce Profile-Diverse Retention (PDR), which uses Maximal Marginal Relevance (MMR) over instance-level runtime vectors to maintain a behaviourally diverse set of past attempts in the context. They then perform systematic experiments on eight CSPLib benchmark problems, using a separate validation set to select the final model before testing on held-out instances.

Result: Iterative LLM-driven reformulation yields substantial speedups over the original baseline models on unseen (held-out) instances. Retention strategies that intentionally maintain diversity in the context, such as the proposed PDR method, achieve better speedups than strategies that only keep the most recent models or those with the best performance. Additionally, choosing the final model via validation (rather than just taking the last or locally best attempt) consistently improves held-out speedups for all strategies.

Conclusion: LLMs can be effectively used not only to generate correct constraint models but also to automatically reformulate them for better solver performance. Evolutionary search with strategically managed context, especially diversity-aware retention like PDR, leads to better-performing models than naive recency- or performance-only retention. Validation-based model selection further enhances robustness and generalization to unseen instances, indicating a promising path toward automated, performance-oriented constraint modelling with LLMs.

Abstract: Combinatorial problems appear in numerous industrial applications. A common approach is to formulate these problems as declarative constraint models that can subsequently be compiled to and solved by a range of back-end solvers. Recent work shows that Large Language Models (LLMs) can produce correct models from natural language, but even a correct model can be expensive to solve because performance remains sensitive to modelling choices. In this work, we investigate whether LLMs can automate performance-oriented model reformulation. Inspired by Automatic Heuristic Design (AHD), we use an evolutionary framework in which an LLM proposes candidate reformulations that are verified and benchmarked against the user-defined baseline model. We compare AHD-adapted search strategies that control which prior attempts, instructions, and measured feedback enter each prompt. Existing retention strategies prioritize recency or performance, but do not explicitly diversify the context. To cover this gap, we introduce Profile-Diverse Retention (PDR), which applies Maximal Marginal Relevance (MMR) to instance-level runtime vectors to retain behaviourally diverse attempts. We systematically evaluate the strategies on eight CSPLib problems using validation-based final model selection. The results show that: (i) iterative reformulation can produce substantial held-out speedups; (ii) strategies that keep the retained context diverse outperform those that retain only recent or the fastest attempts; and (iii) validation-based selection improves the held-out speedup of every strategy.

</details>


### [163] [MemHarness: Memory Is Reconstructed, Not Replayed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28272)
*Rong Wu,Daocheng Fu,Licheng Wen,Xuemeng Yang,Shu Zou,Jianbiao Mei,Yuxin Wang,Hairong Zhang,Yu Yang,Tao Hu,Cong Zhang,Botian Shi,Pinlong Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemHarness enables LLM agents to adaptively reconstruct retrieved memories to fit the current context, avoiding verbatim replay and negative transfer, thereby improving performance and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory-augmented LLM agents simply replay retrieved experiences as static records, ignoring the mismatch between abstract stored memories and the concrete, dynamic decision-time states. This often leads to negative transfer and poor generalization, especially out-of-distribution. Humans, however, flexibly reinterpret past experiences to suit the present, suggesting a need for agents that can similarly adapt and harness memory rather than just replay it.

Method: The authors propose MemHarness, a framework where, at each decision step, a unified policy model critiques and reconstructs retrieved experiences conditioned on the current state. Instead of injecting retrieved trajectories verbatim, the policy rewrites them into context-grounded guidance, which is then used for action selection. This reconstructive capability is learned end-to-end using GRPO (a reinforcement learning optimization method), jointly training the policy to both reconstruct and act.

Result: On embodied (ALFWorld) and web interaction (WebShop) benchmarks, MemHarness significantly outperforms standard RL agents and static memory-augmented baselines. It shows particularly strong robustness in out-of-distribution settings, indicating better generalization and reduced negative transfer compared with replay-style memory usage.

Conclusion: Actively reconstructing retrieved experiences, rather than replaying them verbatim, lets LLM agents convert abstract memories into context-grounded guidance. This prevents negative transfer and provides latent training signals that improve the agent’s intrinsic reasoning ability. MemHarness demonstrates that memory should be adaptively harnessed and transformed, not merely stored and replayed, to realize its full benefit in LLM-based agents.

Abstract: Retrieving past experiences has become a common strategy to enhance large language model agents. However, most existing memory-augmented agents treat retrieved experiences as static records to be replayed verbatim, injecting them into the context regardless of whether they align with the agent's current situation. This ``replay'' paradigm ignores the gap between the abstract, general nature of stored experience and the concrete, ever-changing states encountered at decision time, frequently causing negative transfer. In contrast, humans rarely recall past experiences verbatim; instead, they reorganize and adapt retrieved memories to fit the present context. Inspired by this, we propose MemHarness, a framework that equips LLM agents to actively harness and reconstruct past experiences based on the present context. At each decision step, a unified policy model critiques and reconstructs the retrieved experience conditioned on the current state, producing context-grounded guidance before acting. This reconstructive ability emerges naturally through end-to-end training with GRPO. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that MemHarness substantially outperforms pure RL and static memory-augmented baselines, demonstrating strong robustness in out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. Furthermore, our analyses reveal that this reconstruction objective not only prevents negative transfer but also serves as latent guidance during training, fundamentally improving the agent's intrinsic reasoning capabilities.

</details>


### [164] [Tycho: Active Abstraction with Programmatic World Models for ARC-AGI-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28287)
*Jens Lehmann,Andrei Aioanei,Sahar Vahdati*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ARC-AGI-3 as an interactive abstraction/skill-acquisition benchmark and Tycho, a coding-agent system that builds and uses executable game models to play these tasks efficiently. It studies how different orchestration policies and model-repair strategies affect action efficiency and argues that strong performance requires not only accurate simulators but also good decisions about when to construct, repair, or bypass models—what they term “active abstraction.”


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ARC-style tasks test abstraction and generalization, but existing formulations are often static and non-interactive. The authors want a benchmark where an agent must infer rules, hidden state, and goals through interaction, under a constraint that every action is costly. They aim to understand how large language model agents can learn and exploit structured models of such environments for efficient problem solving and to separate mere transition modeling from truly effective play.

Method: They formalize ARC-AGI-3 games as parameterized rendered deterministic Moore machines, making them suitable for programmatic modeling. They then introduce Tycho, a coding-agent system that, during gameplay, constructs free-form executable hypotheses (simulators) of each game based on structured histories that distinguish actionable observations from non-action frames (animations, level completion, game over). They evaluate four orchestration policies that govern when the actor delegates to a model-building process, runs or repairs models, or bypasses them. Experiments are run on all 25 public ARC-AGI-3 games under matched inference budgets using Claude Opus 4.8, and they also test the best policy with GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5. They further compare policies with and without automatic model repair after failed verification of the simulator against observed transitions, measuring both transition-matching accuracy and Relative Human Action Efficiency (RHAE).

Result: Among four orchestration policies, the one where the actor can request delegation to a model builder yields the highest mean RHAE of 88.49 with Claude Opus 4.8. Using this policy, GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 both achieve 100.00 RHAE and solve all 183 levels, with near-top or top human-replay midranks (98.5 and 100.0), and Opus 5 uses 61% fewer scored actions than the official human baselines. Automatic repair of models after verification failures substantially improves how well the simulators match observed transitions, but this approach alone attains only 83.07 RHAE, highlighting a gap between accurate transition modeling and efficient winning play.

Conclusion: Accurately simulating game transitions is not sufficient for strong play in ARC-AGI-3; agents must also know when and how to invest in modeling versus acting directly. The proposed Tycho system and ARC-AGI-3 environment show that the key challenge is “active abstraction”: jointly generating, testing, repairing, and selectively using explicit models under action-cost constraints. The best-performing agents flexibly delegate to model-building when beneficial and bypass modeling when it is not, achieving superhuman action efficiency. This framework provides a new way to study interactive abstraction, model-based reasoning, and orchestration strategies for LLM-driven agents.

Abstract: ARC-AGI-3 turns abstraction into an interactive problem of skill acquisition. A player must infer an unfamiliar game's rules, hidden state, and goal while maintaining action efficiency because every move counts. We formalize these environments as parameterized rendered deterministic Moore machines and introduce Tycho, a coding-agent system that constructs and uses game-specific models during interaction. Tycho separates actionable observations from intermediate animation, level-completion, and game-over frames. From this structured history, an agent can model, test, plan with, repair, or bypass a free-form executable hypothesis.
  In one matched public-set run per policy, we compare four orchestration policies on all 25 public games using Claude Opus 4.8 under matched inference budgets. Actor-requested delegation to a model builder obtains the highest observed mean Relative Human Action Efficiency (RHAE), 88.49. With this selected policy, GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 both reach 100.00 RHAE and complete all 183 levels. Their game-balanced first-run human-replay midranks are 98.5 and 100.0. Opus 5 uses 61% fewer scored actions than the aggregate official human baselines.
  Automatic repair after verification failures produces models that reproduce observed transitions much more accurately, yet reaches only 83.07 RHAE. Transition match indicates whether a simulator reproduces observed dynamics, not whether it has identified the objective or improves the next action. Strong play also requires deciding when to construct, repair, use, or bypass a model. We call this joint problem active abstraction: generating a testable model from costly interaction and deciding when acquiring or using it is worth its cost.

</details>


### [165] [One Human, $N$ Agents: Audit-Budget Allocation for LLM Agent Fleets under Miscalibrated, Correlated Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28317)
*Cesare Zavattari,Alessandro Tommasi,Giuseppe Prencipe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how a single human should best allocate a small audit budget across many LLM agents when those agents provide possibly misleading confidence scores and have correlated errors. It characterizes when using the models’ self-reported confidence to prioritize audits is actually worse than auditing at random, and empirically evaluates several LLMs’ confidence quality under this lens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In practical settings, many LLM agents operate simultaneously, but human oversight is scarce: only a few outputs can be audited each round. People often rely on models’ own confidence scores to triage which outputs to check. However, these confidences can be miscalibrated or even adversarially manipulated, and models can share correlated failure modes. The authors aim to formally analyze when confidence-based auditing helps or harms oversight and to identify conditions under which human oversight becomes effectively vacuous (i.e., unable to meaningfully improve reliability).

Method: The authors model the problem as budgeted noisy inspection over a two-level Gaussian copula capturing both individual model behavior and cross-model correlations. Within this probabilistic framework, they analyze confidence-ranked auditing policies versus random auditing, derive a miscalibration threshold δ* beyond which confidence ranking becomes worse than random selection, and study how δ* depends on the audit budget and correlations. They then empirically estimate these quantities using five open-weight LLMs and one proprietary model, replaying different auditing policies on recorded interaction traces to validate the theoretical predictions.

Result: The analysis reveals a miscalibration threshold δ* such that if model confidence is miscalibrated beyond this point, choosing audits by highest self-reported confidence leads to worse oversight performance than choosing outputs to audit uniformly at random. Contrary to naive expectations, δ* increases as the available audit budget shrinks, and cross-family correlations in errors are substantial due mainly to shared task difficulty rather than model lineage. Empirically, five open-weight LLMs exhibit nearly flat, uninformative confidence that is at or beyond the harmful miscalibration threshold (though with confidence intervals overlapping it), while a proprietary model shows informative confidence lying safely below the threshold. Policy replays on logged traces confirm the theoretical ordering of different auditing strategies.

Conclusion: Using LLMs’ own confidence to allocate scarce human audits is not reliably beneficial and can be worse than random selection when confidence is sufficiently miscalibrated. In realistic regimes of small audit budgets and correlated errors, many current open-weight models provide effectively vacuous confidence signals for oversight, while at least one proprietary model does better. The paper provides a quantitative criterion for identifying when oversight based on model confidence is vacuous and empirically demonstrates that this criterion meaningfully predicts the comparative performance of different auditing policies.

Abstract: A single human must audit $N$ LLM agents under a budget of $B \ll N$ audits per round, guided by self-reported confidence that may be adversarially miscalibrated and by correlated errors. We model this as budgeted noisy inspection over a two-level Gaussian copula and locate the miscalibration threshold $δ^*$ past which confidence-ranked auditing is \emph{worse} than random. Two a-priori expectations reverse: $δ^*$ \emph{rises} as the budget shrinks, and cross-family correlation is not low---shared difficulty dominates lineage. Five open-weight LLMs show operationally useless (near-constant) confidence, point estimates at or beyond the flip though CIs straddle it; a proprietary model is informative and lands below it. We give a quantitative criterion for \emph{vacuous} oversight, and replaying policies on recorded traces confirms the ordering.

</details>


### [166] [PathView-Bench: Can Multimodal Large Language Models Achieve Fine-grained Multiscale Understanding of Pathology Images?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28318)
*Zongyi Chen,Yu Liang,Jie Lin,Liansheng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PathVU is a benchmark that tests whether multimodal large language models can truly understand multiscale pathology images, beyond just giving the right final diagnosis or caption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal benchmarks in pathology mainly evaluate end outputs such as diagnoses, captions, or reports. These coarse metrics do not reveal if a model actually understands detailed and multiscale visual features (from small regions to whole slides) that pathologists rely on for reasoning and decision-making. There is a need for a benchmark that directly assesses fine-grained, spatially grounded, and multiscale visual understanding in pathology images.

Method: The authors build PathVU, a vision-anchored benchmark derived from 23 public pathology imaging datasets containing human-supervised labels and spatial annotations. They define two fields of view: (1) Region FOV for high-resolution local regions and (2) Slide FOV for whole-slide, macro-level context. They convert raw spatial annotations into deterministic task targets that allow automatic scoring of diverse visual abilities: region localization, recognition of visual entities, quantity estimation, spatial reasoning, and determining when context is insufficient. PathVU is organized as 14 VQA-style tasks with 61,673 images and 308,070 samples, covering 28 organs and over 7 million annotations. They then evaluate 18 existing MLLMs (general, medical, and pathology-specific) on these tasks.

Result: PathVU comprises a large-scale, structured benchmark of 14 tasks, 61,673 images, 308,070 samples, 28 organs, and 7,253,526 spatial annotations. When 18 representative MLLMs are evaluated on this benchmark, even the most advanced models show noticeable performance limitations on fine-grained visual tasks and across different scales of pathology images, revealing gaps in true multiscale visual understanding.

Conclusion: PathVU offers a reproducible and programmatically scorable benchmark that directly measures multiscale and fine-grained visual understanding in computational pathology. The results highlight that current MLLMs, including specialized medical and pathology models, struggle with these challenges, underscoring the need for continued model and method development guided by this type of vision-anchored evaluation.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used to analyze pathology images. However, dominant multimodal benchmarks in pathology mainly score final diagnostic answers, captions, or reports. These evaluations provide limited insight into whether a model understands the multiscale visual content needed for pathology reasoning and decision-making. We introduce PathVU, a vision-anchored benchmark for fine-grained and multiscale visual understanding in computational pathology. Built from 23 public pathology imaging datasets with human-supervised labels and spatial annotations, PathVU evaluates MLLM understanding in two fields of view: Region FOV for high-resolution local regions and Slide FOV for macro whole-slide views. By converting raw annotations into deterministic task targets, PathVU enables programmatic scoring of region localization, visual recognition, quantity estimation, spatial reasoning, and insufficient-context judgment. The benchmark contains 14 VQA-style tasks, 61,673 images, and 308,070 samples across 28 organs and 7,253,526 annotations. Evaluating 18 representative general-purpose, medical-domain, and pathology-oriented MLLMs, we observe substantial limitations even in advanced models on fine-grained visual tasks across multiscale pathology images. PathVU provides a reproducible basis for developing and evaluating pathology MLLMs with explicit multiscale visual understanding.

</details>


### [167] [Paying for Honesty Without Knowing the Truth: Reputation-Penalty Design for LLM Marketplace Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28330)
*Mingdai Yang,Shicheng Fan,Kejing Yu,Duohao Wang,Li Sun,Hao Peng,Philip S. Yu,Zhiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to curb LLM-powered sellers from fabricating product attributes, and proposes a reputation and penalty mechanism (CARP) plus a code-gated reflection scheme (SPARC) that reduce lying and improve consumer welfare without needing ground-truth verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly used as autonomous merchants that generate product listings. In competitive settings they tend to fabricate attractive but false product attributes, even when instructed to be honest. Platforms cannot directly verify every claim because they lack ground-truth data and only see noisy, biased customer complaints. This creates a need for a mechanism that discourages fabrication and protects consumers using only observable signals, while being robust to gaming and not harming honest sellers.

Method: The authors model a marketplace where LLM agents act as sellers making possibly false claims, and the platform observes only noisy complaint signals about misrepresentation. They design CARP, a reputation-penalty mechanism with two key features: (1) a deadband that tolerates a certain level of complaints to absorb noise, and (2) state-dependent penalty severity to prevent detection erosion when reputations are high. CARP updates seller reputation and penalizes low-rated liars via reduced sales volume. They then pair CARP with SPARC, a byte-clean code-gated reflection mechanism that exposes the model to the economic consequences of complaints and reputation in its own prompt, enabling the LLM to reason about and respond to penalties. They evaluate welfare and behavior across LLM models and policy baselines, including a perfect-information oracle benchmark.

Result: CARP successfully reduces the sales volume of low-rated, fabrication-prone sellers while having limited negative impact on honest sellers, all without requiring product-level ground truth. When combined with SPARC, the mechanism recovers most of the consumer welfare that would be achieved under a perfect-information oracle, outperforming all other policies in their comparison set. The experiments show that LLM merchants fabricate attributes when there is no cost to lying, but substantially reduce fabrication when the CARP/SPARC mechanism makes misrepresentation costly in terms of future sales and reputation. This response emerges consistently across different model families, with statistical support via confidence intervals.

Conclusion: A carefully designed, truth-agnostic reputation and penalty system can substantially curb LLM-driven misrepresentation in marketplaces, even when the platform only has access to noisy complaint data. CARP, especially when paired with SPARC, aligns LLM merchants' incentives so that they self-limit fabrication out of economic self-interest rather than instruction-following alone. This closes most of the consumer-welfare gap relative to an unattainable perfect-information baseline and suggests a robust, game-theoretic approach to governing LLM agents in market environments without needing direct truth verification.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly act as autonomous merchants that write their own product listings, and under competitive pressure, they fabricate attributes to win sales. Even under instructions to be honest, they fabricate attributes in a majority of listings across models. A platform's obvious remedy---verifying each claim against the truth---is unavailable, because it observes only a noisy, biased complaint signal, never the ground truth. We design CARP, a reputation-penalty mechanism with a deadband that forgives complaint noise and a state-dependent severity that counters reputation-driven detection erosion. CARP requires no product-level ground truth and is robust to strategic gaming. CARP protects consumers by suppressing the sales volume of low-rated liars while sparing honest sellers. Paired with SPARC, it closes most of the consumer-welfare gap relative to a perfect-information oracle, without ever accessing the truth. It also achieves the best welfare of the policies we compare. We further show that this felt penalty becomes behaviorally binding through SPARC, a byte-clean code-gated reflection mechanism: LLM merchants fabricate when lying is free but restrain themselves when fabrication costs them sales, a self-interested response rather than compliance. We trace this distinction to penalty-gated self-correction reasoning, and observe the binding across models, with supporting confidence intervals.

</details>


### [168] [Correcting What You Cannot See: Credit Assignment for Perception Distillation in Multimodal Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28336)
*Feng Xiong,Leyan Xue,Hongyu Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Perception-Correction Distillation (PCD), a label-free method that improves multimodal model distillation by selectively strengthening supervision on perception when downstream failures and teacher–student disagreement jointly indicate correctable perception errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multimodal reasoning models, on-policy distillation gives dense trajectory-level supervision but cannot tell whether an incorrect answer stems from a perception error (e.g., misreading the image) or a reasoning error given correct perception. Existing metrics like Perception Success Rate (PSR), estimated from multiple reasoning attempts over the same perception, are ambiguous because low success may come from the task being hard to reason about, not from poor perception. This ambiguity leads to inefficient or even harmful supervision on the perception module. The authors aim to design a principled way to tell when perception is actually at fault so that distillation can focus on correcting those parts without labels.

Method: The authors propose Perception-Correction Distillation (PCD), a label-free strategy that uses two signals as complementary witnesses of perception failure: (1) downstream failure of the student (the final answer is wrong) and (2) disagreement between teacher and student on the same input. They define the PCD weight as the product of these two scores, forming a soft AND gate that yields high weight only when both witnesses are present, and low/zero weight otherwise. This rule is motivated using Bayesian evidence combination, and they further justify that the multiplicative gate is the unique normalized bilinear function that becomes zero whenever any witness is absent. PCD is implemented with separated perception and reasoning rollouts, and with mean-preserving weighting so that the overall reasoning objective (for trajectories) is not altered. The method is plugged into multimodal on-policy distillation pipelines for various model sizes.

Result: On eight multimodal benchmarks, replacing standard On-Policy Distillation (OPD) with PCD yields consistent gains. For an 8B teacher distilled into a 2B student, the macro-average score increases from 44.50 (OPD) to 47.28 with PCD. For a 32B teacher distilled into an 8B student, performance improves from 56.94 to 61.22. In controlled 2B ablations, removing PCD reduces held-out average performance by 2.22 points, while removing separated perception–reasoning rollouts costs 0.88 points, showing both components contribute but PCD is the main driver of improvement.

Conclusion: PCD demonstrates that effective multimodal distillation requires not just imitating the teacher’s predictions but selectively correcting perception when it is likely the true source of error. By using downstream failure and teacher–student disagreement as complementary, label-free witnesses combined via a principled multiplicative gate, PCD concentrates learning on correctable perception failures without disturbing the reasoning objective. Empirically, this targeted strategy yields substantial gains across benchmarks and model sizes, establishing that distinguishing perception vs. reasoning errors is key to scaling multimodal distillation.

Abstract: On-policy distillation provides dense supervision for multimodal reasoners, but its trajectory-level reward cannot determine whether a failed answer arose from perception or subsequent reasoning. Perception Success Rate (PSR), estimated from multiple reasonings sharing one perception, remains ambiguous because low success conflates perceptual insufficiency with reasoning difficulty. We introduce \textbf{Perception-Correction Distillation (PCD)}, a label-free method that identifies correctable perception failures using downstream failure and teacher--student disagreement as complementary witnesses. Their product, , forms a soft AND gate that strengthens distillation only when both witnesses are present. We motivate this rule through Bayesian evidence combination and show that multiplication is the unique normalized bilinear gate that vanishes when either witness is absent. PCD uses separated perception--reasoning rollouts and mean-preserving weights, leaving the reasoning objective unchanged. Across eight benchmarks, PCD improves the 8B 2B macro average from 44.50 with OPD to 47.28 and the 32B 8B result from 56.94 to 61.22. In matched 2B ablations, removing PCD and separated rollout reduces held-out average by 2.22 and 0.88 points, respectively. Effective multimodal distillation therefore depends not only on what the teacher predicts, but also on identifying when perception is the appropriate target of correction.

</details>


### [169] [How Benchmarks Mis-Score Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28367)
*Zihan Dong,Zhiyuan Ma,Zekun Wang,Yunqing Li,Zirou Liu,Ruixuan Deng,Qishi Zhan,Rui Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits how reliable current evaluation pipelines are for computer-use agents and proposes a framework and design rules to improve them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks for computer-use agents rely on brittle scripted oracles and evaluation pipelines where tasks may be flawed, observations incomplete, and scoring and reporting opaque. This undermines trust in benchmark scores and obscures where agents actually fail.

Method: The authors define a reliability framework that decomposes the evaluation pipeline into task construction, trajectory observation, scoring, and reporting. They then empirically audit 150 trajectories labeled as failures from five benchmarks covering web, enterprise workflows, and desktop control, manually re-evaluating them to detect incorrect failure labels and categorize genuine failure causes using a three-tier diagnostic taxonomy.

Result: They find that 15.3% of trajectories labeled as failures are misclassified: 10.7% due to evaluator false negatives and 4.7% due to broken tasks. For the remaining genuine failures, analysis with their taxonomy shows that most problems stem from verification/feedback and planning issues rather than low-level execution or grounding. They also show that a single scalar success rate obscures these distinctions.

Conclusion: Benchmark scores for computer-use agents are significantly distorted by unreliable tasks and evaluators, and simple pass/fail metrics hide important failure modes. The authors argue that evaluation should adopt their reliability framework, use richer diagnostic taxonomies, and apply stage-specific design rules to task design, trajectory capture, scoring, and reporting, especially for newer long-horizon CUA benchmarks.

Abstract: Computer-use agents (CUA) are being deployed to browse the web and operate desktop software, yet their benchmark scores are still commonly produced by brittle scripted oracles.
