A score is the output of a pipeline in which tasks can be stale, trajectories can omit decisive visual evidence, evaluators can reject valid alternatives, and aggregate reports can hide the cause of failure. We organize these problems into a reliability framework spanning task construction, trajectory observation, scoring, and reporting. We then audit 150 public failure-scored trajectories from five web, enterprise-workflow, and desktop-control benchmarks, find that 15.3\% of FAIL verdicts are wrong: 10.7\% are evaluator false negatives and 4.7\% are broken tasks. For genuine failures, a three-tier diagnostic taxonomy shows that verification/feedback and planning failures dominate execution/grounding errors, while a single scalar success rate can not explain. We connect these findings to newer long-horizon CUA benchmarks and derive stage-specific design rules for CUA evaluation.

</details>


### [170] [HyperClaim: Fine-Grained Cross-Modal Hypergraph Reasoning for Video Misinformation Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28375)
*Xiangbo Wang,Jiasheng Zhang,Xingtong Yu,Luoqiang Lei,Delvin Ce Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces HyperClaim, a temporal hypergraph-based model that detects video misinformation by explicitly modeling higher-order interactions between query text, contextual text, and video frames, outperforming prior fusion and reasoning approaches on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video misinformation detectors either use global multimodal fusion or free-form multimodal reasoning. Both tend to overlook fine-grained, localized authenticity cues that appear only when specific query phrases, context tokens, and short temporal snippets of video are considered jointly. Standard pairwise graph models cannot adequately represent these inherently higher-order, multi-way cross-modal dependencies, creating a need for a model that directly captures such complex interactions while remaining discriminative and efficient.

Method: The paper proposes HyperClaim, a discriminative temporal hypergraph framework for classifying the authenticity of video samples. Using the video title or provided paired text as a claim-like query, HyperClaim constructs a sparse heterogeneous hypergraph with three node types: query tokens, evidence tokens, and sampled video frames. It then: (1) builds compact evidence units by confidence-aware filtering and source budgeting, grouping text and temporally local frames; (2) performs adaptive soft-incidence reasoning over this hypergraph, with residual calibration between text and video representations; and (3) applies a discrepancy-aware readout that aggregates textual, visual, and hyperedge states for final classification, without relying on generated rationales or external tools.

Result: On the FactGuard temporal benchmark, HyperClaim reaches 83.7% accuracy on FakeSV, 82.0% on FakeTT, and 87.3% on FakeVV, surpassing both strong discriminative baselines and reasoning-focused models. The learned hyperedge incidence and attention distributions provide interpretable structures at the token and frame levels, highlighting which text and video segments contribute most to authenticity judgments.

Conclusion: Modeling video misinformation detection as temporal hypergraph reasoning over query text, contextual text, and video frames enables better capture of localized, higher-order cross-modal authenticity cues than global fusion or pairwise graphs. HyperClaim’s temporal hypergraph, adaptive incidence reasoning, and discrepancy-aware readout collectively yield superior accuracy across multiple datasets, while also exposing interpretable token- and frame-level evidence patterns. This suggests hypergraph-based multimodal modeling is a promising direction for fine-grained misinformation detection and related authenticity assessment tasks.

Abstract: Video misinformation detection is often approached through global multimodal fusion or free-form multimodal reasoning. Both paradigms can under-represent localized authenticity cues that arise from coupled interactions among query phrases, contextual text, and short temporal spans of frames. Because such interactions are inherently higher-order, pairwise graph formulations are insufficient to capture multi-way cross-modal dependencies, whereas hypergraphs offer a suitable representation for these relations. We propose HyperClaim, a discriminative temporal hypergraph framework for sample-level authenticity classification. Using the title or benchmark-provided paired text as a claim-like query, HyperClaim constructs a sparse heterogeneous hypergraph over query tokens, evidence tokens, and sampled frames; applies confidence-aware filtering and source budgeting to form compact text-frame and short-range temporal evidence units; performs adaptive soft-incidence reasoning with residual text-video calibration; and aggregates textual, visual, and hyperedge states through a discrepancy-aware readout. Without relying on generated rationales or external tool calls, HyperClaim preserves fine-grained cross-modal and temporal structure that global fusion tends to flatten. Under the FactGuard temporal protocol, it achieves 83.7%, 82.0%, and 87.3% accuracy on FakeSV, FakeTT, and FakeVV, respectively, outperforming strong discriminative and reasoning-centric baselines. Learned incidence and attention weights further reveal token- and frame-level structure.

</details>


### [171] [When Specifications Conflict: A Symmetry-Based Framework for Measuring LLM Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28384)
*Tairan Wang,Liang Zhou,Zikang Zhan,Pingchuan Yan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a controlled framework to study how LLMs choose between conflicting specifications and shows they follow systematic, representation-dependent preferences rather than behaving randomly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often see multiple, sometimes inconsistent sources of guidance (instructions, examples, specs), but we lack a clear, controllable way to analyze which source they prioritize and why, especially in a way that is attributable and comparable across representation types.

Method: The authors construct synthetic tasks with explicitly conflicting specifications (e.g., different descriptions of the same function), designed symmetrically so that each representation type is treated fairly. They then evaluate LLM behavior on an executable mathematical benchmark with 550 conflict instances across 11 function families, using four specification representations: pure natural language, formal language, naturalized formal language, and input–output examples. They extend this to heterogeneous conflicts in Boolean algebra, code generation, and clinical tasks.

Result: Across 550 controlled conflicts, LLMs exhibit stable, non-random preference patterns over specification representations, consistently favoring formal and naturalized-formal specifications over pure natural-language descriptions, and those over input–output examples; effects vary with model capability and function family. The extended experiments show similar applicability of the framework in Boolean algebra, programming, and clinical scenarios.

Conclusion: LLMs resolve conflicts between competing specifications in systematic, representation-sensitive ways. The proposed framework offers a unified, controlled methodology to elicit, measure, and compare these preferences across tasks and domains, enabling more attributable analysis of how LLMs reconcile conflicting information.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly required to integrate multiple sources of information that may be inconsistent or conflicting. However, there is still a lack of controllable and attributable methods for analyzing how models resolve conflicts between competing specifications. We propose a controlled experimental framework for studying model preferences under conflicting specifications. By constructing specifications with explicit conflicts, the framework enables model choices between competing specifications to be directly observed and analyzed. A symmetry-based design further reduces confounding factors, allowing preferences across representation types to be compared systematically.
  We evaluate the framework on an executable mathematical benchmark with 550 conflict instances spanning 11 function families, comparing four representation types: pure natural language, formal language, naturalized formal language, and input--output examples. Results show systematic preference patterns rather than random behavior, with a consistent ordering: $ \text{Formal} \approx \text{Naturalized Formal} > \text{Pure Natural Language} > \text{Input--Output Examples} $. Example effects further depend on model capability and function family.
  We extend the framework to heterogeneous specification conflicts in Boolean algebra, code generation, and the clinical domain, demonstrating its applicability across diverse tasks and specification forms. The framework provides a unified approach for measuring how LLMs resolve conflicts between competing sources of information.

</details>


### [172] [QuantWAMs: Calibrating at the Right Granularity for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28405)
*Jiacheng Zhou,Jinfan Lv,Ruixuan Li,Longtai Zhang,Yan Wang,Wenqiang Zhang,Lizhe Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuantWAMs is a post-training quantization framework tailored for World Action Models that preserves performance while greatly reducing memory and improving speed, even in real-robot deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World Action Models are powerful but expensive to deploy because they require iterative denoising and closed-loop execution, and existing post-training quantization approaches do not match their structure or deployment distributions, leading to poor efficiency-accuracy trade-offs.

Method: The paper proposes QuantWAMs, which aligns quantization with the actual calibration context of WAMs using three key strategies: (1) shared-basis outlier calibration that only shares activation statistics across structurally compatible modules; (2) co-training-objective saliency that uses empirical-Fisher scores from the joint video-action gradient to allocate layer-wise weight precision; and (3) fixed-intervention rollout auditing that audits closed-loop rollouts to adjust which denoising steps are protected, without changing the overall precision budget.

Result: On Fast-WAM and LingBot-VA across RoboTwin 2.0, LIBERO, and real-robot AgiBot G2 manipulation benchmarks, QuantWAMs under a mainly W4A4 configuration keeps performance within 0.2–0.7 percentage points of FP16, while cutting peak weight-and-activation memory to ~29% of FP16 and achieving 1.4–1.6× speedups at the block level.

Conclusion: QuantWAMs demonstrates that task- and structure-aware PTQ can make World Action Models significantly more efficient for both simulation and real-robot deployment, preserving near-FP16 performance while substantially reducing memory and improving speed without changing model architecture or requiring retraining.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) jointly predict future observations and actions, but their iterative denoising and closed-loop execution make efficient deployment costly. Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods are poorly suited to WAMs because they rely on open-loop objectives, homogeneous model assumptions, and calibration distributions that do not reflect deployment. We present QuantWAMs, a PTQ framework that aligns quantization decisions with the calibration context defined by model structure, rollout distribution, and task objective. QuantWAMs introduces three strategies: shared-basis outlier calibration, which pools activation evidence only across coordinate-compatible modules; co-training-objective saliency, which computes empirical-Fisher scores from the joint video--action gradient and assigns weight precision at a calibration-stable layer granularity; and fixed-intervention rollout auditing, which revises denoising-step protection schedules using reachable closed-loop states without changing the precision budget. We evaluate QuantWAMs on Fast-WAM and LingBot-VA across RoboTwin 2.0, LIBERO, and real-robot manipulation with an AgiBot G2. Under a W4A4-dominant setting, the reported simulation means differ from FP16 by 0.2--0.7 percentage points. Real-robot trials further establish deployment feasibility on three manipulation tasks. For the targeted video and action blocks, QuantWAMs reduces peak weight-and-activation memory to about 29\% of FP16 and provides 1.4--1.6$\times$ block-level speedups.

</details>


### [173] [When Derived Measurements Mislead: Quantifying and Mitigating LLM Over-Trust with Privileged-Modality Reliability Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28421)
*Zongheng Guo,Tao Chen,Tianli Li,Mingzhe Cui,Yang Jiao,Lei Xie,Yi Pan,Xiao Hu,Manuela Ferrario*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces and quantifies “derived-feature over-trust” (DFOT), where LLMs treat instance-dependent derived measurements (like signals processed from sensors) as universally valid facts, and proposes an evaluation framework using physiological sensing (PPG vs ECG) to measure and partially mitigate this problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many LLM-based systems now ingest not only raw facts but also derived measurements (e.g., sensor-based estimates, model-generated scores). These derived values can be wrong for specific instances or only valid under certain conditions, yet downstream LLMs often treat them as hard facts. This "over-trust" can be dangerous in high-stakes domains like medicine. There is a lack of systematic ways to define, detect, and quantify this type of error, particularly when the LLM itself never sees the gold-standard source (e.g., ECG) but only a derived signal (e.g., PPG-based rhythm estimates).

Method: The authors formalize derived-feature over-trust (DFOT) and design two diagnostic tasks: (D1) whether an LLM accepts a PPG-derived heart rhythm that conflicts with an offline ECG reference, and (D2) whether it wrongly rejects a reliable PPG rhythm when misled by a severe clinical history. They define five estimands: (1) Conflict Over-Trust Rate (COTR) for D1, (2) Context-Induced Error Rate (CIR) for D2, (3) Correct Repair Rate (CRR) for fixing frozen errors when new evidence arrives, (4) Evidence-Specific Repair Margin (ESRM) to compare matched vs shuffled evidence, and (5) Utility Harm Rate (UHR) capturing unnecessary verification in high-reliability cases. They instantiate this framework in a physiological sensing case study using 50,000 paired PPG-ECG records. ECG acts as privileged supervision to train an ECG-to-PPG distillation baseline, but the LLM only operates on PPG-derived information at inference time. Evaluation is done on a protocol-locked 187-patient test set.

Result: Using their ECG-supervised distillation as a baseline reliability generator and PPG-only inference, the authors apply their DFOT evaluation on a 187-patient locked test set. The baseline model improves four repair/specificity-related endpoints (including CRR and ESRM-related measures) by 1.82–6.69 percentage points relative to a comparison system, with all paired confidence intervals excluding zero, indicating statistically significant gains. However, the Utility Harm Rate (UHR) slightly increases by 0.67 percentage points, with a 95% confidence interval of −0.4 to +1.7, meaning the increase in unnecessary verification is small and not statistically conclusive. Overall, the method reduces harmful over-trust and improves evidence-specific repair without clearly worsening utility.

Conclusion: DFOT is proposed as a general concept and measurement framework for evaluating how LLMs handle derived measurements they might over-trust. The paper shows, in a PPG vs ECG rhythm case study, that using privileged supervision (ECG) to train a better reliability generator can significantly improve multiple DFOT-related metrics while only marginally affecting verification behavior. The framework is independent of any specific reliability generator and can serve as a standard evaluation target for future, stronger mitigation approaches to prevent LLMs from treating instance-dependent derived features as universally reliable facts.

Abstract: Derived measurements increasingly enter large language model (LLM) pipelines as direct facts despite their instance-dependent validity. We define derived-feature over-trust (DFOT) as the failure in which a downstream LLM assigns such a measurement the epistemic status of a direct fact or uses it outside its valid scope. Using physiological sensing as a case study, D1 tests acceptance of a PPG-derived rhythm contradicted by offline ECG, whereas D2 tests rejection of an offline-confirmed reliable PPG rhythm under misleading severe history. ECG supplies training supervision and offline reference construction but is never shown to the LLM. Five estimands quantify this chain: conflict over-trust rate (COTR) and context-induced error rate (CIR) characterize D1/D2; correct repair rate (CRR) measures frozen-error repair; evidence-specific repair margin (ESRM) contrasts matched and patient-disjoint shuffled evidence; and utility harm rate (UHR) measures unnecessary verification among HIGH-reliability cases used without verification at baseline. The framework does not depend on a particular reliability generator. We demonstrate it on 50,000 paired PPG-ECG records using ECG-to-PPG privileged distillation as an illustrative baseline and PPG-only inference. On a protocol-locked 187-patient test, the baseline improves four repair and specificity endpoints by 1.82-6.69 percentage points, with all paired confidence intervals excluding zero; UHR increases by 0.67 percentage points (95% CI: -0.4 to +1.7). DFOT provides a common evaluation target for stronger mitigation methods. The code is available at https://github.com/Zongheng-Guo/When-Derived-Measurements-Mislead.

</details>


### [174] [A foundation model of numerical intelligence with cross-disciplinary generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28432)
*Chenghan Wu,Zongmin Yu,Liu Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes UNICON, a foundation model for numerical intelligence that can infer and apply predictive relations from numerical, graph-based contexts across diverse scientific and social systems, generalizing even to unseen disciplines and approaching or surpassing specialist performance, especially when combined with language-model agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While large language models show strong intelligence in processing and applying knowledge from text, many real-world scientific and social problems are expressed numerically rather than linguistically. There is a need for a general-purpose model that can learn from numerical context—such as graphs describing systems—and then generalize predictive relationships within and across different disciplines, including those not present in training. Existing specialist models are narrow and require retraining per domain, limiting scalability and transfer.

Method: The authors introduce UNICON (UNified In-Context Operator Networks), a foundation model designed to operate on graph-based numerical examples from various systems. Given a set of contextual examples from a system (graph-structured data with inputs and outputs), UNICON infers the underlying predictive operator shared across these examples. It then applies this inferred operator to new queries from the same system, all in-context, without retraining. The model is trained on a diverse corpus of numerical systems across scientific and social domains, encouraging it to learn general patterns of numerical reasoning. They also integrate UNICON with language-model agents that can orchestrate its use, interpret outputs, and compose multi-step reasoning pipelines.

Result: UNICON demonstrates strong performance across multiple scientific and social systems, often approaching the accuracy of domain-specific specialist models, even on disciplines that were not part of the training data. When combined with language-model agents, UNICON can exceed the performance of state-of-the-art specialist models in at least one discipline that it has not seen during training. Experiments further show that increasing the diversity of the training corpus significantly improves the model’s ability to generalize to entirely new disciplines, highlighting the importance of broad, heterogeneous training data for numerical intelligence.

Conclusion: The study establishes UNICON as a foundation model for numerical intelligence, capable of learning and applying predictive relations from numerical, graph-based context across domains without retraining. Its success on unseen disciplines and its enhanced performance when paired with language-model agents suggest that numerical and linguistic intelligence can be complementary. The findings argue that diverse training data are crucial for cross-domain generalization and position UNICON as a core component for building a wider ecosystem of AI systems that can reason fluidly over both numerical and textual representations in scientific and social applications.

Abstract: Intelligence is commonly understood as the ability to acquire and apply knowledge, adapt to unfamiliar situations and solve new problems. Large language models exhibit this capacity by inferring task-relevant knowledge from textual context and applying it to new tasks. Yet intelligence need not be confined to language. For scientific and social systems, we need models that acquire and apply knowledge from numerical context-an ability we call numerical intelligence. Here we introduce UNified In-Context Operator Networks (UNICON), a foundation model that exhibits numerical intelligence across disciplines. Using graph-based examples from a system as context, UNICON infers the predictive relation shared across them and applies it to queries from the same system. Across scientific and social systems, including those from disciplines absent from training, the same model approaches specialist performance without retraining. Combining UNICON with language-model agents yields further gains, enabling it to surpass state-of-the-art specialists in a discipline unseen in training. We further show that training-corpus diversity improves generalization to unseen disciplines. Together, these results establish UNICON as a foundation model of numerical intelligence and position it as a building block for a broader ecosystem of artificial intelligence.

</details>


### [175] [LeanCSP: A Framework for Certifying Constraint Reformulation and Solving in Lean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28459)
*Pablo Manrique,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a Lean-based framework to formally verify both constraint model transformations and solver results, achieving end-to-end certified solving for constraint programming problems with practical performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constraint programming is widely used for critical combinatorial tasks such as scheduling and verification, but current workflows rely on untrusted reformulations and solvers. There is a need for strong, machine-checked guarantees that (1) modeling transformations preserve the intended semantics, and (2) solver answers are correct, ideally in a reusable and scalable way across families of instances.

Method: They implement a framework in the Lean theorem prover that operates at two levels. At the formulation level, they formalize constraint models and prove parametric properties like equivalence, equisatisfiability, and correctness of symmetry-breaking constraints for whole families of problems. At the instance level, they develop backends that translate Lean-certified models to standard solver formats such as MiniZinc, SMT-LIB, and OPB, and then check solver-produced certificates (for satisfiable and unsatisfiable cases) back inside Lean, avoiding trust in external solvers.

Result: The framework supports end-to-end certification: from a high-level Lean specification through model transformations to solver calls and certificate checking, ensuring that claimed (un)satisfiability results are formally justified. Experiments show that verified symmetry breaking, proved once per problem family, can be reused for all instance sizes and dramatically reduces solver search, by up to a factor of 2×10^7. Despite this strong verification, full in-Lean certification for their largest instances remains practically feasible, completing in at most a few minutes.

Conclusion: Formal verification of constraint programming workflows is practical and beneficial. By integrating parametric reasoning about model transformations with certificate checking for individual instances, their Lean-based framework removes the need to trust external solvers while also delivering substantial performance gains via verified symmetry breaking. This suggests that similar proof-based certification approaches can scale to real-world combinatorial problem domains.

Abstract: Constraint programming is a core technology for solving complex combinatorial problems in scheduling, planning, configuration, and verification. Trusting its results therefore demands guarantees at two levels: that reformulations applied beforehand are semantics-preserving, and that solvers produce correct answers. In this work, we introduce a framework that addresses both verification levels in the Lean theorem prover: it can be used to prove formulation-level properties, such as equivalence, equisatisfiability, and the correctness of symmetry-breaking constraints, parametrically for entire problem families; and to check solver-produced certificates for individual instances via translation backends to external formats such as MiniZinc, SMT-LIB, and OPB. Combining both levels yields an end-to-end workflow that establishes the satisfiability or unsatisfiability of a constraint problem without trusting the external solver. Experimental results show that our framework's verified symmetry breaking also pays off in practice: a single parametric proof per problem family, reused across all instance sizes, reduces solver search effort by a factor of up to 2x10^7, while the entire in-Lean certification stays affordable, taking at most a few minutes for our largest instances.

</details>


### [176] [A report-grounded vision-language foundation model for colonoscopy from 280000 routine reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28466)
*Jia Yu,Yan Zhu,Yili He,Zilong Wang,Xinyang Jiang,Peiyao Fu,Ruijie Yang,Tianyi Chen,Siyuan Li,Zhihua Wang,Fei Wu,Quanlin Li,Xian Yang,Pinghong Zhou,Shuo Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EndoCLIP is a colonoscopy vision-language foundation model trained by recovering lesion-level image–text pairs from routine clinical reports, achieving strong performance on retrieval, reporting, and multiple diagnostic tasks, approaching expert endoscopist performance for malignancy classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Routine colonoscopy reports contain rich textual descriptions of lesions but are only weakly aligned with individual image frames, limiting the use of vision-language models for endoscopy. Manual lesion-level annotation is costly and does not scale, creating a need for methods that can exploit existing clinical documentation as supervision for image understanding tasks.

Method: The authors automatically recover lesion-level finding-to-frame correspondences from 280,476 routine colonoscopy records, producing 125,756 image–text pairs. They then train EndoCLIP, a colonoscopy-specific vision-language foundation model, and evaluate it on lesion-level image–text retrieval, structured report generation, and several clinical classification benchmarks. Performance is compared with general-purpose and biomedical vision-language encoders in zero-shot and linear-probe regimes, and with expert endoscopists for benign vs malignant classification in a blinded study.

Result: EndoCLIP consistently outperforms general-purpose and biomedical vision-language encoders on lesion-level retrieval, report generation, and six multi-centre clinical classification tasks in both zero-shot and linear-probe settings. For benign-versus-malignant lesion classification, a linear probe on EndoCLIP achieves performance close to that of 12 expert endoscopists in a blinded reader study.

Conclusion: Systematically recovering lesion-level alignments between routine colonoscopy images and textual findings enables training a strong, domain-specific vision-language foundation model without exhaustive manual annotation. EndoCLIP shows that routine documentation can be turned into scalable supervision, allowing clinical tasks and targets to be specified directly in language and then transferred across multiple downstream applications in colonoscopy.

Abstract: Vision-language models remain underused in colonoscopy despite the rich expert descriptions recorded in routine reports. These reports document lesion appearance, size and location but summarise entire procedures rather than caption individual frames, leaving clinical findings only weakly linked to the corresponding images. Here we develop EndoCLIP, a colonoscopy vision-language foundation model trained on 125,756 lesion-level image-text pairs progressively recovered from 280,476 routine colonoscopy records. Across lesion-level image-text retrieval, structured report generation and six multi-centre clinical classification tasks, EndoCLIP outperforms general-purpose and biomedical vision-language encoders in both zero-shot and linear-probe settings. On benign-versus-malignant classification, its linear probe approaches the performance of expert readers in a blinded study involving 12 endoscopists. These results suggest that recovering finding-to-frame correspondence can transform routine documentation into scalable supervision, enabling clinical targets to be specified in language rather than separately annotated for each task.

</details>


### [177] [Towards Autonomous Aircraft Surveillance from Nanosatellites through On-Board Inference and Generative Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28470)
*Antonio Delgado-Rosa,David Muñoz-Valero,Enrique Adrian Villarrubia-Martin,Juan Moreno-Garcia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a CubeSat-based workflow that performs on-board aircraft detection and uses a diffusion model for synthetic data augmentation, improving detection accuracy—especially for rare aircraft classes—while staying within nanosatellite compute and downlink limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Airborne surveillance from nanosatellites is constrained by limited downlink capacity, making transmission of raw imagery inefficient, and by scarce, highly imbalanced satellite datasets of aircraft, which degrade detector performance on rare aircraft classes. There is a need for a method that both reduces the reliance on raw data downlink and improves recognition of minority aircraft classes for timely, autonomous decision-making.

Method: The authors design a workflow where a 6U CubeSat carries a low-power edge tensor accelerator that runs an on-board, quantised object detector for aircraft. In parallel, they fine-tune a diffusion model via low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to generate synthetic images of minority aircraft classes. These images are automatically pseudo-labelled by an intermediate detector and combined with standard data augmentation to form a more balanced training set. The detector is then trained or retrained on this enriched dataset and quantised to fit the on-chip memory while meeting real-time frame-rate requirements.

Result: Using the balanced dataset formed by combining synthetic minority-class images with classical augmentations, the global mean average precision of the aircraft detector increases from 77.9% to 82.2%, while the minority class F1 score improves from 0.683 to 0.811. The quantised detector fits within the memory constraints of the on-board accelerator and achieves an estimated 25–30 frames per second on orbit, satisfying real-time surveillance needs.

Conclusion: The proposed workflow effectively addresses both the downlink bottleneck and the class-imbalance problem in nanosatellite-based airborne surveillance. By moving inference on-board and using diffusion-based synthetic data generation to balance training data, the system enhances detection performance, particularly on rare aircraft classes, while remaining practical for deployment on a 6U CubeSat. This contrasts with traditional bent-pipe architectures and supports more autonomous, real-time decision support from small satellites.

Abstract: Airborne surveillance from low Earth orbit is hindered by two interconnected bottlenecks: nanosatellites have a limited downlink budget, yet the conventional approach still transmits terabytes of raw imagery to the ground for processing, and open satellite datasets for aircraft are scarce and severely class-imbalanced. These limitations either delay timely decision-making or prevent standard detectors from learning robust representations of rare aircraft classes. In this paper, a workflow that combines on-board inference with generative data augmentation is proposed to address both limitations jointly. Inference is executed on a 6U CubeSat equipped with a low-power edge tensor accelerator, while a diffusion model fine-tuned through low-rank adaptation generates synthetic minority-class imagery. This synthetic output is automatically annotated, pseudo-labelled, by an intermediate detector and merged with classically augmented samples. The results show that the balanced dataset increases global mean average precision from 77.9% to 82.2%, with the minority class rising from F1=0.683 to F1=0.811, and that the quantised detector fits the on-chip memory and projects 25-30 frames per second on orbit. This approach contrasts with the conventional bent-pipe architecture, in which the satellite acts as a passive data collector. Therefore, the computational tests support the proposed workflow as a decision-support tool for real-time, autonomous airborne surveillance from nanosatellites.

</details>


### [178] [A Fuzzy Rule-based Neuro-Symbolic Approach for Pipe Severity Prediction in Sewer Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28481)
*Ngoc Thai Le,Thanh Ma,Umberto Straccia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose an interpretable neuro-symbolic system for sewer pipe severity assessment that separates visual defect detection from symbolic severity reasoning and outperforms end-to-end image classifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated sewer pipe assessment systems treat images as direct inputs to a black-box classifier, so operators cannot see how specific visual defects lead to final severity scores. Utilities need not only good predictive performance on highly imbalanced severity classes but also transparent, traceable reasoning that aligns with human inspection codes and practices.

Method: They design a two-stage neuro-symbolic framework. First, a perception module based on a Swin Transformer predicts 14 multilabel inspection CODE degrees from pipe images. Second, a symbolic reasoning module uses a decision tree (Weka J48) trained on ground-truth CODEs and severity labels; tree paths are converted into 19 human-readable IF–THEN rules. During inference, the system applies fuzzy logic: t-norms compute activation strengths of CODE conditions, rule confidence weights them, and s-norms aggregate them into severity class evidence. They test different fuzzy operator pairs (Product, Łukasiewicz, Hamacher) on 3,244 images with five imbalanced severity classes. Ground-truth severity labels come from consensus across five LLMs interpreting original inspector notes.

Result: Compared to a baseline that classifies severity directly from images, their framework improves accuracy, balanced accuracy, macro F1, and MCC by 17.9%, 12.2%, 23.0%, and 17.3%, respectively. It maintains competitive performance despite class imbalance while providing explicit mappings from predicted CODE degrees to rule activations and severity evidence.

Conclusion: Separating neural perception from symbolic, fuzzy-rule-based reasoning yields a sewer assessment system that is both more accurate and more interpretable than direct image classification. The approach enables traceable decision paths grounded in inspection codes and shows that neuro-symbolic, fuzzy logic-based models can be practical for real-world infrastructure condition assessment problems with imbalanced data.

Abstract: Standard automated sewer pipe severity assessment relies on direct image classification, creating a "black box" where the link between visual defects and final severity scores remains implicit. This study introduces a modular, fuzzy rule-based neuro-symbolic framework that bridges this gap by decoupling neural perception from symbolic reasoning. The perception module utilizes a Swin Transformer to predict 14 multilabel inspection CODE degrees directly from images. For reasoning, a DT, specifically Weka's J48, algorithm is trained on ground-truth CODEs and severity labels, and its paths are converted into 19 fixed IF--THEN rules. Inference operates via fuzzy logic: t-norm activations from CODE conditions are weighted by rule confidence and combined with corresponding s-norms to produce interpretable class evidence. We assessed Product, Łukasiewicz, and Hamacher operator pairs using a dataset of 3,244 images spanning five highly imbalanced severity classes. Ground-truth labels were robustly generated via consensus from five independent large language models analyzing original inspector notes. Our results show an improvement of accuracy, balanced accuracy, Macro F1 and MCC by 17.9%, 12.2%, 23.0%, and 17.3%, respectively, over image-only based classification.
  Overall, the framework combines competitive class-balanced performance with traceable reasoning from predicted CODE degrees to rule supports and severity evidence.

</details>


### [179] [SCOPE: Supply-Chain Operations through Coupled Policies for End-to-End Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28488)
*Yunhao Liang,Xianqi Cao,Pujun Zhang,Yuan Qu,Yongzhi Qi,Ningxuan Kang,Max Z. J. Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SCOPE, a unified AI framework that jointly optimizes multiple coupled replenishment decisions in supply chains, outperforming traditional siloed approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real supply chains, key replenishment decisions—assortment, sourcing, replenishment frequency, and routing—are tightly interdependent. However, companies typically optimize them in organizational and algorithmic silos (different departments and systems), which breaks these couplings and causes stockouts, excess inventory, and inefficient transportation. There is a need for AI methods that can capture and coordinate these cross-department operational dependencies to enable truly end-to-end planning.

Method: The authors propose SCOPE (Supply-Chain Operations through Coupled Policies for End-to-End Coordination), a composite policy-learning framework. Supply-chain entities (e.g., locations, products, facilities, routes) are represented as tokens and embedded into a shared operational context. For each token type, the model defines a decision interface (e.g., which products a location carries, its source facility, replenishment frequency, and routing choices). Decisions are made sequentially: each new decision is conditioned on the partial plan formed by prior decisions, and the full plan is evaluated via a shared system-level utility function that reflects end-to-end supply-chain performance. The framework is instantiated for urban fresh-retail replenishment, where service-frequency, assortment, capacity constraints, and road-network routing interact strongly.

Result: Using real operational data from two large-scale supply chains—Dingdong and JD.com—at different replenishment echelons, SCOPE is empirically compared to methods that optimize each decision stage separately and to practice-oriented baselines used in industry. In all tested scenarios, SCOPE achieves better performance on system-level metrics (e.g., fewer stockouts, lower inventory exposure, and more efficient transportation), demonstrating the benefits of coordinated, coupled decision-making.

Conclusion: The study concludes that modeling and learning cross-department operational couplings within a unified policy framework leads to superior end-to-end supply-chain performance compared with siloed optimization. SCOPE provides a practical architecture for token-based, context-aware, sequential decision-making across multiple operational levers, indicating a promising direction for future AI systems in complex, interconnected operations such as supply chains.

Abstract: Can supply-chain AI move beyond isolated decision modules toward unified operational planning? A complete replenishment plan specifies which products each location carries, which upstream facility supplies it, how often it is replenished, and how deliveries are routed. These decisions are operationally coupled: the selected assortment changes the demand and load passed to later stages; source assignment and replenishment frequency reshape the delivery requests; and route feasibility and cost, in turn, determine the system value of the earlier choices. Yet in modern supply chains, these decisions are often handled by separate departments and optimized through separate systems, which can lead to stockouts, inventory exposure, and avoidable transportation. We propose SCOPE: Supply-Chain Operations through Coupled Policies for End-to-End Coordination, a composite policy model that represents supply-chain entities as tokens, contextualizes them through a shared operational representation, and maps each token type to the corresponding decision interface. Each decision builds on the partial plan formed by earlier decisions while the completed plan is evaluated using a shared system-level utility. We instantiate this framework in urban fresh-retail replenishment, where service frequency, assortment, capacity pressure, and road-network routing interact strongly, and evaluate it on real operational data from Dingdong and JD.com, two large-scale supply chains operating at different replenishment echelons. Across both settings, SCOPE consistently outperforms methods that optimize each decision stage separately, as well as practice-oriented baselines commonly used in supply-chain operations. These results show that learning and coordinating cross-department operational couplings lead to more effective end-to-end supply-chain decisions.

</details>


### [180] [InfoOps Bench: A live information operations safety benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28503)
*Dorian Quelle,Lisa-Maria Neudert,Jonathan Bright,John Gallacher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a dynamic benchmark that evaluates how easily frontier language models can be used for state-backed information operations, showing large variation in refusal, fact-checking, and harmfulness across 17 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language models grow more capable, they may be misused for coordinated information operations by state-backed actors. Existing benchmarks are typically static, narrow, and easily saturated. The authors want a real-world, continuously updated way to measure whether models resist being co-opted into amplifying or generating such operations, and how safety trade-offs manifest across different providers and countries.

Method: The authors build a live benchmarking pipeline driven by more than 2,100 real information operations tracked from Russian, Chinese, and Iranian state-backed media assets. They convert these operations into prompts under four different framings, and evaluate 17 models from 8 providers. Integrity is quantified mainly as the refusal rate (percentage of prompts the model declines), along with qualitative analysis of responses: fabrication of details, harmfulness relative to source, and fact-checking frequency. They compare behavior across models and across benign vs. critical claims, including country-specific patterns.

Result: Integrity scores (refusal rates) vary dramatically across models, from 8.8% to 94.5%, with the spread not simply explained by model size. Many models can be used to support information operations, but they differ substantially: some add fabricated and more harmful content, others comply while partially defusing claims, and fact-checking rates span from 2.9% to 72.9%. Chinese-developed models (except GLM 5.2) sharply reduce compliance on factually grounded but China-critical prompts, showing a 48–70 percentage point drop relative to matched benign prompts.

Conclusion: The benchmark shows that frontier models differ widely in their resistance to being used for information operations, and that higher refusal often comes at the cost of rejecting benign content, highlighting a fundamental usability–safety trade-off. The dynamic, real-world nature of the benchmark helps avoid saturation and reveals provider- and country-specific behavior, including systematic drops in compliance on China-critical content by most Chinese-developed models. This framework can guide model evaluations, safety improvements, and transparency around geopolitical biases in model behavior.

Abstract: In this paper we present an active, constantly updated AI benchmark which measures the integrity of frontier language models against being co-opted for state-backed information operations. We draw on over 2,100 information operations from a live monitoring pipeline which tracks Russian, Chinese and Iranian state-backed information assets. Alongside this paper, we release a companion website that tracks the most prominent claims spread by state-backed media outlets, updated weekly, available from: pattrn.ai/research/infoopsbench. The dynamic nature of the benchmark makes it resistant to saturation.
  In the benchmark, we test 17 models from 8 providers across four prompt framings. We find that most models can be co-opted for information operations. Integrity scores, defined as the percentage of refused requests, range from 8.8% to 94.5%, an 85.7-percentage-point spread not explained by model size. Model choice also changes the character of the resulting operation. Some models fabricate details and produce output more harmful than the source material, others defuse claims even while complying, and fact-checking rates vary from 2.9% to 72.9%.
  Integrity against information operations is at least partly related to refusal to produce content even for benign claims, illustrating the challenge of balancing model usability with safety. With one exception (Z.ai's GLM 5.2), the Chinese-developed models sharply cut compliance on factually grounded but China-critical claims, dropping 48-70 percentage points relative to matched benign claims.

</details>


### [181] [Selective Credibility-Limited Belief Update](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28523)
*Theofanis Aravanis,Costas D. Koutras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper proposes a new framework, selective credibility-limited belief update, which generalizes and unifies standard Katsuno-Mendelzon update and existing credibility-limited updates by allowing source-dependent, partial acceptance of complex inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard belief update (Katsuno-Mendelzon) assumes any new information can be incorporated from every initially possible world, which is unrealistic when agents have limited trust or reachability. Existing credibility-limited updates improve this by restricting which successor worlds are credible from each source world, but they treat each epistemic input as an all-or-nothing package. In many situations, however, only part of a complex piece of information should be accepted or can be realized. The paper aims to model such selective, source-dependent acceptance within belief update.

Method: The authors define a new class of belief update operators called selective credibility-limited updates. For each source world and each epistemic input, a transformation function maps the original input to a weaker proxy formula that is still credible from that world; then a credibility-limited transition is applied relative to that proxy. They provide semantic characterizations (via transition systems or model structures) and corresponding axiomatic postulates for these operators. They further define and analyze two subclasses: (1) consistency-preserving operators, which guarantee that if the original input is consistent, then for each source world there exists a credible (consistent) proxy; and (2) maximal consistency-preserving operators, which choose, among all credible consequences of the input, a proxy that is maximally informative. They investigate representation theorems and relationships among these classes.

Result: The paper shows that selective credibility-limited update operators admit clean semantic and axiomatic characterizations. It identifies structural conditions under which an operator is consistency-preserving or maximal consistency-preserving, and proves that these conditions correspond to natural logical postulates. The authors demonstrate that standard credibility-limited belief update can be represented as a special case of their framework where the transformation is trivial (no weakening), and that Katsuno-Mendelzon belief update is recovered when there are no credibility restrictions and transformation functions are identities. Thus, the new framework strictly generalizes both existing approaches and captures a broader range of update behaviors, including those involving partial acceptance of compound inputs.

Conclusion: Selective credibility-limited belief update offers a unified and more expressive framework for modeling belief change with source-dependent credibility and partial acceptance of information. By introducing transformation functions that weaken inputs relative to source worlds and by characterizing well-behaved subclasses (consistency-preserving and maximal consistency-preserving), the framework subsumes traditional Katsuno-Mendelzon update and previous credibility-limited models. This shows that many familiar update operators are special cases of a more general theory that better aligns with realistic scenarios where agents only selectively accept complex information.

Abstract: Belief update concerns changes in an agent's beliefs induced by changes in the underlying world. Standard Katsuno-Mendelzon update assumes that an epistemic input can be incorporated from every initially possible world, whereas credibility-limited belief update restricts, for each source world, the successor worlds regarded as credible or reachable. Nevertheless, existing credibility-limited approaches treat the epistemic input as an indivisible whole, and therefore cannot represent cases in which only part of a compound epistemic input can be realized. We introduce selective credibility-limited belief update, in which the epistemic input is transformed, relative to each source world, into a weaker proxy before the credibility-limited transition is performed. We provide semantic and axiomatic characterizations of the resulting class of update operators. We then identify two well-behaved sub-classes; namely, consistency-preserving update operators, which require every transformed epistemic input to be credible from its source world whenever the original epistemic input is consistent, and maximal consistency-preserving update operators, which additionally require the selected proxy to be maximally informative among the credible consequences of the original epistemic input. Finally, we establish the generality of the proposed framework by showing that credibility-limited belief update is recovered as a special case, while Katsuno--Mendelzon belief update emerges when credibility restrictions are removed and the transformation functions are taken to be identities. These results demonstrate that the framework provides a unified and strictly more expressive account of belief update, encompassing established approaches while supporting source-dependent selective acceptance.

</details>


### [182] [MANTA: Multi-Agent Network Topology Adaptation for Self-Evolving Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28527)
*Mao-xun Huang,Jerry Wang,Yi-Cheng Lai,Zhengxin Zhang,Claire Cardie,Hen-Hsen Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MANTA, a framework that lets multi-agent LLM systems dynamically adapt their communication network topology during inference to better solve complex tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most multi-agent LLM systems use a fixed or offline-optimized communication topology, which can be suboptimal when task demands change or are not fully known beforehand. There is a need for systems that can adjust how agents are organized and communicate in real time, based on how collaboration unfolds, without increasing resource budgets or changing the external task interface.

Method: The authors present MANTA, a framework that (1) initializes a task-conditioned communication topology from prior structural experience, and then (2) monitors collaboration traces during deployment to detect when the current organization is inadequate. It then performs bounded structural updates that can change agent roles, communication links, execution order, information visibility, and validation pathways, while keeping the task interface and overall agent budget constant. The framework is evaluated across diverse benchmarks for information seeking, tool use, planning, workflow execution, and mathematical reasoning.

Result: Across five benchmarks, MANTA attains the highest average performance score of 74.0, outperforming the strongest baseline by 5.8 percentage points and achieving the best result specifically on the PlanCraft benchmark. These gains demonstrate the effectiveness of inference-time adaptation of the multi-agent communication topology.

Conclusion: Inference-time self-improvement can be extended beyond agent behaviors and prompts to the structure of collaboration itself. Dynamically adapting the communication topology of multi-agent LLM systems, as implemented in MANTA, yields consistent performance improvements over fixed-topology baselines across multiple complex problem-solving domains.

Abstract: Large language model-based multi-agent systems improve complex problem solving through task decomposition, agent specialization, information exchange, and intermediate validation. However, existing systems typically treat communication topology as a fixed design choice or an offline optimization target. We introduce MANTA, a framework for Multi-Agent Network Topology Adaptation that enables communication structures to self-evolve at inference time. Before execution, MANTA initializes a task-conditioned topology from prior structural experience. During deployment, it monitors collaboration traces and applies bounded structural updates when the current organization becomes insufficient. These updates can modify agent roles, communication links, execution order, information visibility, and validation pathways while preserving the task interface and agent budget. We evaluate MANTA against representative single-agent and multi-agent baselines on five benchmarks spanning information seeking, tool use, planning, workflow execution, and mathematical reasoning. MANTA achieves the highest average score of 74.0, outperforming the strongest baseline by 5.8 percentage points and obtaining the best result on PlanCraft. These results show that inference-time self-improvement can extend to the architecture of collaboration itself.

</details>


### [183] [Rethinking Inference-Time Scaling in Local Computer-Use Agents: Failure Modes and Compute Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28573)
*Woongkyu Lee,Jungwook Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically studies how different kinds of inference-time scaling (more context, more steps, structural decomposition, and parallel runs) affect the performance of small, locally deployed computer-use agents, finding that extra compute gives limited and often inefficient gains and changes how agents fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local autonomous computer-use agents are attractive because they better protect privacy, lower costs, and can be more practically deployable than large cloud models, but they run under strict hardware limitations. Prior work shows that giving big frontier agents more inference-time compute can improve their performance, yet it is unclear whether similar strategies work well for smaller, resource-constrained local models. A careful study is needed to understand when and how extra compute helps or harms local CUAs, and what design principles should guide their deployment.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic empirical evaluation of inference-time scaling along four dimensions—contextual (more history/context tokens), temporal (longer action horizons/more steps), structural (two-stage or decomposed agents), and parallel (multiple concurrent trajectories). They test several local models—Qwen3-VL-8B/30B-A3B, UI-TARS-1.5-7B, and OpenCUA-7B—on the OSWorld benchmark, analyzing both success rates and detailed failure modes such as trajectory stalls, repetition, and premature termination.

Result: They find that increasing compute generally yields diminishing returns and substantially alters failure patterns. Contextual scaling improves grounding and stabilizes trajectories, increasing accuracy up to a point, but its benefits saturate as token usage grows, and errors shift from loops/stalls to overconfident false successes. Temporal scaling reduces long stalls but does not significantly raise overall task success, suggesting that extra steps often just prolong incorrect behavior. Structural decomposition into two-stage agents can add planning and formatting overhead that harms performance for local models, while parallel scaling can alleviate some of these issues but at high computational cost.

Conclusion: The study concludes that naively adding more inference-time compute is not an efficient way to improve local CUAs. Instead, designers should use selective compute allocation, explicitly model and control common failure modes, and build agentic frameworks tailored to the strengths and constraints of local models. Efficient local CUAs must be engineered with awareness of both the limited hardware budget and the qualitative ways in which extra compute changes agent behavior and failure patterns.

Abstract: Deploying autonomous computer-use agents (CUAs) locally is increasingly important for privacy, cost efficiency, and practical usability, yet improving their performance under strict hardware constraints remains challenging. While recent studies show that inference-time scaling can improve frontier computer-use agents through additional computation during execution, its effectiveness for resource-constrained local models remains poorly understood. We present a systematic empirical study of inference-time scaling in local CUAs across contextual, temporal, structural, and parallel dimensions. We evaluate Qwen3-VL-8B/30B-A3B, UI-TARS-1.5-7B, and OpenCUA-7B on the OSWorld benchmark. Our results show that additional computation often yields diminishing returns while changing failure modes. Contextual scaling provides historical grounding that improves trajectory stability and task accuracy, but its gains saturate as token cost increases and failures shift from repetitive or stalled trajectories toward premature false successes. Temporal scaling similarly reduces max-step stalls, yet does not substantially improve task success, indicating that longer horizons often extend erroneous trajectories rather than correct them. We further find that structural decomposition can introduce planning and formatting overhead in local two-stage agents, while parallel scaling partially mitigates these failures at a substantial computational cost. Overall, our findings suggest that efficient local CUAs require selective compute allocation, failure-aware control mechanisms, and agentic frameworks designed around the capabilities and limitations of local models.

</details>


### [184] [DualG-MRAG: Decoupling Macro-Reasoning and Micro-Matching for Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28580)
*Jiacheng Tao,Qingyun Sun,Haonan Yuan,Ziwei Zhang,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes DualG-MRAG, a dual-tier graph-based framework for multimodal retrieval-augmented generation that separates global reasoning from fine-grained evidence matching to improve multi-hop QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal RAG systems struggle with complex multi-hop reasoning because instance-level matching cannot capture relationships across documents and modalities. Graph-based methods help but either explode in size and noise when using fine-grained visual features or lose important details when using coarse representations. A new approach is needed to balance global structure modeling with local evidence precision for multimodal reasoning.

Method: DualG-MRAG introduces a dual-tier architecture with two decoupled graphs: a Macro Graph for global structural reasoning and routing across documents and modalities, and a Micro Graph for fine-grained, local evidence verification. Retrieval is formulated as query-driven message passing in a GNN-based retriever, enabling dynamic propagation of relevance among heterogeneous evidence. For generation, instead of feeding isolated chunks, the model uses a dynamic programming decoding mechanism that extracts explicit reasoning paths from the GNN’s forward pass to guide the LLM.

Result: Across multiple benchmarks, DualG-MRAG achieves higher evidence recall and better accuracy on complex question answering tasks compared to existing multimodal RAG and graph-enhanced baselines, indicating more effective multi-hop reasoning and reduced retrieval noise.

Conclusion: Decoupling global reasoning from local matching via dual-tier graphs effectively addresses the trade-off between structural modeling and fine-grained evidence in multimodal RAG. The query-driven GNN retriever and path-based decoding provide explicit, structured guidance to the generator, yielding more accurate and reliable multimodal multi-hop QA performance.

Abstract: While Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (MM-RAG) has shown promising results, it still struggles with complex multi-hop reasoning tasks. Existing methods primarily focus on independent instance-level matching, which often fails to capture explicit relationships across modalities and documents. Although Graph-enhanced methods introduce structural modeling, they face a fundamental challenge in multimodal scenarios: incorporating fine-grained visual features leads to rapid graph expansion and retrieval noise, whereas coarse-grained representations cause the discarding of critical local evidence. To address this dilemma, we propose DualG-MRAG, a Dual-tier framework that introduces a decoupled architecture comprising Macro-reasoning and Micro-matching Graphs for Multimodal RAG. Specifically, to suppress retrieval noise by isolating global structural reasoning from fine-grained evidence matching, we construct a Macro Graph for global topological routing and a Micro Graph for precise local verification. Subsequently, to enable dynamic relevance propagation across heterogeneous evidence sources, we formulate retrieval as a query-driven message passing process via a GNN Retriever. Furthermore, to provide the generative model with coherent structural guidance, we introduce a dynamic programming decoding mechanism that extracts explicit reasoning paths directly from the GNN's forward pass, replacing the standard input of isolated document chunks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DualG-MRAG outperforms baselines in both evidence recall and complex QA accuracy.

</details>
